Structural Zn2+ molecules are depicted in yellow, and the metal bound at the regulatory site is shown in red. The side chains and backbone of the coordinating residues of the regulatory metal-binding site are shown in green. The view in (b) is rotated approximately 40◦ to the right and upward relative to the view in (a). The PDB ID for reduced S. pyogenes PerR is 4I7H.


A number of OhrR orthologs have been identified that control larger groups of target genes and, along with OhrR, constitute a peroxide-sensing subgroup within the MarR family. Among these are OspR, MgrA, SarZ, and SarA (Chen et al., 2009; Cheung and Projan, 1994; Lan et al., 2010; Luong et al., 2006). Pseudomonas aeruginosa OspR senses organic peroxides (Atichartpongkul et al., 2010), regulates a glutathione peroxidase encoding gene (PA2826), and also directly regulates the expression of genes involved in quorum sensing (see Section 21) and tyrosine metabolism (Lan et al., 2010). MgrA, SarZ, and SarA are global regulators in Staphylococcus aureus that sense peroxide and at least one other signal to initiate the adaptive shift necessary for growth inside a host (Cheung et al., 2008). MgrA is known to regulate, directly or indirectly, over 340 genes, including those encoding a variety of virulence factors as well as several antibiotic resistance determinants (Luong et al., 2006). SarZ, a close homolog of MgrA, also acts as a global regulator of genes involved in the metabolic shift to anaerobic growth as well as virulence and peroxide and antibiotic resistance (Chen et al., 2009). 

The crystal structure of the reduced form of B. subtilis OhrR binding to DNA is shown in Figure 10.1.4a (Hong et al., 2005). In its reduced form, OhrR dimers bind cooperatively at conserved AT-rich inverted repeat sequences within target gene promoters via winged-helix DNA-binding domains inserted in the major groove. This generally leads to repression (Chuchue et al., 2006; Fuangthong and Helmann, 2002; Mongkolsuk et al., 2002; Oh et al., 2007). De-repression is the result of a process that begins with the oxidation of a cysteine residue and usually renders OhrR incapable of binding DNA, thus leading to the de-repression of target genes (Fuangthong and Helmann, 2002; Panmanee et al., 2002, 2006). However, there is one known case where oxidized OhrR (OhrRox) binds DNA and functions as a transcription activator (Oh et al., 2007). 

OhrR and its orthologs sense peroxides via the oxidation of a conserved cysteine residue located in the N-terminal region of the protein. In B. subtilis, OhrR, OspR, MgrA, and SarZ, this “sensing” cysteine (C15 in B. subtilis OhrR) is sufficient for peroxide-mediated regulation (Hong et al., 2005; Panmanee et al., 2006), whereas other OhrR–MgrA members can contain an additional C-terminal cysteine residue (C127 in X. campestris OhrR) that participates in peroxide sensing (Panmanee et al., 2006). 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/d070d93a-9a38-4d08-ab41-83365fc0221e/b13d1bb4f09631710604825b9eccd7165f580b0d75f5ef65fb40778388a56f83.jpg)



Figure 10.1.4 (a) The structure of reduced 1-Cys B. subtilis OhrR (Green Ribbon) bound to the ohrA operator (DNA sugar phosphate backbone in brown); and (b) the known redox cycles of the sensing cysteine of B. subtilis OhrR. The position of the sensing cysteine is indicated in yellow. The PDB ID for reduced B. subtilis OhrR bound to the ohrA operator is 1Z9C. Adapted from Dubbs and Mongkolsuk (2012).


Similar to peroxide sensing in OxyR, peroxide-mediated derepression in both 1-Cys and 2-Cys OhrR–MgrA members begins with oxidation of the solvent-accessible sensing cysteine residue to a Cys-sulfenic acid (C-SOH) (see Figures 10.1.4 and 10.1.5) (Fuangthong and Helmann, 2002; Panmanee et al., 2006). Organic peroxide specificity in OhrR is due to hydrophobic amino acid residues lining the access channel to the sensing cysteine (Hong et al., 2005). At this point, the oxidation of the sensing cysteine to sulfenic acid does not affect DNA binding (Lee et al., 2007). OhrR inactivation leading to de-repression requires that at least one C-SOH derivative undergo further modification (Eiamphungporn et al., 2009). This second modification step differs in 1-Cys and 2-Cys OhrRs. 

Studies of the 1-Cys OhrR from B. subtilis have identified three possible fates for the oxidized sensing cysteine, C15-SOH (Figure 10.1.4b). C15-SOH can either form a mixed disulfide with bacillithiol, a recently identified 398 Da thiol, or react with the amino group of an adjacent amino acid residue to form a cyclic sulfenamide (Chi et al., 2011; Lee et al., 2007). Both of these modifications result in the loss of DNA binding, which can be reversed in vitro by reduction with dithiothreitol (Lee et al., 2007; Soonsanga et al., 2008b). The third possible inactivating modification involves the irreversible higher order oxidation of C15-SOH to cysteine sulfinic acid (C15-SOOH) for example (Lee et al., 2007). 

The 2-Cys class is typified by X. campestris OhrR, which contains an N-terminal sensing cysteine, C22, and an additional C-terminal cysteine, C127, that are both required for regulator function (Panmanee et al., 2002, 2006). Following organic peroxide-mediated oxidation, C22-SOH forms an intermolecular disulfide bond with C127 of the opposing monomer (Figure 10.1.5b) (Panmanee et al., 2006). As with mixed disulfide formation in the 1-Cys class, disulfide bond formation in the 2-Cys class is required for OhrR inactivation (Panmanee et al., 2006). The consequences of disulfide bond formation in X. campestris OhrR are apparent when comparing the crystal structures of the reduced and oxidized proteins shown in Figure 10.1.5a. The structure of reduced X. campestris OhrR is similar to the structure of reduced 1-Cys OhrR, with both proteins containing conserved residues thought to perform similar functions (Hong et al., 2005; Newberry et al., 2007). Disulfide bond formation in X. campestris OhrR induces structural changes that result in the rotation of each winged helix-turn-helix DNA-binding domain of the dimer by 28◦, rendering them unable to make productive contacts with DNA (Newberry et al., 2007).

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/d070d93a-9a38-4d08-ab41-83365fc0221e/9444b75c0d9cd58e2941fc21a4d60a04a40572611fe51ae74d07724455eb9cde.jpg)



Figure 10.1.5 (a) An overlay of the crystal structures of oxidized (green ribbon) and reduced (purple ribbon) dimers of the 2-Cys OhrR from X. campestris; and (b) the redox cycle of a 2-Cys OhrR. Note the peroxide-induced shift in the positions of the DNA-binding helices, which are colored dark purple and dark green and indicated by the bracket. The redox-active cysteines in reduced OhrR are colored brown, while the intermolecular disulfide bonds in the oxidized form are rendered in yellow (also indicated by an arrow in the left-hand monomer). The PDB IDs for oxidized and reduced X. campestris OhrR are 2PFB and 2PEX, respectively. Adapted from Dubbs and Mongkolsuk (2012).


Comparison between the oxidized forms of a 1-Cys and 2- Cys OhrR is not possible since no crystal structure is available for a 1-Cys OhrR containing a mixed disulfide. However, the crystal structures of the reduced and oxidized forms of the 1- Cys OhrR ortholog, SarZ, are available and give at least some insight into what may be occurring with a 1-Cys OhrR (Poor et al., 2009). SarZ, in which the sensing cysteine is oxidized to C-SOH, retains many of the hydrogen-bonding interactions found in the reduced protein and shows only minor changes in the positions of the DNA-binding helices relative to the reduced protein. This is in agreement with the finding that both the reduced and sulfenic acid–containing forms bind DNA (Oh et al., 2007; Poor et al., 2009). Mixed disulfide formation in SarZ induces a rotation of the monomers within the dimer that repositions the DNA-binding helices; however, the degree of rotation is less than that seen in disulfide-containing X. campestris OhrR shown in Figure 10.1.5a. It seems reasonable to assume that a similar monomer rotation and DNA-binding helix repositioning occur in the mixed disulfide form of a 1-Cys OhrR, especially given the fact that the 1-Cys B. subtilis OhrR can be converted to a functional 2-Cys OhrR by the introduction of a C residue at positions corresponding to either C127 or C131 in X. campestris OhrR (Soonsanga et al., 2008a). 

As with OxyR, both 1-Cys and 2-Cys OhrRs are recycled to the active form via reduction of the mixed or intermolecular disulfide-containing forms by a cellular reducing system. In B. subtilis, OhrR containing a mixed disulfide with bacillithiol is reduced through the action of the bacilliredoxins, BrxA and BrxB (Gaballa et al., 2014), whereas the disulfide-containing 2-Cys OhrR in Chromobacterium violaceum is specifically reduced via a cellular thioredoxin (da Silva Neto et al., 2012). 

Finally, a recent study has shown that phosphorylation of the sensing cysteine in SarA (a MgrA family regulator), MrgA, and SarZ of S. aureus plays a role in regulatory signaling (Sun et al., 2012). Phosphorylation of SarA, SarZ, and MgrA was shown to reduce DNA binding to target genes, thereby altering their expression. Phosphorylation and dephophorylation were mediated by a cellular phosphorylase (Stk1) and dephosphorylase (Stp1) pair. In vivo manipulation of the phosphorylation state of the regulators, through inactivation of the cellular dephosphorylase, reduced both virulence and resistance to cell wall–targeting antibiotics (Sun et al., 2012). Furthermore, in vitro oxidation of the regulators by peroxides blocked subsequent phosphorylation, raising the possibility that pools of the different modified forms of a given regulator could exist in the cell simultaneously (Sun et al., 2012). It will be interesting to see whether other Cys-based peroxide-sensing regulators are subject to similar modification. 

## 10.1.5 Conclusion

The importance of ROS in general and peroxides in particular to the life of the bacterial cell is evident in the number and distribution of systems that have evolved to sense their presence and prevent or mitigate their deleterious effects. We know a lot about the basic mechanisms of both the thiol- and mononuclear iron center-based peroxide-sensing systems and have a detailed structural understanding of representatives from both groups. But our understanding is far from complete, and there is plenty of new territory yet to be explored. 

While still primarily thought of as peroxide sensors, it is clear that the three groups of regulators described in this chapter have a wider sensory repertoire that likely includes inputs that have yet to be identified. The significant organism-specific structural differences, especially between OxyR and PerR orthologs, are also poorly understood. While in some cases variations in OxyR primary sequence appear to explain aspects of peroxide-dependent DNA binding, they could also reflect organism-specific regulatory signal preferences (Svintradze et al., 2013). The fact that OxyR orthologs can contain one, two, and possibly three cysteines involved in sensing complicates things even further. Therefore, the functional role of OxyR in E. coli is likely to be different from OxyR’s role in organisms that inhabit a different environment. This is certainly also true for PerR. 

Peroxide-sensing regulators are of interest due partly to their role in the adaptation of pathogens to growth in the host. The role of MgrA, SarZ, and SarA in virulence appears to be their primary function; however, OxyR (Charoenlap et al., 2011; Gryllos et al., 2008; Horsburgh et al., 2001; Lau et al., 2005; Rea et al., 2005; Sund et al., 2008; Wen et al., 2011) and OhrR (Atichartpongkul et al., 2010; Charoenlap et al., 2011) have also been shown to affect virulence. While peroxide stress defense plays a critical role, it is perhaps not surprising that OxyR and PerR are known to regulate genes encoding extracellular virulence factors (Melstrom et al., 2007; Ramsey and Whiteley, 2009; Wen et al., 2011). OxyR is also involved in the oxidation state–independent regulation of phase variation of cell envelope components in several organisms (Broadbent et al., 2010; Cota et al., 2012; Wallecha et al., 2002, 2003), and both OxyR and PerR have been shown to play a role in biofilm formation (Oh and Jeon, 2014; Seib et al., 2007; Shanks et al., 2007; Wu et al., 2008; see Section 22). Their role in adaptation to environmental change extends beyond host pathogen interaction, as different types of environmental stress such as osmotic (Hoper et al., 2006; Weber and Jung, 2002; see Section 11), heat (Gunasekera et al., 2008; see Section 13), and pH (King et al., 2010; Maurer et al., 2005; see Section 16) stress can all result in the induction of oxidative stress–related genes. OxyR in particular is necessary for the maintenance of osmotic stress resistance in E. coli (Rothe et al., 2012).

The role of oxidative stress in the action of antibiotics is unclear. Oxidative stress has been implicated in the killing mechanism of certain antibiotics (Dwyer et al., 2007, 2014; Kohanski et al., 2007), and it has been shown that the OxyR regulon is induced in response to beta-lactam and fluoroqinolone exposure (Akhova and Tkachenko, 2014). However, a recent study (Liu and Imlay, 2013) has challenged these findings, so the exact role(s) of oxidative stress in antibiotic killing has yet to be fully worked out.

For soil bacteria, these and other peroxide-sensing regulators are certainly important for adaptation to environmental changes and successful competition for living space in the crowded and diverse soil environment. One goal now is to examine the structure, function, and physiological role of these regulators in a diverse set of organisms to gain a better understanding of how the structure and function of these fascinating proteins are tailored for a particular lifestyle.

# Regulation of oxidative stress–related genes implicated in the establishment of opportunistic infections by Bacteroides fragilis

Felipe Lopes Teixeira, Regina Maria Cavalcanti Pilotto Domingues, and Leandro Araujo Lobo 

Departamento de Microbiologia Medica, Instituto de Microbiologia Paulo de G ´ oes, Universidade Federal do Rio de Janeiro, Rio de ´ Janeiro, Brazil 

## 10.2.1 Introduction

Molecular oxygen (O ) is essential for life, but all microorganisms have a limited tolerance for it. Although the hazard is more evident in obligate anaerobes and microaerophiles, high concentrations of $\mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ can also be harmful for aerobic organisms (Imlay, 2003). The endogenous generation of reactive oxygen species (ROS), like superoxide anions $\left( \mathbf { O } _ { 2 } ^ { - } \right)$ , hydrogen peroxide $\left( \mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } \right) ;$ , and hydroxyl radicals (HO⋅), is a major source of oxidative stress causing destruction of important iron–sulfur clusters, inactivation of key metabolic enzymes, and DNA lesions that can lead to cell death (Meehan et al., 2012). Usually, $\mathrm { O } _ { 2 } ^ { - }$ and $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ are generated by accidental autoxidation of nonrespiratory flavoproteins (Imlay, 2013), while HO⋅ formation can occur when ferrous iron transfers an electron to $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ (Imlay, 2003). 

Several mechanisms are used by bacteria to withstand the oxidative stress damage caused by ROS such as the production of detoxifying enzymes and iron-storage proteins (ferritins) (Rocha and Smith, 2013). Escherichia coli, for example, contains three superoxide dismutases (SOD), responsible for keeping ${ \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } } ^ { - }$ below nanomolar concentrations in the cell (Imlay, 2013; Imlay and Fridovich, 1991). Several enzymes responsible for degradation of $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ , like catalases and peroxidases, are also present (Imlay, 2013; Seaver and Imlay, 2001). Although it was once thought that obligate anaerobiosis was related to the lack of ROS-detoxifying enzymes (McCord et al., 1971), those enzymes can be found in strict anaerobes such as Bacteroides fragilis, which contain catalase, SOD, and several peroxidases (Sund et al., 2008). 

B. fragilis is an anaerobic Gram-negative bacterium commonly found in the gastrointestinal tract of humans (Shah, 1992). Even though it is considered an obligate anaerobe, since it is unable to grow in the presence of air (21% $\mathrm { O } _ { 2 } )$ , it can survive for up to 72 h in the presence of atmospheric oxygen (Rocha and Smith, 2004). B. fragilis is the anaerobe most frequently isolated from endogenous infections (Brook, 2010), and the high aerotolerance shown by this species has an important role in its virulence, since cells must survive the relatively high concentrations of oxygen in the peritoneal cavity (6–7% O ) (Sawyer et al., 1991). The B. fragilis oxidative stress response (OSR) is robust, with almost half of the genome undergoing changes in transcription due to oxygen exposure (Sund et al., 2008). Several detoxification enzymes and iron-storage protein genes are upregulated during exposure to oxidative stress, like ahpCF (alkyl hydroperoxide reductase), dps (nonspecific DNA-binding protein from starved cells), ftnA (ferritin), katB (catalase), sod (superoxide dismutase), and tpx (thioredoxin peroxidase), among others (Sund et al., 2008). To coordinate this response, a strong regulatory network is needed, but few regulators associated with OSR have been characterized in B. fragilis. The most studied so far has been OxyR. The purpose of this review is to provide an insight into the known regulatory mechanisms of the OSR in B. frag ilis. 

## 10.2.2 OxyR

OxyR is a 34.4 kDa tetrameric protein of the LysR family first described in Salmonella enterica serovar Typhimurium (Christman et al., 1985, 1989; Kullik et al., 1995). OxyR is a well-established transcriptional regulator generally found in Gram-negative bacteria and associated primarily with the global regulation of the peroxide stress response (Dubbs and Mongkolsuk, 2012; see Chapter 10.1). A cysteine residue becomes oxidized upon exposure to $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ , leading to a conformational change of OxyR that is responsible for its activation (Zheng et al., 1998). In E. coli, this can occur when $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ concentrations reach ∼100 nM inside the cell, a relatively low concentration compared to the concentration at which growth is inhibited (∼2 μM) (Gonzalez-Flecha and Demple, 1997; Seaver and Imlay, ´ 2001). The oxidized OxyR then promotes the transcription of several genes within the chromosome that help keep the intracellular $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ levels low (Dubbs and Mongkolsuk, 2012; Storz et al., 1990). Despite acting mainly as a transcriptional activator during oxidative stress, OxyR is able to regulate genes in both reduced and oxidized conditions, functioning as either an activator or a repressor (Dubbs and Mongkolsuk, 2012). In Xanthomonas campestris, for example, OxyR induces ahpC expression in the oxidized conformation and represses it in the reduced one (Loprasert et al., 2000). 

The B. fragilis OxyR was the first OSR regulator found and characterized in an obligate anaerobic bacterium (Rocha et al., 2000). It was identified after analysis of a peroxide-resistant strain that overexpressed KatB, AhpC, and Dps (Rocha and Smith, 1998). An amino acid substitution near a redox-active cysteine in OxyR caused a conformational change that led to a permanently activated regulator and, thus, constitutive expression of katB, ahpC, and dps. Further analysis showed that deletion of oxyR in both the peroxide-resistant mutant and the wildtype strain resulted in high sensitivity to $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } ,$ suggesting that OxyR is responsible for the peroxide resistance in B. fragilis (Rocha et al., 2000). 

In 2003, the role of the OxyR regulon on the OSR of the species was elucidated. It was demonstrated that OxyR regulon members are among the first genes induced during oxidative stress exposure and can be activated by as little as 0.5% $\mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ or 10 μM $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } .$ , with maximum expression of most of them around 2% $\mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ and 50 $\mu \mathrm { M } \mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ (Rocha et al., 2003). The tpx operon was also added to the pool of genes controlled by OxyR after transcription analysis showed that the operon was induced by an OxyR-dependent pathway during exposure to air or $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ (Herren et al., 2003). Later, it was shown that the absence of OxyR influenced the expression of ftnA during both hydrogen peroxide and oxygen exposure. However, the induction of ftnA by OxyR seems to be in an indirect way, probably by interaction with other regulatory mechanisms involved in iron homeostasis. Furthermore, even though the expression of ftnA was decreased in an oxyR mutant, some induction was still found after exposure to oxygen, suggesting that another unknown regulatory mechanism, both oxygen dependent and OxyR independent, is involved in ftnA expression as well (Rocha and Smith, 2004). 

More recently, it has been reported that the OxyR regulon genes are highly induced during exposure to $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } { \mathrm { : } }$ 28-fold for katB, 77-fold for ahpC, 70-fold for ahpF, sevenfold for tpx, and 25-fold for dps (Sund et al., 2008). This high induction is completely lost in the absence of OxyR. Also, it was shown that oxyR mutant strains were more sensitive to $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ and that growth arrest occurred faster in the oxyR mutant when cultures were suddenly exposed to aerobic conditions. An in vivo assay showed that the oxyR mutant was significantly impaired during intra-abdominal infection as well. The parent strain was threeto fivefold more likely to induce abscess formation than the oxyR mutants, suggesting a requirement for rapid $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ detoxification during this event (Sund et al., 2008). 

The OxyR regulon of B. fragilis comprises a relatively small set of genes when compared to other organisms like E. coli and Salmonella enterica. The induction of only five genes seems to be directly regulated by OxyR in the former (ahpC, ahpF, dps, katB, and tpx), while in the latter OxyR is responsible for the expression of almost 40 genes, protecting the cell from hydrogen peroxide toxicity by either inducing or repressing them (Chiang and Schellhorn, 2012). This would suggest a much smaller role of OxyR in the regulatory network of B. fragilis, especially on an organism with such a complex response during oxygen exposure. Even so, the OxyR regulon seems to have a major impact during the acute phase of oxidative stress, preventing the kind of damage that would induce the more robust metabolic response usually triggered during exposure to atmospheric oxygen (Sund et al., 2008). 

The association of the OxyR regulon with the peroxide response has been well documented and can be found in a variety of organisms. However, some important differences can be found among the several species that employ OxyR in its OSR. Studies with organisms like E. coli, Pseudomonas putida, and Rhodobacter capsulatus have shown that the OxyR regulon can be responsible for the regulation of thioredoxin genes (Hishinuma et al., 2008; Zeller et al., 2006; Zheng et al., 2001). Nevertheless, no evidence has been found in B. fragilis linking OxyR to the regulation of those genes (Reott et al., 2009). Also, in contrast to the E. coli OxyR that represses itself in both reduced and oxidized conformations (Christman et al., 1989; Tao et al., 1991), B. fragilis OxyR does not appear to control its own expression. Instead, evidence has been found that the regulator is constitutively expressed in this species, with its basal level not being altered by oxidative stress either (Rocha et al., 2000). Among the Bacteroidetes with a functional OxyR characterized, B. fragilis seems to be the only where OxyR is not permanently active. Both Porphyromonas gingivalis and Tannerella forsythia have an OxyR molecule with a locked-on oxidized conformation that results in constitutive expression of the OxyR regulon (Diaz et al., 2006; Honma et al., 2009). 

## 10.2.3 MarR

The multiple antibiotic resistance regulator (MarR) family is composed of winged helix proteins that bind directly to the DNA to control a variety of biological processes in both bacteria and archaea (Alekshun et al., 2000; Sulavik et al., 1995). The prototypical member of this family, MarR, was first described in E. coli and was associated with multidrug resistance in the species. In non-inducing conditions, MarR was shown to act as a repressor of the marRAB operon, binding to two direct repeats within the marO operator region to block the transcription of the operon (Martin and Rosner, 1995). New members have been identified in different species over the years, with several of these associated with the OSR. SlyA, in Salmonella typhimurium, is related to survival within macrophages and susceptibility to ROS (Buchmeier et al., 1997; Libby et al., 1994). MexR derepresses a multidrug efflux operon in Pseudomonas aeruginosa after sensing the oxidative stress generated from the bactericidal effect of certain antibiotics (Chen et al., 2008; Kohanski et al., 2007). Members of an oxidant-responsive subgroup of the MarR family, such as OhrR, OspR, MgrA, and SarZ, have peroxide-sensing mechanisms triggered by initial oxidation of a cysteine residue that is responsible for its regulatory role (Dubbs and Mongkolsuk, 2012; see Chapter 10.1). 

To date, little is known about the MarR family in B. frag ilis, but at least three putative proteins have been annotated as MarR family regulators in strain B. fragilis 638R and four in strain ATCC 25285. The protein known as BmoR (BF638R 0574) from B. fragilis 638R is the only one of them that has been studied to date (Teixeira et al., 2013). Evidence of the involvement of BmoR with OSR in B. fragilis was first found on a transcriptomic analysis of the OSR response in this species. When cultures were exposed to atmospheric oxygen, bmoR was upregulated 4.8- fold in the wild-type strain and 5.3-fold in an oxyR-null mutant (Sund et al., 2008). Though the role of BmoR is still unknown, those results indicate that its regulatory system is independent of OxyR, and since the absence of the latter actually leads to a small increase of the expression, BmoR could actually be trying to compensate for the lack of OxyR. Further studies show that the inactivation of bmoR has a direct impact on the B. fragilis OSR. The absence of the regulator on the mutant strain leads to decreased tolerance to $\mathrm { O } _ { 2 } ,$ as evidenced by a larger inhibition zone during exposure to atmospheric oxygen in soft agar tubes and by a longer lag phase during growth in oxidized medium (Teixeira et al., 2013). Despite the fact that the previous transcriptomic analysis showed no significant upregulation of bmoR during exposure to hydrogen peroxide (Sund et al., 2008, supporting information), the lack of BmoR in a mutant strain caused increased sensitivity to $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ as well (Teixeira et al., 2013). Nevertheless, the data collected so far seem to indicate a more important role for BmoR in atmospheric oxygen–related stress rather than hydrogen peroxide, especially if compared to OxyR. A soft agar tube assay performed with an oxyR mutant showed no difference in growth when compared to the parent strain (Rocha et al., 2007), while the bmoR mutant was significantly impaired (Teixeira et al., 2013). Meanwhile, disk diffusion assays with 3% hydrogen peroxide seemed to have a higher impact in oxyR mutants (Sund et al., 2008) than in bmoR mutants (Teixeira et al., 2013). Even though a more direct comparison between the two distinct regulators is definitely necessary to really correlate them, it gives us a better understanding of BmoR’s role in B. frag ilis OSR. 

Several MarR family members are encoded adjacent to the genes that they regulate (Perera and Grove, 2010). Although the 

BmoR regulatory network is still unknown, a gene annotated as a putative pyridine nucleotide oxidoreductase (BF638R 0572) is located directly upstream of the bmoR gene, in the same orientation, and likely to be a part of the BmoR regulon. BF638R 0572 has a conserved coenzyme A (CoA) disulfide reductase domain, described in Staphylococcus aureus as responsible for the reduction of CoA disulfide by NADPH, and employed by the species in a thiol–disulfide redox system (delCardayre et al., 1998). Due to the proximity of bmoR and BF638R 0572, either they share the same promoter and work as an operon or the bmoR promoter is located inside of the oxidoreductase gene. In either case, an impact in BF638R 0572 expression would be seen if BmoR binds to its own promoter region, as seen with other MarR family proteins (Alekshun et al., 2000). 

Despite possessing a similar structure, MarR homologs usually have significant differences in amino acid sequences and composition. Though this contributes to the broad diversity in its targets and regulatory mechanisms, it also complicates the comparison between the different proteins. Recently, for example, it was discovered that copper acts as a natural inducer for E. coli MarR (see Chapter 2.12). Copper(II) generates disulfide bonds between two MarR dimers by oxidizing a cysteine residue (Cys80) of the DNA recognition helix (α4); thus, it causes the formation of a tetramer that dissociates from the DNA (Hao et al., 2014). However, the cysteine residue in this case (Cys80) is not well conserved among MarR family proteins, like other amino acid residues near the winged helix motif are (Teixeira et al., 2013). BmoR is most likely not induced by copper(II) the way MarR is, since none of its two cysteine residues (Cys5 and Cys36) are located on or near the DNA recognition helix. BmoR is clearly related to oxygen-related stress in B. fragilis, but no information is available about the role of its homologs. The expression of two MarR homologs in strain 638R remained unchanged in transcriptomic analysis under oxidative stress caused by atmospheric oxygen and hydrogen peroxide (Sund et al., 2008), but their roles as regulators of oxidative stress caused by different stressors cannot be discarded. Proteins of the MarR family are also known to regulate the expression of outer membrane proteins and interfere with influx and efflux of substrates, as well as antimicrobial agents. More work is needed to clarify the mechanics of BmoR, as well as the other MarR homologs in this species. 

## 10.2.4 Fur

The ferric uptake regulator Fur recognizes and binds specific DNA sequences and functions as a global regulator for iron assimilation (Griggs and Konisky, 1989; see Section 19). When iron is abundant, Fur binds to a conserved DNA region, the Fur Box, blocking the transcription of genes involved in iron acquisition (de Lorenzo et al., 1987). Iron is a micronutrient essential for bacteria and is used as a cofactor of several important ROS detoxification enzymes, such as SODs and hydroperoxidases, but iron in excess leads to accumulation of hydroxil radical formed by the Fenton reaction (Touati, 2000), hence bacteria must strictly regulate its iron supply to avoid oxidative stress. Although, for anaerobic bacteria growing under anaerobic conditions, iron is found in the ferrous state and the formation of oxygen radicals is not a problem, when these bacteria are exposed to oxygen they must limit their iron availability in order to avoid formation of ROS. In B. fragilis, a ferritin-like protein (FtnA) is produced in response to iron levels, but the expression of FtnA is more robust when oxygen stress is present than under anaerobic conditions (Rocha and Smith, 2004). Two other genes coding for proteins of the ferritin family are induced under oxygen and iron stress: the DNA-binding and starvation protein (Dps) and the Dps-like proteins (Sund et al., 2008). OxyR indirectly regulates the ftnA gene, but another unknown regulator is thought to play a role in the process (Rocha and Smith, 2004). Although no evidence for an involvement of Fur is available in this case, other bacterial species have a clear link between oxidative stress and iron metabolism, and fur expression is regulated by other transcriptional regulators such as OxyR (Zheng et al., 1999). 

The expression of at least four outer membrane proteins (OMPs) of B. fragilis is regulated by iron levels (Otto et al., 1990). Among those, a 44 KDa OMP was characterized as the heme-binding protein HupA responsible for the transport of heme into the bacterial cell (Otto et al., 1996). The HupA protein is part of a complex of OMPs that mediate acquisition of heme and was found in an operon downstream of a putative Fur-binding DNA sequence, the Fur box (Otto et al., 1996). Furthermore, B. fragilis possesses at least two hemolysins, HlyA and HlyB, whose expression is regulated by Fur in an irondependent mechanism (Robertson et al., 2006). Hemolysins are cytolytic proteins with activity toward erythrocytes and neutrophils (Lobo et al., 2013), which may contribute not only to resistance to the host immune response but also to heme scavenging. Even though the mechanisms that regulate iron acquisition and the connection of iron metabolism with the OSR in B. fragilis are still elusive, recent evidence is showing that the connection between these two systems is paramount for survival in oxygenated tissues during infection (Rocha and Smith, 2013). 

## 10.2.5 Conclusion

The utility of the intestinal commensal species B. fragilis in maintenance of the health of the host – where, for example, it contributes to the digestion of complex polysaccharides (Spence et al., 2006) and stimulates the maturation of the immune system and drives it into a state of equilibrium that protects the host from autoimmune disorders (Mazmanian et al., 2008) – is well documented in the literature. Concurrently, B. fragilis is the most common anaerobic bacteria isolated from infectious processes in humans, is regarded as an important and opportunistic pathogen, and has numerous virulence factors described (reviewed in Wexler, 2007). Nevertheless, the details that lead to the duality of this interaction, and the transition from a commensal state to a pathogenic one, are not completely understood. Undoubtedly, for strict anaerobic bacteria, a prompt genetic response that translates to adaptation and survival to the oxidative stress naturally found in the host tissue and posed by the immune response is vital. Under these circumstances, B. fragilis may be considered a true survivor among anaerobic species. In fact, B. fragilis was shown to grow in nanomolar concentration of oxygen and actually benefits from this condition (Baughn and Malamy, 2004). The same group found that the inactivation of a single gene, called oxe, was sufficient to allow survival for longer periods at normal atmosphere and even grow in the presence of up to 2% oxygen (Meeham et al., 2012). Clinical isolates were more resistant to oxidative stress than fecal isolates, and a correlation between oxygen resistance and polymorphisms in oxe was observed (Meeham et al., 2012). The mechanisms that regulate the OSR in B. fragilis are not deciphered yet, but it is known that the OSR can increase resistance to antimicrobial agents and promote adaptation to harsh environments in a number of species (Poole, 2012). This is often accomplished by the induction of modification in the cell envelope, such as the expression of efflux and influx systems and alteration of the lipopolysaccharide (LPS). Comprehending how anaerobic bacterial pathogens regulate their OSR can lead to the discovery of new therapeutic targets or new methods to control and modify the microbial population that inhabit our bodies and have a significant impact on the treatment of infections caused by this group of microorganisms.

# Investigation into oxidative stress response of Shewanella oneidensis reveals a distinct mechanism

Jie Yuan, Fen Wan, and Haichun Gao 

Institute of Microbiology and College of Life Sciences, Zhejiang University, Hangzhou, Zhejiang, China 

## 10.3.1 Introduction

Emergence of the ability to respire on O in prokaryotes had been a great evolutionary success story, leading the way to more efficient energy use, faster growth, greater cell populations for natural selection to act upon, and eventually the birth of higher organisms (Madigan et al., 2012). However, every coin has two sides; along with all the benefits of aerobic respiration come the reactive oxygen species (ROS) and oxidative stress (Storz and Imlay, 1999). 

Superoxide $( \mathrm { O _ { 2 } } ^ { - } ) , \mathrm { H _ { 2 } O _ { 2 } } ,$ and organic peroxides (OP) are all ROS species commonly encountered by microbes (Dubbs and Mongkolsuk, 2012 (see Chapter 10.1); Imlay, 2008). They damage DNA, RNA, proteins, lipids, and virtually any cellular component, causing oxidative stress (Imlay, 2013; Storz and Imlay, 1999). Because one major source of ROS is the process of aerobic respiration per se, its ROS species become an unavoidable drag of the aerobic lifestyle (Imlay, 2013; Madigan et al., 2012). In defense, microorganisms have evolved sophisticated mechanisms to sense, respond, and battle against these damaging molecules. 

Figure 10.3.1 summarizes the comprehensive oxidative stress response dedicated to battling against various ROS and their damages. Although a diversity of genes are involved, and specific genes differ for different ROS species, the oxidative stress response can be broadly divided into two tiers of defense. The primary defense focuses on removing the ROS (e.g., catalase and peroxidase for $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ , and superoxide dismutase for ${ \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } } ^ { - } .$ ), and the secondary defense is concerned with cellular component repair or removal (Imlay, 2008, 2013). 

Also illustrated in Figure 10.3.1 are the four major sensortranscriptional regulators well characterized in model organisms, OxyR, PerR, OhrR, and SoxRS. As shown, each of these regulators specializes in sensing a set of different ROS species, with OxyR and PerR both being $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ sensors but found in different organisms, OhrR-sensing OPs and SoxRS-sensing superoxide (albeit in an indirect manner) (Imlay, 2013; see 

Chapters 10.1 and 10.2). OxyR was first discovered in a Salmonella mutant hyperresistant to $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ , and later found in a variety of microorganisms as the principle $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ -responding regulator governing the expression of a similar set of genes (OxyR regulon) across species. OxyR features a key cysteine residue (C199 in E. coli) prone to $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ oxidation. Although still a point of debate, this oxidation most likely results in the formation of a disulfide bond (between C199 and C208 in E. coli) that leads to OxyR activation and eventually increased expression of genes for scavenging enzymes such as peroxidase and catalase (Ahp and KatG) and other proteins that have a role in the oxidative stress response. Although OxyR is widely distributed, Bacillus subtilis employs PerR in its place. PerR is a repressor that can be deactivated by $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ through oxidation of a histidine residue, and its regulon overlaps to a great extent with OxyR (Dubbs and Mongkolsuk, 2012; see Chapter 10.1). It is interesting to contemplate how such distinct $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ -sensing mechanisms have evolved, but no compelling hypothesis has been put forward. SoxR senses superoxide-generating conditions (rather than ${ \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } } ^ { - }$ per se) through redox reaction of its 2Fe-2S clusters and often modulates the oxidative stress response by regulating the expression of its cognate transcriptional regulator SoxS (Imlay, 2013). OhrR senses organic peroxide in a manner similar to that of OxyR with $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ , except that it functions mainly as a repressor. In X. campestris OhrR (Mongkolsuk et al., 2002), it was found that OP-triggered formation of the intermolecular disulfide between the sensory residue C22 and the C127 residue of the other subunit of the homodimeric protein leads to the derepression of Ohr (organic hydroperoxide-resistant protein) and other oxidative stress–related genes. It is worth mentioning that a subset of OhrR proteins known as Cys-1 contains only one conserved sensing cysteine (e.g., C15 in B. subtilis). It forms either mixed disulfide with cellular free thiol or cyclic sulfonamide with the amino group of a nearby amino acid upon organic peroxide oxidation. 

While studies in model organisms undoubtedly have aided in putting together a general picture of the oxidative stress response, it is also of great value to expand the territory by characterizing other interesting organisms, especially those thriving in redox-stratified environments prone to ROS generation, and investigate their specific strategies of dealing with ROS. Shewanella oneidensis MR-1, a Gram-negative facultative anaerobe, is such an organism. Endowed by a diverse collection of iron- or heme-containing respiratory proteins encoded in its genome, this bacterium is able to respire on a variety of electron acceptors, such as oxygen, nitrate, Fe (III), and Cr(VI), to name a few (Fredrickson et al., 2008). These characteristics all put S. oneidensis in the front seat for ROS attack (Imlay, 2013; Storz and Imlay, 1999), and our study aims to shed light on how this microorganism defends itself.

## 10.3.2 Materials and methods

Bacterial strains and growth conditions: Detailed information of all bacterial strains and plasmids used in this study can be found in Jiang et al. (2014) and Li et al. (2014). All chemicals were bought from Sigma Co. (China) unless otherwise noted. Information for all of the primers used in this study is available upon request. Unless otherwise noted, E. coli and S. oneidensis strains were grown under aerobic conditions in lysogeny broth (LB) medium (Difco, USA) at 37 and $3 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ , respectively. When needed, the growth medium was supplemented with the following antibiotics: ampicillin at 50 μg/ml, kanamycin at 50 μg/ml, and gentamicin at 15 μg/ml. 

Phenotypic analysis of S. oneidensis upon $H _ { 2 } O _ { 2 } ;$ Disk diffusion assay was employed to determine the impacts of $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ on bacterial lawn. Briefly, 200 μl cultures of mid-log phase $( \mathrm { O D } _ { 6 0 0 } \approx 0 . 3 )$ were spread onto LB plates and incubated at $3 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ . After 6 h, paper discs of 6 mm in diameter loaded with 10 μl $5 \mathrm { M H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ were placed onto the bacterial lawn, and the plates were incubated for 16 h. Plating defect (viability) was also examined. Cells of mid-log phase were adjusted to approximately $1 0 ^ { 8 }$ cfu/ml with fresh LB, followed by four 10- fold serial dilutions. 5 μl of each sample was spotted onto LB plates without additives. The plates were incubated for 24 h or longer before being read. All experiments were repeated at least three times. 

Microarray analysis: For each strain under aerobic conditions, a fresh culture grown overnight was diluted to an optical density at 600 nm $\mathrm { ( O D _ { 6 0 0 } ) }$ of 0.01 with fresh LB medium. 20 ml of the diluted cultures were shaken in a 100-ml shake flask on a rotary platform (250 rpm) until the mid-log phase. $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ was added to the cultures to a final concentration of 0.2 mM, and cells were harvested 5 min after the addition. All cultures were centrifuged at 14,000 rpm for 30 s at room temperature, and the pellets were frozen immediately in liquid nitrogen and stored $\mathrm { a t } - 8 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ . In total, four biological replicates of cells grown under each condition were prepared. 

DNA microarrays were constructed by using polymerase chain reaction (PCR)-amplified fragments of each annotated open reading frame (ORF) from S. oneidensis, as previously described (Gao et al., 2004). Total RNA extraction, complementary DNA (cDNA) labeling, hybridization, slide scanning, and data analysis were conducted according to standard procedures used previously (Gao et al., 2004) (refer to NCBI GEO accession no. GSE31053 for raw microarray data). 

Mutagenesis and complementation: In-frame deletion strains derived from E. coli MG1655 and S. oneidensis were constructed by the Red recombination deletion method and the att-based fusion PCR method, respectively (Datsenko and Wanner, 2000; Jin et al., 2013). For S. oneidensis, a fusion fragment containing attB sites and a small genespecific segment were introduced into plasmid pHGM01, and transferred into S. oneidensis via conjugation. Integration of the mutational constructs into the chromosome was selected by resistance to gentamicin and confirmed by PCR. Verified transconjugants were grown in LB broth in the absence of NaCl and plated onto LB medium supplemented with 10% sucrose. Gentamicin-sensitive and sucroseresistant colonies were screened by PCR for deletion of the target gene. All mutants were verified by sequencing of the mutated regions. Site-directed mutagenesis was performed for the $o x y R _ { S O }$ and $o h r R _ { S O }$ genes according to a method described previously (Sun et al., 2013). Plasmids pHG101 and pHG102 (Wu et al., 2011) were used for genetic complementation of mutants using either the native promoter of the gene or the constitutively active S. oneidensis arcA promoter (Gao et al., 2010), respectively. All vectors were maintained in E. coli WM3064 and transferred into relevant S. oneidensis strains via conjugation, and their presence was confirmed by plasmid purification and restriction enzyme digestion. 

Expression assay: Quantitative reverse transcriptase PCR (qRT-PCR) analyses were carried out with an ABI7300 96-well qRT-PCR system (Applied Biosystems, USA), as described previously (Yuan et al., 2011). The expression level of each gene was determined from three replicates in a single realtime qRT-PCR experiment. The cycle threshold (CT) values for each gene of interest were averaged among replicates and normalized against the CT value of the 16S rRNA gene. 

For the β-galactosidase activity assay, ∼400 bp upstream sequences of the gene of interest were amplified and placed in front of the full-length E. coli lacZ gene on plasmid pTP327 (Gao et al., 2010). Cell samples were prepared as in the microarray analysis. During the assay, cells were lysed with lysis buffer (0.25 M Tris-HCl (pH 7.5), 0.5% Triton X-100), and β-galactosidase activity was determined by monitoring color development at 420 nm using a Sunrise microplate reader (Tecan, Switzerland) (Wu et al., 2011). The protein concentration of the cell lysates was determined by using a Bradford assay with bovine serum albumin (BSA) as a standard (Bio-Rad, USA). 

Bacterial one-hybrid assay: A bacterial one-hybrid (B1H) system was used to investigate DNA–protein interactions in vivo in E. coli cells (Guo et al., 2009). Briefly, plasmid constructs were created by cloning the bait DNA and target $\mathrm { O x y R } _ { \mathrm { S O } }$ into the pBXcmT and pTRG vectors and verified by sequencing. The resultant plasmids were used to cotransform BacterioMatch II Validation Reporter competent cells on M9 salt agar plates containing 25 mg/ml chloramphenicol and 12.5 mg/ml tetracycline with or without 3- amino-1,2,4-triazole (3-AT). The plasmid pair of pBXcmT-$\mathrm { P } _ { c y d } { \mathrm { - p T R G - C r p } _ { \mathrm { S O } } }$ was used as the positive control, and a 300 bp DNA fragment of the 16S rRNA gene promoter $\mathrm { ( p B X c m T - P } _ { I 6 S } )$ was used as the negative control. The plates were incubated for 24 h and then moved to room temperature for an additional 16 h (the colonies indicating positive interactions usually appeared between 18 and 24 h). 

The positive interactions were confirmed by streaking colonies onto plates containing both 3-AT and streptomycin (12.5 mg/ml). 

## 10.3.3 Results and discussion

## 10.3.3.1 ${ \bf H } _ { 2 } { \bf O } _ { 2 }$ -exposure-induced transcriptomic changes in S. oneidensis

For a global evaluation of the S. oneidensis oxidative stress response, transcriptomic changes elicited by the most common ROS, $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } ,$ , were profiled with microarrays carrying probes for 4648 ORFs. Compared to unstimulated samples, a 5 min treatment of 0.2 mM $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ is sufficient to trigger significant changes in almost a quarter of the genome. Figure 10.3.2 summarizes the results for 671 of the 1097 significantly changed genes (excluding genes encoding hypothetical proteins; please refer to table S1 of Jiang et al. (2014) for a full list), organized according to their functional categories, color coded by the direction of changes (i.e., up- or downregulated), and ranked by the proportion of upregulated genes. As shown, genes involved in amino acid synthesis (A), protein synthesis (L), and transcription (P) were collectively downregulated, whereas those in signal transduction (O) were predominately upregulated, consistent with cells reallocating resources and energy from supporting growth to dealing with the abrupt environmental challenge. Similar general patterns were also reported for other stresses (Gao et al., 2004; Qiu et al., 2005, 2006). It is interesting to note that genes of mobile and extrachromosomal element functions (J) were also largely upregulated (albeit no more than fourfold), probably reflecting a cellular strategy for survival by exploring opportunities in altering its genome; see Chapter 2.10. 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/d070d93a-9a38-4d08-ab41-83365fc0221e/27e56ab8a070887501b3e1181b26fc02f15ab9ae1637b90fb6a370336c5e405f.jpg)



Figure 10.3.2 Summary of transcriptomic changes induced by $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ exposure. Transcriptomic changes were evaluated with microarray. On the y-axis, bar height corresponds to the number of significantly changed genes (blue, downregulated; red, upregulated) for each functional category. On the x-axis, the functional categories are ranked such that the proportion of upregulated genes increases from left to right. A, amino acid biosynthesis; B, biosynthesis of cofactors, prosthetic groups, and carriers; $\mathrm { C } ,$ cell envelope; D, cellular processes; $\mathrm { E } ,$ central intermediary metabolism; F, DNA metabolism; G, energy metabolism; H, fatty acid and phospholipid metabolism; J: mobile and extrachromosomal element functions; K, protein fate; L, protein synthesis; M, purines, pyrimidines, nucleosides, and nucleotides; N, regulatory functions; O, signal transduction; P, transcription; Q, transport and binding proteins; R, unknown function. Hypothetical proteins were not included in this figure.


To have a more focused view of the potential mechanism employed by S. oneidensis to combat oxidative stress, we zoomed in on the most significantly upregulated genes, excluding those known to be involved in the general stress response, such as rpoS (Pratt and Silhavy, 1996). As shown in Figure 10.3.3, six of the 10 most upregulated genes are analogs of $\mathrm { O x y R } _ { \mathrm { E C } }$ regulon members, coding for $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ -scavenging proteins including catalase (katB, katG-1) and alkyl hydroperoxide reductases (ahpF, ahpC), iron storage protein for DNA protection (dps), as well as disulfide reduction proteins (SO1563) (see Chapter 10.1). On the other hand, another set of welldefined $\mathrm { O x y R } _ { \mathrm { E C } }$ regulon genes encompassing those required in heme synthesis (hemH-1), FeS cluster assembly (sufC, sufE), as well as iron import control (fur), which usually constitute the secondary defense against ROS, remained unchanged upon $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ treatment. Furthermore, a number of unexpected genes increased significantly (Figure 10.3.3). Although the uniqueness of upregulated ccpA, a gene coding for a c-type cytochrome peroxidase that reduces $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ with electrons from a cytochrome c (Mongkolsuk et al., 1998), can be easily discounted as achieving the same goal but with different tools, seeing ohr and pspA up on the list was by all means unusual. 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/d070d93a-9a38-4d08-ab41-83365fc0221e/7b3f0cc34cc107a4479b1a57219334bd2c2949a8cd079089ec9b42fc93423be8.jpg)



Figure 10.3.3 Venn diagram showing the overlap and distinction of the 10 most significantly $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ -induced genes, and the analogs of $\mathrm { \Delta \mathrm { \Omega } \mathrm { \mathrm { { O x y R } _ { E C } } } }$ regulon members in S. oneidensis MR-1. Gene names are arranged alphabetically in each group; numbers in parentheses next to gene names are the value of log 2 transformed fold changes calculated from microarray data. ahpC, Alkyl hydroperoxide reductase, C subunit; ahpF, alkyl hydroperoxide reductase, F subunit; dps, Dps family protein; katB, catalase; katG-1, catalase/peroxidase; ccpA, cytochrome c 551 peroxidase; ohr, organic hydroperoxide resistance protein; $p s p A ;$ , phage shock protein A; SO4170, unknown function; fur, ferric uptake regulation protein; hemH-1, ferrochelatase; sufE, cysteine desulfurase; sufC, FeS assembly ATPase.


The $p s p A$ gene encodes a phage shock protein that was thought to be involved in the maintenance of the protonmotive force (PMF), and had been found to be induced upon phage infection, high temperature, osmotic stress, and so on in other Gram-negative bacteria, but not upon $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ stimulation (Michan et al., 1999). However, most interestingly, ohr, encoding an organic hydroperoxide resistance protein well characterized in E. coli, is known to respond specifically to OP under the regulation of OhrR, rather than $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ (Jenkins et al., 2008; Mongkolsuk et al., 2002; Rowley et al., 2006; see Chapter 10.1). The significant increase of ohr expression observed here hints at the potential existence of cross-talking between the $\mathrm { O x y R } _ { \mathrm { S O } }$ and $\mathrm { O h r R } _ { \mathrm { S O } }$ regulation in S. oneidensis. 

Collectively, these data suggest that, in addition to certain features of the general theme described in Figure 10.3.1, the response to $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ -induced oxidative stress in S. oneidensis has its own distinctive flavor. 

## 10.3.3.2 Cross-talk of OxyR and $\pmb { \mathrm { o h r R } } _ { \mathsf { S O } }$ regulation

Microarray analysis yielded a general picture and identification of potential primary defenders against $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ -induced oxidative stress in S. oneidensis, but did not generate enough clues to identify the regulator-in-chief. Therefore, we moved on to 

search for potential global oxidative stress sensors and regulators by BLASTing the known E. coli OxyR $( \mathrm { O x y R } _ { \mathrm { E C } } )$ and SoxRS, B. subtilis PerR, and OhrRs from Xanthomonas campestris and Pseudomonas aeruginosa against the S. oneidensis proteome. While no analog was found for SoxRS or PerR, SO1328 and SO0977 were identified as putative homologs of OxyR and OhrR, respectively. 

As shown in Figure 10.3.4a (left panel), deletion of SO1328 indeed resulted in insensitivity to $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ in S. oneidensis. Similarly, ΔSO0977 was also insensitive to an $\mathrm { O P }$ compound, tBOOH (tertiary-butylhydroperoxide) (Figure 10.3.4a, right panel), consistent with their roles as $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ sensor and OP sensor in other organisms (Dubbs and Mongkolsuk, 2012; Mongkolsuk et al., 2002). More convincingly, the key cysteine residues for sensing respective oxidants (Dubbs and Mongkolsuk, 2012; Fuangthong and Helmann, 2002) were also identified (Figure 10.3.4a) (Jiang et al., 2014; Li et al., 2014). We thereby named SO1328 as oxy $R _ { S O } .$ , and SO0977 as $o h r R _ { S O }$ .

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/d070d93a-9a38-4d08-ab41-83365fc0221e/f48b84260062ac8b0dc6af10c93e08ba58c73a71b7ddafa10929affb0cc6974d.jpg)

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/d070d93a-9a38-4d08-ab41-83365fc0221e/072668f038d8e08079fcb69703872fee785acf1054c5276614a3fef4e99cee10.jpg)



Figure 10.3.4 Cross-talk of S. oneidensis OxyR and OhrR regulation. Data presented in the left half of the figure were all collected using $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ as the source of oxidative stress, and those in the right using tBOOH. (a) OxyR and OhrR govern $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ and OP response in S. oneidensis, respectively. (b) KatB is the main protein protecting cells from $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } ,$ whereas Ohr and Ahp battle against OP. (c) OxyR regulates multiple catalases and peroxidases in response to H $_ { \cdot 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } ,$ and acts as both activator and repressor. OhrR regulates ohr and SO1563 expression in response to OP, while ahp is not controlled by OhrR. Bar height corresponds to the expression level (measured with quantitative reverse transcriptase polymerase chain reaction (Q-RT-PCR)) relative to that of the corresponding gene in an unstressed wild-type strain. (d) OhrR also mediates SO1563 response to $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } ;$ , while $\mathrm { O x y R }$ regulates its regulon in response to OP. Bar height represents the same as in (c), just measured with the β-galactosidase assay.


To further validate that these two potential regulators indeed mediate cellular responses to oxidative stress, we examined their impact on expression of $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ -induced genes identified in the microarray analysis. Interestingly, disc diffusion assay results in Figure 10.3.4b demonstrated that despite the upregulation of multiple catalases and peroxidases, only KatB is indispensable for combating $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ stress, indicating its role as the single predominant catalase in S. oneidensis. Although ohr was found to be upregulated upon $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ addition, and deletion of ohr was detrimental to cell survival in the presence of tBooH (Figure 10.3.4b, right), it did not appear to be required to combat $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ . Similarly, compared to the wild type, ΔahpC was also mildly more sensitive to tBooH, but identical in $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ sensitivity. Expression of the most strongly $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ -induced genes in an $\Delta o x y R _ { S O }$ mutant displayed three distinct patterns (Figure $1 0 . 3 . 4 c ,$ , left panel): 1), no longer responsive to $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ , but expression stayed high in the $\Delta o x y R _ { S O }$ strain (katB and $d p s )$ , consistent with OxyR de-repressing their expression upon $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ stimulation and the $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ -insensitive-but-hyperresistant phenotype of $\Delta o x y R _ { S O }$ (Figure $1 0 . 3 . 4 \mathrm { a } ) ; 2 )$ , irresponsive to $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ , and expression remained low in the $\Delta o x y R _ { S O }$ strain $( k a t G -$ 1, ahpC, and $c c p A )$ , suggesting that $\mathrm { O x y R } _ { \mathrm { S O } }$ activates their expression in response to $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } ;$ and ${ } ^ { 3 ) , }$ , responsive to $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ despite the absence of $\mathrm { O x y R } _ { \mathrm { S O } }$ (ohr and 1563), indicating that their induction upon $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ stimulation was OxyR independent. These data confirm that $\mathrm { O x y R } _ { \mathrm { S O } }$ indeed is the chief regulator in charge of managing the defense against $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ in S. oneidensis by modulating katB expression. However, the OxyR-independent response of ohr strongly hints at potential cross-talk. Therefore, after confirming that the response of ohr to tBooH (Figure 10.3.4c, right panel) was truly mediated by oh $\cdot R _ { S O }$ , we examined whether there is cross-talk between $\mathrm { O x y R } _ { \mathrm { S O } }$ and $\mathrm { O h r R } _ { \mathrm { S O } }$ regulating the oxidative stress response. As shown in Figure 10.3.4d, SO1563 expression was greatly de-repressed in the $\Delta o h r R _ { S O }$ strain, and became barely responsive to $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ (left panel) whereas the katB, ahpC, and ccpA genes all displayed an OxyR-dependent response to tBOOH (right panel). 

Taken together, these data suggest that although S. oneidensis employs OxyR-regulated catalase KatB and OhrR-regulated Ohr to protect the cell against oxidative stresses posted by $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ and organic hydroperoxides, the regulatory properties of $\mathrm { O x y R } _ { \mathrm { S O } }$ differ greatly from those of $\mathrm { O x y R } _ { \mathrm { E C } } ,$ and cross-talk exists between the two systems in that the two regulators both respond to $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ as well as tBOOH, and the $\mathrm { O x y R } _ { \mathrm { S O } }$ -regulated alkyl hydroperoxide reductase (ahp) plays a role in cellular resistance to organic hydroperoxide. 

## 10.3.3.3 S. oneidensis $\tt o x y R _ { 5 0 }$ is distinctive from its E. coli counterpart

Despite also being the central mediator of $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ response in S. oneidensis, $\mathrm { O x y R } _ { \mathrm { S O } }$ appears to have diverged considerably from $\mathrm { O x y R } _ { \mathrm { E C } }$ in terms of both sequence (35% similarity) and function (i.e., it de-represses, rather than activates, the main catalase in response to $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } .$ , and is responsive to tBOOH). Although such divergence was not compelling in superimposed computational protein structure models (Jiang et al., 2014), reciprocal complementation of OxyR (i.e., using $\mathrm { O x y R } _ { \mathrm { E C } }$ to complement the S. oneidensis oxy $R _ { S O }$ mutant, and vice versa) was not possible in either bacterium (Figure 10.3.5a), suggesting that the OxyR counterparts in E. coli and S. oneidensis were not at all interchangeable. 

Moreover, since OxyR belongs to the LysR regulator family known to exert their regulation by binding directly to the upstream sequences of their targets, we also examined the DNA target specificity of OxyR by both scanning for the consensus sequence of other known OxyR proteins in the S. oneidensis genome computationally, and inspecting the interaction of $\mathrm { O x y R } _ { \mathrm { S O } }$ with promoters of several genes belonging to regulons of other bacterial OxyRs using the B1H assay. Figure 10.3.5b summarizes the results of the B1H assay: Although positive results were obtained for $\mathrm { O x y R } _ { \mathrm { S O } }$ and promoters of katB, $d p s ,$ , ahpC, and $c c p A$ genes, no interaction was detected for targets of other OxyRs $( b f r$ and $f t n )$ . Meanwhile, the computational searches returned no hit either, suggesting that $\mathrm { O x y R } _ { \mathrm { S O } }$ binds to sequences unique to other OxyRs. 

Even though a $\Delta o x y R _ { S O }$ mutant of S. oneidensis was moreresistant to $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ owing to KatB de-repression, viability of thisstrain was compromised in the absence of $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ or any exter-nal stress (Figure 10.3.6a). Since no SoxSR analog is encodedin the genome, and given the significant difference of $\mathrm { O x y R } _ { \mathrm { S O } }$ 号from $\mathrm { O x y R } _ { \mathrm { E C } }$ described here, we considered the possibility that$\mathrm { O x y R } _ { \mathrm { S O } }$ may also regulate the cellular defense against otherROS species. To test this possibility, we attempted to modulatethe Fenton-reaction-associated hydroxyl radical (OHa) level by ˙manipulating the medium’s iron concentration, and accidentallydiscovered that supplementation of iron in the medium rescuedthe viability defect, while 50 μM of the iron chelator dipyridylis sufficient to abolish $\Delta o x y R _ { S O }$ mutant growth without affect-ing the wild type (Figure 10.3.6a). Supplementation of $\mathrm { M n } ^ { 2 + }$ alsohelped considerably with viability (Figure 10.3.6a). But a fullrestoration to the wild-type level was only achieved with iron,suggesting that the observed viability defect was probably dueto cellular iron deficiency.

Since intracellular iron homeostasis is controlled by iron storage proteins (Almiron et al., 1992), namely ferritins (Ftn), bacterioferritins (Bfr), and Dps, and $d p s$ was found to be regulated by $\mathrm { O x y R } _ { \mathrm { S O } } ,$ one logical explanation for the observed iron-deficiency-caused viability defect of the $\Delta o x y R _ { S O }$ mutant was emerging. To be thorough, we examined the expression of all four genes coding for iron storage proteins found in the S. oneidensis genome (SO0139 for a Ftn; SO1112 and SO1111 for Bfr1 and Bfr2, respectively; and $d p s$ for a Dps), in the presence and absence of $\mathrm { O x y R } _ { \mathrm { S O } }$ and $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ (Figure 10.3.6b). In addition, mutants lacking individual iron storage proteins were also tested for $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ sensitivity and viability under no stress (Figure 10.3.6c). Our results confirmed that deletion of $\mathrm { O x y R } _ { \mathrm { S O } }$ led to de-repression of Dps, which probably caused overscavenging of iron from the media, and in turn resulted in the iron-deficiencycaused viability defect of the $\Delta o x y R _ { S O }$ mutant, whereas other Ftn and Bfr were not involved. Interestingly, deletion of dps in $\Delta o x y R _ { S O }$ did not restore the wild-type-level viability, indicating that additional players answering to the calls of $\mathrm { O x y R } _ { \mathrm { S O } }$ remain to be identified.

(b) Strength of interaction between OxyR and selected promoters determined by bacteria one-hybrid assay.

Jointly, these data demonstrate that $\mathrm { O x y R } _ { \mathrm { S O } }$ of S. oneidensis differs significantly in multiple aspects from its E. coli counterpart, despite sharing the main job title as the key regulator against $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ . 

## 10.3.3.4 S. oneidensis Ohr regulates a new member

As shown in Figure 10.3.4, although SO1563 was found to be highly upregulated in response to $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ in the microarray analysis, its transcriptional change actually followed the same pattern as ohr: They were both upregulated upon either $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ or tBOOH stress, such upregulation required Oh $\mathrm { \cdot R } _ { \mathrm { S O } } ,$ , and $\mathrm { O h r R } _ { \mathrm { S O } }$ appeared to repress both ohr and SO1563 in unstressed wildtype cells. In addition, a DNA segment resembling the highly conserved OhrR-binding motif (Atichartpongkul et al., 2010; Chuchue et al., 2006; da Silva Neto et al., 2012; Fontenelle et al., 2011; Mongkolsuk et al., 2002; Oh et al., 2007) was also identified in the upstream region of SO1563, but not in the canonical operator position like in the case of ohr (Li et al., 2014). These features led us to conclude that SO1563, encoding a Gpx family protein, is very likely to be under the direct regulation of $\mathrm { O h r R } _ { \mathrm { S O } ^ { \ddagger } }$ , adding a new member to the $\mathrm { O h r R } _ { \mathrm { S O } }$ regulon. Since 

SO1564 and SO1563 are divergently transcribed, sharing the $\mathrm { O h r R } _ { \mathrm { S O } }$ -binding motif, we also examined the SO1564 expression in the presence and absence of OhrR and tBooH, and found that neither $\mathrm { O h r R } _ { \mathrm { S O } }$ nor tBooH had any impact (Qiu et al., 2006). Lastly, since Gpx family proteins are also known to be involved in the oxidative stress response (Toppo et al., 2009), we assessed the physiological effect of SO1563 with mutational analysis. Unfortunately, the ΔSO1563 mutant displayed an identical phenotype as the wild-type strain under either stressed or normal conditions, even in the ohr $\cdot a h p ^ { - }$ background, leaving the physiological function of SO1563 to be identified. 

## 10.3.4 Conclusion

As summarized in Figure 10.3.7, our research presented here allowed us to portray a relatively rare circuit regulating oxidative stress response in S. oneidensis. 

For $\mathrm { O x y R } _ { \mathrm { S O } }$ -mediated regulation, the $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ -responding regulator $\mathrm { O x y R } _ { \mathrm { S O } }$ unusually functions as both an activator and a repressor (chiefly a repressor); among the many catalases and peroxidases under its control, only KatB appears to be functional, and the predominant force protecting the microorganism from harmful $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ relies on de-repression of the katB and dps genes upon inactivation of $\mathrm { O x y R } _ { \mathrm { S O } } .$ , which presumably results in rapid removal of the oxidant and restriction of intracellular iron concentrations, respectively; $\mathrm { O x y R } _ { \mathrm { S o } }$ seems not to be involved in the regulation of genes encoding proteins participating in the secondary defense, such as those for heme synthesis, FeS cluster assembly, or divalent cation import. This feature may partially account for the hypersensitivity of S. oneidensis (in comparison to E. coli) to $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ (Jiang et al., 2014). Moreover, $\mathrm { O x y R } _ { \mathrm { S O } }$ cannot functionally complement $\mathrm { O x y R } _ { \mathrm { E c } }$ or vice versa. As far as we are aware, this is the first case in which an OxyR analog could not work as a functional replacement in E. coli.

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/d070d93a-9a38-4d08-ab41-83365fc0221e/44d7b4786272425f9db11e0d00df075ee33ce49bd368656a3243c60cf79a41e0.jpg)



Figure 10.3.6 Iron deficiency caused by dps de-repression leads to viability defect in ΔoxyR strain. (a) ΔoxyR strain displayed a viability defect under no external stress, which deteriorated in the presence of iron chelator and can be fully rescued with iron. (b) Expression of all four genes coding for iron storage proteins found in S. oneidensis genome in the presence and absence of OxyR and $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } . \left( \mathrm { c } \right) \mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ sensitivity of mutants lacking individual iron storage proteins (top); and no-stress viability of mutants indicated (bottom) in regular (upper) or iron-supplemented (lower) media. ΔoxyRΔbfr was similar to ΔoxyRΔftn and was omitted for simplicity.


The most distinctive feature we have observed in S. oneidensis is perhaps the cross-talk between $\mathrm { O x y R } _ { \mathrm { S O } }$ and $\mathrm { O h r R } _ { \mathrm { S O } }$ systems. More specifically, these two regulators can both respond to $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ as well as OP; and OxyR-regulater Ahp contributes considerably to OP scavenging. Finally, it is also worth mentioning that the $\mathrm { O h r R } _ { \mathrm { S O } }$ regulon differs from its characterized counterparts in that it at least contains one extra member SO1563, a glutathione peroxidase, although its role in the ROS stress response remains elusive. 

This intriguingly unusual arrangement (in comparison to E. coli) and the observation that S. oneidensis is far more sensitive to $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ (but not to OP) than E. coli led us to postulate that organic peroxides are probably more of a threat than $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ in the natural habitat of this microorganism. Given that E. coli usually dwells in human gut, it probably often encounters $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ attack from the host immune system (Rada and Leto, 2008). In contrast, S. oneidensis most typically resides in aqueous environments relatively rich in organic matters and minerals. A recent report (Zhao et al., 2014) has demonstrated that simple organic matters can be effectively converted to acyl hydroperoxides in the presence of $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ and minerals such as $\mathrm { F e } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 3 } , \mathrm { A l } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 3 }$ , and $\mathrm { T i O } _ { 2 } { \mathrm { : } }$ , supporting our theory. 

Last but not least, we would like to acknowledge that these findings just open up the investigation of the S. oneidensis oxidative stress response; the molecular detail of how $\mathrm { O x y R } _ { \mathrm { S O } }$ and $\mathrm { O h r R } _ { \mathrm { S O } }$ sense both types of ROS remains to be elucidated, and whether the KatG-1 and KatG-2 are just useless evolutionary leftovers or a secret arsenal against some other ROS threat would be interesting – not to mention that, despite owning a superoxide dismutase (SOD) in the genome, no Sox analogs are detected. All these puzzles invite further studies to put together a complete roadmap of the S. oneidensis oxidative stress response.
