## Bork A. Berghoff and Gabriele Klug

Institut fur Mikrobiologie und Molekularbiologie, Interdisziplin ¨ ares Forschungszentrum (IFZ), Justus-Liebig-Universit ¨ at, Giessen, ¨ Germany 

## 10.4.1 Introduction

In oxic environments, all organisms have to cope with oxidative stress, which is caused by the presence of reactive oxygen species (ROS). Electron transfer to the ground state triplet oxygen generates hydrogen peroxide, superoxide, or hydroxyl radicals, all of which are very harmful since they oxidize proteins, lipids, and nucleic acids. These ROS are mostly generated as byproducts of aerobic respiration. In contrast to these ROS, singlet oxygen carries the same number of electrons as triplet oxygen, but has a different arrangement and spin of its electrons, which leads to high reactivity. Singlet oxygen is generated by energy transfer to ground state oxygen. The energy stems from light, and photosensitizers promote the generation of singlet oxygen. Since chlorophylls are very potent photosensitizers, the generation of singlet oxygen and the resulting photo-oxidative stress are special risks in phototrophic organisms. But also nonphototrophic bacteria can be exposed to singlet oxygen from the environment or generate singlet oxygen at, for example, protoporphyrins upon illumination (reviewed in Glaeser et al., 2011). 

Initial studies on the harmful effects of singlet oxygen in bacteria were performed with non-phototrophs. It was demonstrated that the structure of the cell wall plays an important role in susceptibility to singlet oxygen (Dahl et al., 1989) and that singlet oxygen generated by the presence of the photosensitizer methylene blue causes DNA damage in Salmonella Typhimurium upon illumination (Epe et al., 1989). Thioredoxin was identified as a cellular singlet oxygen quencher (Das and Das, 2000). However, little was known in regard to the bacterial singlet oxygen response until Rhodobacter sphaeroides emerged as a model system to study such response mechanisms and the underlying regulation. R. sphaeroides is a facultative phototroph that can generate adenosine triphosphate (ATP) by aerobic respiration but starts to produce photosynthetic complexes when oxygen tension is decreasing (Zeilstra-Ryalls and Kaplan, 2004). At intermediate oxygen levels, light inhibits formation of photosynthetic complexes in order to prevent photo-oxidative stress (Braatsch et al., 2002, 2004). Carotenoids have the expected important role in the defense against singlet oxygen in R. sphaeroides (Anthony et al., 2005; Glaeser and Klug, 2005); however, carotenoid levels do not increase in response to photo-oxidative stress (Glaeser and Klug, 2005). 

The question of how bacteria respond to photo-oxidative stress has been addressed since the 2000s by taking advantage of different omics approaches, revealing genes with relevant physiological functions on a large scale. This review will compare the different omics studies and focus on recent insights gained for R. sphaeroides that considerably contributed to our general understanding of the bacterial photo-oxidative stress response. 

## 10.4.2 Early work in the model organism R. sphaeroides

## 10.4.2.1 Microarray-based transcriptomics

A first glimpse on the response of R. sphaeroides to photooxidative stress stems from experiments designed to study the response to blue-light illumination at the global level by applying microarrays. Although blue light can induce a specific response at light quantities that do not induce singlet oxygen formation (e.g., Fruhwirth ¨ et al., 2012), it will also induce a photooxidative stress response if light quantities are high enough. The study of Braatsch et al. (2004) revealed an expected repression of photosynthesis genes by blue light but at the same time an increased expression of genes encoding deoxypyrimidine photolyase (PhrA), glutathione peroxidase, and quinol oxidoreductase with a putative role in the photo-oxidative stress response. While photolyases repair DNA damage caused by light, glutathione peroxidases contribute to the reduction of peroxides, and quinol oxidoreductases help to scavenge semiquinol radicals. Most genes that were upregulated by blue light in these experiments shared one of two promoter consensus sequences. 

One of them matched the consensus sequence for the alternative sigma factors RpoE (Newman et al., 1999). Previous studies revealed that activity of RpoE is controlled by its antisigma factor ChrR (Newman et al., 1999). All messenger RNAs (mRNAs) with the RpoE consensus sequence, including rpoE itself, and the mRNA for the alternative sigma factor $\mathrm { R p o H } _ { \mathrm { I I } }$ showed a fast and transient increase within 5 min of onset of illumination. All other upregulated genes showed a much later peak of expression and also share a common promoter consensus sequence, which is distinct from the RpoE consensus sequence. It was hypothesized that these promoters are recognized by $\mathrm { R p o H } _ { \mathrm { I I } } ,$ , since the gene for this sigma factor was activated upon blue-light illumination. This assumption was later confirmed (Nuss et al., 2009), and it was demonstrated that $\mathrm { R p o H _ { I I } }$ and $\mathrm { R p o H _ { I } }$ partly target the same genes in response to singlet oxygen (Nuss et al., 2010). 

Another microarray-based study compared the transcriptome profiles of a R. sphaeroides wild-type strain and a mutant lacking the antisigma factor ChrR, consequently showing increased RpoE activity under aerobic growth conditions (Anthony et al., 2005). This study revealed that mRNAs from ∼180 genes (∼60 operons) were over threefold more abundant in the strain with increased RpoE activity. Among these ∼180 genes, six genes or operons showed an RpoE consensus promoter, and RpoEdependent transcripts were detected by in vitro transcription. Besides the genes for RpoE, $\mathrm { R p o H } _ { \mathrm { I I } } ,$ and the photolyase PhrA, which had been described in the study by Braatsch et al. (2004), these were RSP 1409 (encoding beta-Ig-H3/fasciclin), RSP 1091–1087 (annotated as the hypothetical proteins shortchain dehydrogenase/reductase and flavin-containing amine oxidoreductase), and RSP 0296 (for cytochrome $c _ { 2 } )$ . 

An overview of microarray-based transcriptome studies for R. sphaeroides can be found in Table 10.4.1. The comprehensive analysis of the singlet oxygen response in R. sphaeroides by Berghoff et al. (2013) will be the focus of Section 10.4.3. 

## 10.4.2.2 Response of the R. sphaeroides proteome to singlet oxygen

An initial proteomic analysis on the response to singlet oxygen in R. sphaeroides was based on two-dimensional gel electrophoresis (2D GE) after in vivo pulse labeling of proteins with $\mathrm { L } { - } \bigl [ \mathrm { } ^ { 3 5 } \mathrm { S } \bigr ]$ -methionine (Glaeser et al., 2007). This approach allows determination of synthesis rates of individual proteins under a given condition and therefore represents an accurate measure of stimulons (Hecker et al., 2008). To discriminate the singlet oxygen–specific response from a more general oxidative stress response, parallel experiments were performed in the presence of hydrogen peroxide. For 63 proteins, representing 12% of all protein spots, synthesis rates were significantly changed in response to singlet oxygen (43 proteins were increased, and 20 decreased) (Glaeser et al., 2007). Genes for many of these proteins were not described as RpoE dependent in the transcriptome study by Anthony et al. (2005). The majority of proteins with different synthesis rates in the presence of singlet oxygen was not affected by hydrogen peroxide. When wild-type cultures were exposed to high light conditions without the addition of an artificial photosensitizer, the pattern of changed synthesis rates was very similar to that observed upon singlet oxygen exposure, albeit with lower changes. This is in agreement with the view that high light intensities in the presence of bacteriochlorophyll (BChl) stimulate this response. Proteome analysis was also performed with the mutant lacking ChrR in the presence and absence of singlet oxygen. The protein pattern observed for this mutant not only was very similar to the pattern obtained for the wild type upon singlet oxygen stress but also provided hints to an RpoE-independent singlet oxygen response of R. sphaeroides. Most importantly, singlet oxygen–induced proteins extended our understanding of the photo-oxidative stress response. The view that proteins are the main targets of singlet oxygen (Davies, 2003) was supported by the fact that some proteins with increased synthesis rates are involved in folding, replacement, repair, or degradation of proteins (e.g., the putative chaperone GrpE, SufD for iron–sulfur cluster assembly, and RSP 0355 for a putative serine protease). The removal of organic peroxides that are generated by the action of singlet oxygen is most likely assisted by alkylhydroperoxide reductase PrxQ (RSP 2973) and glutathione peroxidase Gpx (RSP 2389). Proteins damaged by peroxidation are removed by proteases and peptidases (RSP 0197: Clp protease subunit; RSP 0355: putative serine protease; and RSP 1096/1097: putative zinc peptidase PqqL). Increased synthesis of ABC transporters may assist the excretion of toxic waste products. Several proteins that carry out glutathione-dependent reactions, including those for methylglyoxal removal, showed increased synthesis upon singlet oxygen stress, underlining the important role of the glutathione system. 

## 10.4.3 An integrative approach for the global analysis of the singlet oxygen response

Most surveys of the singlet oxygen response in R. sphaeroides have focused on distinct sigma factors, mainly RpoE, RpoH , and $\mathrm { R p o H } _ { \mathrm { I I } } .$ There are two general strategies one could follow: (i) revealing sigma factors’ regulons without generating singlet oxygen, and (ii) measuring sigma factor–dependent changes in singlet oxygen exposure. The above-mentioned study of Anthony et al. (2005) used the first strategy by comparing transcript levels between wild type and strain ΔChrR without generating singlet oxygen. In further studies from the Donohue lab, RpoE, RpoH , and $\mathrm { R p o H } _ { \mathrm { I I } }$ were expressed independently of singlet oxygen, and genomic binding sites revealed by chromatin immunoprecipitation–DNA microarray (ChIP–chip) experiments. Predicted promoter sequences were subsequently tested experimentally in vivo and in vitro (Dufour et al., 2008, 2012). In our studies, we applied the second strategy and identified sigma factor–dependent regulation upon singlet oxygen exposure. For this purpose, wild-type cultures were subjected to singlet oxygen and compared to RpoE, RpoH , and RpoH deletion strains. In three comparative studies, proteomes were assessed by 2D GE after pulse labeling of proteins with $\mathrm { L } { - } \bigl [ \mathsf { } ^ { 3 5 } \mathrm { S } \bigr ]$ - methionine (Glaeser et al., 2007; Nuss et al., 2009, 2010). The information on RpoE, RpoH , and RpoH -dependent genes was subsequently used to predict promoter sequences using bioinformatic tools (Glaeser et al., 2007; Nuss et al., 2010). Despite the fact that both strategies considerably increased our knowledge on sigma factor regulons, only the study of Glaeser et al. (2007) aimed to investigate a general singlet oxygen response using 2D GE (see Section 10.4.2.2 and Figure 10.4.1). Furthermore, none of the studies assessed the transcriptome of R. sphaeroides wild type after singlet oxygen exposure. To complete the picture of singlet oxygen–dependent regulation, we designed a global approach integrating three omics: transcriptomics, translatomics, and proteomics (Figure 10.4.1) (Berghoff et al., 2013).

(2011b)</td></tr><tr><td><eq>RpoH_{I}</eq> and <eq>RpoH_{II}</eq> regulon definition (inducible <eq>RpoH_{I}</eq> and <eq>RpoH_{II}</eq> expression; promoter sequence search)</td><td>Hfq colP seq</td><td>Dufour et al. (2012)</td></tr><tr><td>Comprehensive analysis of the <eq>^1O_2</eq> response (polysomal and total RNA; SILAC labeling of proteins)</td><td>2D GE</td><td>Berghoff et al. (2013)</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Microarray</td><td>ChIP-chip</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Microarray</td><td>LC-MS</td></tr></table>

The singlet oxygen transcriptome was analyzed by Custom Gene Expression Microarrays from Agilent Technologies (USA) designed for R. sphaeroides 2.4.1 (8×15K; ID no. 027061). The same microarray design was used to assess the singlet oxygen translatome, which represents the sum of mRNAs captured in ribosomes for translation. For this purpose, RNA was extracted from polysome fractions that had been prepared by sucrose density gradient centrifugation. Investigation of the translatome adds a new layer of information to expression studies and has been successfully applied to a variety of microbes. For example, in the haloarchaeal model species Halobacterium salinarum and Haloferax volcanii, microarray analyses of free and polysome-bound mRNAs were used to monitor translational efficiency at different growth stages (Lange et al., 2007). In the Gram-positive bacterium Lactococcus lactis and in the fission yeast Schizosaccharomyces pombe, global ribosome profiling reported on ribosome occupancy and density (Lackner et al., 2007; Picard et al., 2012). In Saccharomyces cerevisiae, the translatome was analyzed by ribosome footprinting together with RNA sequencing or purification of affinity-tagged ribosomes combined with microarray analysis (Halbeisen and Gerber, 2009; Ingolia et al., 2009). Changes on the translatome level can shed light on posttranscriptional regulation that might occur at the stage of translation initiation. To complement the R. sphaeroides approach, the singlet oxygen proteome was investigated using liquid chromatography–mass spectrometry (LC-MS). One of the main challenges in LC-MS is the relative quantification of protein levels between samples. Isotope labeling of proteins represents a valuable tool to overcome this problem. Proteins can be labeled either in vitro after cell lysis, for example by the ICPL (isotope-coded protein label) method (Schmidt et al., 2005), or in vivo, for instance by using SILAC (stable isotope labeling with amino acids in cell culture). SILAC enables metabolic protein labeling by feeding cells with isotope amino acids. The method was originally applied to mammalian cell lines by Ong et al. (2002) but since then has been used in a diverse range of organisms, including yeast, newts, and nematodes (de Godoy et al., 2008; Looso et al., 2010, 2012; Studencka et al., 2012). In recent years, SILAC has also been recognized as a powerful method to quantify the proteomes and phosphoproteomes of model bacteria like Bacillus subtilis and Escherichia coli (Ravikumar et al., 2014; Soares et al., 2013; Sommer et al., 2010; Soufi et al., 2010). For our integrative approach, we created a bacterial SILAC standard by labeling prototrophic R. sphaeroides wild type with $^ { 1 3 } \mathrm { C } _ { 6 } \mathrm { - l y s i n e ~ ( L y s 6 ) }$ in minimal salt medium under different physiological conditions (Berghoff et al., 2013). Our labeling procedure is similar to the so-called native SILAC (nSILAC) method published for yeast and E. coli (Frohlich ¨ et al., 2013) and does not rely on lysine auxotrophic strains. The Lys6 incorporation rate of 96% in R. sphaeroides was comparable to the incorporation of $^ { 1 3 } \mathrm { C } _ { 6 } / ^ { 1 5 } \mathrm { N } _ { 2 } \mathrm { - l y s i n e }$ (Lys8) in E. coli (92%) (Frohlich ¨ et al., 2013). The bacterial SILAC standard was used as a spike-in standard for protein quantification similar to the super-SILAC approach used in human tumor tissue (Geiger et al., 2010, 2011). We finally compared transcriptome, translatome, and SILAC data (Berghoff et al., 2013) to a 2D GE study (Glaeser et al., 2007) to define a comprehensive set of singlet oxygen responsive genes in R. sphaeroides.

The results of these four approaches were combined to define the main singlet oxygen response (MSOR) of R. sphaeroides as depicted in Figure 10.4.2. 2D GE, two-dimensional gel electrophoresis; LC-MS, liquid chromatography–mass spectrometry. Data from Glaeser et al. (2007) and Berghoff et al. (2013).


## 10.4.3.1 How many genes respond to singlet oxygen?

Most genes with differential expression upon singlet oxygen exposure emerged from transcriptomics, which is not surprising since several time points (7, 45, and 90 min) were analyzed (Berghoff et al., 2013). In total, 173 genes were upregulated, and 101 genes were downregulated (Figure 10.4.2a). The translatome analysis revealed that 129 mRNAs were enriched and 24 mRNAs were depleted in the polysome fraction. The 2D GE approach identified 43 proteins triggered for synthesis, while 20 proteins were repressed (Glaeser et al., 2007). Protein synthesis should strongly correlate with changes in mRNA translation and protein abundance. Indeed, 34 proteins with changed synthesis in the 2D GE approach were validated by translatome analysis, SILAC, or both. For the remaining 29 proteins, no sufficient data were available. Finally, the SILAC approach revealed 68 proteins with increased and 45 proteins with decreased abundance (Figure 10.4.2a). In total, the integrative approach identified 382 R. sphaeroides genes that were differentially expressed after exposure to singlet oxygen in at least one of the three omics. The 2D GE study of Glaeser et al. (2007) adds another 25 genes, which results in a final list of 407 singlet oxygen responsive genes (Figure 10.4.2b). This set of genes is termed here MSOR (main singlet oxygen response) and will be discussed further in this chapter. 

## 10.4.3.2 Regulatory features of the main singlet oxygen response

Among the 407 genes in the MSOR, 239 genes were upregulated and 165 genes were downregulated. For the three genes RSP 1942 (cysI), RSP 2585, and RSP 2878 (coxS), results were somewhat controversial because the trend of regulation changed either between time points or between approaches. This indicates that some genes are subject to highly dynamic regulation in terms of time and mechanism. When comparing all omics data sets at 90 min of stress, it turned out that many genes are regulated on one level while not being regulated on the others

(Berghoff et al., 2013). An intriguing example represents proteins involved in photosynthesis. Many structural proteins of the photosynthetic apparatus and many enzymes for BChl and carotenoid biosynthesis were clearly decreased in abundance, as shown by SILAC, although the corresponding mRNAs were not changed on the transcriptome or translatome level. This is indicative of posttranslational regulation through degradation by general or stress-specific proteases. Furthermore, the transcriptome time-course data revealed that different clusters of expression dynamics exist and that several genes can be classified as short-term responsive or late adaptation genes (Berghoff et al., 2013). Another regulatory feature applies to operon polarity, which has also been demonstrated for Mycoplasma pneumoniae (Guell et al., 2009, 2011). Several operons exhibited a staircase-like expression pattern, with the first gene usually being expressed at the highest level. The singlet oxygen data, however, suggest that operon polarity can be a stress-inducible process. 

## 10.4.3.3 The main singlet oxygen response in the light of sigma factors

We inspected the MSOR for genes covered repeatedly by different techniques. The ${ } ^ { I } O _ { 2 }$ score indicates how many approaches identified the gene as singlet oxygen responsive. A score of 4 means that the corresponding genes were found in all four approaches (Figure 10.4.2c). Genes were then screened for dependence on the sigma factors RpoE, $\mathrm { R p o H } _ { \mathrm { I } } ,$ and $\mathrm { R p o H } _ { \mathrm { I I } }$ by using 2D GE results (Nuss et al., 2009, 2010), ChIP–chip data, and regulon predictions (Dufour et al., 2008, 2012). This analysis revealed that transcription of all genes with $\mathrm { ~ a ~ } ^ { 1 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ score of 4 is driven by RpoE or $\mathrm { R p o H } _ { \mathrm { I I } }$ (eight genes) (Figure 10.4.2c). The same analysis was performed for genes with a $\mathrm { { } ^ { i } O } _ { 2 }$ score of 3 (40 genes) (Figure 10.4.2d). The majority is $\mathrm { R p o H } _ { \mathrm { I I } }$ (17 genes), $\mathrm { R p o H _ { I } }$ and $\mathrm { R p o H } _ { \mathrm { I I } }$ (nine genes), RpoE (two genes), or RpoHI dependent (two genes). However, for 10 genes, transcriptional regulation is not known. It should be noted that only two genes with $\mathsf { a } \ ^ { 1 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ score of 3–4 were downregulated, RSP 0069 (fliC) and RSP 0967 $( s u c C )$ . In summary, analysis of the top candidates in the MSOR shows that ∼80% are induced by those sigma factors that are known to play important roles during singlet oxygen stress in R. sphaeroides. There is, however, substantial regulation occurring that cannot be easily explained by the well-known factors. Early work identified the transcriptional regulator OmpR to be singlet oxygen induced (Glaeser et $a l .$ , 2007), which was later shown to depend on $\mathrm { R p o H _ { I } }$ and $\mathrm { R p o H } _ { \mathrm { I I } }$ (Nuss et al., 2009, 2010). Our recent omics study identified further transcription factors (RSP 2719 (lrp) and RSP 2853) to be increased on the transcriptome level, as well as one repressor (RSP 1997 (lexA)) and one sigma factor–antisigma factor system (RSP 3094–3095) to be triggered on the translatome level (Berghoff et al., 2013). How these factors affect the MSOR and how they are regulated themselves remain exciting future issues. 

Because of the comprehensive nature of our analysis, we assumed that it can be used to validate sigma factor promoter predictions and regulon definitions in the context of singlet oxygen stress. For example, the RpoE regulon is composed of 15 genes (Dufour et al., 2008), 13 of which were proven by our integrative approach to belong to the MSOR (1O2 score $\geq 1 )$ (Figure 10.4.2e). However, we could not validate RSP 3336 and RSP 6222 (both hypothetical proteins). Considering this together with their weak activity during in vitro and in vivo reporter studies (Dufour et al., 2008), it appears overconfident to propose singlet oxygen–relevant functions for these two proteins. The study of Nuss et al. (2010) predicts two different $\mathrm { R p o H } _ { \mathrm { I I } }$ promoters, one containing a hexameric and one containing a heptameric −10 motif. The MSOR validated 35% of genes with a hexameric −10 motif, indicating that 65% of genes with a predicted hexamer $\mathrm { R p o H } _ { \mathrm { I I } }$ promoter are unlikely to be induced by singlet oxygen (Figure 10.4.2e). It raises the question of whether this set of predicted sequences is not targeted at all or whether there are additional factors required to activate transcription under conditions different than singlet oxygen stress. The same rationale applies to genes with a predicted heptameric −10 motif to an even greater extent. The finding that only 5% of these genes were supported by the MSOR (Figure 10.4.2e) challenges a major role of heptamer $\mathrm { R p o H } _ { \mathrm { I I } }$ promoters in the singlet oxygen response. The MSOR was finally compared to $\mathrm { R p o H _ { I } }$ and $\mathrm { R p o H } _ { \mathrm { I I } }$ regulon predictions based on ChIP–chip and microarray data (Dufour et al., 2012). Our integrative omics approach is in fairly high agreement with the ChIP data for $\mathrm { R p o H } _ { \mathrm { I I } }$ and $\mathrm { R p o H _ { I } { - } R p o H _ { I I } }$ binding sites (62% and 71%, respectively), which underlines the proposed importance of genes with such promoter motifs in the singlet oxygen response. However, the overlap between the predicted RpoH regulon and the MSOR was rather weak (15%) (Figure 10.4.2e). Previous data have suggested that $\mathrm { R p o H _ { I } }$ is mainly responsible for the heat shock response, and the low coverage of RpoHI-dependent genes in the MSOR therefore supports this view (Dufour et al., 2012; Nuss et al., 2009, 2010). 

## 10.4.3.4 Evocation of the singlet oxygen response

Our investigation of the singlet oxygen response is based on R. sphaeroides cultures that have been treated with the artificial photosensitizer methylene blue in the presence of oxygen and high light intensities $( \sim 8 0 0 \mathrm { W } \mathrm { m } ^ { - 2 } )$ , as originally described in the study by Glaeser and Klug (2005). However, it was shown that high light alone is sufficient to trigger a specific response in anoxygenic phototrophic bacteria due to BChl-mediated singlet oxygen generation (Berghoff et al., 2011a; Glaeser et $a l .$ , 2007). The destructive effect of light due to singlet oxygen is one possible explanation for the existence of light-sensing proteins with regulatory functions in phototrophic organisms. The first blue light receptor to be identified in R. sphaeroides was AppA (Braatsch et al., 2002; Masuda and Bauer, 2002), which had already been implicated in photosynthesis gene regulation several years before (Gomelsky and Kaplan, 1995). In later years, other blue-light receptors such as LOV and CryB were identified and characterized (Fruhwirth ¨ et al., 2012; Hendrischk et al., 2009a,b; Metz et al., 2012). The study of Braatsch et al. (2004) aimed to reveal the general response to blue light in R. sphaeroides by transcriptome profiling. As expected, repression of photosynthesis genes occurred after exposure to blue light, and AppA might be mainly responsible for this regulation. Surprisingly, several genes with a proposed singlet oxygen– specific function were found to be upregulated (see Section 10.4.2.1). We re-investigated the blue-light response and compared it to the MSOR. There was a distinct overlap of 25 features among the upregulated genes, most of them depending on RpoE, RpoH , and $\mathrm { R p o H } _ { \mathrm { I I } }$ (Figure 10.4.3a). The majority, however, was RpoE dependent, which implies that the low fluence rate of blue light used in the study (10 μmol of photons $\mathrm { m } ^ { - 2 } \ s ^ { - 1 } )$ (Braatsch et al., 2004) was not sufficient to induce a full singlet oxygen response. Singlet oxygen failed to be measured in blue light–illuminated cultures and might therefore only be produced sporadically in a minor fraction of the cells. 

In contrast, the overlap between the MSOR and the response to hydrogen peroxide (Zeller et al., 2005) was more pronounced, comprising 87 genes with the vast majority depending on $\mathrm { R p o H } _ { \mathrm { I I } }$ (Figure 10.4.3b). Since $\mathrm { R p o H } _ { \mathrm { I I } }$ is activated downstream of RpoE, the strong emergence of RpoH -dependent genes indicates full activation of the $\mathrm { R p o E - R p o H _ { I I } }$ signaling cascade. Former work suggested that RpoE is activated by hydrogen peroxide (Hendrischk et al., 2007) as well as organic hydroperoxides (Nam et al., 2013; Nuss et al., 2013), as was also observed for the RpoE homolog in Caulobacter crescentus (Lourenco and Gomes, 2009). Comparison of the hydrogen peroxide response to the MSOR strongly supports the view that peroxides can activate RpoE and thereby evoke a response similar to the singlet oxygen response.

## 10.4.4 A new layer of regulation – the posttranscriptional network

It is widely accepted that posttranscriptional events play nonnegligible roles in gene regulation and that bacterial small RNAs (sRNAs) represent a group of powerful regulators acting on this level (see Section 5). Most sRNAs described to date have sizes of ∼50–250 nucleotides and function through base-pairing interactions with their cis- or trans-encoded mRNA targets, thereby modulating mRNA translation and/or stability (for reviews, see Papenfort and Vogel, 2009; Storz et al., 2011). However, sRNAs might also function as decoys for global mRNA-binding proteins, such as CsrA and RsmA–RsmE in enteric bacteria and Pseudomonas species, respectively, and regulate mRNAs more indirectly (Kay et al., 2005; Weilbacher et al., 2003). Besides posttranscriptional modes of regulation, some sRNAs affect transcription either by mimicking open promoters and titration of RNA polymerase like 6S RNA (Barrick et al., 2005; Trotochaud and Wassarman, 2005; Wassarman and Storz, 2000) or by influencing Rho-dependent transcription termination (Bossi et al., 2012). Most, if not all, sRNAs respond to environmental stimuli, and many of them have a profound impact on regulation of virulence and stress responses. Recent advances in RNA sequencing (RNA-seq) motivated us to perform a global search for sRNAs with a potential role in the singlet oxygen response. 

## 10.4.4.1 Identification of small RNAs

Bacterial sRNAs are supposedly primary transcripts from intergenic regions and should therefore be enriched by any technique that, for example, depletes processed RNAs. Differential RNA-seq (dRNA-seq) addresses this issue by treating samples with terminator 5′ -phosphate-dependent exonuclease (TEX), which depletes all RNAs with $5 ^ { \prime } .$ -monophosphates, while primary transcripts remain stable due to their $5 ^ { \prime } .$ -triphosphates. TEX-treated samples are then compared to untreated samples using RNA-seq. As originally established for investigation of the primary transcriptome in the human pathogen Helicobacter pylori, dRNA-seq led to the identification of sRNAs in a wide range of prokaryotes (Sharma et al., 2010; Sharma and Vogel, 2014; for a recent review on sRNAs in photosynthetic bacteria, see Hess et al., 2014). The dRNA-seq approach for R. sphaeroides identified 107 putative sRNAs and 15 antisense transcripts (Berghoff et al., 2009). The sRNA candidates were named “RSs” for Rhodobacter sphaeroides sRNAs. Downstream analyses revealed that RSs0019 is RpoE dependent and therefore specifically responds to singlet oxygen and peroxides (Berghoff et al., 2009; our unpublished data). The three sRNAs RSs0680a, RSs1543, and RSs2461 are transcribed from Rpo $\mathrm { { f _ { I } } - }$ Rpo $\mathrm { f _ { I I } } ^ { } .$ -dependent promoters and consequently respond to heat shock as well (Nuss et al., 2010). Finally, RSs0827 was found to be induced by iron limitation and singlet oxygen (Berghoff et al., 2013; Peuser et al., 2012), although the responsible transcription factors have not yet been discovered (Figure 10.4.4). We propose important regulatory functions for these sRNAs in the singlet oxygen response and, depending on the nature of their induction, in other stress responses like the response to heat shock or iron depletion. Their exact physiological functions and mechanisms of action are currently under investigation.

Figure 10.4.4 Singlet oxygen–responsive sRNAs and the role of Hfq as a dual posttranscriptional regulator in R. sphaeroides. sRNAs were identified by dRNAseq (differential RNA sequencing) (Berghoff et al., 2009), by Hfq coIP seq (sequencing of RNA co-immunoprecipitated with Hfq) (Berghoff et al., 2011b), or by an integrative omics approach (Berghoff et al., 2013). An hfq deletion strain (Δhfq) was compared to R. sphaeroides wild type by 2D GE. Genes in the red box exhibited increased protein synthesis in Δhfq, whereas the green box represents genes with diminished protein synthesis in Δhfq.


## 10.4.4.2 The RNA chaperone Hfq as a dual posttranscriptional regulator in the singlet oxygen response

Trans-acting sRNAs usually target multiple mRNAs with limited complementarities and therefore need platforms to establish stable sRNA-mRNA interactions. The vast majority of transacting sRNAs depends on the RNA chaperone Hfq, which was originally discovered as an essential host factor for replication of bacteriophage Qβ (Franze de Fernandez et al., 1968). The homohexameric rings formed by Hfq monomers expose at least three surfaces (proximal, distal, and lateral) that are involved in RNA binding (Sauer, 2013; Vogel and Luisi, 2011). Hfq increases the local concentrations of RNAs and/or modulates their folding to facilitate sRNA–mRNA duplex formation. Release of the duplexes is realized by rapid turnover as proposed in the “active-cycling” model (Fender et al., 2010; Wagner, 2013). Hfq might also recruit additional factors like RNase E or poly(A) polymerase for degradation of RNAs or affect mRNA translation more directly (Desnoyers and Masse, 2012; Salvail et al., 2013; Vogel and Luisi, 2011). Since Hfq interacts with manifold RNAs, it is considered as an important global regulator (Sittka et al., 2008). We used 3×FLAGtagged Hfq for co-immunoprecipitation (coIP) of RNA that was subsequently analyzed by RNA-seq. This survey identified at least 19 sRNAs and six antisense transcripts to be enriched in the Hfq coIP library under conditions of singlet oxygen stress (Berghoff et al., 2011b). The RpoH –RpoH -dependent sRNAs (RSs0680a, RSs1543, and RSs2461) and RSs0827 were unambiguously revealed as strong Hfq binders. Although not enriched in the initial coIP seq approach, RSs0019 functionally depends on Hfq and precipitates with Hfq when expressed at high levels (our unpublished data). Thus, R. sphaeroides induces at least five Hfq-dependent sRNAs as part of the singlet oxygen response (Figure 10.4.4). Hfq mutants generally establish pleiotropic phenotypes, comprising diverse growth defects, increased stress sensitivity, and attenuated virulence (e.g., Barra-Bily et al., 2010a,b; Guisbert et al., 2007; Lybecker et al., 2010; Sittka et al., 2007; Tsui et al., 1994; Wilms et al., 2012). Deletion of hfq in R. sphaeroides represents another intriguing example of how a single factor affects multiple pathways. The R. sphaeroides Hfq mutant exhibits reduced pigmentation, formation of minicells, and higher sensitivity toward singlet oxygen (Berghoff et al., 2011b). In order to gain further

LH, light-harvesting complex; RC, reaction center; DH, dehydrogenase; $\mathrm { F A D H } _ { 2 } ,$ , reduced flavin adenine dinucleotide; $^ { \mathrm { i } } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } ,$ singlet oxygen; ${ \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } } ^ { - }$ , superoxide radical; $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } ,$ hydrogen peroxide; 19Fu-FA, 19 carbon furan-containing fatty acid; GSH, glutathione.


insights into Hfq-dependent regulation of the singlet oxygen response, R. sphaeroides wild type was compared to the Hfq mutant by 2D GE (Figure 10.4.4). Interestingly, several RpoH – $\mathrm { R p o H } _ { \mathrm { I I } }$ -dependent proteins were affected by Hfq upon singlet oxygen exposure. They were either repressed or induced in synthesis, indicating that Hfq exerts a dual regulatory function. It is likely that repression is mainly driven by sRNAs that inhibit translation and/or decrease stability of the respective mRNAs with the aid of Hfq. In E. coli, many protein regulators are repressed by sRNAs in an Hfq-dependent fashion, which applies to, for example, repression of rpoS by OxyS or csgD by OmrA/B (Holmqvist et al., 2010; Zhang et al., 1998). Among the repressed proteins in R. sphaeroides, we also found a transcriptional regulator of the OmpR family (Figure 10.4.4). Repression of ompR by one of the singlet oxygen–inducible sRNAs would provide an elegant mechanism to fine-tune expression of OmpR and OmpR-regulated genes, but this remains speculative. In the case of induced protein synthesis, sRNAs might activate translation by an “anti-antisense mechanism”, in which an inhibitory structure in the mRNA 5′ UTR (untranslated region) is resolved by an sRNA (Frohlich and¨ Vogel, 2009). It is also feasible that Hfq stabilizes mRNAs and thereby promotes translation more indirectly. Hfq-dependent stabilization has been indicated for the puf mRNA that encodes structural components of the photosynthetic apparatus and would help to explain the reduced pigmentation in the R. sphaeroides Hfq mutant (Berghoff et al., 2011b). Our investigation of the posttranscriptional network revealed that Hfq affects numerous genes with a function in the MSOR. This is the likely main reason for the higher singlet oxygen sensitivity of the Hfq mutant and consequently adds Hfq together with the singlet oxygen–induced sRNAs to the MSOR in R. sphaeroides. 

## 10.4.5 A functional map for the bacterial singlet oxygen response

Singlet oxygen is generated at the BChls of the photosynthetic complexes. It perturbs photosynthetic and respiratory electron flow, leads to elevated levels of methylglyoxal, and damages DNA and proteins. Perturbation of the respiratory electron flow might give rise to additional ROS, such as superoxide and hydrogen peroxide, which are scavenged by superoxide dismutase SodB and catalase CatA. Singlet oxygen also leads to RpoE activation by mechanisms that are only partly understood to date. Degradation of the antisigma factor ChrR by the proteases RSP 3242 (DegS homolog) and RSP 2710 (RseP homolog) is stimulated to release RpoE (Nuss et al., 2013). The localization of ChrR at the membrane is still speculative. The RSP 1090 and RSP 1091 gene products are involved in ChrR degradation, but their exact function in this process remains elusive (Nam et al., 2013; Nuss et al., 2013). A recent study provided evidence that at least RSP 1091 is involved in the synthesis of 19 carbon furan–containing fatty acids (19Fu-FAs) together with RSP 2144 (UfaM–CfaS), which was identified as an S-adenosyl-dependent fatty acid methylase (Lemke et al., 2014). A strain lacking ChrR and consequently exhibiting high RpoE activity shows increased accumulation of 19Fu-FAs, and the presence of singlet oxygen leads to turnover of 19Fu-FAs in vivo (Lemke et al., 2014). RpoE activation is the first step in a signaling cascade that triggers $\mathrm { R p o H } _ { \mathrm { I I } }$ expression and consequently dozens of proteins with functions in counteracting singlet oxygen–dependent damages and in removing toxic products (Figure 10.4.5). Small RNAs and Hfq might fine-tune some of these processes on the level of posttranscriptional regulation.

## 10.4.6 Perspectives



Berghoff BA, Glaeser J, Sharma CM, Zobawa M, Lottspeich F, et al. 2011b. Contribution of Hfq to photooxidative stress resistance and global regulation in Rhodobacter sphaeroides. Molecular Microbiology 80(6): 1479–1495.

Tsui HCT, Leung HCE, Winkler ME. 1994. Characterization of broadly pleiotropic phenotypes caused by an Hfq insertion mutation in Escherichia Coli K-12. Molecular Microbiology 13(1): 35–49. 



Studies on the singlet oxygen response in R. sphaeroides since the 2000s have considerably advanced our understanding on this important stress response in bacteria. Nevertheless, there are still a number of mysteries to be answered. To date, the roles of sRNAs and Hfq have just begun to emerge, and further work needs to identify the exact molecular mechanisms of regulation. Another open question pertains to the mechanisms that lead to singlet oxygen–dependent activation of genes, which are not under control of RpoE or RpoH –RpoH . Furthermore, the exact mechanisms triggering ChrR proteolysis are still not understood, and it is not yet resolved how organic hydroperoxides activate RpoE. The response to organic hydroperoxides, which are generated during singlet oxygen exposure, is independent of RSP 1090/1091 and independent of DegS and RseP (Nuss et al., 2013). It will be challenging to find out whether the newly discovered production of 19Fu-FAs is just a consequence of the stress or may at the same time function as a signal to activate this response. We expect interesting new insights in bacterial stress-sensing mechanisms from future research on this topic.

# Regulators of oxidative stress response genes in Escherichia coli and their conservation in bacteria

Herb E. Schellhorn, Mohammad Mohiuddin, Sarah M. Hammond, and Steven Botts 

Department of Biology, McMaster University, Hamilton, Ontario, Canada 

## 10.5.1 Introduction

Oxidative stress in the bacterial cell, caused by endogenous metabolism or encountered from exogenous sources, elicits a complex response that can be divided into two distinct components. The preventative component consists of enzymes that mitigate oxidative stress by converting the reactive species into less toxic intermediates through reduction or dismutation. The bacterial cell also has a battery of enzymes that can repair damage caused by oxidative stress. In this review, we will focus on recent findings on oxidative stress regulators to understand how the preventative component has arisen through evolutionary processes. 

Characterization of oxidative stress effects in bacterial cells has been greatly aided by the use of genomic technologies (including microarray and proteomics) that have facilitated the identification of many new, previously unknown components of the protective response. Technologies that enable probing events at the single-cell level have also contributed to our understanding since bacteria exist as populations of cells and even small numbers of cells can contribute enormously to the subsequent outgrowth of populations. Bacterial oxidative stress–mediated damage, although historically studied at the population level using enzyme assays performed on culture samples, can now be probed and individual cells can be studied using microscopic and fluorometric-based assays that provide information on the status of single cells. These data have shown that oxidative stress does not affect all cells uniformly and that, in fact, heterogeneity in aging cultures is the hallmark of bacterial growth that is undoubtedly important in survival from toxic levels of stress. This review will focus on the role of oxidative stress responses in E. coli as a model with comparisons to key bacterial groups that share regulatory homologs. 

## 10.5.2 Levels of bacterial catalase and superoxide dismutase are closely linked to the flux of reactive oxygen species in the cell

Reactive oxygen–mediated stress in the bacterial cell may be regarded as episodic in nature, depending on the mode of metabolism of the cell (respiratory versus fermentative) or the presence of exogenous sources of reactive oxygen species (ROS) (Figure 10.5.1). Regulators directly sense the presence of ROS through oxidation and reduction of specific amino acid residues. 

Superoxide dismutase is produced in response to both high oxygen tension and excess iron. MnSOD is induced by SoxRS (Pomposiello and Demple, 2000), and in Salmonella, MnSOD together with FeSOD are activated by the Fur protein through small RNAs, rfrA and rfaB (Troxell et al., 2011). The CuZnSOD-encoding sodC gene is repressed by the Fnr regulator and thus is not expressed under anaerobic conditions (Gort et al., 1999) when superoxide is absent. Both peroxidase enzymes in E. coli are part of the OxyR regulon and are thus expressed during increased peroxide flux (Tartaglia et al., 1989). Both CuZnSOD (Gort et al., 1999) and the HPII catalase (Schellhorn and Hassan, 1988) increase 20–30-fold during growth to the stationary phase, allowing the cell to adapt to a slow growth state where specific de novo induction may be less feasible. E. coli peroxidases are controlled by OxyR (Tartaglia et al., 1989) and are induced by low concentrations of hydrogen peroxide. 

## 10.5.3 Oxidative stress overlaps with other stress responses, including the RpoS regulon

During the exponential phase, the cell can rapidly adjust to changes in the flux of ROS by induction of the appropriate enzymes. However, in the stationary phase, de novo protein synthesis is substantially reduced; yet, at the high cell densities during this phase of growth, oxidative stress can be elevated due to increased cell density and increased respiratory activity as cells use carbon sources that require respiratory metabolism. Under such conditions, rapid adaptation mechanisms such as those controlled by OxyR and SoxRS are likely less effective. However, in E. coli, during adaptation to the stationary phase, many genes are controlled by the second vegetative sigma factor, RpoS, which controls many diverse functions. RpoS is a general stress response regulator that controls hundreds of genes required for adaptation. Consistent with this physiological role, catalases (e.g., KatE–HPII) are important in eliminating hydrogen peroxide levels at high concentrations, whereas peroxidases (including AphCF and HPI) are critical at lower concentrations (Imlay, 2013). 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/d070d93a-9a38-4d08-ab41-83365fc0221e/fc14aee9bf24da64b45d36c95229ff00c2e30ba741766aa6a3aaf6414a264490.jpg)



Figure 10.5.1 Regulation of oxidative stress enzymes in Escherichia coli. “A” is a cellular reductant.


The rpoS gene is found in proteobacteria, primarily in the gamma-, beta-, and deltaproteobacteria (Chiang and Schellhorn, 2010), having arisen through a duplication of the rpoD gene encoding the main vegetative sigma factor, sigma 70. Though controlling many functions, not surprisingly, some oxidative stress genes are specifically expressed in the stationary phase. Both catalase (HPII) and superoxide dismutase (SodC) enzymes are highly RpoS dependent and thus are part of the protective regulon that ensures cell survival during periods of slow growth. Examination of RpoS regulon composition between Pseudomonas aeruginosa and E. coli indicates that relatively few members are conserved between these two proteobacteria. The RpoS regulon may therefore represent a reconfigurable system that allows its incorporation in expression of protective functions that are needed for a particular or specific bacterial niche (Schellhorn, 2014). Proteobacteria constitute a large group of bacteria that possess substantial metabolic diversity, and they include many pathogens that may experience oxidative stress within the host or as free-living bacteria. Conserved control of components of the oxidative stress response by the RpoS regulator allows the bacterium to express high levels of relevant protective activity precisely calibrated to its immediate metabolic needs. 

## 10.5.4 Evolutionary conservation of the OxyR peroxide regulator

OxyR, a member of the LysR family of transcriptional regulators, serves as a bacterial primary defense mechanism against hydrogen peroxide stress (Jang and Imlay, 2010; Zheng et al., 2001; see Chapters 10.1 and 10.2). Characteristic of the LysR protein family, OxyR negatively regulates its own expression but positively regulates oxyS, an adjacent small RNA that integrates the peroxide stress response with the cellular stress response (Li and He, 2012). 

In response to elevated levels of hydrogen peroxide (intracellular or extracellular), OxyR, in E. coli, activates the transcription of 28 dependent promoters as reviewed in Chiang and Schellhorn (2012). This induction depends on the oxidation state of OxyR and cooperative binding of oxidized OxyR with RNA polymerase (Lee et al., 2004). The cooperatively bound RNA polymerase then positively regulates transcription (Lee et al., 2004; Tao et al., 1995). Reduced OxyR is a negative regulator of genes (e.g., agn43, stiA, and pntAA) in some species, including Salmonella enterica and P. aeruginosa (Henderson and Owen, 1999; Seymour et al., 1996; Wei et al., 2012). The OxyR regulon, as part of the response against oxidative stress, includes gorA (glutathione reductase) and grxA (glutaredoxin). The glutaredoxin–glutathione system itself comprises a feedback control mechanism for OxyR, slowly reducing oxidized OxyR to the fully reduced form (Garcia-Santamarina et al., 2014). In addition to peroxide resistance, OxyR also protects against heat stress (Wei et al., 2012), singlet oxygen (Kim et al., 2002), lipid peroxidation–mediated oxidative stress (Daugherty et al., 2012; Yoon et al., 2002), and phagocyte ingestion (Staudinger et al., 2002). 

OxyR regulon homologs are present in the Proteobacteria, Actinobacteria, and Bacteroidetes phyla, as reviewed in Chiang and Schellhorn (2012). Among the five classes of Proteobacteria, OxyR homologs are present in the most recently diverged gammaproteobacteria, in betaproteobacteria, and in alphaproteobacteria and are absent in delta- and epsilonproteobacteria (Rodionov et al., 2004; van Vliet et al., 1999). Although functional regulation by OxyR is conserved in most gammaproteobacteria, one exception (at least) is Legionella pneumophila. In L. pneumophila, the OxyR regulon, denoted as $\mathrm { O x y R } _ { \mathrm { L p } } ,$ instead of responding to oxidative stress, regulates the transition of this bacterial species from a vegetative state to a resilient cyst-like transmissible form (LeBlanc et al., 2008). The composition and size of the OxyR regulon vary among classes of Proteobacteria (Chiang and Schellhorn, 2010). OxyR provides protection against exposure to atmospheric oxygen in the anaerobic phylum Bacteroidetes (Diaz et al., 2006). Bacteroidetes fragilis OxyR, with 40% identity to aerobic bacteria, regulates an acute response regulon of 13 genes to minimize the impact of ROS to maintain intracellular redox balance (Sund et al., 2008). In Actinobacteria, OxyR also confers protection against hydrogen peroxide. For instance, in Streptomyces spp. and in Mycobacterium marinum, overexpression of OxyR confers hydrogen peroxide resistance through induction of ahpCD and ahpC expression, respectively (Hahn et al., 2002; Pagan-Ramos et al., 2006). 

## 10.5.5 Control of superoxide responses by SoxRS

The adjacent and divergently transcribed genes soxR and soxS comprise a two-component regulatory system for mediating oxidative stress in E. coli. First identified in 1991, the SoxRS regulon is composed of more than 100 genes (Blanchard et al., 2007; Wu and Weiss, 1991). In E. coli, the activation of SoxR (superoxide response) induces expression of soxS, which regulates key genes involved in the superoxide radical response (Nunoshiba et al., 1992). 

SoxR regulates resistance to oxidative stress in E. coli following exposure to the superoxide inducer paraquat (Greenberg et al., 1990), nitric oxide (Nunoshiba et al., 1993), and hydrogen peroxide (Manchado et al., 2000). In addition to these compounds, SoxR is also directly activated by redox-cycling drugs in anaerobic environments (Singh et al., 2013). The oxidation of two (2Fe-2S) clusters in SoxR results in a 100-fold induction of soxS (Hidalgo and Owen, 1994). Upon oxidation, SoxR complexes with the spacer region of the soxS promoter to facilitate binding of RNA polymerase and downstream transcription (Hidalgo and Owen, 1997). Several SoxRS regulon genes are active in mitigating oxidative stress, including sodA (superoxide dismutation) and nfo (DNA repair) (Li and Demple, 1994). 

Homologs of the SoxR regulator have been found in both proteo- and actinobacteria, although they remain unidentified in Bacteroidetes (Ohara et al., 2006). SoxRS responds to oxidative stress in both Escherichia and Shigella spp., where soxRS mutants show increased sensitivity to superoxide (Daugherty et al., 2012). The SoxRS regulon additionally confers multipledrug resistance in both E. coli and the genera Salmonella and Klebsiella in gammaproteobacteria (Kehrenberg et al., 2009; Mosel et al., 2013). SoxR homologs in Vibrio spp. and 

P. aeruginosa, respectively, share 55 and 62% sequence identity with E. coli (Kobayashi and Tagawa, 2004; Vattanaviboon et al., 2003). The SoxRS-mediated superoxide radical response is present in many gammaproteobacteria (E. coli, Shigella flexneri, Salmonella spp., Klebsiella pneumoniae, and Vibrio spp.) but is absent in Pseudomonas spp., where SoxRS regulates drug efflux pump genes (Palma et al., 2005). 

## 10.5.6 Oxidative stress and bacterial persistence

Within homogeneous populations of bacteria exposed to a given antibiotic, a small fraction of cells are nongrowing and therefore refractile to the lethal effects of bactericidal antibiotics. These so-called persister cells (see Chapter 6.3) represent an important type of phenotypic plasticity that allows a population of cells to survive transient exposure to antimicrobial agents or conditions. Whether persister cells arise through stochastic or deterministic processes is an important issue, and although this question is not fully resolved, it is clear that genetic determinants can affect the fraction of persistent cells within a population. Populations of cells deficient in rpoS have higher levels of persister cells. This may be due to reduced levels of protective enzymes that result in oxidative stress–mediated inhibition of growth within subpopulations (Wang et al., 2011). Consistent with the idea that inhibition of growth is key to persistence, toxin–antitoxin systems (Dorr et al., 2010; Wang et al., 2011; see Chapter 2.7 and Section 6) have been implicated in the development of persistence. These results suggest that the status of individual cells within a population may be a key to understanding how bacterial populations survive oxidative stress and related antibiotic stress to propagate successfully. 

## 10.5.7 Complexity of oxidative stress responses revealed by transcriptome technologies

Oxidative stress responses reflect an interplay between the cell’s physiological adaptation to ROS, intracellular iron availability, and repair factors that maintain viability. Uncovering such complexity requires interrogation of genome-wide responses using transcriptome-based techniques, including microarrays and RNA-seq experiments, under specific conditions such as slow growth or altered expression in pathogenic strains or in relation to other genetic factors such as phosphate regulation (Table 10.5.1). While many oxidative stress–related responses have been defined by comparing expression results of wildtype strains to regulatory deletion mutants, a more nuanced approach, using mutants that robustly model physiologically relevant low-level ROS fluxes, may provide important information. For example, initial characterization of the OxyR response (Zheng et al., 2001) revealed many genes that form the core primary defense against hydrogen peroxide. Later studies extended this, using catalase and peroxide mutants in which exposure levels mimic normal intracellular levels of this oxidant (Mancini and Imlay, 2015) that are known to modulate activity of the OxyR protein (Imlay, 2015). These types of studies reveal that oxidative stress responses are intimately associated with biosynthetic processes, including heme biosynthesis (Mancini and Imlay, 2015).

## 10.5.8 Concluding remarks



Hidalgo E, Demple B. 1997. Spacing of promoter elements regulates the basal expression of the soxS gene and converts SoxR from a transcriptional activator into a repressor. EMBO Journal 16(5): 1056–1065. 





Vattanaviboon P, Panmanee W, Mongkolsuk S. 2003. Induction of peroxide and superoxide protective enzymes and physiological crossprotection against peroxide killing by a superoxide generator in Vibrio harveyi. FEMS Microbiology Letters 221(1): 89–95. 



Bacterial responses to oxidative stress, controlled by conserved regulators, are highly adaptable, allowing the cell to increase expression of many metabolic functions that are coordinately controlled to reduce the effects of stress. It is now clear that oxidative stresses overlap with other, related bacterial responses, and considerable future work will be needed to uncover the physiological responses to complex stresses. For example, proline catabolism in E. coli appears to produce sufficient oxidative stress to induce the OxyR regulon, indicating that nonrespiratory pathways may contribute to the bacterial stress response (Zhang et al., 2015). Selective killing by oxidative stress results in population bottlenecks, which can provide strong selection of new stress-related survival traits. For this reason, responses in individual cells are likely critical in allowing populations to propagate, and understanding stochastic and deterministic factors at the individual cell level will likely be a focus of future research.

# Hydrogen peroxide resistance in Bifidobacterium animalis subsp. lactis and Bifidobacterium longum

Taylor S. Oberg and Jeff R. Broadbent 

Department of Nutrition, Dietetics, and Food Sciences, Utah State University, Logan, Utah, USA 

## 10.6.1 Introduction

Bifidobacterium species are Gram-positive, obligately anaerobic, non-spore-forming, nonmotile, irregularly rod-shaped bacteria with a high (>51%) guanine + cytosine (G+C) content. Species of bifidobacteria have been isolated from multiple sources, including food, sewage, insects, and the gastrointestinal (GI) tract of humans and animals (Lamendella et al., 2008). Some species show the ability to grow in different host animals, whereas others demonstrate highly specialized adaptation to the gut of one particular species (e.g., rabbits, cows, or chickens) (Scardovi, 1986; Turroni et al., 2009). 

Bifidobacterium species are chemoorganotrophs able to metabolize a variety of carbohydrates through fermentation to produce organic acids but not gas (Scardovi, 1986). Although a majority of the Bifidobacterium species can utilize complex carbohydrates, most strains will favor simple sugars over more complex sugars if they are available. One characteristic feature of bifidobacteria is that they lack genes involved in the electron transport chain for oxidative phosphorylation and ferment hexose sugars exclusively through the fructose-6-phosphate phosphoketolase pathway, commonly termed the bifid shunt, with a theoretical yield of 3 mol of acetate and 2 mol of lactate for every 2 mol of glucose consumed (de Vries and Stouthamer, 1967; Scardovi, 1986; Sgorbati et al., 1995). 

## 10.6.1.1 Probiotic health benefits

Specialized adaptation of certain bifidobacteria to the GI tract is illustrated by the fact that these microbes are evolved to ferment complex carbohydrates that the host cannot digest (Schell et al., 2002; Sela et al., 2008), and the oligosaccharide transporters and glycosyl hydrolases needed to utilize these sugars may account for as much as 10% of the total genomic content of individual strains (Ventura et al., 2007). Some species of bifidobacteria, including Bifidobacterium longum subsp. infantis, are major components of the GI microbiota of healthy, breast-fed infants, and research suggests the composition of GI microbiota in infants and children may influence the development of diarrheal, inflammatory, and allergic diseases (Salminen et al., 2005). Bifidobacteria are also thought to promote several positive health-related functions in adult humans, even though they are relatively minor components of the GI microbiota at this stage of life (Ballongue, 1998; Stanton, 2001). 

The relationship between bifidobacteria and human health has stimulated consumer interest in bioactive foods that contain bifidobacteria (Sanders, 1998). Two bifidobacteria species that are widely used in commercial probiotic products are Bifidobacterium lactis and B. longum. Conclusive data are not available on a minimal effective probiotics dose for humans, but results from several clinical trials suggest that effective doses range from 107 to 109 CFU/mg per day (Bertazzoni et al., 2013). As a result, the World Health Organization’s definition for probiotics emphasizes the need for their administration in “adequate amounts” (Reid et al., 2003). Successful application of probiotic bifidobacteria in foods is therefore dependent not only upon scientific research that demonstrates their efficacy in providing a human health benefit, but also on technologies to sustain high cell numbers during food manufacture and storage. 

## 10.6.1.2 Challenges for delivery in food systems

Industrially, bifidobacteria are usually preserved or distributed in liquid, spray-dried, frozen, or lyophilized forms (Hassan and Frank, 2001; Mayr¨ a-M¨ akinen and Bigret, 1998). Unfortunately,¨ most culture preservation and production methods subject cells to stress conditions, such as freezing, drying, nutrient starvation, and concentration stress, which serve to diminish cell viability (Mayr¨ a-M¨ akinen and Bigret, 1998). As a result, problems¨ in large-scale cultivation and preservation of bifidobacteria continue to limit the commercial application of these cultures (Ross et al., 2005). 

At present, yogurt and fermented milk are the most common vehicle foods for delivery of probiotic bifidobacteria, but cheese, ice cream, infant formula, fruit juice, and other foods are also used to a lesser extent (Ross et al., 2005). Regardless of the food type, efforts to maintain high numbers of viable bifidobacteria in the product are challenged by the fact that foodmanufacturing processes commonly expose cells to stress conditions (Ross et al., 2005). Food-processing steps that involve dehydration (low $\mathrm { A } _ { \mathrm { w } } ) _ { \mathrm { : } }$ exposure to high or low temperature or pH, and elevated oxygen or NaCl levels may be deleterious to the viability of bifidobacteria (Cotter and Hill, 2003; Maus and Ingham, 2003; Ross et al., 2005). To overcome this challenge, processors may employ very large inocula or add specific growth promoters or protectants (Ross et al., 2005; Tamime et al., 1995). Finally, probiotic bacteria must also be able to survive the harsh gastric barrier, where the low pH of the stomach and bile salt exposure in the small intestine can destroy microbial cells (Godward et al., 2000). 

## 10.6.1.3 Environmental stress resistance

The toll that environmental stress conditions place on survival of probiotic bifidobacteria has fueled research interest on factors that affect stress tolerance and stress adaptation, as well as the molecular mechanisms that influence these phenotypes. Exposure of bacteria to adverse conditions typically results in cell damage, but strain-to-strain variability in the intrinsic resistance of a particular species to such stressors is frequently observed (Doyle et al., 2001; Payne, 1978; Russell, 2003). 

Under certain conditions, resistance of bifidobacteria and other microbes to environmental stressors can dramatically exceed the measured level for innate tolerance via deliberate induction of an adaptive or inducible stress response (Hecker and Volker, 2001; Oberg et al., 2011; Saarela et al., 2004; Schmidt and Zink, 2000; Takahashi et al., 2004). These responses are characterized by the transient induction of specific genes that encode for protective proteins, enzymes, and metabolic pathways (Mager and De Kruijff, 1995). For example, one of the consequences that cells must negotiate during many different stress conditions is protein damage resulting in misfolded and nonfunctioning proteins. To overcome this problem, cells utilize molecular chaperones (see Section 14) to aid proper protein folding and stabilization during stress and, when proteins are damaged, employ proteolytic enzymes to prevent their accumulation (Gottesman et al., 1997; Wickner et al., 1999). Genetic analyses of B. lactis and B. longum strains has confirmed that heat and bile stress induce the expression of chaperones and proteases and their associated regulatory proteins (De Dea Lindner et al., 2007; Sanchez´ et al., 2005; Savijoki et al., 2005). 

The knowledge that sublethal stress treatments can promote cell robustness is already exploited in the manufacture and use of probiotic cultures (Shah, 2000), but the degree of protection induced by a particular stress treatment can differ significantly between bifidobacteria species and strains (Ahn et al., 2001; Maus and Ingham, 2003; Oberg et al., 2013, 2015; Saarela et al., 2004; Schmidt and Zink, 2000; Simpson et al., 2005; Takahashi et al., 2004). Thus, a more detailed understanding of stress adaptation in bifidobacteria is needed to improve the stability of these bacteria in probiotic foods. 

## 10.6.1.4 Oxidative stress response

Oxidative stress poses a particularly difficult challenge to the incorporation of high bifidobacteria numbers in functional foods. Oxygen toxicity results from cell exposure to activated oxygen compounds such as superoxide, hydrogen peroxide $\left( \mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } \right)$ , and hydroxyl radicals, which damage biomolecules through various mechanisms (e.g., oxidation of sulfhydryl groups and peptide breaks in protein, fatty acid peroxidation and propagation, and DNA damage through depolymerization and depurination) (Avery, 2011; Briuoukhanov and Netrusov, 2007; Cabiscol et al., 2000; see other chapters of Section 10). As was noted in Section 10.6.1.2, yogurt is one of the most common vehicle foods for probiotic bifidobacteria, and the starter cultures used in yogurt manufacture, Lactobacillus delbrueckii subsp. bulgaricus and Streptococcus thermophilus, have been shown to produce inhibitory concentrations of $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ under conditions seen during bioactive food production and storage (Shimamaru et al., 1992). 

Anaerobic species like bifidobacteria lack genes for enzymes such as superoxide dismutase and catalase, which aerobes deploy to detoxify oxidative free radicals (Iuchi and Weiner, 1996). However, many anaerobic bacteria have developed alternative systems to mitigate $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ toxicity such as NADH peroxidases, peroxiredoxin, thioredoxin reductase, ferritin-like iron-binding proteins, and DNA repair enzymes (Brioukhanov and Netrusov, 2007; Yamamoto et al., 2011). Genomic analysis of B. lactis and B. longum strains has revealed that each possesses genes for peroxiredoxin and a thioredoxin reductase–thioredoxin system (Barrangou et al., 2009; Sela et al., 2008). Moreover, bile exposure has been found to produce an oxidative stress response via generation of oxygen free radicals (Begley et al., 2005), and this stress has been shown to trigger expression of the thioredoxin reductase system and classic stress response chaperones in bifidobacteria (Sanchez ´ et al., 2007b, Savijoki et al., 2005). 

## 10.6.1.5 The cytoplasmic membrane is modified in response to stress

The cellular membrane is the first line of defense against environmental stress, and many bacteria rapidly modify the fatty acid moieties and head groups in the cell envelope in response to various stressors (Zhang and Rock, 2008; see Section 18). Changes in cell membrane fatty acid (CMFA) composition alter membrane fluidity and permeability in a manner that often enhances cellular resistance to the stress condition (Mykytczuk et al., 2007; Oberg et al., 2013; Vigh et al., 2005; Zhang and Rock, 2008). Among bifidobacteria, a bile salt–resistant B. lactis mutant has been shown to increase the unsaturated-to-saturated fatty acid ratio of its CMFA during bile salt exposure, whereas wild-type cells show a decrease in the unsaturated-to-saturated fatty acid ratio (Ruiz et al., 2009). 

Additionally, the membrane lipids of B. lactis and B. longum include plasmalogens, phospholipids that possess a vinyl ether bond at the SN1 position (as opposed to an ester bond), which display distinct physical properties (Oberg et al., 2012). Plasmalogens are widespread among eukaryotes, where they are associated with oxidative environments and an increase in cell membrane fluidity (Snyder et al., 2002). The vinyl ether bond is more easily oxidized than the carbon–carbon double bond of unsaturated fatty acids and, unlike oxidized unsaturated fatty acids, does not propagate free radicals in response to peroxides (Catala, 2009; Magnusson and Haraldsson, 2011). Because´ of these characteristics, plasmalogens have been proposed to act as antioxidants in membrane physiology via protection of unsaturated fatty acids and membrane proteins from harmful oxidation (Engelmann, 2004; Wang and Wang, 2010). Ahn et al. (2001) have reported that oxygen-tolerant fecal isolates of Bifidobacterium have a high membrane plasmalogen content, and they have provided data to support a direct correlation between oxygen tolerance and higher CMFA concentrations of C19:0 cyclopropyl plasmalogens. Thus, deliberate manipulation of the CMFA profile may provide a means to enhance cellular resistance of bifidobacteria to oxidation and other stressors. 

## 10.6.2 Intrinsic and inducible ${ \bf H } _ { 2 } { \bf O } _ { 2 }$ resistance among B. longum and B. lactis strains

Our survey for intrinsic $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ resistance among commercial strains of B. longum and B. lactis revealed statistically significant $( p < 0 . 0 5 )$ , strain-specific differences (Table $1 0 . 6 . 1 )$ (Oberg et al., 2011). Although certain strains showed higher $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ resistance than others, the lethal $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ concentration for all strains was relatively low (2.55–5.25 mM) and within the concentration range of $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ produced by lactic starter cultures during manufacture of yogurt (Shimamaru et al., 1992). 

Similarly, screening for an inducible $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ stress response has shown that most strains were unable to mount a response under the conditions tested (Oberg et al., 2011). However, 20 min sublethal $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ treatment does significantly $( p < 0 . 0 5 )$ improve survival against a lethal 2.55 mM $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ challenge in B. longum strains NCC2705 and D2957, and against a 5.25 mM $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ challenge in B. lactis strains BL-04 and RH-1 (Figure 10.6.1). Moreover, a 60 min sublethal $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ treatment of B. longum NCC2705 significantly $( p < 0 . 0 5 )$ increases the survival of this strain at both lethal $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ concentrations (Figure 10.6.2). Collectively, these results indicate that sublethal $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ exposure might be applied to significantly enhance $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ resistance of some B. longum and B. lactis strains.

## 10.6.3 Influence of ${ \bf H } _ { 2 } { \bf O } _ { 2 }$ stress on global gene expression in B. longum strains

To explore the genetic basis for strain-specific differences in $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ resistance between B. longum NCC2705 and D2957, we performed transcriptomics after 5 or 60 min of sublethal $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ exposure. Twenty-one B. longum D2957 genes were significantly $( p < 0 . 0 5 )$ differentially expressed (DE) compared to control cells after 5 min, and 90 DE genes were detected after 60 min exposure. The numbers of DE genes were considerably higher in B. longum NCC2705, with 288 DE genes after 5 min exposure to sublethal $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ levels, and 114 after 60 min exposure (Oberg et al., 2015). 

A majority (60–76%) of the B. longum DE genes had a predicted function, and functional grouping showed $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ exposure triggered upregulation in both strains of genes encoding enzymes known to mitigate the toxic effects of $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ in other bacteria, including the thioredoxin reductase system as well as peroxiredoxin and ribonucleotide reductase alpha and beta subunits (Oberg et al., 2015). Significant upregulation of thioredoxin reductase and peroxiredoxin were noted after 5 and 60 min sublethal $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ exposure in B. longum NCC2705, and after 60 min exposure of B. longum D2957 (Oberg et al., 2015). Induction of ribonucleotide reductase alpha and beta subunits was significant after 5 min treatment in NCC2705, and the beta subunit was upregulated after 60 min exposure in strain D2957. 

Under favorable growth conditions, the thioredoxin reductase system functions with ribonucleoside reductase to use NADPH to reduce the $2 ^ { \prime } \mathrm { O H }$ group of ribose for deoxynucleotide production, as well as to maintain cytoplasmic redox for disulfide bond production in proteins (Arner and Holmgren, 2000; Nordlund and Reichard, 2006). This system also donates electrons to peroxiredoxin for the reduction of $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ to $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } .$ . During oxidative stress, however, cells may use thioredoxin reductase and peroxiredoxin to direct NADPH toward the removal of oxidative free radicals via the reduction of $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ and toxic lipid hydroperoxides (Lu and Holmgren, 2014; Meyer et al., 2009). Schell et al. (2002) suggested these enzymes might be one of the primary defense mechanisms against oxidative stress in bifidobacteria, and other research has shown upregulation of thioredoxin, thioredoxin reductase, and peroxiredoxin in response to oxygen stress (Ruiz et al., 2009; Xiao et al., 2011). 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/d070d93a-9a38-4d08-ab41-83365fc0221e/1e3e7833d8c3600a3547ecfca1027af36bdce40e709eb83a4d54e8246e3879fb.jpg)


![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/d070d93a-9a38-4d08-ab41-83365fc0221e/13e1a844696c11bfafdc2778c36df41d15f3daffcc16645261950beba578b0e5.jpg)



Figure 10.6.1 Percent survival of Bifidobacterium strains after a 20 min experimental stress response induction at 1.25 mM H O followed by lethal challenge at (a) 2.55 mM $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ or (b) 5.25 mM $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ . Lanes: a B. longum NCC2705; b B. longum D2957; c B. lactis BL-04; d B. lactis DSM10140. Open square, control; filled square, induced. Each value is the mean of four replicates. Error bars correspond to the standard error of the mean. Asterisk ( ∗) denotes bars that have a mean percent survival that is significantly different $( p < 0 . 0 5 )$ between control and induced cells.


Oxidative stress damages proteins, so protein protection and turnover are important components of oxidative stress resistance in bacteria (Cabiscol et al., 2000; Shacter, 2000; Stadtman and Levine, 2000). Bifidobacteria possess highly conserved chaperones and chaperonins that act to protect proteins (e.g., DnaJ– DnaK and GroEL–GroES; see Section 14), but their role in $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ resistance is unclear. B. longum NCC2705, for example, showed significant downregulation of genes for several general stress response proteins, including DnaJ and UspA, and no change in expression of groES or groEL in response to $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ exposure (Oberg et al., 2015). This observation is consistent with prior studies, which report no change in expression or downregulation of the general stress response genes in response to acid stress (Jin et al., 2012; Sanchez´ et al., 2007a; see Section 16). In contrast, strain D2957 did show upregulation of groES or groEL after 60 min exposure, but no change in expression of genes for DnaJ and UspA (Oberg et al., 2015). 

Transcriptomes from both strains indicated that protein turnover was important for $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ resistance. B. longum NCC2705 showed upregulation of genes for three proteases (BL0555, BL0781, and BL0945) and an adenosine triphosphate (ATP)-dependent metallo-protease (BL1682) after 5 min $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ exposure, and strain D2957 showed upregulation of two ATPdependent Clp proteases (BL0944 and BL0945) after 60 min exposure. Overall, B. longum transcriptome data support the suggestion that thioredoxin reductase and peroxiredoxin are key defense mechanisms against oxidative stress in this species (Ruiz et al., 2009; Schell et al., 2002; Xiao et al., 2011), and indicate that efficient protein stabilization and turnover are also critical to cell survival. Differences in the timing, total number, and fold change of gene induction in B. longum strains NCC2705 versus D2957 also suggest that the greater $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ resistance of strain NCC2705 (Figures 10.6.1 and 10.6.2) may be linked to more rapid deployment of protective enzymes.
