Figure 11.2.4 Interlinked anabolic and osmostress-adaptive proline biosynthetic pathways in B. subtilis. The ProB–ProA–ProI and ProJ–ProA–ProH enzymes mediate the anabolic and osmostress-adaptive proline biosynthetic routes, respectively; they are interlinked via the proA-encoded γ-glutamyl-phosphate-reductase (ProA) (Brill et al., 2011a, 2011b). Like proI and proH, the proG gene encodes a Δ1-pyrroline-5-carboxylate reductase, but the true physiological role of the ProG enzyme remains unclear. (a) The anabolic proline biosynthetic enzymes are encoded by the proBA operon and by the proI gene. These genes are under the regulatory control of an mRNA-based T-box control mechanism that inhibits transcription when the intracellular proline concentration has a level sufficient for anabolic purposes. Northern blot analyses of the proBA and proI transcripts in cells grown in SMM in the absence or the presence of NaCl were carried out. (b) Synthesis routes of proline under osmotic stress conditions (yellow boxes) and for anabolic purposes (blue boxes) from the biosynthetic precursor proline. Proline inhibits the anabolic glutamate-kinase (ProB) at the level of enzyme activity via a negative feedback loop. (c) Genetic organization of the osmotically controlled proHJ operon and of the proG gene, and levels of their mRNA in cells grown in the presence of various salinities (assessed by Northern blot analysis). This figure was crafted based on previously published data (Brill et al., 2011a, 2011b)


The biochemistry and genetics of the anabolic ProB–ProA– ProI route in B. subtilis are geared to prevent a wasteful overproduction of proline and to adjust the proline pool with the ongoing protein biosynthetic activities of the cell. This is achieved (i) through a sensitive feedback regulation of the enzyme activity of the γ-glutamyl kinase (ProB) by proline (Figure 11.2.4b) (Chen et al., 2007), and (ii) through transcriptional control of the proBA and proI genes through a T-box regulatory mechanism (Figure 11.2.4a) (Brill et al., 2011b). T-box-controlled genes possess long nontranslated 5′ messenger RNA (mRNA) sequences that can fold in two mutually exclusive secondary structures and thereby affect the transcription of the full-length coding sequence (Gutierrez-Preciado et al., 2009). When the proline pool is sufficient to fuel protein biosynthesis, proBA and proI expression is limited through the folding of the 5′ UTRmRNA region into a terminator structure that thereby prevents the transcription of the coding regions. When the cells starve for proline, the 5′ UTR-mRNA region assumes an antiterminator structure that allows the transcription of the fulllength proBA and proI genes. This antiterminator structure is stabilized by the specific binding of the uncharged prolyl-tRNA via a proline-specific specifier codon; hence, the loading status of a tRNAPro is used by the cell as readout of an insufficient proline pool (Gutierrez-Preciado et al., 2009). In contrast, the prolyltRNA charged with proline cannot make such interactions with the T-box device and thus promote premature transcription termination of the proBA and proI mRNAs (Brill et al., 2011b). 

It is obvious that the biochemistry and genetics of the ProB– ProA–ProI route are unable to provide the very large pools of proline (up to 0.5 M) needed for osmostress protection (Brill et al., 2011a; Whatmore et al., 1990; Zaprasis et al., 2013b). Hence, B. subtilis has developed a second route (ProJ– ProA–ProH) (Figure 11.2.4b and 11.2.4c) that produces proline under osmotic stress conditions (Brill et al., 2011a). Although not proven experimentally, the enzyme activity of the ProJ γ- glutamyl kinase, unlike that of ProB, cannot be strongly influenced by proline-mediated feedback control since this type of posttranslational control sets in already at μM concentrations of the effector molecule (Chen et al., 2007). The major osmotic control of the ProJ–ProA–ProH biosynthetic route occurs at the level of transcription of the proHJ operon (Figure 11.2.4c) (Brill et al., 2011a). It is expressed from a SigA-responsive and osmotically controlled promoter. Detailed reporter gene fusion analysis of proH–treA constructs has shown that proHJ expression is strongly increased both subsequent to sudden osmotic upshifts and during sustained increases in the external osmolarity. Once the external osmolarity has exceeded a certain threshold value, proHJ expression increases linearly in response to stepwise increases in the osmolarity (Brill et al., 2011a) and thereby satisfies the need of the B. subtilis cell for an increased proline biosynthetic capacity as a defense against the detrimental effects of high osmolarity on cellular hydration and turgor (Figure 11.2.3c). 

## 11.2.8.2 Synthesis of glycine betaine from the precursor choline

The trimethlyammonium compound glycine betaine is, without any doubt, the most widely employed compatible solute in nature since members of each domain of life use it as an osmostress protectant (Yancey, 2005). In bacteria, glycine betaine can be synthesized either through the stepwise methylation of glycine or through the oxidation of the precursor molecule choline. The de novo synthesis of glycine betaine through the methylation route requires regeneration of the cofactor of the biosynthetic enzymes, S-adenosyl-methionine, a process that demands high energy. The methylation route is therefore infrequently used by microorganisms to produce glycine betaine (Nyyssol ¨ a¨ et al., 2000). Instead, glycine betaine is most frequently produced through the oxidation of choline that typically must be imported by the cells prior to its conversion into glycine betaine. Various combinations of enzymes can be used to synthesize glycine betaine from choline. 

In B. subtilis, a combination of an alcohol dehydrogenase (GbsB) and a glycine betaine aldehyde dehydrogenase (GbsA) is used to enzymatically convert choline to glycine betaine (Figure 11.2.5) (Boch et al., 1996). Since B. subtilis cannot synthesize choline, it needs to import it from the environment. Two ABC transporters with high affinity for choline are used for this purpose, OpuB and OpuC. The OpuB system only imports choline and the intermediate in glycine betaine synthesis, glycine betaine aldehyde, whereas OpuC cannot only import these two compounds but exhibits a very broad substrate specificity (for an overview of the types of compatible solutes imported via OpuC, see Hoffmann and Bremer (2011) and Broy et al. (2015)). The synthesis of glycine betaine from choline confers a considerable degree of osmotic stress resistance; it should be noted in this context that choline is no compatible solute per se, and its import therefore does not confer osmostress protection unless it is converted into glycine betaine (Boch et al., 1994, 1996). 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/d070d93a-9a38-4d08-ab41-83365fc0221e/81babec4ed928b5561a7fd0151f802e039ddad48e2271bb5656a6ed4923d73ce.jpg)



Figure 11.2.5 Synthesis of the osmostress protectant glycine betaine from the precursor choline. Shown are the OpuB and OpuC ABC transporters that are able to take up choline and/or glycine betaine and the GbsB–GbsA-catalyzed glycine betaine synthesis pathway from the precursor choline. Transcription of the corresponding operons (opuB, gbsAB) is under control of the choline-responsive glycine betaine synthesis repressor protein GbsR. This figure was crafted based on previously published data (Kappes et al., 1999; Nau-Wagner et al., 2012).


B. subtilis is a bacterium that frequently lives in association with plants or plant detritus (Belda et al., 2013) and therefore can acquire choline from degraded phospholipids of eukaryotic cell membranes. Since neither choline nor glycine betaine can be used as nutrients by B. subtilis (Boch et al., 1994), enhanced import of the glycine betaine precursor makes physiological sense only under conditions when the cells need to synthesize glycine betaine for osmoprotective purposes and when choline is present in the environment. The regulatory circuit that governs glycine betaine synthesis from choline reflects this fact. The GbsR repressor, a member of the MarR-type family of transcriptional regulators (Figure 11.2.5), controls both the gbsAB glycine betaine synthesis genes and the operon encoding the choline-specific OpuB transporter. Choline serves as the effector molecule for the GbsR repressor protein, and its binding prevents DNA binding by GbsR to its cognate operator sequences (Nau-Wagner et al., 2012). A model has been proposed that envisions a structural rearrangement of the Nterminal DNA-reading head relative to the dimerization domain of GbsR once the effector molecule choline has been bound (Nau-Wagner et al., 2012). Interestingly, glycine betaine aldehyde (Figure 11.2.5) also serves as an effector molecule for GbsR and thereby ensures that this toxic intermediate in glycine betaine synthesis (Boch et al., 1996; Kappes et al., 1999) never accumulates to significant intracellular levels. 

B. subtilis sets its intracellular glycine betaine pool very precisely and in tune with the degree of the osmotic stress that is experienced by the cell (Hoffmann et al., 2013). The GbsRmediated regulatory circuit described in this chapter will lead to continuous glycine betaine synthesis as long as the inducer choline is present in the environment, regardless of the osmotic needs of the cell. This would be very wasteful, as the cell would be eventually forced to expel the newly synthesized glycine betaine to avoid physiologically inadequate values of turgor. Therefore, in the GbsR-mediated regulatory system, a negative feedback loop has been built in that re-establishes GbsR-mediated repression of the gbsAB and opuB operons once the cell has produced enough glycine betaine to balance the osmotic gradient across the cytoplasmic membrane. Not surprisingly, glycine betaine served as the cellular cue to accomplish this, and GbsR is the target of this genetic negative feedback loop. The GbsR regulatory protein thus records and integrates cellular and environmental signals for both the onset and the repression of the synthesis of the osmoprotectant glycine betaine in B. subtilis (Nau-Wagner et al., 2012). 

## 11.2.9 Osmotic control of gene expression

Due to the transient nature of the pulse in SigB-controlled gene expression (Young et al., 2013), implementation of specific stress-adaptive pathways, such as the synthesis and uptake of osmoprotectants, is required for cellular adaptation to sustained environmental challenges (Bremer, 2002; Bremer and Kramer, ¨ 2000). However, there is an interesting interlink between the general stress response system and specific adaptation reactions of the B. subtilis cell exposed to sustained high-osmolarity environments. This interlink is manifested in the genetic control of the opuE and opuD genes. 

OpuE mediates the import of proline as an osmostress protectant (von Blohn et al., 1997). Genetic control of the opuE gene integrates general and specific stress responses via two intertwined and osmotically controlled promoters: One is controlled by the housekeeping sigma factor of B. subtilis, SigA, and the other is responsive to SigB (Figure 11.2.2b) (Spiegelhalter and Bremer, 1998). In the wild-type opuE gene, the level of transcription increases linearly in response to incremental, but lasting, increases in the external salinity (Figure 11.2.2c). The inactivation of either the SigA- or the SigB-type promoters revealed their individual contributions to the osmotic control of opuE expression in either salt-shocked or salt-adapted cells. The SigAtype promoter mediated a dose-dependent response in cells subjected to sustained salt stress, whereas the SigB-type promoter is completely inactive under these conditions (Figure 11.2.2c); however, it permitted a rapid but transient induction of opuE transcription subsequent to a salt shock. The SigA-type promoter was also responsive to a salt shock, but the kinetics of its activation was somewhat delayed in comparison with the SigBtype promoter (Figure 11.2.2d). Hence, the combined activities of these two promoters allow both a rapid and well-graded physiological response to salt challenges through the OpuE-mediated import of the osmoprotectant proline. 

Interestingly, the opuD glycine betaine transporter gene (Kappes et al., 1996) is also part of the SigB-controlled general stress response system (Nicolas et al., 2012). As detailed for opuE, it is expressed from closely spaced SigA- and SigB-type promoters, whose activity is enhanced in response to osmotic stress (our unpublished data). As a consequence, the double osmotic control of opuD expression provides enhanced glycine betaine transport capacity both of osmotically upshocked cells and to B. subtilis cells continuously challenged by high osmolarity (Kappes et al., 1996). 

Genome-wide transcriptional analysis has been performed with both salt-shocked and continuously osmotically stressed B. subtilis cells (Hahne et al., 2010; Kohlstedt et al., 2014; Nicolas et al., 2012; Steil et al., 2003), and thereby provides a comprehensive overview of the transcriptional landscape of osmotically stressed B. subtilis cells. It is beyond the scope of this overview to discuss in detail each of these changes in gene expression; instead, we focus on a few selected examples of genes where more detailed information is already available from targeted genetic and physiological studies. 

In the transcriptome analysis reported by Steil et al. (2003), approximately 5% of the then annotated 4107 protein-coding genes differ significantly (at least threefold) in their expression levels between high- and low-salinity-grown cells. Transcription of 101 genes is downregulated, and most of these genes are functionally associated with either the synthesis of the flagellum, chemotaxis, or (unknown at that time) the production of an extracellular polysaccharide (EPS) matrix. Indeed, highsalinity-challenged B. subtilis cells can no longer swim (Steil et al., 2003); the synthesis of the Hag protein, the major component of the flagellum, is drastically reduced (Hoffmann et al., 2002); and the production of the sugar matrix involved in building the B. subtilis biofilm is downregulated (Rubinstein et al., 2012). Expression of 123 genes is upregulated in the study conducted by Steil et al. (2003), and of these 21 are only indirectly affected by high salinity since such growth conditions triggered in the B. subtilis strain used a severe iron limitation (Hoffmann et al., 2002). The group of 21 salt-inducible genes is functionally annotated either in connection with iron acquisition or with the synthesis of the iron chelator bacillibactin; a bioinformatics analysis revealed that they are all members of the Fur regulon (Hoffmann et al., 2002; Steil et al., 2003). Hence, the expression of 102 genes has been found to be truly induced by high salinity, and genes encoding either uptake or synthesis systems for compatible solutes are prominently represented in this group. A substantial number of genes involved in cell wall synthesis or modification are also represented among the salt-induced genes, indicating that B. subtilis modifies its cell wall when it is continuously exposed to high salinity (Steil et al., 2003). Genes falling into this functional group have also been identified as osmotically controlled in other studies (Fischer and Bremer, 2012; Lopez et al., 1998, 2000; Palomino et al., 2009). 

A substantial overlap (20 out of 102) of osmotically induced genes (Steil et al., 2003) with members of the DegSU regulon (Mader ¨ et al., 2002) has been observed. This includes (i) the structural genes for the DegS sensor kinase and its cognate response regulator DegU, and (ii) an operon (rapG–phrG) that encodes an exported regulatory pro-peptide (PhrG) and a PhrG-responsive regulator (RapG) that controls the DNAbinding activity of DegU-P via protein–protein interactions (Ogura et al., 2003). This implies that the level of DegSU is upregulated in high-salinity-grown cells and that a fine-tuning regulatory circuit exists that operates via the RapG–PhrG signaling system and sets the level and activity of DegU–DegU-P at high salinity. These findings are of special interest for two reasons: (i) The DegSU two-component regulatory system has been previously implicated in the salt stress response of B. subtilis through functional studies (Kunst and Rapoport, 1995; Ruzal and Sanchez-Rivas, 1998); and (ii) the DegS sensor kinase is one of only two sensor kinases out of 36 sensor kinases identified in B. subtilis that are localized in the cytoplasm (Fabret et al., 1999). As we have discussed here in the context of the stressosome controlling the expression of the SigB general stress response system (Marles-Wright et al., 2008), the cytoplasmic localization of DegS requires that the sensing of salt stress imposed by the environment has to rely on a derived cytoplasmic signal that then can be perceived by DegS. The nature of this signal and the way it might be recognized by the DegS sensor kinase are unknown. 

The DegSU-regulated genes found by Steil et al. (2003) comprise only about one-fifth of the B. subtilis genes whose transcription is upregulated in response to sustained high osmolarity. Hence, the DegSU two-component system is definitely not the overarching osmotic stress-sensing and response system of B. subtilis, as has been suggested previously (Ruzal and Sanchez-Rivas, 1998). This conclusion is substantiated by detailed reporter studies and degSU mutant analysis in the framework of studies addressing the properties of the SigAdependent opuA promoter, a promoter that is strongly inducible by high osmolarity but is not influenced by the DegSU twocomponent system (Hoffmann et al., 2013). It also should be noted in this context that the DegSU system is involved in the regulation of a diverse set of cellular processes that probably generate different types of environmental and cellular signals to which the DegS sensor kinase will then respond (Cairns et al., 2013). In no case have the precise type(s) of signal(s) and the molecular mechanism(s) of their perception by DegS been elucidated. 

Here, we describe the osmotic control of the opuA, proHJ, and yqiHIK operons from B. subtilis since detailed transcriptional and mutational studies have been reported for their promoters. The opuA operon (opuAA–opuAB–opuAC) encodes the components of the OpuA ABC transport system for glycine betaine (Figure 11.2.1) (Hoffmann et al., 2013; Kempf and Bremer, 1995), proHJ encodes the key enzymes for the osmostress adaptive proline biosynthetic route (Figure 11.2.4b,c) (Brill et al., 2011a), and the yqiHIK operon encodes a lipoprotein exposed at the cell surface (YqiH), an extracellular Nacetyl-muramyl-L-alanine amidase (YqiI), and a cytoplasmic glycerophosphodiester phospodiesterase (YqiK) (Fischer and Bremer, 2012). In each of these three operons, the osmotically controlled promoter has been mapped by primer extension analysis (Figure 11.2.6a), and its activity has been studied genetically via reporter gene fusion analysis and site-directed mutagenesis studies. The sequences of the −10 and −35 regions of all three promoters resemble those of SigA-type housekeeping promoters from B. subtilis but deviate in key positions from the consensus sequence (Figure 11.2.6a). Mutations that improve the match of the opuA, proHJ, and yqiHIK promoters to the consensus sequence lead to enhanced gene expression both at low and at high salinity, indicating that the deviations from the SigA-type consensus sequence serve to keep the activity of these osmotically controlled promoters low in cells grown in low-salinity minimal media. In none of the studied promoter variants is the osmotic control entirely lost, but it is often strongly reduced (Fischer and Bremer, 2012; Hoffmann et al., 2013; T. Hoffmann, M. Bleisteiner, and E. Bremer, unpublished data). 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/d070d93a-9a38-4d08-ab41-83365fc0221e/eb0be5114c1230aebf75aab1259291201f4d248276432ffa53c0b18a5a37ffaf.jpg)


![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/d070d93a-9a38-4d08-ab41-83365fc0221e/1388660b88622d566a1d90a086d1171979b5ef3ac4e226308a55eaee1b7537c1.jpg)



Figure 11.2.6 DNA sequences of osmotically regulated promoters of B. subtilis that have been studied experimentally. (a) Alignments of promoter sequences of B. subtilis that have been shown to be osmotically inducible. Green blocks mark the characteristic −10 and −35 regions of SigA-type promoters, and the yellow block marks a TG motif often found in B. subtilis promoters at position −16. The transcription initiation sites that have been mapped via primer extension analysis are marked in red. (b) Potential cytoplasmic cues that might trigger osmotically controlled gene expression.


Transcriptional studies with reporter gene fusions show that the activity of the promoters of the opuA, proHJ, and yqiHIK operons is finely tuned and sensitively linked to the degree of the osmotic stress imposed onto the cell. However, the pattern of gene expression is strikingly different among the three studied systems: (i) opuA transcription increases linearly with the environmental salinity up to a level of 0.6 M NaCl, and then levels off (Hoffmann et al., 2013); (ii) proHJ transcription remains at a basal level up to medium salinity of 0.2 M NaCl, and then increases linearly up to the maximum tested salinity of 1.0 M NaCl (Brill et al., 2011a); and (iii) yqiHIK transcription remains at a basal activity level up to the substantial salinity of 0.6 M NaCl, and thereafter increases linearly up to the maximum tested salinity of 1.2 M NaCl (Fischer and Bremer, 2012). These examples illustrate that the B. subtilis cell is able to transform information gleaned about environmentally imposed osmotic stress into an individual pattern of promoter activity. 

The fact that the B. subtilis cell can really sense osmotic stress, rather than NaCl-elicited stress alone, has been established through studies with the opuA and proHJ promoters (Brill et al., 2011a; Hoffmann et al., 2013). Both promoters required for their osmotic activation the establishment of a trans-membrane osmotic gradient, since enhanced opuA and proHJ promoter activity could not be elicited by glycerol, an osmolyte that is readily membrane-permeable at high external concentrations. Conversely, both ionic (NaCl and KCl) and non-ionic (sucrose and lactose) osmolytes that are not membrane permeable triggered strongly enhanced levels of opuA and proHJ transcription. Curiously, the tested non-ionic osmolytes elicited a far stronger increase in gene expression in comparison with isoosmotic concentrations of ionic osmolytes, an observation that is so far not rationally explainable (Brill et al., 2011a; Hoffmann et al., 2013). Interestingly, the synthesis (e.g., proline or glycine betaine) or uptake (e.g., glycine betaine or carnitine) of compatible solutes downregulates the level of opuA and proHJ transcription. This fits with the idea that the degree of osmotic stress, as reflected by the steepness of the osmotic gradient between the outside and the inside of the cell, is sensed by the B. subtilis cell and is then equated into the transcriptional control of the opuA and proHJ promoters. 

## 11.2.10 Biofilm formation and osmotic forces

B. subtilis has long been a favorite microorganism for bacterial geneticists and physiologists, and their work has made this bacterium the model system for Gram-positive bacteria. However, the long life in the laboratory led to the domestication of B. subtilis, and in the course of this process several mutations were acquired by the B. subtilis 168 lineage of laboratory strains that resulted in the loss of a key developmental trait, namely the ability to form robust biofilms on solid surfaces and at liquid– air interfaces (see Section 22). The ability of nondomesticated B. subtilis strains to form such biofilms (Branda et al., 2001) is genetically determined by rather complex regulatory circuits, and we refer the reader to several excellent recent reviews on this topic (Lopez et al., 2009b; Vlamakis et al., 2013). It suffices here to state that the life of individual cells in liquid media is very different from that of cells encased in the biofilm that forms multicellular communities in which different cell types can coexist. This is particularly true when one considers the osmotic forces that exists within the biofilm and that shape its growth (Seminara et al., 2012). The biofilms matrix formed by B. subtilis consists of a copious amount of EPS and an amyloid fiber-forming protein (TasA) (Vlamakis et al., 2013). The surface of the biofilm is coated with a layer of the BslA protein, a hydrophobin that provides properties to the surface of these structures resembling those of Teflon; hence, water does not penetrate from above into the biofilm (Hobley et al., 2013). The considerable osmotic potential of the EPS matrix results in the influx of water from the agar surface into the biofilm, and the thereby generated osmotic forces lead to the spreading of the biofilm (Seminara et al., 2012). 

Within the biofilm, microscopic water channels exist that transport nutrients and waste products. 

The increase in osmotic pressure caused by the formation of the EPS is a cue that downregulates the gene cluster (epsA-O) required for the synthesis of the sugar component of the extracellular matrix and the operon (tapA–sipW–tasA) that directs the formation of the amyloid-like fibers (Nicolas et al., 2012; Rubinstein et al., 2012). These EPS-elicited nonspecific osmotic pressure effects activate the histidine kinase KinD, which in turn directs the phosphorylation of the master regulatory protein of sporulation and biofilm formation, Spo0A, which at high levels represses matrix gene expression (Rubinstein et al., 2012). The physiological and genetic consequences of high osmotic pressure within the biofilm are not yet fully comprehended, but Rubinstein et al. (2012) suggested that the sensing of this physical cue might be a strategy to coordinate multicellular behavior in the confines of the biofilm (Rubinstein et al., 2012). 

An interesting connection between the genetic system that directs biofilm formation in B. subtilis and osmotically controlled expression of genes encoding uptake systems for compatible solutes has recently been uncovered in a study conducted by Kearns and coworkers (Winkelman et al., 2013). A key regulator of biofilm formation, SinR, negatively controls expression of the eps and tapA–sipW–tasA operons. The RemA activator protein antagonizes the repressing effects of SinR on eps and tapA–sipW–tasA expression and thereby stimulates biofilm formation. In connection with a DNA-profiling study of a remA mutant strain, the expression of the opuA, opuB, and opuC operons in osmotically nonstressed cells was found to be affected (Winkelman et al., 2013). DNA-footprinting studies with the purified RemA protein at the opuA promoter region revealed an extended RemA-binding region upstream of the opuA −35 sequence; it is composed of five distinct subsites (Winkelman et al., 2013). The opuA regulatory region is thus a direct target of a transcription control protein that is essential for biofilm formation by B. subtilis (Winkelman et al., 2009). Reporter gene studies showed that RemA, like its actions at the eps and tapA– sipW–tasA regulatory regions (Winkelman et al., 2009, 2013), serves as an activator protein and is crucial for opuA expression under both low- and high-salinity growth conditions (T. Hoffmann and E. Bremer, unpublished data). However, it is not yet clear whether RemA transduces the osmotic signal to the opuA promoter, or whether it just serves a role of a general activator of opuA transcription, a function that would be independent from the signal transduction pathway that confers osmotic control of promoter activity. 

We find the observation quite remarkable that the structural genes for three osmostress protectant uptake systems (OpuA, OpuB, and OpuC) are members of the RemA regulon (Winkelman et al., 2013). It bears the question of how B. subtilis cells embedded in a biofilm might actually profit for their physiology and well-being from the import of compatible solutes. 

## 11.2.11 The big challenge: sensing osmotic changes

Since the 1990s, the basic cellular adjustment strategies of B. subtilis to fluctuations in the external salinity have been worked out in quite some detail. However, many questions remain with respect to the precise physiological functions of those genes whose transcription is upregulated either under acute osmotic upshifts or under sustained salt stress (Hahne et al., 2010; Hoper ¨ et al., 2005., 2006; Kohlstedt et al., 2014; Nannapaneni et al., 2012; Nicolas et al., 2012; Steil et al., 2003). The most pressing question that needs to be answered is how increases in the external osmotic pressure are perceived, genetically processed, and then transduced to the transcription apparatus so that altered gene expression will ensue. Players in such a process have been implicated in the case of the SigB-controlled stress response (via the stressosome), biofilm formation (via KinD), and the DegSU two-component regulatory system. KinD is a membrane-embedded sensor kinase, whereas the stressosome and the DegS sensor kinase are located in the cytoplasm. As discussed in this chapter in the context of these latter two systems, both the stressosome and DegS must perceive a cytoplasmic signal that is indirectly derived from changes in the external osmolarity. Although KinD, the stressosome, and DegS have all been implicated in the cellular osmostress response, it is unclear how the B. subtilis cell actually uses these devices to measure – either directly or indirectly – a change in an external physical parameter, environmental osmolarity. It should be noted here that the systems directly involved in ameliorating the dire consequences of high osmolarity on cellular physiology (e.g., systems for uptake and synthesis of compatible solutes) (Figure 11.2.1) are either fully independent of (e.g., opuA and proHJ), or are only partially dependent on (e.g., opuE and opuD), these rather general regulatory systems. 

An overarching osmosensory and regulatory system that might operate in B. subtilis has not been detected. This might be due to the fact that such a system might be essential, that the screening procedures used to search for it might be unsuitable, or that it simply does not exist. If one just considers the last scenario to be correct, how might osmotic control of the SigAtype promoters of the opuA, opuB, opuC, opuD, opuE, proHJ, and yqiHIK genes (Figure 11.2.6a) be achieved in the absence of an osmostress-specific regulatory protein? High osmolarity alters the ionic composition and biophysical properties of the cytoplasm (Cayley and Record, 2003; Whatmore et al., 1990; Wood, 2011). Such changes might be sensed directly by the RNA polymerase, and in combination with altered DNA-superhelicity (Higgins et al., 1988; Krispin and Allmansberger, 1995), specific promoter sequences and their flanking regions might change the transcriptional activity of a given osmoregulated promoter (Figure 11.2.6b). It will be a challenge to experimentally verify or refute such a model. The proHJ promoter with its essentially linear dependence for its activity on the external osmolarity (Brill et al., 2011a) might provide a suitable test system for the outlined hypothesis.

# Hyperosmotic response of Streptococcus mutans: from microscopic physiology to transcriptomic profile

Lu Wang and Xin Xu 

State Key Laboratory of Oral Diseases, West China Hospital of Stomatology, Sichuan University, Chengdu, People’s Republic of China 

## 11.3.1 Introduction

Dental plaque biofilm is an ecologic niche with densely packed bacteria. The oral cavity consists of distinct habitats colonized with dynamic microbiota (Xu et al., 2014). Microbial residents in the oral cavity experience a wide range of stresses and need to cope with the fluctuations of nutrients and the environmental stimuli in order to survive (Lemos and Burne, 2008). Osmotic stress is one of the environmental challenges to which bacteria are frequently exposed, and it has significant impact on dental plaque ecology and the development of dental caries (Lemos et al., 2005). Streptococcus mutans is a common resident in dental plaque; it has been recognized as the primary causative agent of dental caries for decades. S. mutans is able to take advantage of high carbohydrate consumption and acid accumulation, and it eventually emerges as a numerically predominant resident within cariogenic plaque (Lemos and Burne, 2008; Lemos et al., 2005). S. mutans as an acidogenic bacteria within dental plaque has the ability to metabolize carbohydrate to produce organic acids, which decrease the environmental pH and increase ionic strength of the plaque fluid due to tooth demineralization and consequent calcium and phosphate accumulation (Gao et al., 2001). Hence, S. mutans is likely to develop sophisticated machineries to counter the detrimental effects of hyperosmotic stress, so as to persevere within the dental plaque. Microorganisms usually adapt to hyperosmotic challenges by increasing the intracellular levels of K+ and accumulating compatible solutes (Sleator and Hill, 2002; Weber and Jung, 2002). The complete genome sequence of S. mutans has revealed several genes that share homology with K+ transporters and the Opu family of adenosine triphosphate (ATP)-binding cassette (ABC) transporters of Escherichia coli (Ajdic et al., 2002; Epstein, 2003). These data suggest that S. mutans may mobilize a series of regulated genes and pathways to internalize $\mathrm { K } ^ { + }$ and accumulate compatible solutes to counter hyperosmotic challenges. Quite a few candidates involved in the hyperosmotic challenge response of S. mutans have been identified in a previous study, and cross-talk between osmotic and oxidative stress responses in S. mutans has been suggested (Abranches et al., 2006). Here, we have profiled the transcriptomic alterations of S. mutans in response to hyperosmotic stress by using microarrays. We identified several significantly changed genes and pathways that were closely associated with the hyperosmotic stress response of S. mutans. Two genes (gtfB and comC) were identified to be responsible for the phenotypic changes of the organism observed under hyperosmotic challenges. Data from our work provide a comprehensive understanding of the sophisticated machineries adopted by S. mutans to better fit the physiologically relevant hyperosmolality and thus persevere within the oral cavity. 

## 11.3.2 Materials and methods

Bacterial strains and culture conditions: S. mutans UA159 was purchased from the American Type Culture Collection (ATCC, USA). The strain was grown in brain heart infusion (BHI) medium (Difco, USA) at $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ in a 5% $\mathrm { C O } _ { 2 }$ atmosphere. To determine the subinhibitory concentration of NaCl, a microdilution method was used as described in Xu et al. (2011). BHI medium supplemented with 0.05, 0.1, 0.2, 0.4, 0.5, 0.6, 0.8, and 1.0 M of NaCl was used to grow the bacteria. To construct growth curves of S. mutans UA159 under various osmotic conditions, we used a Bioscreen C analyzer $( \mathrm { O y }$ Growth Curves AB Ltd., Finland) to monitor the optical density of the cultures at 600 nm (Olsen et al., 2010). The effects of increasing concentrations of NaCl on the formation of S. mutans biofilm were quantified in a 96-well microtiter plate as described in Reuter et al. (2010). Specifically, S. mutans UA159 $( 1 \times 1 0 ^ { 6 }$ CFU/ml) was grown in BHI supplemented with 1% (wt./vol.) sucrose and NaCl (0.05–1.0 M) at $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 24 h. The culture supernatant from each well was then decanted, and the adherent biofilm was washed three times with phosphate buffered saline (PBS), fixed with methanol for 15 min, and stained with 0.1% (wt./vol.) crystal violet (Sigma-Aldrich Corp., USA) for 5 min. Subsequently, the wells were rinsed with deionized water until the blank wells appeared colorless; 200 μl of 95% ethanol was then added. The plates were shaken at room temperature for 30 min, and the absorbance at 595 nm was recorded. The same method was used to quantify the biomass of S. mutans after exposure to 0.4 M NaCl for 15 min. All experiments were performed three separate times, and the average was calculated. 

Biofilm viability assay: After short-term (15 min) exposure to 0.4 M of NaCl, 24 h pre-established S. mutans biofilms were gently collected and suspended in PBS to yield an $\mathrm { \ O D } _ { 6 0 0 \mathrm { n m } }$ of 1.0. After serial dilution, the bacterial suspension was cultured on BHI agar plates at $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ in a 5% CO atmosphere for 48 h. Subsequently, colony-forming units (CFUs) of biofilms were enumerated. The live/dead fluorescence stain of treated biofilms was performed with LIVE/DEAD BacLight-Bacterial Viability Kit 7012 (Invitrogen, Molecular Probes, Inc., USA) according to the manufacturer’s instructions. The biofilms images were captured using a Leica TCS SP2 confocal laser-scanning microscope (Leica, Germany), and the percentage of viable cells was calculated by Image Pro-Plus 6.0 (Media Cybernetics Inc., USA). 

Microbial biofilm configuration: Scanning electron microscopy (SEM) was used to investigate the alterations of S. mutans biofilm configuration during hyperosmotic challenges, as described previously (Hasan et al., 2012). S. mutans biofilms grown on glass slides supplemented with 0.4 M of NaCl for 24 h as well as the 24 h pre-established biofilms treated with 0.4 M of NaCl for 15 min were used to investigate the long-term and short-term effects of hyperosmotic conditions, respectively. After incubation with hyperosmotic media, biofilm samples were washed two times with sterile PBS to eliminate planktonic cells and fixed with 2.5% glutaraldehyde at $4 ~ { } ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ overnight. The samples were then dehydrated in a graded series of ethanol (50%, 60%, 70%, 80%, 90%, 95%, and 100%), dried in a freeze dryer, gold-coated, and examined under a SEM (FEI, USA). We also double-labeled the biofilm samples with the method described by Xiao et al. (Xiao and Koo, 2010; Xiao et al., 2012). The extracellular polysaccharides (EPS) matrix of S. mutans biofilm was labeled by 2.5 μmol 1-1 of Alexa Fluor647–labeled dextran conjugate (10,000 MW; absorbance/fluorescence emission maxima of 650/668 nm; Molecular Probes Inc.). The bacterial cells in biofilms were labeled by means of SYTO 9 green fluorescent nucleic acid stain (2.5 μmol 1-1, 480/500nm; Molecular Probes Inc.). The images of the biofilms were captured by a Leica TCS SP2 confocal laser-scanning microscope (Leica) and analyzed with an image-processing software, COMSTAT, as described in Heydorn et al. (2000). The three-dimensional architecture of the biofilms was reconstructed using AmiraTM5.0.2 (Mercury Computer Systems, USA). 

RNA isolation: S. mutans planktonic cells at the midlogarithmic phase $( \mathrm { O D } _ { 6 0 0 \mathrm { n m } } = 0 . 5 )$ were treated with 0.4 M of NaCl at $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 15 min. Cells were collected and then treated with RNA-protect reagent immediately after hyperosmotic exposure (Qiagen, USA). RNeasy Mini kit (Qiagen) was used to extract total RNA (Xue et al., 2010). Genome DNA was eliminated by an Rnase-Free DNase Set (Qiagen). Total RNA concentrations were determined by a Nanodrop ND 1000 spectrophotometer (Thermo Fisher Scientific, USA), and an Agilent 2100 Bioanalyser (Agilent Technologies, USA) was used to evaluate the RNA quality (Figure 11.3.1). The isolated RNA was stored a $\mathrm { t } - 8 0 { } ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for use. 

Microarray procedures: We adopted S. mutans UA159 (NC004350) NimbleGen Genechip $( 4 \times 7 2 ~ \mathrm { K } )$ whole-genome array to profile the transcriptome of S. mutans under hyperosmotic conditions. The oligoarrays included 1949 S. mutans UA159 open reading frames with twelve 24-mer probe pairs (PM/MM) per gene, and each probe was replicated three times. The design also included random GC and other control probes. A Gene $\operatorname { P i x } ^ { \circledast }$ 4000B Microarray Scanner (Axon Instruments, USA) was applied to scan array images. To analyze microarray data, the robust multiarray average (RMA) algorithm was employed to normalize raw data using Roche NimbleScan software version 2.6. The average value of each replicate probe was utilized to represent the signal intensity of the corresponding gene. All values were log transformed for further analyses. The normalized data with annotation information were processed by combining several different R/Bioconductor packages. In order to identify the differentially expressed genes (DEGs), a nonparametric statistical method was performed (Hong et al., 2006). With 100,000 permutation tests, we identified genes with a minimum twofold change and a false discovery rate (FDR) smaller than 0.1 as DEGs. Furthermore, the whole S. mutans UA159 pathway database was established based on the KEGG Pathway, and pathways involved in hyperosmotic stress response of S. mutans were determined by using gene set enrichment analysis (GSEA) (KEGG, 2016; Subramanian et al., 2007). 

Quantitative real-time polymerase chain reaction (PCR) was performed to analyze selected genes (Table 11.3.1) by using a SYBR Green reverse transcription PCR (RT-PCR) kit (TaKaRa, China) according to the manufacturer’s instructions. The results of quantitative PCR showed acceptable consistency with the microarray data. 

Statistical analysis: Data were statistically analyzed with R software. A Student’s t-test was used to compare the nontreated control groups with the treatment groups. Data were considered significantly different if the two-tailed p-value was <0.05. 

Microarray data accession: All the microarray data have been submitted to the NCBI Gene Expression Omnibus database under the accession number of GSE47170 (http://www .ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/geo/query/acc.cgi?acc=GSE47170).

## 11.3.3 Results and discussion

## 11.3.3.1 Hyperosmotic conditions initiate biofilm dispersal

By constructing the growth curve of S. mutans under increasing concentrations of NaCl, we identified 0.4 M of NaCl as the subinhibitory level of hyperosmotic stress on the growth of S. mutans (Figure 11.3.2a). We then investigated the short-term and long-term effects of 0.4 M NaCl on S. mutans biofilm configurations. We showed that the biomass of S. mutans was significantly reduced after hyperosmotic challenge (Figure 11.3.2b and 11.3.2c). Nevertheless, the results of a live/dead fluorescence stain of biofilm (Figures 11.3.2d and 11.3.2e) and the biofilm CFU count (Figure 11.3.2f) showed that the percentage of viable cells after treatment with 0.4 M NaCl was not significantly decreased compared to that of the nontreated control. These data indicated that the observed biomass reduction was more likely attributed to the dispersal of biofilm under hyperosmotic conditions rather than to direct growth inhibition by physiologically relevant hyperosmolality. 

This speculation was further confirmed with fluorescence double labeling and SEM (Figure 11.3.3), as the hyperosmotic challenge reduced not only microbial content but also the EPS matrix within S. mutans biofilms. 

To investigate the underlying molecular machineries, we further used a whole-genome microarray to profile the transcriptomic changes of S. mutans during short-term exposure (15 min) to 0.4 M of NaCl. Microarray data identified 40 differentially expressed genes (DEGs), including 14 upregulated and 26 downregulated genes (Table 11.3.2 and Figure 11.3.4) of S. mutans after exposure to 0.4 M of NaCl for 15 min. Quantitative real-time PCR was performed to further validate the DEGs, and significant downregulation of gtfB and comC was identified (Figure 11.3.5). EPS is known as a significant factor that mediates the cellular adherence and biofilm formation of S. mutans. Glycosyltransferase B encoded by gtfB is one of the Gtf ’s that are responsible for the EPS synthesis (Koo et al., 2009). Therefore, the downregulation of gtfB expression under hyperosmotic conditions can result in reduction of EPS synthesis, and more likely lead to bacteria breaking their bonds. Consequently, a less condensed S. mutans biofilm with reduced biomass is formed.

The other downregulated gene comC encodes competencestimulating peptide (CSP), which is a member of the bacterial quorum-sensing system involved in competence development, acid tolerance, and biofilm formation of S. mutans (Ahn et al., 2006). Importantly, recent findings from Levesque’s group have ´ demonstrated that a high level of CSP may act as an “alarmone,” triggering “guard cells” autolysis and release of environmental DNA (eDNA) necessary for the genetic diversity and survival of the whole community (Perry et al., 2009a,b). Hence, the downregulation of comC may cause the lifestyle shift of S. mutans cells from a static biofilm-based style to a more motile planktonic one. Taken together, we believe that hyperosmotic challenge is able to initiate biofilm dispersal, possibly by downregulating the expression of $g t f B$ and comC of S. mutans.

## 11.3.3.2 S. mutans increases carbohydrates consumption in response to hyperosmotic challenge

An osmotic upshift forces bacteria to change their physiology and related gene expression so as to maintain the cell turgor. Most bacterial do not possess active transporters for water; therefore, pooling “compatible solutes” is believed to be the usual strategy adopted by most bacteria to deal with hyperosmotic conditions (Kempf and Bremer, 1998). Bacteria have evolved sophisticated machineries to accumulate compatible solutes during elevated osmolality. Bacteria can either synthesize compatible solutes intracellularly or internalize necessary compounds by upregulating specific transport systems to counter hyperosmolality (Sleator and Hill, 2002). It has been reported that ABC transporter homologous genes (opcA and opuAA) may be responsible for the uptake of compatible solutes after short-term exposure to hyperosmotic challenge (Abranches et al., 2006). In our study, we found that several genes involved in the phosphotransferase system (PTS) and carbohydrate metabolism were significantly upregulated upon short-term hyperosmotic challenge (Table 11.3.2). By further categorizing the majority of those differentially expressed genes into 12 KEGG pathways, we found that pathways involved in carbohydrates consumption, including PTS, galactose metabolism, fructose–mannose metabolism, and pyruvate metabolism, were significantly upregulated (Figures 11.3.6 and 11.3.7). Our data indicate that in order to compensate the increased energy demand for active internalization of compatible solutes in hyperosmotic condition, S. mutans upregulates genes involved in carbohydrates transport and metabolism to enhance energy generation. 

## 11.3.3.3 Hyperosmotic challenge prepares S. mutans for better fitness under multiple environmental stimuli

Downregulation of gtfB in hyperosmotic condition has also been reported in S. mutans during oxygen challenge. In addition, genes and pathways involved in carbohydrates metabolism are also upregulated during oxygen challenge (Ahn et al., 2007), suggesting a potential crosstalk between hyperosmotic stress responses and other environmental responses of S. mutans. This speculation has been further supported by Burne’s previous study, in which two oxidative stress genes, sodA and nox, were induced during hyperosmotic stress (Abranches et al., 2006).

In the current study, we identified two specific genes, lpl and clpB, that were significantly upregulated in hyperosmotic condition (Table 11.3.2 and Figure 11.3.5). lpl is an indispensable gene involved in acid tolerance of S. mutans (Matsui and Cvitkovitch, 2010; see Section 16). Thereby, consequently, the observed upregulation of lgl in response to hyperosmotic challenge may enhance the aciduricity of S. mutans to better counter the adversity of acid stress. clpB is involved in the recovery of the cell from heat-induced damage by assisting unfolding the denatured protein amassed during heat shock (Motohashi et al., 1999; see Section 13). A previous study from Burne’s group has also shown a significant upregulation of clpB in S. mutans after oxygen challenge (Ahn et al., 2007). Therefore, clpB induced by hyperosmotic stress may enhance the fitness of S. mutans in detrimental environmental conditions such as oxidative and hyperthermic conditions. 

Furthermore, since dispersal cells from biofilm can colonize more niches than the original biofilm (McDougald et al., 2012), the observed biofilm dispersal under hyperosmotic condition may be another mechanism adopted by S. mutans to better fit the fluctuating environment in the oral cavity. Hyperosmotic challenges initiate S. mutans biofilm dispersal, to a certain extent enhancing the colonizing capacity of S. mutans cells, and ultimately benefit its fitness under the fluctuating environment within the oral cavity.

Transcript enrichments are displayed in the heat map from low (blue) to high (red). As shown on the top of the heat map, the similar expression pattern transcripts are clustered together. On the right of the heat map, Gene IDs and their associated gene annotations are displayed. The reuse of this figure (Liu et al., 2013) is licensed under a Creative Commons Attribution License (http://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/2.0/).


In conclusion, although hyperosmotic challenge may induce significant stress response in S. mutans, this bacterium has developed a sophisticated transcriptional network to counter those elicited detrimental effects. In addition, S. mutans can mobilize genes and pathways to take full advantage of hyperosmotic stimuli to better fit the fluctuating environments within the oral cavity, and thus emerge as the numeric-predominant bacteria under cariogenic conditions such as frequent sugar uptake (Figure 11.3.8).

# Defective ribosome maturation or function makes Escherichia coli cells salt-resistant

Hyouta Himeno, Takefusa Tarusawa, Shion Ito, and Simon Goto 

Department of Biochemistry and Molecular Biology, Faculty of Agriculture and Life Science, Hirosaki University, Hirosaki, Japan 

## 11.4.1 Introduction

Osmotic upshift (hyperosmotic shock) triggers water efflux from bacterial cells, inducing physical changes in cells including cell shrinkage (Koch, 1984), which are sensed by osmosensors embedded in the cytoplasmic membrane (Wood, 1999). It promotes rapid influx of potassium ions through the transporters, Kdp and Trk, to counteract the osmotic pressure, which accompanies efflux of putrescine (McLaggan et al., 1994; Poolman et al., 2004). Concomitantly with immediate accumulation of potassium ions, glutamate begins to accumulate in the cell, and potassium glutamate switches the sigma70-dependent general transcription to sigma S–specific transcription (Lee and Gralla, 2004; Rajkumari et al., 1996; see Chapters 2.4, 2.15, 4.4). This promotes uptake and synthesis of osmoprotectants such as glycine betaine, proline, and trehalose (Battesti et al., 2011; Lucht and Bremer, 1994), which attenuate accumulation of potassium ions in the cell (see Chapters 11.2, 11.4). 

Osmotic upshift stimulates another stress response pathway governed by sigma E, an alternative sigma factor that is involved in expression of periplasmic and outer membrane proteins (Alba and Gross, 2004; Raivio and Silhavy, 2001). Sigma E is usually sequestered in the cytoplasmic membrane by the antisigma factor RseA. Upon exocytoplasmic stresses, including salt stress, the inner membrane–anchored protease DegS and the membraneembedded protease YaeL cleave RseA to release sigma E into the cytoplasm, allowing activation of sigma E (Alba et al., 2002) (see Section 18). 

Osmotic upshift also stimulates KdpD–KdpE and EnvZ– OmpR two-component systems. The sensor kinase KdpD senses osmotic upshift to upregulate the expression of the kdp operon encoding the ATPase potassium transporter Kdp (KdpFABC) (Ballal et al., 2007). EnvZ also senses osmotic upshift to regulate the expression of outer membrane proteins, including porins, although contribution of this regulation to resistance of the cell to osmotic stress remains obscure (Pratt et al., 1996). 

Thus, multiple regulation pathways operate upon osmotic upshift, whereas involvement of the ribosome has not been focused on (Figure 11.4.1). The ribosome is a huge molecular complex that facilitates protein synthesis through multiple steps, including binding of initiator transfer RNA (tRNA) to the small (30S) subunit with the initiation factors to form an initiation complex, subunit association to initiate the elongation process, binding of a ternary complex (aminoacyltRNA/EF-Tu/guanosine triphosphate (GTP)) to the ribosome, GTP hydrolysis by EF-Tu for accommodation of aminoacyltRNA into the A-site, peptidyl transfer from peptidyl-tRNA in the P-site to aminoacyl-tRNA in the A-site, translocation of peptidyl-tRNA from the A-site to the P-sites driven by EF-G, release of deacylated tRNA from the E-site, peptide release by peptide release factors to terminate translation, and ribosome recycling (Schmeing and Ramakrishnan, 2009). To accomplish these complicated tasks for protein synthesis, the ribosome contains multiple functional regions, three tRNA-binding sites, a peptidyl-transferase center, a decoding region, and a GTPase-associated center, which are elaborately coordinated with each other. Each subunit of the ribosome is built up from one or a few RNAs and dozens of proteins through multiple steps, including transcription for the ribosomal RNA (rRNA) precursors, core formation of the internal domains, sequential binding of ribosomal proteins to the rRNA precursor, stepwise cleavage and trimming of the rRNA precursor into mature rRNA, stepwise folding and conformational changes of rRNAs and proteins, modification of rRNAs and proteins, and formation of the global structure through interaction between domains (Goto et al., 2013; Kaczanowska and Ryden-Aulin, ´ 2007). These processes require dozens and hundreds of transacting factors (ribosome maturation factors) in bacteria and eukaryotes, respectively. Here, we present our novel finding showing that defective ribosome maturation or function provides Escherichia coli cells with resistance to osmotic (salt) stress. 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/d070d93a-9a38-4d08-ab41-83365fc0221e/cc22bcac5744899e0ed0f463aeb243a6cd6ea7d8b49e96dc93f229ed289f44a7.jpg)



Figure 11.4.1 Multiple regulation pathways in response to osmotic upshift in E. coli. Osmotic upshift promotes influx of potassium ions through Trk and Kdp transporters. The accumulated potassium glutamate represses the sigma70-dependent general transcription, while it activates the sigma S-specific transcription (Lee and Gralla, 2004; Rajkumari et al., 1996). In parallel, sigma S, which is regulated at the levels of transcription, translation, and protein stability, becomes actively expressed (Hengge, 2009; Loewen and Hengge-Aronis, 1994; Majdalani et al., 2001). Sigma S promotes transcription of the genes for uptake and synthesis of osmoprotectants (Battesti et al., 2011), which in turn attenuates accumulation of potassium ions in the cell. Osmotic upshift is sensed by the sensor kinase KdpD, which phosphorylates KdpE (the KdpD–KdpE two-component system), leading to activation of transcription for the Kdp transporter (Ballal et al., 2007). Osmotic upshift is also sensed by another two-component system, EnvZ–OmpR, which regulates the expression of outer membrane proteins, including the OmpC and OmpF porins (Pratt et al., 1996). Osmotic upshift also stimulates DegS and YaeL proteases that degrade the antisigma factor RseA, which releases sigma E from the cytoplasmic membrane to activate it (Alba and Gross, 2004; Raivio and Silhavy, 2001). Activated sigma E specifically transcribes the stress-related genes (e.g., those for periplasmic and outer membrane proteins to maintain the cell envelope integrity) (Hayden and Ades, 2008). Sigma E also promotes expression of small noncoding RNAs that regulate the expression of outer membrane proteins downstream of the EnvZ–OmpR two-component system at the translational level (Valentin-Hansen et al., 2007).


## 11.4.2 Depletion of RsgA results in resistance of E. coli cells to salt stress

RsgA (ribosome small subunit–dependent GTPase A) has a faint GTPase activity that is activated by the 30S subunit of the ribosome by two orders of magnitude (Daigle and Brown, 2004; Himeno et al., 2004). Deletion of the gene for RsgA from the E. coli genome results in a slow-growth phenotype, defective subunit assembly of the ribosome, and accumulation of immature 30S subunits containing 17S RNA, a predominant precursor of 16S rRNA, in the cell (Himeno et al., 2004). Thus, RsgA has become recognized as a maturation factor for the 30S subunit (Goto et al., 2013). RsgA binds around the decoding center of the 30S subunit (Kimura et al., 2008) to dissociate RbfA, another ribosome maturation factor, from the 30S subunit or its precursor in nearly the last stage of maturation (Goto et al., 2011). 

As described here, the growth rate of the RsgA-deficient strain is significantly lower than that of the wild-type strain. As expected, the growth rates of RsgA-deficient and wild-type strains decrease when a high concentration of NaCl is added to the culture medium. Intriguingly, the difference in growth rate between the two strains decreases with increase of the salt concentration in the culture medium (Hase et al., 2009). At a higher salt concentration, the situation is reversed: In lysogeny broth (LB) medium containing around 1.0 M NaCl, the growth rate of the RsgA-deficient strain exceeds that of the wild-type strain. These observations indicate that removal of RsgA provides cells with resistance to a high degree of salt stress. Increased salt resistance is also provided by a single point mutation that inactivates the ribosome small subunit–dependent GTPase activity of RsgA (Hase et al., 2009). 

When a final concentration of around 1.0 M NaCl is added to the mid–log phase cells so that they are subjected to salt shock, the $\mathrm { O D } _ { 6 0 0 }$ value increases immediately after salt shock, indicating cell shrinkage, and then it turns to mild decrease to a certain value around 0.6 within a few dozens of minutes, indicating recovery from cell shrinkage (Koch, 1984). RsgA-deficient cells resume growth after several hours of suspension, whereas the growth of wild-type cells remains stopped and is resumed much later (Figure 11.4.2a).

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/d070d93a-9a38-4d08-ab41-83365fc0221e/469264ebf9cf4009bd74aa46ee2ef1281b961084123d958a846525a28d9694ae.jpg)



Figure 11.4.2 (a,b) Growth of E. coli cells after salt shock. (a) Wild-type (gray) and RsgA-deletion (black) cells of E. coli strain MG1655 were grown in LB medium at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ with shaking. When $\mathrm { O D } _ { 6 0 0 }$ reached 0.7, a final 0.9 M NaCl was added to the culture medium. The $\mathrm { O D } _ { 6 0 0 }$ value subtracted from that measured immediately after salt shock is plotted against time after salt shock. The growth of cells was carried out in 9 ml of culture medium in a test tube (φ18 mm), and the $\mathrm { O D } _ { 6 0 0 }$ value was automatically monitored at a 10 min interval using a noninvasive turbidity meter. (b) E. coli MG1655 cells were grown in LB medium supplemented with (black) or without (gray) 60 μg/ml kasugamycin. When $\mathrm { O D } _ { 6 0 0 }$ reached 0.7, a final concentration of 0.9 M NaCl was added to the culture medium. The $\mathrm { O D } _ { 6 0 0 }$ value subtracted from that measured immediately after salt shock is plotted against time after salt shock.


## 11.4.3 Possibility of deleterious effect of RsgA for maturation of the 30S subunit after salt shock

Interestingly, salt shock suppresses the defects in subunit assembly of the ribosome and processing of 17S RNA into 16S rRNA in RsgA-deficient cells (Hase et al., 2009). In addition, the intracellular level of RsgA considerably decreases immediately after salt shock due to protein degradation (Hase et al., 2009). These results raise the possibility that RsgA is no longer dispensable or even deleterious for the cells in the early phase of salt adaptation. Salt shock dramatically alters the intracellular condition: It rapidly increases the cellular concentration of potassium ions and decreases that of putrescine (McLaggan et al., 1994; Poolman et al., 2004). It is evident that a large number of monovalent and divalent cations contribute to the conformation of RNA as well as RNA-based macromolecules such as the ribosome (Guttman et al., 1995; Klein et al., 2004; Moore and Steitz, 2003). It also affects the ribosome maturation processes, such as the interaction of the maturation intermediates with the ribosome maturation factors (Guo et al., 2013; Yang et al., 2014). Thus, it is plausible that rapidly increased potassium ions and decreased putrescine in the cell immediately after salt shock modulate the conformation of the 30S subunit, the interaction of RsgA with the 30S subunit, and the GTPase activity of RsgA on the 30S subunit. Indeed, the interaction of RsgA with the 30S subunit of the ribosome is inhibited by a high salt concentration (Daigle and Brown, 2004). 

The decreased cellular level of RsgA after salt shock is gradually restored for several hours (Hase et al., 2009). This suggests that the missing requirement of RsgA is restored in the later phase after salt shock, when potassium ions accumulated in the cell in the early phase are considerably replaced by osmoprotectants. It has been reported that osmoprotectants, potassium ions, and polyamines have significant effects on the conformation of RNA (Lambert and Draper, 2007; Tabor and Tabor, 1976), the conformation of the ribosome (Guttman et al., 1995; Klein et al., 2004), cell-free protein synthesis (Brigotti et al., 2003; Igarashi et al., 1988), and ribosome biosynthesis or an in vitro reconstitution of the ribosomal subunits (Kakegawa et al., 1986; Kashiwagi et al., 1989; Semrad and Green, 2002). 

As discussed here, it seems reasonable to assume that the pathway of ribosome maturation varies depending on the cellular condition, such as that after salt shock, and that some but not all ribosome maturation factors are dispensable or even deleterious after salt shock. The absence of such a deleterious factor would be preferable for ribosome maturation under a condition of potassium ion excess immediately after salt shock. Even if this is true, not all the maturation factor(s) would be deleterious in the cell after salt shock. However, salt resistance induced by depletion of a ribosome-associated factor has become recognized as a more general event than we initially thought, as described further in this chapter. 

## 11.4.4 Salt resistance is also provided by depletion of other ribosome maturation factors or a ribosomal protein

Our recent study has shown that salt resistance is also caused by depletion of other maturation factors for the 30S subunit, RbfA and RimM (Hase et al., 2013). RsgA, RbfA, and RimM bind to different regions of the 30S subunit with different timing: RsgA binds to the A-site and the P-site at nearly the last stage of maturation, RbfA binds in the helix 44 prior to action of RsgA (Datta et al., 2007), and RimM binds to the head of the 30S subunit at an earlier stage (Guo et al., 2013). Thus, salt resistance would not be an RsgA-depletion-specific phenomenon. In addition, deletion of rpsF encoding the ribosomal protein S6 causes salt resistance (Hase et al., 2013). These results suggest that defective maturation of the 30S subunit is involved in salt resistance of cells. Furthermore, salt resistance is also provided by depletion of RrmJ, a maturation factor for the 50S subunit (Hase et al., 2013). Thus, the theme should be more generalized: Defective maturation of the ribosome results in resistance of cells to high salt stress. Alternatively, a defective functioning of the ribosome due to its defective maturation would be a cause of salt resistance. 

Note that a significant level of immature ribosomes containing 17S RNA accumulate in each of RsgA-, RbfA-, RimM-, S6-, and RrmJ-depleted cells (Hase et al., 2013). This raises the possibility that accumulation of immature ribosomes containing 17S RNA is involved in salt tolerance. Consistently, accumulation of 17S RNA is suppressed by depletion of RsgA after salt shock (Hase et al., 2009). 

## 11.4.5 Some kinds of ribosome-targeting antibiotics result in resistance of E. coli cells to salt stress

It has also been found that E. coli cells grown with a sublethal concentration of some kinds of protein synthesis inhibitors, such as kasugamycin and chloramphenicol, provide E. coli cells with resistance to high salt (Figure 11.4.2b) (Hase et al., 2013). Kasugamycin binds within the messenger RNA (mRNA) path of the 30S subunit to inhibit binding of initiator tRNA during the initiation process of translation (Schluenzen et al., 2006), and chloramphenicol binds to the peptidyl transferase center in the 50S subunit to inhibit peptidyl transfer during the elongation process (Hansen et al., 2003). In contrast, neither the DNA synthesis inhibitors nor transcription inhibitors examined are effective (Hase et al., 2013). It should be noted that not all the protein synthesis inhibitors are effective: For example, kanamycin, streptomycin, and fusidic acid have little or no effect (Hase et al., 2013). 

Unlike in the case of depletion of a ribosome maturation factor or a ribosomal protein, kasugamycin does not promote accumulation of 17S RNA in the cell or subunit dissociation into a 70S ribosome (Hase et al., 2013). This indicates that neither accumulation of 17S RNA nor efficient subunit association of the ribosome is necessarily required for salt resistance of the cell. The increased salt tolerance of cells is attributable to either defective ribosome maturation or defective translation. 

## 11.4.6 Activation of sigma E after salt stress

Sigma E is usually sequestered in the cytoplasmic membrane by the antisigma factor RseA, and it is released into the cytoplasm in response to exocytoplasmic stresses, such as overproduction of outer membrane porins, temperature upshift, or salt stress (Mecsas et al., 1993). Activated sigma E regulates the expression of periplasmic and outer membrane proteins to maintain the cell envelope integrity (Dartigalongue et al., 2001; Raivio and Silhavy, 2001) as well as those of other stress-related factors, including sigma E itself (Bianchi and Baneyx, 1999). Thus, the level of activated sigma E can be detected by measuring the level of mRNA of a gene controlled by sigma E. 

Sigma E is transiently activated a few hours after salt shock, and the timing of activation is delayed with an increase in the strength of salt shock (Hase et al., 2013). Intriguingly, activation of sigma E in RsgA-depleted cells occurs a few hours earlier than it does in wild-type cells (Hase et al., 2013). Premature activation of sigma E also occurs in RbfA-, RimM-, S6-, and RrmJdepleted cells and in wild-type cells treated with kasugamycin or chloramphenicol (Hase et al., 2013). Thus, hastened activation of sigma E seems to have a correlation with hastened recovery from interruption of cell growth by salt shock. 

## 11.4.7 Concluding remarks

A novel ribosome-related salt resistance pathway has been revealed, although the underlying mechanism remains largely unknown. Earlier activation of sigma E would make a significant contribution to defending the cell against salt shock, considering that activated sigma E is capable of reinforcing the cell envelope. In fact, inhibition of the sigma E activity results in cell envelope defects (Hayden and Ades, 2008) and decreased salt tolerance of Salmonella Typhimurium (McMeechan et al., 2007). However, we do not know to what degree earlier activation of sigma E contributes to the salt-resistant phenotype. 

How a defect in ribosome maturation or function results in earlier activation of sigma E is another unresolved issue. It is possible that decreased or aberrant periplasmic protein synthesis by defective function of the ribosome affects homeostasis of the cell membrane, leading to earlier activation of sigma E to make cells salt-resistant. For example, SecM de-represses the translation of SecA, which is associated with the Sec translocon involved in protein secretion, through its translational arrest (Muto et al., 2006) and assists formation of the functional conformation of SecA (Nakatogawa et al., 2005). It is also possible that the defective function of the ribosome decreases the translational level of a gene that causes growth arrest in response to osmotic stress as well as regulates the activation of sigma E. 

The connection between the ribosome-related salt resistance pathway and other established stress response pathways has yet to be investigated. For instance, pppGpp (guanosine pentaphosphate) or ppGpp (guanosine tetraphosphate; see Chapter 7.1), of which the cellular level increases upon amino acid starvation or other stresses and during the stationary growth phase (Srivatsan and Wang, 2008), is a likely candidate as the mediator between the ribosome and sigma E, since it is produced from adenosine triphosphate (ATP) and guanosine triphosphate (GTP) or guanosine diphosphate (GDP) by RelA or SpoT associated with the ribosome (Block and Haseltine, 1975; Jiang et al., 2007) and it directly binds to sigma E to modulate the pattern of gene expression (Costanzo et al., 2008). However, our recent study has suggested that the ribosome-related salt resistance pathway does not depend on (p)ppGpp, although it has a capacity to provide cells with salt resistance as well as to hasten the timing of transient activation of sigma E after salt shock (Tarusawa et al., 2016).
