## Desiccation tolerance and drought stress

# Consequences of elevated salt concentrations on expression profiles in the rhizobium S. meliloti 1021 likely involved in heat and desiccation stress

Jan A.C. Vriezen1, Caroline M. Finn1, and Klaus Nusslein ¨ 2 

1Department of Biological Sciences, Smith College, Northampton, Massachusetts, USA 

2Department of Microbiology, University of Massachusetts, Amherst, Massachusetts, USA 

## 12.1.1 Introduction

Microorganisms have developed multiple direct and indirect mechanisms that protect them against the environmental stresses they encounter. One of the most severe and widespread problems facing crop production is the degradation of soil quality due to desiccation and salinity, and almost 40% of the world’s land surface is affected by salinity-related problems (Veron et al., 2006; Zahran, 1999). Water, and its availability, is one of the most vital environmental factors to affect the growth and survival of microorganisms (Potts, 1994). 

Within the soil environment, indirect protection of cellular structures and contents can be provided by cell surface coating with clay minerals or close association with organic substances. Some bacteria (Bacillus spp. and Actinobacteria) form heat-resistant spores to withstand dry conditions and high temperatures, whereas other microorganisms encase individual cells or aggregates of cells in polymeric substances or slime layers to form an extensive exopolymeric matrix or biofilm (see Section 22). These outer structures enable them to adhere to inorganic (e.g., soil pore walls, water conduits, and mineral surfaces) or organic (e.g., root) surfaces to insulate the entire microbial community against the effects of high temperature and the associated lack of available water (Berendsen et al., 2012; Jozefaciuk et al., 2006; Rolli et al., 2015). Despite the potential physiological and lifestyle adaptations to desiccation available to soil microorganisms, many microbes yield to heat stress with damages caused by desiccation. When desiccation advances too rapidly, cellular contents including proteins and nucleic acids are irreversibly damaged, which will inhibit microbial growth (Raivio, 2005). With increasing desiccation, membranes and cell walls are ruptured, effectively killing the cells (Bushby and Marshall, 1977; de Goffau et al., 2011; Salema et al., 1982; Vriezen et al., 2007). 

Microbes respond differently to heat-induced desiccation stress, and the overall microbial diversity in soil changes, leading to an imbalanced system due to loss of the functions provided by stress-sensitive microbes (Fierer et al., 2003; Griffiths et al., 2003). Soil moisture deficit not only affects microbial diversity but also changes the availability of substances for microbial metabolism (Dominguez-Ferreras et al., 2006; Kremer, 2012); similarly, salinity has a direct effect on the composition of microbial communities (Rath and Rousk, 2015). 

Rhizobia are rod-shaped, Gram-negative bacteria with the ability to produce nodules on the roots of leguminous plants (Oldroyd et al., 2011). They belong to the family Rhizobiaceae, which are part of the alphaproteobacteria. Soil environmental conditions are critical factors in the activity, persistence, and survival of rhizobia in the soil. The changes in the rhizospheric environment can affect both growth and saprophytic competence, which will influence competitiveness and persistence (Dowling and Broughton, 1986), and new attempts to remediate this with soil additives are actively pursued (Jiang et al., 2015). 

Desiccation is one of the most common stresses that soil microorganisms have to face, and it produces many stress responses in the microbial cell. The majority of microbes cannot multiply below a water availability of 0.900 (Manzoni et al., 2012; Stevenson et al., 2015), and the responses of bacterial cells to desiccation can include shrinkage of the bacterial cytoplasm and capsular layers, increase in intracellular salt levels, crowding of macromolecules, damage to external layers (pili and membranes), changes in ribosome structure (see Chapter 11.4), and decrease in growth (Vriezen et al., 2007). Reactive oxygen species can also damage proteins and DNA, leading to accumulation of mutations (Potts, 1994; see Section 10). Death of rhizobial cells during desiccation was suggested to be associated with changes in cell walls and membrane integrity (Bushby and Marshall, 1977). It has been hypothesized that during dehydration, the removal of water hydrogen bonds to the phospholipid head groups of the membrane decreases the spacing between adjacent lipids. The membrane is then converted from the liquid crystalline into the gel phase already at room temperature. Subsequent rehydration results in a further phase transition of the membrane back to the liquid crystalline phase. As a consequence, the membrane barrier is disrupted, leading to leakage of membranes (Leslie et al., 1995; Potts, 1994; Welsh, 2000; see Chapter 13.1). 

Desiccation tolerance is the ability of cells to undergo nearly absolute dehydration through air-drying, without being irreversibly damaged or killed. This is the most severe water deficit stress since the removal of the cell-bound water imposes such structural, physiological, and biochemical stresses to which cells must adapt or die (Billi and Potts, 2002; Reina-Bueno et al., 2012). Of particular importance to crop production is the impact of these harsh environmental conditions on the persistence and survival of rhizobia, and thus understanding rhizobial expression responses to common environmental stressors is paramount (Lopez-Leal´ et al., 2014), especially in view of a fast-changing climate with immediate consequences as seen in agriculture, such as the current drought in California. 

Water availability determines both the vitality and functionality of living systems, and desiccation is among the most significant environmental stresses encountered by terrestrial bacteria. Still, the genetic mechanisms underlying sensing and providing resistance to this stress are still poorly understood (Cytryn et al., 2007). The objective of this review is to obtain a comprehensive understanding of the genetic mechanisms involved in desiccation tolerance by members of the Rhizobiaceae, and to get closer to a model that can delineate the consequences of elevated salt concentrations on rhizobial expression profiles. 

## 12.1.2 Responses to NaCl elevating desiccation tolerance

The ability of microorganisms to survive or even proliferate in a stressful, nutrient-poor environment such as the soil remains poorly understood. Most likely, and as we tried to argue in this review, understanding the desiccation responses in conjunction with NaCl and heat stress may be crucial to understanding soil microbial ecology and diversity. In particular, quick changes in water availability may be one of the stresses that microorganisms are exposed to with a large impact on their ability to survive in soil and thus on the microbial community. Although not a panacea for desiccation-related problems, at least some organisms have evolved the ability to respond to osmotic stress induced by the presence of salt or low water activity $( \mathrm { A } _ { \mathrm { w } } )$ in order to survive desiccation (Vriezen et al., 2007). One example is the study by Chen and Alexander (1979) in which acclimation in media with low $\mathrm { A } _ { \mathrm { w } }$ led to a better ability to survive subsequent desiccation. Furthermore, Mary et al. (1986) showed that desiccation survival of Rhizobium strain RCR2011 was increased in the presence of LiCl and NaCl. This increase is strain specific. Vriezen et al. (2006) also showed that the model organism Sinorhizobium meliloti 1021 increases desiccation survival in PMM medium, a defined minimal medium with a phosphatebased buffer, in the presence of 400 mM NaCl. This increase is physiological in origin rather than a protective mechanism of the solutes present. In 2013, Vriezen et al. presented the study of a locus involved in survival after desiccation in the presence of 400 mM NaCl, providing support for NaCl response mechanisms also involved in survival during this stress. This locus (ngg) is also found in Pseudomonads (Sagot et al., 2010) with a conserved function in NaCl tolerance. RpoE2 (smc01506) is a sigma factor with extracytoplasmic function (see Chapter 2.6) involved in the general stress response of S. meliloti. It is also upregulated in the response to NaCl, and an rpoE2::hph mutant reduces the ability to survive desiccation (Sauviac and Bruand, 2014). 

## 12.1.3 The search for loci conserved in the response to NaCl in S. meliloti and Rhizobium etli

Although the heterogeneity of soil and the many different bacterial species present predict an uncountable number of strategies to respond to the stresses imposed, related organisms may have stress responses in common. We hypothesize that the beneficial response to NaCl (i.e., an increase in the ability to survive desiccation) is conserved in a range of soil-borne microorganisms. To find conserved loci in two kinds of rhizobia, two lists with NaCl-inducible loci were accumulated: (i) a list with Sinorhizobium meliloti 1021 (Taxonomy ID 266834) genes upregulated in a response to NaCl from three different studies (Dominguez-Ferreras et al., 2006; Ruberg et al., 2003; Vriezen et al., 2013), and (ii) a list with NaCl-responsive loci from Rhizobium etli (Lopez-Leal et al., 2014). The compilation of the NaCl-inducible list in S. meliloti contains a total of 579 loci. Only one locus (smb20481 or asnO) was identified in all three studies, even though the operon in which asnO resides was identified in all three studies. All in all, the three studies have some loci in common and appear complementary. 

The list with R. etli NaCl-responsive loci was compiled by using the amino acid sequence of every translated open reading frame (ORF) of NaCl-inducible loci within the R. etli genome retrieved from the National Center for Biotechnology Information (NCBI) (R. etli CFN42, Taxonomy ID 347834) as query in the S. meliloti 1021 database search at the INRA in Toulouse, France (Institut National de la Recherche Agronomique). The original list with NaCl-upregulated loci in R. etli contains the 102 NaCl-only inducible loci and 106 (51% of total) NaCl- and heat-inducible loci (Lopez-Leal et al., 2014). Searching for conserved loci in the S. meliloti 1021 genome yielded 76 and 84 (53% out of total of 160 NaCl upregulated loci) sequences with similarity to the R. etli CFN42 query sequence, respectively. 

The list with 519 S. meliloti and 160 R. etli loci were compared, and 38 NaCl-responsive loci in R. etli $( 3 8 / 1 6 0 = 2 3 . 8 \%$ , or 38/5963 = 0.59%; Gonzales et al., 2006) are also NaCl responsive in S. meliloti and represent 35 different loci in this organism (35/519 = 6.7%, or 35/6218 = 0.56%) (Table 12.1.1.) (Barnett et al., 2001; Finan et al., 2001; Capela et al., 2001; Galibert et al., 2001). 

Sequence similarity to Smb21031, Smb20587, and Smc02365 was found twice. Striking is the similarity of the number of selected loci relative to the total number of protein-coding genes

Two additional studies identified loci important for growth at increased NaCl concentrations (Miller-Williams et al., 2006; Wei et al., 2004), but none of these loci were significantly upregulated in response to NaCl stress in genomics studies for S. meliloti and R. etli. 

## 12.1.4 Conserved responses to NaCl in S. meliloti and R. etli

The list of conserved NaCl-responsive loci in S. meliloti and R. etli contains loci that are NaCl inducible and are potentially important for NaCl-mediated desiccation survival in soil. In this list, two loci, sma0934 and smb21445, were found targeting DNA. The first locus is a conjugal transfer protein found on rhizobial plasmids (Stiens et al., 2006). The second locus encodes for a DNA topoisomerase and is found just downstream of glgX2, a locus most likely involved in glycogen debranching. DNA-targeting enzymes are well known for their involvement in desiccation survival (Humann et al., 2009), and finding conserved, NaCl-inducible DNA-targeting proteins is thus not surprising. Locus smb21445 is under control of NtrR, a negative regulator of nodulation and nitrogen fixation genes, and under microoxic conditions (Puskas et al., 2004). A high amount of NaCl reduces the solubility of oxygen, and Vriezen et al. (2013) found several NaCl-responsive loci also responsive to low oxygen. A candidate small RNA (sRNA) coding gene (B42) between Smb21444 and Smb21445 was identified by del Val et al. (2007). Locus Smb21446 (glgX2), involved in glycogen metabolism, may aid in restoring cell volume after osmotic shock in Yersinia pestis as hypothesized by Han et al. (2005). This locus is also upregulated as a response to a shift to acidic pH (Hellweg et al., 2009). Schluter¨ et al. (2013) identified three asRNAs (smb asRNA2938–40) in the smb21446 ORF, indicating expression of this locus is under complex control by sRNAs. In addition, expression of one of these RNAs (smb asRNA2938) may be controlled by RpoD, RpoH1, and RpoH2. Interestingly, Dominguez-Ferreras et al. (2009) proposed to rename Smb20447, a locus just upstream of Smb20446, to TreZ for its involvement in trehalose metabolism. When all five loci, smb21444–smb21448, are drawn out with their respective stress responses, a short regulatory mechanism can be proposed (Figure 12.1.1). 

Increased NaCl concentrations and microoxic and acidic conditions positively regulate transcription from a NtrRregulated promoter, bringing to expression an amylase that may be involved in restoring cell volume and a DNA topoisomerase. Assuming the intragenic region between smb21446 and smb21447 contains a bidirectional promoter under the same control mechanisms (Schluter¨ et al., 2013), TreZ would also be transcribed in cohort with a putative DNA polymerase. The accumulation of trehalose and the simultaneous stimulation of DNA repair or replication functions would be an expected response to NaCl-mediated desiccation. 

It appears that several loci involved in nutrient uptake are upregulated. These loci are smb20227, a nutrient deprivation– induced locus (NdiA1), and smb20574, a glucosyl mutase (TreY) (Flechard et al., 2010). Smb20229 (NdiB; strain C22 in Milcamps et al., 1998) was first found in a search for loci induced by a lack of nutrients. Further characterizations and regulation studies indicate that this gene is carbon- and nitrogen-limitation induced, NaCl induced, with increased expression during the stationary phase, and under TspO control (Davey and de Bruijn, 2000). TspO is upregulated during desiccation in B. japonicum (Cytryn et al., 2007). Smb20227 is an ORF immediately upstream of ndiB, and is most likely regulated in the same way. At least, ndiA1 responds in the same manner to NaCl (Sauviac et al., 2007; Vriezen et al., 2013), carbon, and nitrogen starvation (Djordjevic et al., 2003; Sauviac et al., 2007), and is expressed during the stationary phase (Sauviac et al., 2007). Smb20227 is also responsive to a decrease in pH (Hellweg et al., 2009), is under RelA control (Krol and Becker, 2011), and mutants have an attenuated symbiotic phenotype. RelA mutants are sensitive to desiccation (Humann et al., 2009), and it has been hypothesized that an incomplete stringent response is responsible for the lack of a restart of metabolism, which may lead to the appearance of viable but nonculturable (VBNC) cells after desiccation (Vriezen and de Bruijn, 2015; Vriezen et al., 2012). 

The locus smb20574 (TreY) is involved in the biosynthetic accumulation of the compatible solute trehalose under hyperosmolar conditions. Smb20574 is under RpoE2 control, and stationary growth-phase induction is abolished in the mutants (Flechard et al., 2010). Even though McIntyre et al. (2007) reported very relevant phenotypes for treY mutants in R. leguminosarum bv trifolii, Dominguez-Ferreras et al. (2006) found only very minor phenotypes in S. meliloti 1021. 

The locus smb21587, a putative adenosine triphosphate (ATP)-binding cassette (ABC) transporter for pentoses, is the first gene in an operon for pentose ABC uptake, possibly under control of a GntR-type regulator. Smb21587 is induced by fucose, arabinose, and talose (Mauchline et al., 2006), which is surprising considering that Smb21587 is a putative pentose ABC transporter. Most interestingly, just upstream of smb21587 is the gene for a putative glutathione synthetase (gshB2) located and transcribed in the same direction. Glutathione is involved in the stress response to radical oxygen species and therefore may have a role in survival during desiccation in plants and bacteria (Fredrickson et al., 2008; Pukacka and Ratajczak, 2006). 

Smc01642 encodes for the uptake of proline–betain under low or high osmolarity. Proline, proline–betain, betain, and stachydrine are taken up by osmotically stressed cells (Alloing et al., 2006; Phillips et al., 1998). Yurgel et al. (2013) studied the N-response of S. meliloti. Smc01642 and hisX (smc00672) are expressed when NtrC is phosphorylated when nitrogen is limited. Expression of these loci is also upregulated as a consequence of the general stress response. Vriezen and de Bruijn (2015) hypothesized that HisX may have a function in the formation of VBNC cells upon desiccation. Expression of Smc01642 is also dependent on AbcR1 and AbcR2, sRNAs that directly target ribosome-binding sites (RBS) with anti-Shine–Dalgarno motifs on the RNAs (Torres-Quesada et al., 2010). A change in temperature has an instant effect on translation, and regulatory RNAs may function as thermometers (Shapiro and Cowen, 2012). However, this locus has not been found in attempts to find heat-upregulated loci in either R. etli or S. meliloti. Therefore, if these loci are involved in the heat response, regulation may occur on the translational rather than transcriptional level. 

Smc01827 is a putative uracil or uridine ABC transporter. Uridine is important in transcription, which may have a role in the startup of metabolism after cells have adapted to the new condition. Uracil and uridine induce this locus (Mauchline et al., 2006), and Smc01827 transcription is under strong negative regulation by Hfq (Sobrero et al., 2012; Torres-Quesada et al., 2010). 

Other ABC transporters for sugars are smc02774 and smc02471. Smc02471 encodes for a putative periplasmic ABCtype transporter of sugars. It has an upstream ROK-type negative regulator, and a gene encoding for a structural lipoprotein downstream. Smc01774 is in an operon for the uptake of fucose and pyruvate, and is located between the two transposable elements Trm19 (smc02779) and Trm20 (smc02762). All genes in this stretch of the S. meliloti genome are in the same orientation with the exception of smc02777, a HTH-type transcriptional regulator targeting DNA. Smc02774 is induced by fucose and pyruvate (Mauchline, 2006) and is used as a biosensor for these compounds (Bourdes` et al., 2012). Smc02774 is ∼fourfold upregulated by exogeneous cyclic adenosine monophosphate (cAMP) (Tian et al., 2006), leading to the proposal that expression of smc02774 is $\sigma ^ { 5 4 }$ (NtrA) dependent as well as dependent on the transcriptional modulator NtrR under microoxic conditions (Puskas et al., 2004). The sigma factor $\sigma ^ { 5 4 }$ is higher expressed in desiccated Bradyrhizobium japonicum strains (Cytryn et al., 2007). 

In the paragraphs above, regulatory mechanisms for expression of these genes are briefly mentioned. The Ntr, Hfq, RelA, and TspO-mediated NaCl responses conserved between S. meliloti and R. etli underlie expression of these loci. Humann et al. (2009) identified more regulators involved in survival during desiccation. The regulators found are RelA (Smb02659), RpoE2 (Smc01506), and Hrp (Smc02754). Smc01506 controls expression of smc01505 (RsiA1), an antisigma factor for $\mathfrak { O } ^ { 2 4 } ;$ , a response regulator of the response to increased extracellular stress such as NaCl (Sauviac et al., 2007). This locus is also found conserved between the S. meliloti and R. etli genome responsive to NaCl and leads to a desiccation-sensitive phenotype in a smc01506 mutant (Sauviac and Bruand, 2014). In addition, smc01504 (rsiB1) is upregulated 1.6–7-fold over 72 h of desiccation in Bradyrhizobium japonicum (Cytryn et al., 2007). Clearly, this RpoE2-regulated pathway in the response to NaClmediated desiccation resistance is conserved in several rhizobia. Expression of smc01505, however, is independent of RelA (Krol and Becker, 2011), and expression of smc01504 is decreased in a TolC mutant (Santos et al., 2010). 

Other conserved NaCl-induced regulators are Sma1493, a LysR-type DNA targeting regulator located just upstream of uncharacterized oxidoreductases, and Smc02844, an uncharacterized TetR-type regulator. None of these loci have been studied in detail; however, Sma1493 mutants have a slight reduction in the number of pink nodules and thus have a symbiotic phenotype (Luo et al., 2005). Yet another NaCl-responsive regulator is Smc02366 (RagA), with a putative histidine kinase sensor immediately downstream. This regulatory system may be involved in the response to heavy metals, but it was not found in a screen for heavy metal–responsive loci by Rossbach et al. (2008). VBNC cells (see Section 23) are a product of desiccation, as shown by Vriezen et al. (2012), and copper is a metal known to be toxic and to induce a VBNC state in Rhizobium (Alexander et al., 1999; Manahan and Steck, 1997). Smc02366 is also upregulated upon an acidic shift (Hellweg et al., 2009). Therefore, this locus may have a function in acidic environments rich in heavy metals and may be useful for remediation of acidic, metal-contaminated sites such as acid mine drainage (Becerra et al., 2009). 

Upstream of ragA, DegP1 (Smc02365), a periplasmic serine protease involved in oxygen, heat, and envelope stress (Raivio, 2005), was also found upregulated in both organisms during NaCl stress. Due to these features, Vriezen et al. (2007) hypothesized the involvement of DegP1 in survival during desiccation in these organisms. Later, DegP1 was found to be significantly downregulated in a hfq mutant (Torres-Quesada et al., 2010), and upregulated in bacA and tolC mutants (Karunakaran et al., 2010; Santos et al., 2010). 

Oxygen stresses caused by the accumulation of radical oxygen species during desiccation are a substantial cause of damage and a reduction of viability (see Section 10). Expression of degP1 and also sodC (smc02597) seem appropriate responses when cells are being desiccated. Other superoxide dismutases have been found upregulated during drying (e.g., SodF in desiccated B. japonicum) (Cytryn et al., 2007) cells as well as long-term desiccated Nostoc commune (Shirkey et al., 2000). Valverde et al. (2008) predicted that expression of smc02597 is sRNA mediated but is induced during infection (Ampe et al., 2003). 

Finally, finding a cold shock protein (Smc04319) (O’Connell and Thomashow, 2000) indicates that the extraction of water by hyperosmotic stress has a similar effect as the immobilization of water when cooling. The extraction of free water due to drying, increasing osmotic stress, or cooling may be the common signal and mechanism in the response to these stresses and explains the finding of an NaCl-upregulated locus involved in the cold shock response. The expression of Smc03419 is reduced under microoxic conditions in an ntrR mutant (Puskas et al., 2004). 

## 12.1.5 NaCl-responsive hypothetical proteins

Eye-catching is the large amount of (conserved) hypothetical proteins this list represents (15/35 = 43%), indicating many possible loci yet to be studied for their involvement in these stress responses. Three of these loci are reviewed here. Under carbon starvation, smc00800 and smc00885 are RelA-independent targets of RpoE2. Under nitrogen starvation, however, expression of these loci is RelA dependent (Krol and Becker, 2011). Both proteins exhibit increased expression levels during the stationary phase and at $4 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Sauviac et al., 2007). The Smc00885 contains a PRC-H barrel domain, as described by Anantharaman and Aravind (2002). These domains are found in the photosynthetic complex in purple bacteria, and have been hypothesized to be involved in non-photosynthetic electron transfer reactions in rhizobia (Anantharaman and Aravind, 2002). Expression of smc00885 is increased in Medicago sativa nodules, in the reference strain as well in a bacA mutant (Capela et al., 2006); is under RpoE2 control (Bastiat et al., 2010); and is upregulated in the presence of nitric oxide (NO), a former reactive oxygen species (de Bruijn et al., 2006; Lindermayr and Durner, 2015). 

The third locus in this group is smc01590. Vriezen et al. (2013) first reported that exposure to NaCl upregulates this locus in S. meliloti and R. etli, but no phenotype on NaCl was found. Computational approaches to find promoter sequences identified an upstream promoter for RpoE2 (Schluter ¨ et al., 2013). This is in conflict with what Flechard et al. (2010) reported. This group did not find expression of smc01590 to be RpoE2 dependent. However, Vriezen and de Bruijn (2015) reported that this locus is involved in survival during desiccation. This raises the following question: Is RpoE2-mediated desiccation resistance directly due to transcriptional activation of smc01590, or is there another target for RpoE2 involved in the response to desiccation? Most interestingly, the ability of the mutant strain containing the transcriptional fusion smb01590::tn5luxAB (Sce1) is reduced with increasing temperature, while survival of the reference strain is increased with increasing temperature. It appears that the mutant is not able to correctly adjust membrane stability, resulting in a decrease in survival at higher temperatures compared to the reference strain (Vriezen and de Bruijn, 2015). 

## 12.1.6 The reduced ability of strain Sce1 to survive desiccation at 37 ◦C is inversely related to growth at this temperature

The ability to live or even proliferate in soil is an art in survival. In a recent publication, the authors (Vriezen et al., 2013) describe the effect of a Tn5luxAB insertion in locus smb20482 in S. meliloti 1021 strain Sce11. This mutant strain is reduced in its ability to grow at elevated NaCl concentrations and survive desiccation. It appears that Sce11 is not able to produce the powerful osmoprotectant NAGGN; however, the reduced ability to survive desiccation is not related to the accumulation of osmoprotectants but rather to a secondary function such as cell wall synthesis. 

In the same publication, the authors describe the effect of a Tn5luxAB insertion in locus smc01590 in S. meliloti 1021 strain Sce1. In contrast to the effect of the Tn5luxAB insertion in strain Sce11, this mutant strain has no reduced ability to grow at 400 mM NaCl; however, this mutant is sensitive to desiccation relative to the reference strain at increased temperature $( 3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } )$ , but not when dried at $\downarrow ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Vriezen and de Bruijn, 2015). Therefore, we hypothesized that smc01590::Tn5luxAB, although responsive to a NaCl increase, causes a phenotype when growing at elevated temperature rather than through the presence of NaCl. To test this, mean generation times were estimated when growing in PMM with and without NaCl and at 28 and $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Table 12.1.2). 

Not surprisingly, and similar to the reported observation by Vriezen et al. (2013), S. meliloti 1021 and Sce1 have similar mean generation times when growing at $2 8 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ in the presence and in the absence of NaCl, albeit Sce1 grows marginally slower. In contrast, at ${ 3 7 } ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ Sce1 grows significantly faster $( p = 0 . 0 4 )$ than the reference strain. Therefore, rather than being reduced in the ability to grow at elevated temperature, an increase in growth was observed, indicating a conflict between the ability to survive desiccation and the ability to grow at elevated temperature.

## 12.1.7 The inability of strain Sce1 to survive desiccation is related to stationary phase expression

To test if smc01590::Tn5luxAB is also expressed during the stationary phase, luciferase activity was measured during growth in TY medium (Figure 12.1.2). 

A decrease in luciferase activity was measured during exponential growth, followed by an increase in luciferase activity during the stationary phase. Thus, the ability of strain Sce1 to survive desiccation may be related to slow growth and stress resistance during this phase. To put this hypothesis to the test, the ability of strain Sce1 to survive desiccation was determined in relation to time spent in the stationary phase. Both the reference strain and strain Sce1 were grown on TY plates for 1 to 7 days prior to initiating the desiccation experiment. At days 1, 3, and 7, culture tubes containing 5 ml of liquid TY medium were inoculated with single colonies from TY plates, and incubated for 3 days at $2 8 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ with agitation at 220 rpm. On the third day, culture tubes containing 5 ml of YMB medium were inoculated with 50 μl from the TY culture tubes, and incubated at $2 8 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ with agitation at 220 rpm. At an appropriate OD of 0.1–0.2, samples were washed in fresh YMB medium and dried at 22% relative humidity at $2 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 7 days. In addition, survival of colonies from $3 { \mathrm { - d a y - o l d } }$ TY plates that were incubated for 1, 3, and 7 days in liquid TY medium prior to the desiccation experiment was also determined. Upon rehydrating, the resulting colonyforming units (CFUs) were determined and expressed as percentage survival (Figure 12.1.3). 

The data presented in Figure 12.1.3a and 12.1.3b show that Sce1 inoculum taken from the aging liquid TY cultures leads to YMB cultures increasingly less able to survive desiccation relative to the reference strain (Figure 12.1.3a). Obviously, residing longer in the stationary phase in TY liquid decreases survival of strain Sce1, whereas the reference strain does not show such a phenotype. The variation in survival seen in the reference strain

In contrast, aging in a structured environment such as the agar plate results in no correlation of survival with culture age (Figure 12.1.3b). Therefore, we conclude that the desiccation phenotype of strain Sce1 is related to the time this strain resided in the stationary phase in liquid TY medium. 

## 12.1.8 Heat and desiccation stress and stationary phase expression

To further explore the possibility of a conserved response to increased NaCl, increased temperature, desiccation, and the stationary phase, we evaluated several genomic studies utilizing rhizobia addressing the identification of loci whose expression is affected during the stationary phase (Chen et al. (2003) and Sauviac et al. (2007), using S. meliloti 1021), increased temperature (Lopez-Leal et al., 2014; Sauviac et al., 2007), and desiccation (Cytryn et al. (2007) using B. japonicum USDA110). 

In order to make a direct comparison, all loci upregulated during desiccation in B. japonicum were first retrieved from NCBI and compared to the S. meliloti genome database at INRA using tblastnt. This comparison resulted in only two loci that are upregulated in response to NaCl in both organisms. These loci are the putative DNA topoisomerase (Smb21445, see Figure 12.1.1) and the putative pentose monosaccharide ABC transporter (Smb21587). 

When this list of loci was compared with expression data in response to increased temperature, 11 of the 38 loci (29%) in R. etli responded with increased expression to elevated temperature. This is substantially less than the 51% of total NaClresponsive loci upregulated by heat stress. Similarly, eight of 35 loci (23%) in S. meliloti were increased by temperature stress. Based on random chance, one would expect an overlap of two to three loci between R. etli and S. meliloti, respectively; however, only one locus (smb20227, ndiA) was found. In support of the fact that smb20227 is expressed during heat and saline stress, Schluter ¨ et al. (2013) found in a bioinformatics analysis the promoter sequences for RpoH and RpoE2 upstream of this locus. Therefore, it appears that, except for ndiA, the combined response to NaCl and heat stress is different in both organisms. This supports the observations by Trotman and Weaver (1995) that isolated rhizobia show large differences in their response to desiccation, which is not related to the ability to respond to increased temperature. 

When stationary phase expression is added to the list (S. meliloti only), 12 loci (32%) of the NaCl-responsive loci were upregulated in the stationary phase. Of these, eight were also upregulated in response to heat. These eight loci are mainly those loci encoding for six hypothetical proteins, Smc01505 and NdiA1. The four loci expressed during the stationary phase that are not responsive to temperature are the locus presented in this chapter (smc01590), and the loci involved in uracil, fucose, and proline–betain uptake. Although not conclusive, it appears that genes upregulated during the stationary phase fall into two groups: those that are heat independent and involved in smallmolecule uptake, and those that are heat dependent (mainly hypothetical proteins). 

## 12.1.9 Smc01590 is conserved among a wide range of alphaproteobacteria associated with soil and high-salinity environments

To determine if the Smc01590-mediated stress response to desiccation is conserved, the amino acid sequence of Smc01590 was used as a query against the nonredundant bacterial genomes database of representative genomes using the function tblastn at NCBI. Besides S. meliloti 1021, 16 additional organisms were found with more than 80% overlapping sequence. In addition to many Rhizobium-related microorganisms, this gene was also present in Chelativorans BNC1 (formerly 

Mesorhizobium BNC1, an organism isolated from EDTAenriched soil) (Bohuslavek et al., 2001; Nortemann, 1992, 1999); ¨ Pelagibacterium halotolerance B2, an alphaproteobacterium isolated from the South China Sea (Huo et al., 2012; Xu et al., 2011); Leisingera methylohalidivorans, an alphaproteobacterium from a tidal pool in California, which grows on methyl bromide (Schaefer et al., 2002); and Rhodobacter sphaeroides, an alphaproteobacterium usually found in soil and fresh water (Choudhary et al., 2007; Porter et al., 2011). It appears that only Sinorhizobium meliloti and S. medicae have a full-length copy of this gene. All organisms are associated with soil environments or saline waters. 

To determine if topologies made with the amino acid sequence of this gene are congruent with topologies made with 16S ribosomal RNA (rRNA) sequences, all 17 16S rRNA sequences from these organisms were obtained from NCBI, and aligned using Clustal Omega at the EMBL website using default settings (http://www.ebi.ac.uk/Tools/msa/clustalo/). A gammaproteobacterium, E. coli K-12, functioned as an outgroup for this phylogeny of selected alphaproteobacteria. To create trees from the Smc01590 homologs, the same alignment procedure was followed with the exception to use protein sequences. The Smc01590 sequence aligned well at the C-terminal end of the protein, but was less conserved in the center as well as at the N-terminus. In particular, the sequences of the four organisms described here aligned poorly in the center of the protein. These alignments, in conjunction with a search for conserved domains using Smc01590, indicate that the SH3 domain (AA 41–93) (Vriezen and de Bruijn, 2015) and the DUF1236 domain (AA 151–210) are conserved in all sequences used. Even though the alignments are highly variable at the N-terminus and in the center of the alignment, it appears that Smc01590 function is conserved among the known strains. 

Neighbor-joining (NJ) trees were generated using the software Seaview version 4 (Gouy et al., 2010). 

When comparing the resulting phylogenetic trees (Figure 12.1.4), their overall topology is largely similar, an indication that Smc01590 may be part of the core genome in these organisms. Branch length is longer in the Smc01590 tree, which means that evolutionary rates are higher than in the 16S rRNA tree. The more distantly related alphaproteobacteria are also deep branching in the Smc01590 tree. In the Rhizobium– Agrobacterium clade, topology is the same between the 16S rRNA and Smb01590 topologies; however, bootstrap values are low for the deeper branching nodes. Similarly, even though the topology of the Ensifer-group is the same in both trees, the Smc01590 homolog in S. fredii evolves faster than the 16S rRNA, resulting in a weakly supported node with a bootstrap value of only 63%. This is even more obvious in the Mesorhizobium–Chelativorans group. The nodes in this clade are well supported in the 16S rRNA topology. In contrast, in the Smc01590 topology, the Chelativorans is deep branching whereas the Mesorhizobia are closely related, resulting in lesser supported nodes. Interestingly, the four deep-branching organisms, Rhodobacter sphaeroides, Leisingera methylohalidivorans, Pelagibacterium halotolerance, and Chelativorans sp., all aligned poorly and have a ∼40 amino acid insertion between positions 122 and 169 in the alignment. It appears that the fast evolution of Smc01590 homologs is mainly due to the Nterminus and an interdomain sequence in the center. 

Most interestingly, the insertion in the interdomain sequence in some organisms correlates with the deep-branching organisms, while the variation at the N-terminus separates S. meliloti and S. medicae from the rest of the rhizobia. The fact that Smc01590 is only found in the alphaproteobacteria associated to soil or high salinity may suggest that Smc01590 homologs have a conserved function in these environments and are likely important for survival in these environments. However, this also raises the question of why certain Smc01590 homologs evolve faster than others. For example, Smc01590 in Chelativorans evolves substantially faster than any of the Mesorhizobium alleles. The reason is unlikely the ability to induce the formation of root nodules since that is one of the characteristics that separates the Chelativorans from the Mesorhizobia (Doronina et al., 2010). It is more likely that the occurrence of the previously mentioned insertion is related to the absence of the nodulation ability of the deep-branching organisms studied. A second example is Sinorhizobium fredii, which induces nodule formation on Glycine max, whereas the other Sinorhizobia induce nodules on a wider range of legumes, including Medicago species, but not on Glycine max. It appears that the differences in evolutionary rates are related to the host plants and absence of nodulation abilities; however, it may relate to secondary factors such as geographical distributions of the host plant and local environment. 

## 12.1.10 Summarizing statements and future directions

By identifying conserved mechanisms to NaCl and desiccation stress, we made an attempt to understand molecular and cellular mechanisms that are important for survival during desiccation and changing environmental conditions. To this end, we compared two different lists with loci upregulated during NaCl stress in S. meliloti 1021 and R. etli. We identified 35 loci upregulated in both organisms. Reviewing those loci, potential regulatory mechanisms were identified that are important in the NaCl-mediated response to desiccation. At least two loci (Smc01506 (RpoE2) and Smc01590) are involved in survival during this stress. Interestingly, expression of smc01590 may be RpoE2 mediated despite conflicting reports on this possibility. 

All data considered, it appears the genetic locus smc01590 is conserved in a wide range of α-proteobacteria, and is expressed during exposure to NaCl and during the stationary phase in 


(a)


![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/d070d93a-9a38-4d08-ab41-83365fc0221e/aab0a24bf94069c1df189ed2bb7112d8bf127e490ff28ddabc391e534ea4bd12.jpg)



Figure 12.1.4 (a) Phylogenetic tree based on 16S rRNA with E. coli K12 as an outgroup; (b) tree topology based on Smc01590. Phylogenetic trees were constructed based on neighbor-joining algorithms using observed distances while ignoring gaps. Bootstrap values (%) are from 100 replicates.


S. meliloti 1021. Exposure to the stationary phase dramatically affects the ability to survive desiccation at temperatures higher than $2 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ Growth phenotypes at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ indicate that the presence of low concentrations of NaCl is not detrimental to growth, but rather an increase in temperature allows strain Sce1 to grow faster and suggests that smc01590 encodes for a protein that is involved in the negative regulation of growth and is important in stationary phase survival. This leads to a conflict: An increase in growth rate due to increased temperature leads to a reduced ability to survive desiccation in S. meliloti. Current global changes in weather patterns have led to an increase in short and intense precipitation events (Dore, 2005) and subsequently the re-drying of soil in intermittent periods. An inability to respond to this change will be detrimental to the survival of organisms lacking a functional Smc01590 homolog, which is countered by the ability to grow quicker at increased temperature. The fact that smc01590 is well integrated into the genome of S. meliloti and R. etli is an indication that the ability to grow at increased temperature is offset by the ability to withstand periods of drought or other stresses in soil. It can be expected that with current changes in weather patterns, bacteria containing a functional Smc01590 have a competitive advantage and will increase in abundance in soil, independent of temperature. Determining the shift in bacterial communities under NaCl and heat stress can test this hypothesis. To further elucidate the underlying mechanisms leading to a desiccationsensitive mutant, transcriptional profiling of the reference strain as well as mutant Sce1 will reveal the physiological differences between these strains, giving insight into the cellular structures affected.

# Genes involved in the formation of desiccationresistant cysts in Azotobacter vinelandii

## Guadalupe Esp´ın

Department of Molecular Microbiology, Instituto de Biotecnolog´ıa, Universidad Nacional Autonoma de M ´ exico, Cuernavaca Morelos, ´ Mexico ´ 

## 12.2.1 Introduction

Azotobacter vinelandii is a soil bacterium that undergoes differentiation to form cysts that are resistant to desiccation. A mature cyst consists of a contracted cell known as the central body containing numerous granules of the reserve polymer polyhydroxybutyrate (PHB). The central body is covered by a capsule made up of an inner layer called intine, and a laminated outer layer called exine (Figure 12.2.1). The exopolysaccharide alginate is a major component of the intine and exine layers. In the cysts, the membrane phospholipids are replaced by the phenolic lipids alkylresorcinols (ARs) and alkylpyrones (APs) (Reusch and Sadoff, 1983). These phenolic lipids are also components of the exine layer. 

Encystment in A. vinelandii is induced by transferring vegetative cells grown on Burks sucrose or glucose medium to a Burks medium with either n-butanol or β-hydroxybutyrate (BHB) as the sole carbon source (Lin and Sadoff, 1968; Sadoff, 1975). Upon encystment induction, motility and the nitrogen fixation and respiration activities are lost (Sadoff, 1975). Additionally, the synthesis of cyst components alginate, PHB, and ARs and APs is induced. 

In this chapter, we review how mutations in genes involved in the synthesis of cyst components or in genes encoding global regulators affect the development, morphology, and/or resistance to desiccation of A. vinelandii cysts. 

A list of genes whose inactivation affects the synthesis of cyst components, cyst morphology, or cyst resistance to desiccation is given in Table 12.2.1. 

## 12.2.2 Mutations that impair biosynthesis or composition of alginate impair the formation of cysts

Alginate is a linear polymer composed of β-D-mannuronic acid (M) and its C-5-epimer α-1-guluronic acid (G). The genes encoding the enzymes of the synthesis of this exopolysaccharide have been identified and characterized (see, for reviews, Galindo et al., 2007; Hay et al., 2014). All genes but algC are organized in the algD-8-44-K-J-G-X-L-I-V-F-A gene cluster. Strains carrying mutations in the alginate biosynthetic genes algD, or algK (Table 12.2.1) are reported to be unable to form cysts; cells of these mutants induced for encystment lack the intine and exine layers and are unable to resist desiccation (Campos et al., 1996; Mej´ıa-Ru´ız et al., 1997). Thus, alginate is essential for the formation of the cyst capsule and for desiccation resistance. 

Alginate is first synthesized as polymannuronate; the G residues in the alginates are the result of the activities of the periplasmic AlgG epimerase and seven extracellular epimerases AlgE1–7 (Ertesvag et al., 1994, 1995; Ertesvag and Skjak-Bræk,˚ 1999; Svanem et al., 1999). The extracellular epimerases are exported by a type I transporter system encoded by the eexDEF operon (Gimmestad et al., 2006). A strain (MS163171) in which all algE1–7 genes encoding the seven AlgE1–7 epimerases are inactivated produces alginates with a very low G content as compared with the wild type; this mutant is unable to form cysts that are resistant to desiccation (Steigedal et al., 2008). The cysts formed by these mutants lack the intine and exine layers. Similarly, inactivation of the eexDEF genes resulted in strains that produced alginate with less than 2% guluronic acids, whereas the parental wild type produces alginate with 20% content of G residues. When induced for encystment, the eexDEF mutants are unable to survive desiccation (Table 12.2.1) (Gimmestad et al., 2006). Thus, alginates with different G compositions are probably forming the intine and exine layers of the gel coat surrounding the cell. 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/d070d93a-9a38-4d08-ab41-83365fc0221e/27a8b76eea16342231a3d8c9037d815cd96f746004ca1a84705039e33ab9d9b0.jpg)



Figure 12.2.1 Electron micrograph of an Azotobacter vinelandii desiccation-resistant mature cyst.


## 12.2.3 Mutations in genes involved in alkylresorcinols biosynthesis

ARs and APs are components of the cyst membranes and capsule. These phenolic lipids are common to plants but rare in bacteria. 5-n-Heneicosylresorsinol and 5-n-tricosylresorsinol, known as AR1, and their galactoside derivatives, known as AR2, are the main ARs synthesized by cysts (Reusch and Sadoff, 1979). The enzymes for ARs and APs synthesis are coded in the arsABCD operon (Funa et al., 2006). ArsA and ArsC constitute a fatty acid synthase responsible for the synthesis of C22 to C26 fatty acids that serve as substrates for type II polyketide synthases ArsB and ArsC, which synthesize ARs and APs, respectively (Miyanaga et al., 2008). The arsABCD operon is specifically expressed in cells undergoing encystment (Segura et al., 2009). The presence of ARs and APs in cyst membranes has been proposed to contribute to desiccation resistance (Funa et al., 2006; Reusch and Sadoff, 1983). However, strains with mutations in the arsA gene are unable to produce ARs and develop cysts that survive desiccation (Table 12.2.1). Electron microscopy of an arsA mutant induced for encystment revealed cysts with a disorganized exine, in agreement with early reports showing ARs as exine components (Reusch and Sadoff, 1981). Therefore, it has been concluded that ARs play a structural role in cysts but are not essential for their desiccation resistance (Segura et al., 2009).

Transcription of the asrABCD operon is dependent on ArpR, a protein of the LysR family expressed only under encystment induction conditions (Romero et al., 2013). Transcription of arpR (from a constitutive promoter) during vegetative growth does not induce synthesis of alkylresorcinols, suggesting that a co-inducer is required for activation of arsA transcription by ArpR. Indeed, acetoacetyl–coenzyme A (acetoacetyl–CoA) increases the affinity of ArpR for the promoter region of arsA in vitro; furthermore, in vivo addition of acetoacetyl–CoA on Burks sucrose non-encysting medium resulted in the ArpRdependent production of ARs (Table 12.2.1) (Romero et al., 2013). The cyst morphology of an arpR mutant, observed by electron microscopy, and its desiccation resistance are very similar to those of the arsA mutant (Romero et al., 2013). These data further support the conclusion that ARs play a structural role in cysts but do not contribute to desiccation resistance. 

## 12.2.4 Mutations impairing PHB synthesis

PHB together with ARs are the major metabolic products of A. vinelandii cysts; after induction of encystment, PHB rapidly accumulates, and large granules of this polymer are observed in mature cysts (Figure 12.2.1) (Stevenson and Socolofsky, 1966). The phbBAC operon encodes the enzymes of the PHB synthesis and is transcribed from two overlapping promoters: One $\mathrm { P _ { B } } 2$ is activated by PhbR, a transcriptional activator of the AraC family, and another $\mathrm { P _ { B } } 2$ is an RpoS-dependent promoter (Hernandez-´ Eligio et al., 2011; Peralta-G´ıl et al., 2002). Mutations in the phb-BAC genes do not impair the capacity to form cysts resistant to desiccation (Segura et al., 2003). However, no PHB granules are observed in the cysts formed by these mutants, and they possess a thicker exine layer and a higher content of ARs as compared to cysts formed by the wild type (Segura et al., 2003). Thus, PHB is not essential for the formation of cysts resistant to desiccation. However, as encystment under laboratory conditions is induced by BHB, a product of PHB catabolism, in natural soil conditions BHB could be provided by a massive mobilization of PHB (Sadoff, 1975); therefore, the possibility that under natural conditions PHB metabolism has an important role in cyst formation cannot be ruled out. 

A more detailed description of the genes participating in the synthesis of alginate, PHB, and ARs has been reviewed by Galindo et al. (2007) and Segura et al. (2014). 

## 12.2.5 Regulation of encystment by the sigma factor AlgU

The alternative sigma factor AlgU is similar to the Escherichia coli sigma E protein, which is involved in the expression of genes determining resistance to heat (see Section 13) and reactive oxygen species (see Section 10) (Erickson and Gross, 1989; Hershberger et al., 1995; Yu et al., 1995). AlgU and its antisigma protein MucA are involved in the control of the alginate synthesis in A. vinelandii and in Pseudomonas species (for a recent review, see Hay et al., 2014). In Pseudomonas aeruginosa, AlgU activation has been found to take place by a proteolytic cascade that eventually degrades MucA and releases AlgU (Damron and Goldberg, 2012). In A. vinelandii, AlgU is required for transcription of the algD gene from one of its three promoters (Nu´nez ˜ et al., 2000). The algC gene, which is involved in alginate and lipopolysaccharide production, is transcribed from two promoters, one of which, P1, is dependent on AlgU. A mutation in algU reduces 100-fold the transcription of algC (Gaona et al., 2004). Therefore, the lack of alginate production in algU mutants is caused by the AlgU-dependent transcription of algC. Similar to the strains carrying mutations in alginate biosynthetic genes, when induced for encystment, algU mutants are unable to produce alginate and to form cysts resistant to desiccation (Table 12.2.1) (Moreno et al., 1998). Electron microscopy examination of algU mutants induced for encystment showed that, like the mutants in alginate biosynthetic genes, they lack the intine and exine capsule layers (Moreno et al., 1998). As expected, mutations in mucA result in a high AlgU activity, and overproduction of alginate, but do not affect the formation of desiccationresistant cysts (Table 12.2.1), (Nu´nez ˜ et al., 2000). 

In P. aeruginosa, the protein AlgR activates transcription of AlgU-dependent promoters, including the algD promoter, and inactivation of algR abrogates alginate synthesis in this bacterium (Deretic et al., 1989). In A. vinelandii, an algR mutation does not affect alginate synthesis but impairs encystment (Table 12.2.1) (Nu´nez ˜ et al., 1999), suggesting that AlgR activates genes involved in encystment with promoters recognized by AlgU. 

AlgU is also related to the inhibition of flagellar synthesis, and the loss of respiration and nitrogen fixation that occurs upon encystment induction. In A. vinelandii, AlgU has been shown to activate expression of CydR, a homolog of Fnr that represses expression of FlhDC, the master regulator of flagellar biogenesis (see Chapters 22.1 and 22.2) (Leon and Esp ´ ´ın, 2008). CydR is also a repressor of the cydAB genes encoding cytochrome bd, required for aerotolerant nitrogen fixation (Wu et al., 2000). Thus, AlgU activates an AlgU–CydR regulatory cascade where CydR represses motility and respiration. A model for the control of alginate, motility, respiration, and nitrogen fixation upon encystment induction by AlgU is presented in Leon and Esp ´ ´ın (2008). 

## 12.2.6 Role of the Gac–Rsm system in encystment

The two-component GacS–GacA system is conserved in a variety of Gram-negative bacteria and controls the expression of genes mainly involved in secondary metabolism (Heeb et al., 2001; Lapouge et al., 2008). A protein named CsrA in 

E. coli (or RsmA in Pseudomonas species) binds to their target mRNAs, acting as a repressor of translation (Lapouge et al., 2008; Romeo, 1998). GacA homologs activate the expression of regulatory small RNAs that interact with the CsrA–RsmA protein to counteract its repressor activity (Lapouge et al., 2008). In A. vinelandii, the GacS–GacA system is required for cyst formation and for alginate and PHB synthesis. Mutations in gacA or gacS genes abrogated the synthesis of these two polymers and the formation of cysts (Castaneda˜ et al., 2000, 2001). In A. vinelandii, inactivation of gacA also results in a reduction in vegetative cells of the rpoS mRNA, as determined by Northern blot analysis (Figure 12.2.2) (Castaneda ˜ et al., 2001). 

GacA activates transcription of eight RNAs named RsmZ1–7 and RsmY (Hernandez-Eligio´ et al., 2012; Manzo et al., 2012). The algD mRNA and two of these RNAs, RsmZ1 and RsmZ2, have been shown to interact in vitro with the RsmA protein (Figure 12.2.2). Additionally, either inactivation of rsmZ1 or rsmZ2 or overexpression of RsmA diminished alginate synthesis (Manzo et al., 2012). The phbR mRNA has also been shown to interact in vitro with the RsmA protein (Hernandez-Eligio´ et al., 2012). These data indicate that the regulation of alginate and PHB synthesis by the Gac–Rsm system is exerted at the algD and phbR posttranscriptional level (Figure 12.2.2). Together, these data suggest that, upon encystment induction, the Gac–Rsm system must be activated in order to synthesize the cyst components alginate and PHB. As a global regulatory system, it is likely that in addition to the alginate synthesis, the global Gac– Rsm system controls the expression of other proteins involved in the differentiation process to form cysts resistant to desiccation. In fact, a mutation in gacA abrogates the synthesis of alkylresorcinols (unpublished data). 

## 12.2.7 RpoS as a main regulator of cyst formation

The sigma factor RpoS is a central regulator during the stationary phase in bacteria (Navarro LLorens et al., 2010). In A. vinelandii, RpoS participates in the activation of several genes involved in the synthesis of the cyst components alginate, PHB, and ARs. RpoS activates one of the three promoters driving transcription of the alginate biosynthetic gene algD (Castaneda ˜ et al., 2001), one of the two promoters driving transcription of phbB, and transcription of the phbR gene (Hernandez–Eligio ´ et al., 2011; Peralta-G´ıl et al., 2002). RpoS is also required for activation of the arpR gene that codes for the transcriptional activator of the ARs biosynthetic operon (Figure 12.2.2) (Romero et al., 2013). 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/d070d93a-9a38-4d08-ab41-83365fc0221e/db0ba68dd41088892d332fcc18fff82c408578e69dfd2f8baa5be93b3f63d28a.jpg)



Figure 12.2.2 Model for the regulation of gene expression upon encystment induction in Azotobacter vinelandii. The response regulator GacA undergoes phosphorylation by the histidin kinase GacS. The resultant GacA-P activates transcription of the rsmZ1-7 and rsmY genes, and probably of rpoS. RpoS, in turn, activates transcription of genes involved in alginate, PHB, and ARs synthesis and of the hsp20 gene. However, the RsmZ1-7 and RsmY sRNAs interact with the RsmA protein to avoid its binding to the algD, phbR, and hsp20 mRNAs allowing its translation. For further details, see text.


Mutational inactivation of rpoS does not impair alginate synthesis, but it has been shown to significantly reduce the production of PHB and to abrogate ARs synthesis (Castaneda ˜ et al., 2001; Cocotl-Ya ´ nez ˜ et al., 2011; Hernandez-Eligio ´ et al., 2011). As mentioned in this chapter and shown in Table 12.2.1, alginate (but not PHB nor ARs) is essential for the formation of cysts resistant to desiccation; however, in spite of producing alginate, the rpoS mutant is unable to form cysts resistant to desiccation (Table 12.2.1). Electron microscopy examination of the cysts formed by this mutant revealed that they lack the intine and exine layers of the cyst (Cocotl-Ya ´ nez ˜ et al., 2011). This observation suggested that RpoS must control genes involved in the formation of cyst structures such as the intine and exine layers. Indeed, a search for proteins that were upregulated during encystment in the wild type, but downregulated the rpoS mutant, has revealed that the AlgE1 and AlgE6 alginate epimerases are upregulated during encystment, and its expression in this condition is dependent on RpoS (Cocotl-Ya´ nez˜ et al., 2014). All genes (but algE5) encoding AlgE extracellular epimerases are linked in the chromosome and seem to be organized in an operon (Steigedal et al., 2008); thus, it is likely that expression of the AlgE2, AlgE3, and AlgE4 epimerases is RpoS dependent. Interestingly, the cyst phenotypes of the rpoS and the algE1–7 mutant are very similar. Thus, the algE1–7 genes involved in the formation of the cyst capsule are likely regulated by RpoS (Figure 12.2.2). 

Among the proteins that are upregulated upon encystment in the wild type but not in the rpoS mutant, the small heat shock protein Hsp20 has been identified (Cocotl-Ya ´ nez ˜ et al., 2014). In fact, Hsp20 is one of the most abundant proteins in the A. vinelandii cysts (Cocotl-Ya ´ nez ˜ et al., 2014). Transcription of hsp20 has been confirmed to be driven from an RpoSdependent promoter (Figure 12.2.2). Inactivation of hsp20 does not affect the synthesis of cyst components or the formation of cysts. Although electron microscopy of the cysts formed by the hsp20 mutant reveals a phenotype indistinguishable from that of the wild-type cysts, these cysts are unable to resist desiccation (Cocotl-Ya ´ nez ˜ et al., 2014). Thus, Hsp20 is essential for the resistance to desiccation of the A. vinelandii cysts. 



Cocotl-Ya ´ nez M, Sampieri A, Moreno S, N ˜ u´nez C, Casta ˜ neda M, ˜ et al. 2011. Roles of RpoS and PsrA in cyst formation and alkylresorcinol in Azotobacter vinelandii. Microbiology 157: 1685–1693. 





Reusch RN, Sadoff HL. 1979. 5-n-Alkylresorcinols from encysting Azotobacter vinelandii: isolation and characterization. Journal of Bacteriology 139: 448–453. 



In the desiccation state, lack of water is one of the most damaging stresses. Protein–protein aggregation occurs as a consequence of a loss of water. Small heat shock proteins bind proteins avoiding aggregation (Ehrnsperger et al., 1997; Halsbeck et al., 1999; Lee et al., 1997). As a small heat shock protein, Hsp20 is likely to interact with proteins to protect them against misfolding or aggregation. Interestingly, small heat shock proteins have been related to desiccation protection in eukaryotic organisms, such as the encysted embryos of several branchiopod crustaceans (Clegg, 2001, 2005; King et al., 2013; Willsie and Clegg, 2001). Additionally, in the larvae of Polypedilum vanderplanki, Hsp20 transcripts accumulated during desiccation (Gusev et al., 2011). 



Damron F, Goldberg J. 2012. Proteolytic regulation of alginate overproduction in Pseudomonas aeruginosa. Molecular Microbiology 84: 595–607. 





Reusch RN, Sadoff HL. 1981. Lipid metabolism during encystment of Azotobacter vinelandii. Journal of Bacteriology 145: 889–895. 



Although the Hsp20 protein is one of the most abundant in the cysts, the hsp20 mRNA is much more abundant in vegetative cells than in cysts, suggesting a translational regulation (Cocotl-Ya ´ nez ˜ et al., 2014). Indeed, using hsp20–gusA fusions, it has been shown that hsp20 translation is inhibited in vegetative cells. Additionally, a conserved binding site for the translational repressor RsmA is present in the hsp20 mRNA (Cocotl-Ya ´ nez ˜ et al., 2014). These data suggest that, as with AlgD and PhbR, the expression of Hsp20 is under translational control by the Gac–Rsm cascade (Figure 12.2.2).

## 12.2.8 Outlook



Ertesvag H, Doseth B, Larsen B, Skjak-Bræk G, Valla S. 1994. Cloning ˚ and expression of an Azotobacter vinelandii mannuronan C-5- epimerase gene. Journal of Bacteriology 176: 2846–2853. 





Sadoff HL. 1975. Encystment and germination in Azotobacter vinelandii. Bacteriological Reviews 39: 516–539. 



Although several important aspects of the encystment process that A. vinelandii undergoes are now understood – such as the control of gene expression that leads to the synthesis of some cyst components, and the role of these components in cyst structure, development, and resistance to desiccation – many questions remain to be solved. For instance: How does GacA control the expression of RpoS? Is this control exerted during the encystment process? Are there other AlgU- and/or RpoS-dependent genes involved in the encystment process? Is Hsp20 interacting with proteins to preserve cyst viability in the desiccation state? Is the interaction of Hsp20 specific for one or a few proteins involved in desiccation resistance? Is it a nonspecific interaction that includes a wide variety of proteins that are important to maintain cyst viability?

# Osmotic and desiccation tolerance in Escherichia coli O157:H7 and Salmonella enterica requires rpoS (??38)

Zach Pratt1, Megan Shiroda2,4, Andrew J. Stasic3,4, Josh Lensmire4, and C.W. Kaspar4 

1Department of Biology, St. Norbert College, De Pere, Wisconsin, USA 

2Current address: Department of Microbiology and Molecular Genetics, Michigan State University, East Lansing, Michigan, USA 

3Current address: Department of Microbiology, University of Georgia, Athens, Georgia, USA 

4Department of Bacteriology, University of Wisconsin, Madison, Wisconsin, USA 

## 12.3.1 Introduction

Enteric bacteria, like Salmonella and Escherichia coli, encounter fluctuating conditions within their hosts and the environment. Switching cellular processes between growth and survival pathways requires nimble regulatory responses. For optimal success, these pathogens must replicate and increase numbers when conditions are favorable and produce or have in place protection systems to counteract deleterious conditions encountered in the host and environment. Salmonella enterica and E. coli O157 are human pathogens that successfully modulate growth and survival properties to facilitate transmission between hosts (Mand et al., 2012; van Elsas et al., 2011). 

Enterics encounter low-moisture environments during hostto-host transmission and can persist in these environments, like soil, for months to over a year (Natvig et al., 2002; Ongeng et al., 2011; Uesugi et al., 2007). Numerous factors impact survival, including moisture, temperature, available nutrients, and microflora (Hodgon et al., 2009; Semenov et al., 2009; Soupir et al., 2010; Vinton et al., 2002). Survival is typically greatest in moist environments (Ongeng et al., 2011) and at lower temperatures (Semenov et al., 2009). S. enteritidis associated with an outbreak involving almonds was found in soil from the implicated orchard with no history of manure use. Although the source was not determined, the Salmonella survived for long periods in the soil, grew on nutrients available in almond hulls, and penetrated the almond hull and into the kernel under wet conditions (Danyluk et al., 2008; Uesugi et al., 2007). Root exudates can also promote the survival of enterics as well as bacterial communities in the rhizosphere (Gagliardi and Karns, 2002; Marschner et al., 2004; Ongeng et al., 2011). Survival in the phyllosphere is even more challenging, with little available water and fluctuating environmental conditions detrimental to survival; nonetheless, studies have found that Salmonella and E. coli applied to parsley foliage can persist for more than 175 days in the field (Islam et al., 2004). 

Separation of desiccation stress from osmotic stress is difficult because osmotic stress usually precedes desiccation stress as the surroundings lose water and solutes become concentrated. Desiccation damages the external membranes of microbes and increases intracellular salt levels that can modify proteins and influence the kinetics of enzymes and reactions (D’Aoust, 1997; Sleator and Hill, 2002). These changes and others collectively inhibit the growth of microbes. Bacteria employ systems to retain or acquire water from their surroundings that involve the synthesis or acquisition of compatible solutes (Finn et al., 2013; Li et al., 2012; Sleator and Hill, 2002) that can be concentrated without disrupting cellular processes. Glycine betaine, ectoine, proline, and trehalose are the most common compatible solutes used by enterics (Csonka, 1989; Sleator and Hill, 2002). The accumulation of these solutes allows microorganisms to retain or acquire water. proP, which encodes for a broad-spectrum transporter of compatible solutes, and ProU, an ABC transporter of glycine betaine and proline, are produced in response to osmotic stress (Balaji et al., 2005; Csonka, 1989; Sleator and Hill, 2002; Stirling et al., 1989). A third transporter, BetT, transports choline, which is then converted to glycine betaine (Sleator and Hill, 2002). Salmonellae and E. coli are also capable of synthesizing two compatible solutes, trehalose and proline (Li et al., 2012). Overall, the synthesis and transport of compatible solutes are important because they promote the survival of salmonellae in low-moisture environments. In addition, these solutes are partly responsible for cross-protection to other stresses (Hengge-Aronis, 1993; Mattick et al., 2001). For example, habituation of Salmonella to low water activity $( \mathsf { a } _ { \mathrm { w } } )$ or air drying increased tolerance to heat (Kirby and Davies, 1990; Mattick et al., 2000, 2001). Enterics also form filamentous cells during osmotic stress (Pratt et al., 2012; Stackhouse et al., 2012). It is unclear if the formation of filamentous cells is a survival strategy or merely a consequence of stress, but their formation and presence impact viable cell count determinations (Pratt et al., 2012). 

The general stress protection regulon, regulated by the alternative sigma factor RpoS $( \mathrm { i } . \mathrm { e } . , \sigma ^ { 3 8 } )$ , has been found to be equally important to the persistence of E. coli and Salmonella in lowmoisture environments. There are over 100 genes in this regulon, including other regulators, which impart tolerance to acid, desiccation, oxygen radicals, and unfavorable temperature (Battesti et al., 2011; Lacour and Landini, 2004; Loewen et al., 1998; Patten et al., 2004). RpoS is associated with the stationary phase in enterics but is also induced by unfavorable conditions, such as high osmolarity (Battesti et al., 2011), and is present at lower levels during growth (Dong et al., 2008; Mand et al., 2012). Additionally, it regulates the synthesis of trehalose and ProP (Loewen et al., 1998). Deletion mutants (ΔrpoS) are significantly impaired in the ability to tolerate osmotic and desiccation stress (Shiroda et al., 2014; Stasic et al., 2012). Collectively, these findings illustrate the importance of the general stress regulon to the survival of enterics in low-moisture environments. 

## 12.3.2 Materials and methods

## 12.3.2.1 Strains, plasmids, and culture conditions

The strains and plasmids used in this study are shown in Table 12.3.1 Stationary-phase cultures were prepared from a colony inoculated into Luria broth (LB) (BD Difco, USA) and incubated overnight at $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ with shaking (200 rev $\operatorname* { m i n } ^ { - 1 } )$ to an optical density at 600 nm $\mathrm { ( O D _ { 6 0 0 } ) }$ of 1.2. Exponential-phase cultures were prepared by transferring an overnight culture at 1:1000 to LB and incubating until an $\mathrm { O D } _ { 6 0 0 }$ between 0.3 and 0.5 was achieved. LB was prepared in Hungate tubes for anaerobic culture of strains (Cheng and Kaspar, 1998). The tubes were inoculated and sampled using a sterile needle and syringe. 

Filamentous salmonellae were produced by inoculation of trypticase soy agar (TSA) or tryptic soy broth (TSB) plus NaCl with stationary-phase cells. TSA was supplemented with 70 g $\mathrm { L } ^ { - 1 }$ NaCl (TSA-7NaCl) and incubated for 4 days at $3 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ Plates were wrapped with parafilm to prevent evaporation. Cells were harvested from the plates in 1× phosphate-buffered saline (PBS) (140 mmol $\mathrm { L } ^ { - 1 }$ NaCl, 2.7 mmol $\mathrm { L } ^ { - 1 }$ KCl, 4.3 mmol $\mathrm { L } ^ { - 1 }$ $\mathrm { N a } _ { 2 } \mathrm { H P O } _ { 4 } , 1 . 4 7$ mmol $\mathrm { L } ^ { - 1 }$ KH PO4, pH 7.4) and placed on ice to prevent growth or division. TSB was supplemented with 80 $^ \mathrm { g }$ $\mathrm { L } ^ { - 1 }$ NaCl (TSB-8NaCl) and incubated for up to 4 days at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ Both TSA-7NaCl and TSB-8NaCl had a water activity $( \mathsf { a } _ { \mathrm { w } } )$ of 0.93. The ${ \sf a } _ { \mathrm { w } }$ was determined using an Aqualab model 4TE ${ \sf a } _ { \mathrm { w } }$ meter (Decagon Devices, USA) according to the manufacturer’s instructions. 

In strains harboring pBAD30, which contains an arabinoseinducible promoter, arabinose was added to media when appropriate at a final concentration of 0.3% (w/v) to induce gene expression, and ampicillin (100 mg ml−1) was included to retain pBAD30. 

## 12.3.2.2 Mutant and plasmid construction

rpoS was deleted from S. enterica serotype Typhimurium, strain M-09-001A-01 (M-09), and E. coli O157:H7 (EDL 933), as described previously (Datsenko and Wanner, 2000; Shiroda et al., 2014). ΔrpoS strains were complemented in trans with rpoS from the parent strain (Mand et al., 2012; Shiroda et al., 2014). Arabinose (0.3 mg mL−1) induced the expression of RpoS from pBAD30::rpoS to levels similar to that of the parental strain in the stationary phase (Shiroda et al., 2014).

## 12.3.2.3 Survival during desiccation and osmotic stress

Cattle feces were collected from a local farm. Samples (20–25 g) were heated in a glass petri dish on a hotplate (with medium heat) for 15–20 min with constant stirring until an $\mathtt { a } _ { \mathrm { w } }$ of 0.87– 0.89 was achieved, and then they were sterilized at 121 $^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ 15 psi, for 15 min. Sterilization did not significantly change the ${ \bf { a } } _ { \bf { w } }$ of the feces. Portions of the sterilized feces were tested to ensure the absence of viable cells. 

Feces (6 g) were transferred to a sterile glass Petri dish and inoculated with 600 $\mu \mathrm { L }$ of E. coli O157:H7 to achieve $1 \times 1 0 ^ { 7 }$ colony-forming units (CFUs) $\mathbf { g } ^ { - 1 }$ . Another sample $( 6 ~ \mathrm { g }$ of sterile feces) was inoculated with 600 μL sterile broth to monitor ${ \bf { a } } _ { \bf { w } }$ throughout the course of the survival study. After inoculation with E. coli O157:H7 or sterile broth, the feces were mixed for 5 min using a sterile knife and then incubated at $3 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for up to 28 days. Samples (0.5 g) were removed periodically to determine the number of CFUs. The sample was transferred to 10 mL of PBS, mixed, and the particulates were allowed to settle (5 min) before removing a portion for serial dilution and plating on LB agar plates (Difco). The plates were incubated overnight at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ , and the colonies enumerated. 

In addition, survival assays were conducted in LB supplemented with 120 $\textrm { g } \textrm { L } ^ { - 1 }$ NaCl (LB-12NaCl). Stationary- or exponential-phase cultures of S. enterica M-09 or E. coli O157:H7 were inoculated into LB-12NaCl and incubated statically at $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ in capped conical tubes for 5–28 days. Samples were periodically removed to determine the number of CFUs. 

## 12.3.2.4 Microscopy

An Olympus BHS microscope (USA) was used at 1000× magnification for all microscopy. Images were captured with an Olympus DP70 digital camera. The BacLight RedoxSensor 5-cyano-2,3-ditolyl tetrazolium chloride (CTC) vitality kit (Molecular Probes, USA) was used to visualize bacterial respiration, as described in Pratt et al. (2012) and Sieracki et al. (1999). Briefly, S. enterica was harvested from TSA or TSA-7NaCl in PBS, adjusted to an $\mathrm { O D } _ { 6 0 0 }$ of ∼0.1, and then diluted 1:100 in PBS. Samples were incubated with 5 mmol $\mathrm { L } ^ { - 1 }$ BacLight RedoxSensor CTC for 30 min at $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ . Formalin (5%, v/v) was used to fix cells, and then nucleic acids were stained with 1 μmol $\mathrm { L } ^ { - 1 }$ SYTO-24 (Molecular Probes). Cells were wet mounted on a glass slide and visualized with a XF91 filter set (Omega Filters, USA). 

To visualize the membranes and nucleoids of filaments (defined as cells longer than 10 μm), S. enterica was harvested from TSB or TSB-8NaCl by centrifugation, and resuspended in 5% (v/v) formalin (diluted in PBS) to an $\mathrm { O D } _ { 6 0 0 }$ of ∼0.1. Membranes of fixed S. enterica were stained with 15 μg $\mathrm { m l ^ { - 1 } }$ of FM4-64 (Molecular Probes) on glass slides coated with poly-L-lysine (Sigma-Aldrich) and observed microscopically using the XF91 filter set. Nucleoids were stained with 1 μg $\mathrm { m l ^ { - 1 } }$ of $4 ^ { \prime } , 6 \cdot$ -diamidino-2-phenylindole (DAPI) and observed using 

Olympus UG-1 excitation (330 to 385 nm) and L420 emission (>420 nm) filters. 

## 12.3.2.5 Cell lysates

Cultures were grown to stationary or exponential phase under the defined conditions, and the cells harvested by centrifugation. The cells were resuspended in sodium dodecyl sulfate (SDS) lysis buffer (0.1% (w/v) SDS, 62.5 mmol $\mathrm { L } ^ { - 1 }$ Tris-HCl, pH 6.8) and incubated at $9 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 5 min. Cellular debris was removed by centrifugation $( 1 8 , 0 0 0 \times g ,$ 10 min). The protein concentration in lysates was determined using the Pierce BCA kit (USA). 

## 12.3.2.6 Western blots

Equal quantities of protein were loaded into each well of a 4– 12% Bis-Tris NuPAGE gel (Life Sciences, USA), separated by SDS-PAGE (polyacrylamide gel electrophoresis), and Western blots performed as described in Pratt et al. (2012). Blots were incubated for 1 h with either mouse anti-RpoS antibody (Neoclone, USA) or mouse anti-DnaK antibody (Enzo Life Sciences, USA) at 1:1000 dilutions. Primary antibodies were detected with 1:7500 goat antimouse antibody conjugated to alkaline phosphatase (Promega, USA). Protein bands were detected using 5- bromo-4-chloro-3′ -indolyphosphate p-toluidine salt (Promega) and nitro blue tetrazolium chloride (Promega). ImageQuant 5.2 (GE Healthcare, USA) was used to determine the relative band intensities. 

## 12.3.2.7 Statistical analysis

All $\boldsymbol { p }$ values were calculated in Excel using an unpaired Student’s t-test (two-tailed). Statistically significant differences were defined as those with $\textit { p } < 0 . 0 5$ . All experiments were performed in triplicate with the exception of the RpoS Western blots shown in Figure 12.3.5. The rate of survival (in $\log _ { 1 0 }$ (CFUs) $\mathbf { d } ^ { - 1 } )$ of S. enterica during hyperosmotic stress was analyzed using the glm function in R (version 2.15.1) to calculate linear regressions. 

## 12.3.3 Results and discussion

Enterics respond to osmotic and desiccation stress by inducing an array of genes that encode for general stress protection as well as specific countermeasures to retain or acquire water (Finn et al., 2013; Sleator and Hill, 2002). Another response is the elongation of S. enterica and E. coli O157:H7 in the absence of cellular division (Mattick et al., 2000; Pratt et $a l .$ , 2012; Stackhouse et al., 2012; Stasic, unpublished results). This results in elongated and unseptated cells, called filaments, which can reach lengths greater than 100 μm in length (Figure 12.3.1a). $S .$ enterica 4539H filaments were metabolically active and contained a functional electron transport chain as demonstrated by the reduction of the redox sensor CTC. The reduction of CTC resulted in the cytoplasmic deposition of the fluorescent formazan by product (Figure 12.3.1a). The filaments had a

S. enterica strains 4539H (i, iii) and M-09 (ii, iv) were incubated with CTC. Representative images of control cells (i, ii) and filaments (iii, iv) are shown. The reduced formazan product of CTC fluoresces red, and nucleic acid stained with SYTO-24 fluoresces green (a). Nucleoids were visualized with DAPI (blue), and bacterial membranes were stained with FM4-64 (red) (b).
