The percentage of nonfilamented (circles) and filamented (triangles) S. enterica strain 4539H formed on TSA-7NaCl was measured at various time points. The percentages of cells that were 10 μm in length or less (closed triangles), between 10.1 and 15 μm in length (green open triangles), between 15.1 and 20 μm in length (blue open triangles), and greater than 20 μm in length (red open triangles) are shown (c). The number of nucleoids in filaments and nonfilament cells was determined over time of incubation. The numbers of nucleoids per cell are shown for cells of varying lengths; 0–5 μm (open black circles), 5.1–10 μm (gray circles), 10.1–15 μm (green triangles), 15.1–20 μm (blue triangles), and greater than 20 μm (red triangles) are shown (d). Results are the average of three independent experiments. Error bars represent the SD between trials.


continuous membrane as evidenced by staining with FM4-64 (Figure 12.3.1b, panels ii–v) but lacked septa. The FM4-64 stain was optimal for measurements of cell length. Monitoring the elongation of S. enterica strain 4539H during incubation in TSB-8NaCl identified filaments (cells >10 μm in length) after 12 h of incubation (Figure 12.3.1c,d). Cells greater than 20 μm in length were not observed until 24 h of inoculation but comprised a majority of the population by 24–96 h of incubation. The number of nucleoids within filaments and nonfilamentous cells of S. enterica was determined by staining with DAPI (Figure 12.3.1b). A maximum number of nucleoids per filament was identified after 30 h of incubation in TSB-8NaCl and decreased with continued incubation (Figure 12.3.1d). In filaments that were greater than 20 μm in length, the number of nucleoids at 30 h of incubation $( 1 7 . 7 6 + / - 2 . 2 2 )$ was significantly greater than the number of nucleoids observed in similar-sized filaments after 48, 72, and 96 h of incubation (Student’s t-test, two-tailed, $\begin{array} { r }  p < 0 . 0 0 1 \ \end{array}$ . 

The alternative sigma factor RpoS $( \mathrm { i } . \mathrm { e } . , \sigma ^ { 3 8 } )$ is important for the persistence of E. coli and Salmonella during osmotic and desiccation stress but did not directly affect filament formation. However, the RpoS regulon contributed to filament formation by providing stress protection during cell elongation (Pratt et al., unpublished data). The survival of E. coli O157:H7 ΔrpoS and S. enterica M-09 ΔrpoS was significantly less than the parent strains in LB-12NaCl (Figure 12.3.2a,c). The temperature of incubation (30 or $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } )$ did not significantly affect survival of the parent. The number of the parent E. coli O157:H7 strain decreased at a rate $\mathrm { o f - 0 . 2 4 \pm 0 . 0 2 \log _ { 1 0 } C F U d ^ { - 1 } }$ , whereas the ΔrpoS strain decreased at a rate $\mathrm { o f } - 3 . 1 2 \pm 0 . 0 7 \log _ { 1 0 } \mathrm { C F U } \mathrm { d } ^ { - 1 }$ (Student’s t-test, two-tailed, p <0.05). 

During exponential growth, the RpoS level is undetectable or low in comparison to stationary-phase cells (Dong et al., 2008; Mand et al., 2012); therefore, exponential-phase cells are less stress tolerant (Battesti et al., 2011). As expected, the survival of stationary- and exponential-phase cells of S. enterica M-09 in LB-12NaCl decreased at rates $\mathrm { o f } - 0 . 2 9 \pm 0 . 0 1$ and $- 0 . 5 9 \pm 0 . 0 4$ $\log _ { 1 0 }$ (CFUs) $\mathrm { d } ^ { - 1 }$ , respectively (Figure 12.3.2b). The survival rate of the M-09 ΔrpoS strain was significantly less than that of the parent strain regardless of the growth phase (Figure $1 2 . 3 . 2 \mathrm { b } , \mathrm { c } )$ . The survival rates of exponential- and stationaryphase cells were $- 0 . 9 5 \pm 0 . 3 3$ and $- 1 . 2 9 \pm 0 . 0 8 \log _ { 1 0 }$ (CFU) $\mathrm { d } ^ { - 1 }$ , respectively, which were both significantly less than the rate of survival of the parent strain (Student’s t-test, two-tailed, $\begin{array} { r l r } { p } & { { } < } & { 0 . 0 5 ) } \end{array}$ . Complementation of M-09 ΔrpoS with ${ \mathrm { p B A D } } 3 0 { \because } r p o S$ and induced with arabinose restored survival of the mutant to a rate similar to that of the parent M-09 strain (Figure 12.3.2b,c). The rates of survival of the complemented strain in the exponential or stationary phases were $- 0 . 9 5 \pm 0 . 3 1$ and $- 0 . 3 5 \pm 0 . 0 5 \log _ { 1 0 } { \mathrm { ( C F U ) } } \mathrm { d } ^ { - 1 }$ , respectively. Control cells with the pBAD30 vector only (no insert) or cells without pBAD30 and grown with arabinose did not impact the survival of M-09 ΔrpoS (data not shown). 

To evaluate the impact of the rpoS regulon on survival of E. coli O157:H7 in conditions simulating those encountered during host-to-host transmission, a ΔrpoS strain and its isogenic parent were inoculated into sterile bovine feces with a reduced water activity $( \mathrm { a } _ { \mathrm { w } } = 0 . 8 7 – 0 . 8 9 )$ . The water activity of the inoculated feces continued to decrease with incubation at $3 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ;$ day 1 of incubation, $\mathtt { a _ { w } } = 0 . 8 1$ ; day 3 of incubation, $\mathbf { a } _ { \mathrm { w } } = 0 . 5 0 ;$ and greater than 3 days of incubation, $\mathtt { a _ { w } } \ < \ 0 . 5 0$ . The number of $\mathrm { C F U s } { \bf g } ^ { - 1 }$ of the parent strain decreased almost 1 log (14.6% survivors) after 3 days of incubation and nearly 2 logs (3.9% survivors) after 21 days (Figure 12.3.3). In comparison, the CFUs $\mathbf { g } ^ { - 1 }$ of the ΔrpoS strain decreased almost 4 logs (0.05% survivors) after 3 days of incubation and was undetectable (below the limit of detection) after 21 days of incubation or greater. These results clearly demonstrate the importance of the RpoS regulon to survival of E. coli O157:H7 in desiccated feces and the ability of this pathogen to persist in the environment.

Parent E. coli O157:H7 (diamonds) and O157:H7 ΔrpoS (squares) were grown to stationary phase, inoculated into dried cow feces, and incubated at 30 ◦C. 2012; Shiroda et al., 2014). To determine if RpoS impacts growth during stress, M-09 and M-09 ΔrpoS were grown to stationary or exponential phases, then inoculated into LB and LB-6NaCl, and growth was monitored spectrophotometrically. Stationaryphase cells of M-09 ΔrpoS had a significantly longer lag phase in LB-6NaCl than did the parent strain (Student’s t-test, twotailed, $\textstyle P < 0 . 0 5 )$ (Table 12.3.2). There was no significant difference in the length of lag phase of exponential- and stationaryphase cells of M-09 ΔrpoS in LB-6NaCl (Table 12.3.2) due to the absence of RpoS. There was a significant difference in the length of lag phase in the M-09 parent strain $( p < 0 . 0 0 1 )$ ), with a significantly shorter lag phase observed with stationary-phase cells than with exponential-phase cells. This is likely due to the presence of RpoS in the stationary-phase cells used to inoculate 

LB-6NaCl and an absence or low level of RpoS in exponentialphase cells. 

M-09 ΔrpoS was complemented in trans with rpoS in pBAD30. Expression was under control of an arabinoseinducible promoter, and the level of RpoS induced with arabinose (0.3%) was similar to the level found in stationary-phase cells of the parent strain (data not shown). Neither the empty vector (pBAD30) nor arabinose had a significant effect on the lag phase in M-09 ΔrpoS (Table 12.3.2, Student’s t-test, twotailed, $\textstyle P > 0 . 0 5 )$ . Induction of rpoS in M-09 ΔrpoS pBAD::rpoS restored the length of the lag phase to that of the parent strain (Table 12.3.2). 

The lengths of lag phase in M-09 ΔrpoS pBAD::rpoS with and without arabinose were also compared (Table 12.3.2). The length of the lag phase was dependent upon induction and expression of rpoS. The lag phase was nearly 8 h shorter in LB-6NaCl inoculated with stationary-phase cells when rpoS was induced in comparison to uninduced cells (Student’s t-test, two-tailed, $\begin{array} { r }  p < 0 . 0 0 1 \ \end{array}$ ). Likewise, induced exponential cells of M-09 ΔrpoS pBAD::rpoS had a significantly shorter lag phase in LB-6NaCl than non-induced cells (Students’ t-test, two, tailed, $\textstyle P < 0 . 0 5 )$ . 

During residence in their animal hosts, enterics thrive in the anaerobic conditions of the intestinal tract. The amount of RpoS is lower in anaerobically cultured E. coli (King and Ferenci, 2005). To evaluate the impacts of lower levels of RpoS, the survival of E. coli O157:H7 cultured aerobically and anaerobically was evaluated in dried cow feces (Figure 12.3.4a,b). Both stationary-phase (Figure 12.3.4a) and exponential-phase cells (Figure 12.3.4b) were tested, with stationary-phase cells surviving for weeks and exponential-phase cells surviving for days. Aerobically grown stationary-phase cells of the parental O157:H7 survived better in the dried feces than did the ΔrpoS mutant. The anaerobic stationary-phase culture of O157:H7 survived less well than the aerobic stationary-phase culture of O157:H7 (Figure 12.3.4a), but the differences were not statistically significant (Student’s t-test, two-tailed, $\cdot p > 0 . 0 5 )$ due in part

Figure 12.3.4 The impact of anaerobic growth on RpoS levels and survival properties is strain dependent. Cultures of E. coli O157:H7 (squares) and O157:H7 ΔrpoS (diamonds) were grown to stationary (a) or exponential (b) phase under aerobic (closed symbols) or anaerobic conditions (opened symbols), inoculated into dried feces, and the number of $\mathrm { \dot { C } F U s } \dot { \bf g } ^ { - 1 }$ monitored over time (days). Likewise, S. enterica strains M-09 and 4539H were grown to exponential (E) or stationary (S) phase in anaerobic or aerobic conditions at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ . RpoS and DnaK were detected in cell lysates by Western blot. The blot shown is representative of three trials (c). The amount of RpoS in each sample was normalized to the amount of DnaK and is reported relative to a standard sample included in each blot (numbers shown under blot). Cultures grown aerobically or anaerobically were inoculated into LB-12NaCl, and the number o $\mathrm { \Delta [ C F U ~ m l ^ { - 1 } }$ were determined over time. Linear regression of data points was calculated using the glm function in R. The results are the average values from three independent experiments. Symbols denote a statistically significant difference between aerobic and anaerobic incubation within the same growth phase by Student’s t-test, two-tailed $( ^ { * } p < 0 . 0 5 ; \dag p < 0 . 0 1 )$ .


to variation in the starting numbers of CFU $\mathrm { m l ^ { - 1 } }$ between trials. As in previous findings, the O157:H7 $\Delta r p o S$ strain did not survive as well as the parent strain, but there was not a statistical difference in the survival of aerobically and anaerobically cultured cells (Student’s t-test, two-tailed, $\textstyle P > 0 . 0 5 )$ . Exponentialphase cultures survived poorly in dried feces in comparison to stationary-phase cultures (Figure 12.3.4b). The numbers of CFU ml−1 of the O157:H7 ΔrpoS strain were below the limit of detection after 1 day of incubation regardless of aerobic or anaerobic culture. 

Similar results were observed with S. enterica strains M-09 and 4539H (Figure 12.3.4c). The level of RpoS in cell lysates was monitored by Western blot from cultures grown aerobically or anaerobically to either exponential (E) or stationary phase (S). The amount of RpoS was greater in aerobic cultures than anaerobic cultures regardless of the growth phase of the inoculum, although the levels were not significantly different $( p > 0 . 0 5 )$ (Figure 12.3.4c). Aerobically and anaerobically cultured S. enterica were grown to the exponential and stationary phase and inoculated into LB-12NaCl to investigate if anaerobic growth prior to osmotic stress had an effect on the survival of S. enterica. Stationary-phase cultures of both strains survived better than the exponential-phase cultures in both aerobically and anaerobically cultured cells, which is consistent with previous findings (Battesti et al., 2011; Mand et al., 2012). However, stationary-phase cells of M-09 grown aerobically survived significantly better $( p < 0 . 0 5 )$ in LB-12NaCl than those grown anaerobically (Figure 12.3.4c). There was no difference in survival of exponential-phase cells grown aerobically or anaerobically $( p > 0 . 0 5 )$ . In contrast, exponential-phase 4539H grown anaerobically survived in LB-12NaCl better than those incubated aerobically (p < 0.05), and there was no difference in the survival of stationary-phase cells grown aerobically or anaerobically. Similar variations in the levels of RpoS and survival were noted in other S. enterica serovars during aerobic and anaerobic culture (data not shown).

RpoS and DnaK were detected by Western blot periodically over 21 days of incubation of M-09 in LB (a) or LB-12NaCl (b) and M-09 ΔrpoS + pBAD30::rpoS (grown with arabinose to induce rpoS production) in LB-12NaCl with arabinose (c) or LB-12NaCl (d). RpoS and DnaK were detected via Western blot. These blots are representative of two trials.




Li H, Megalis C, Tortello ML. 2012. Transcriptomic analysis of Salmonella desiccation resistance. Foodborne Pathogens and Disease 9: 1143–1151. 





Loewen PC, Hu B, Strutinsky J, Sparling R. 1998. Regulation in the rpoS regulon of Escherichia coli. Canadian Journal of Microbiolog y 44: 707–717. 



Studies comparing the survival of the parent M-09 strain with the ΔrpoS strain demonstrated the importance of RpoS to the survival of S. enterica during osmotic stress (Figure 12.3.2). To evaluate the stability of RpoS during sustained osmotic stress, cells were harvested periodically during 21 days of incubation in LB or LB-12NaCl, and cell lysates tested for the presence of RpoS by Western blot. Viable cells of M-09 were no longer detected after 21 days of incubation in LB-12NaCl. RpoS was detected throughout the 21-day incubation period, although the amount appeared to decrease with incubation in both LB and LB-12NaCl (Figure 12.3.5). RpoS levels were further assessed using M-09 ΔrpoS containing pBAD30::rpoS in which arabinose could be used to control the production of RpoS. M-09 was grown to stationary phase in the presence of arabinose and then incubated in LB or LB-12NaCl. Again, RpoS was detected throughout the 21 days of its incubation in LB or LB-12NaCl (Figure 12.3.5c,d), although the level appeared to be lower in cells incubated in LB compared to LB-12NaCl. Equal loading of total protein was confirmed by Coomassie blue staining (data not shown). These results indicate that RpoS was present, but the level decreased during incubation in LB and LB-12NaCl. 



Mand T, Dopfer D, Ane C, Ingham B, Kaspar CW. 2012. Growth and¨ survival parameter estimates and relation to RpoS levels in serotype O157:H7 and non-O157 Shiga toxin-producing Escherichia coli. Journal of Applied Microbiology 114: 242–255. 



In summary, the results from this study illustrate the dynamic responses of enterics to osmotic and desiccation stress. Central to the persistence of enterics in these low-water environments is the RpoS regulon, which provides protection by a number of pathways, including the synthesis and import of compatible solutes. A greater understanding of the networks and mechanisms of osmotic and desiccation tolerance may provide targets for controlling the environmental persistence and transmission of these enteric pathogens.

# Desiccation of Salmonella enterica induces cross-tolerance to other stresses

Shlomo Sela (Saldinger) and Chellaiah Edward Raja 

Microbial Food-Safety Research Unit, Department of Food Quality and Safety, Institute for Postharvest and Food Sciences, The Volcani Center, Bet Dagan, Israel 

## 12.4.1 Introduction

Stress is commonly defined as any deleterious factor or condition (i.e., physical, chemical, or biological) that adversely affects microbial growth or survival (Vorob’eva, 2003; Yousef and Courtney, 2003). A stress might affect the integrity of the cell membrane, maintenance of the folding of proteins, and the integrity of the DNA (Booth, 2002). The ability to sense and respond to stresses is a fundamental feature evolved to enable bacteria to survive under a changing environment within or outside the host. Exposing bacteria to a mild stress may induce an adaptation response resulting in the development of tolerance to subsequent exposure to a more severe stress of the same type, and in some cases, adaptation to a single stress may also enhance tolerance against subsequent stresses to which the organism had not been previously exposed (De Angelis and Gobbetti, 2004; Dragosits et al., 2013; Lou and Yousef, 1996; Rodriguez-Romo and Yousef, 2005). The latter phenomenon is referred to as cross-protection, or cross-tolerance. Bacterial populations have a remarkable capacity to survive extreme fluctuations in their environments, and the ability to develop cross-tolerance following a previous exposure to a single sublethal stress may provide a fitness advantage in such environments (Dragosits et al., 2013). The phenomenon of cross stress protection has been widely described in many bacterial species and for various stress combinations (Abee and Wouters, 1999; Begley et al., 2002; Berry and Gasch, 2008; Canovas et al., 2001; Fletcher and Csonka, 1998; Garren et al., 1998; Jenkins et al., 1988, 1990; Leyer and Johnson, 1993; Lou and Yousef, 1996, 1997; Mattimore and Battista, 1996; Ryu and Beuchat, 1998; Tesone et al., 1981; Vorob’eva, 2003). Some examples of cross-tolerance in bacteria are listed in Table 12.4.1 

Cross-protection following exposure to a single stress has been widely described in the foodborne pathogen Salmonella enterica (Foster, 1991; Foster and Hall, 1990; Foster and Spector, 1995). For example, pre-exposure of Salmonella to mild pH was shown to increase tolerance to heat, high NaCl concentration, crystal violet, and polymyxin B (Leyer and Johnson, 1993), and increased survival at low temperature (Xu et al., 2008). Likewise, exposure of Salmonella to heat resulted in increased tolerance to other stresses (Podolak et al., 2010; Rychlik and Barrow, 2005; Smith and Marks, 2015). 

Development of cross-tolerance following desiccation stress is a subject of great importance, especially in the agro-food industry, since possible development of tolerance to multiple stresses, such as heat or disinfection, may render standard industrial inactivation processes insufficient for eradicating spoilage microorganisms and foodborne pathogens. This chapter will focus on desiccation stress in the foodborne pathogen S. enterica, review reports on cross-tolerance following desiccation, and discuss current knowledge regarding mechanisms potentially underlying this phenomenon. 

## 12.4.2 Desiccation stress tolerance and food contamination

S. enterica strains are animal and zoonotic Gram-negative pathogens of worldwide importance. Salmonella is considered one of the main etiologic agents of foodborne diseases worldwide and is of great significance for public health and for the economy. Non-typhoidal Salmonella usually causes gastroenteritis, named salmonellosis with an estimated over 9 million cases each year in the world, resulting in approximately 155,000 deaths (Majowicz et al., 2010). Salmonellosis afflicts people in developed as well as developing countries; however, mortality is primarily restricted to the developing countries. S. enterica consists of more than 2600 specific serovars; some are host-specific, but the majority is not restricted to a single host. Salmonella serovars have been traditionally regarded as contaminants of animal-derived foods, since they are carried in the intestinal track of poultry and various livestock animals. However, in the last few decades, salmonellosis associated with fresh produce has become an increasing problem in the United States and Europe (Berger et al., 2010; Callejon´ et al., 2015). Concurrently, Salmonella is continuously involved in outbreaks associated with consumption of low-moisture foods, such as milk powder, infant’s formula, chocolate, peanut butter, and dry spices and herbs (Beuchat et al., 2013; Zweifel and Stephan, 2012), underscoring the persistence capacity of this pathogen in the field and during industrial processing.

Regardless of the route of food contamination, Salmonella must survive periods of dehydration in the soil, on the plant’s exterior, as well as on dry surfaces after harvest and during industrial processing. Desiccation tolerance is one of the key traits that contribute to the ability of the pathogen to cycle between the external environment and the host (Beuchat et al., 2013). 

Numerous reports have documented the ability of S. enterica strains to persist under low-moisture conditions in agricultural settings and in low-water-activity foods. Salmonella may be introduced into crop fields by several routes, including irrigation with contaminated water, use of animal manure fertilizers, runoff water from livestock operations, and wild and domestic animals (Beuchat, 2006; Beuchat and Ryu, 1997). Upon introduction into soil, Salmonella may encounter periods of wetting and dehydration resulting in considerable loss of viability; yet a proportion of the population seems capable of persisting in the soil for long periods of times ranging from days to more than a year (Abell et al., 2009; Bernstein et al., 1982, 2007; Franz et al., 2005; Guo et al., 2002; Islam et al., 2004; Malik et al., 2004; Ongeng et al., 2013). Similarly, Salmonella may encounter desiccation stress when residing on plants’ surfaces, such as the cuticle of leaves (Brandl and Mandrell, 2002; Lindow and Brandl, 2003; Poza-Carrion et al., 2013). Persistence of Salmonella on fresh produce as well as on processed foods is considered a major health hazard and may result in food contamination and subsequent illness among consumers. The presence of Salmonella in produce may also lead to contamination of food operations, through colonization of food-contact surfaces and biofilm formation resulting in contamination of production lines, either by recontamination or by cross-contamination (Carrasco et al., 2012). 

## 12.4.3 Persistence of Salmonella in low-water-activity environment

Comparison of the survival of several bacterial species during dehydration on anhydrous silica gel and subsequent storage at a temperature of $2 2 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for at least 3 months has demonstrated that Gram-positive bacteria and Salmonella strains were more resistant to desiccation compared to other Gram-negative bacteria (Janning et al., 1994). Indeed, numerous studies have further established the ability of Salmonella to survive in dry environments over long periods of time. A low-moisture environment has been generated either by adding humectants in order to modulate water activity (Kieboom et al., 2006; Mattick et al., 2000), or by air-drying usually under constant conditions of temperature and moisture (Finn et al., 2013; Gruzdev et al., 2011, 2012b; Kusumaningrum et al., 2003; Li et al., 2012; Margas et al., 2014). More than 30 years ago, Juven and coworkers (Juven et al., 1984) investigated the survival of two Salmonella serovars in dry milk, cocoa powder, poultry feed, and meat and bone meal at three water activities $( \mathsf { a } _ { \mathrm { w } } )$ in the range of 0.4 to 0.75 up to 14 weeks. Interestingly, survival was greater at ${ \bf { a } } _ { \bf { w } }$ of 0.43 and 0.52 than at $0 . 7 5 \mathrm { a } _ { \mathrm { w } }$ . More recently, Hiramatsu et al. (2005) utilized paper disks with a water activity of 0.5 to 0.6 as a model system for testing Salmonella survival in low-moisture environments, as well as selected dry foods models, such as dried squid chips and plain dried squid. In the experiments utilizing paper disks, the researchers reported that following an initial reduction in the viable count of dehydrated Salmonella strains, during the first 24 h period, viable bacteria were detected at 35 and 70 days following storage at 35 and ${ 2 5 } ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ respectively. Remarkably, the colony count remained constant for up to 2 years during storage at $4 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Hiramatsu et al., 2005). The researchers reported that the survival of dried Salmonella cells substantially increased (up to 79 times) in the presence of sucrose (36%). Survival of Salmonella spp. inoculated in dried squid chips containing sucrose was 23 to 89 times greater than that in the dried paper disks, whereas the populations of Salmonella in dried plain squid without sucrose were almost equal to those in the dried paper disks (Hiramatsu et al., 2005). The authors also reported that in the presence of sucrose, Salmonella might survive for months in foods such as chocolate, peanut butter, and potato chips. They concluded that the combination of high fat and low water activity seems to have a synergistic effect on Salmonella survival (Hiramatsu et al., 2005). 

Gruzdev et al. (2011, 2012b) conducted a series of experiments on the survival of five Salmonella serovars on plastic (polystyrene) surface following air-drying for 22 h at a relative humidity of 40%. The authors described a reduction in the viable counts during dehydration in the range of 20 to 60% (Gruzdev et al., 2011, 2012b). Dehydrated S. enterica serovar Typhimurium (S. Typhimurium) stored at $4 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ had reduced viability (colony-forming units (CFUs)) of less than 1 log during 12 weeks in cold storage, and viable Salmonella cells were still detected after 100 weeks of cold storage (Gruzdev et al., 2012b). Addition of the compatible solutes sucrose and trehalose to the dehydrating Salmonella has significantly facilitated dehydration tolerance compared to control. In contrast, glycine–betaine, a known compatible solute produced by Salmonella in response to osmotic stress (Koo et al., 1991; Wood et al., 2001), has no effect on dehydration tolerance (Gruzdev et al., 2012b). The dissimilar effect of the two molecules on desiccation stress response indicates the presence of distinct mechanisms in the adaptation of Salmonella to desiccation and osmotic stress. 

Survival on stainless steel has important safety implications for Salmonella contamination in food operations. It has been reported that Salmonella can remain viable on dry stainless steel surfaces for up to 96 h when high initial levels were presented (Kusumaningrum et al., 2003). More recently, Margas et al. (2014) studied the survival of 15 Salmonella isolates following dehydration on stainless steel surfaces under controlled temperature $( 2 5 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } )$ and humidity (33%) conditions from 1 h to 30 days. The strains with the highest survival rate after 30 days had reduced viability ranging from 1.3 $\log _ { 1 0 }$ CFU/surface to $1 . 6 \log _ { 1 0 }$ CFU/surface. For most of the strains, following an initial reduction in viability in the first hours $( < 7 2 \ \mathrm { h } )$ , no further reduction was observed through the 30 day period (Margas et al., 2014). 

## 12.4.4 Cross-tolerance to other stresses

Several studies have reported that pre-exposure of Salmonella to low-moisture environments results in the induction of cross-tolerance to other stresses. Perhaps the most commonly reported cross-tolerance phenotype related to desiccation stress in Salmonella is heat tolerance (see Section 13). 

Doyle and Mazzotta have reviewed studies on thermal resistance and concluded that Salmonellae in dried food products are extremely heat resistant and may act more like spores in their thermal tolerance (Doyle and Mazzotta, 2000). Comparable findings were also reported for Salmonella in aged chicken litter where thermal resistance increased significantly when the cells were adapted initially to desiccation (Chen et al., 2013). The degree of heat tolerance is influence by a number of factors, including strain and serotypes tested, previous growth and storage conditions, the physical and chemical food composition, test media, and the media used to recover heat-damaged cells (Podolak et al., 2010). Since thermal treatments during food-manufacturing processes frequently serve as the lethal or $^ { \mathrm { s c } } \mathrm { k i l l } ^ { \mathrm { \Omega } }$ step for foodborne pathogenic bacteria, the thermal tolerance phenomenon raises serious operational concerns as to the effectiveness of this step (Humphrey, 2004). The continuous involvement of low-moisture food, which underwent thermal treatment, in salmonellosis outbreaks underscores this problem (Beuchat et al., 2013). The increased heat tolerance of Salmonella in foods of low moisture necessitates re-evaluation of the temperature and time needed to achieve effective inactivation of the pathogen in each low-moisture food product or ingredient in order to guarantee complete eradication of Salmonella. 

Besides thermal tolerance, desiccation also induces crosstolerance to multiple other stresses that Salmonella may encounter in the environment or during infection of the host.

It has been reported that maintaining Salmonella under lowmoisture conditions may result in a higher resistance to multiple assaults ranging from hypochlorite, a disinfectant commonly used in the food industry, to high NaCl concentration (Kieboom et al., 2006; Mattick et al., 2000), to bile salts (Stackhouse et al., 2012). Some examples of reported cross-tolerance in Salmonella dehydrated on various surfaces, in dried food and feed, or in lowwater-activity models and foods are presented in Table 12.4.2. 

In a recent study, Gruzdev et al. systematically studied the capacity of desiccated S. Typhimurium to develop crosstolerance against several common stresses (Gruzdev et al., 2011). Dehydrated Salmonella cells displayed significantly higher tolerance to multiple stressors, including ethanol (10 to 30%, 5 min), sodium hypochlorite (10 to 100 ppm, 10 min), didecyl dimethyl ammonium chloride (0.05 to 0.25%, 5 min), hydrogen peroxide (0.5 to 2.0%, 30 min), NaCl (0.1 to 1 M, 2 h), bile salts (1 to 10%, 2 h), dry heat (100 ◦C, 1 h), and UV irradiation (125 μW/cm2, 25 min). Strikingly, dehydrated Salmonella has diminished tolerance to acetic and citric acids compared to non-desiccated cells, suggesting that dehydration activates genes or mechanisms that are not involved in the induction of the acid tolerance response (ATR). Indeed, ATR appears to be a specific defense mechanism for acid, since no cross-protection was noted for hydrogen peroxide, SOS, or heat shock (Foster and Hall, 1990). 

The finding that dehydration provides cross-protection to heat, osmotic, and oxidative challenges, but not against acid challenge (Gruzdev et al., 2011), infers that the development of cross-tolerance to other stresses is likely related to the presence of distinct stress response mechanisms evolved to cope with different stresses. In support of this notion, exposure of Salmonella-contaminated tomato or apple slices to acids (ascorbic and citric acids) has reduced Salmonella survival following dehydration (DiPersio et al., 2003; Yoon et al., 2004). On the other hand, Salmonella acid-adapted cells exhibit a greater thermal tolerance than do unadapted cells (Alvarez-Ord ´ o´nez ˜ et al., 2012; Sharma et al., 2005; Yang et al., 2014), as reported for desiccation-adapted cells. The contrasting reports on potential overlapping between the ATR and desiccation stress responses are in favor of the presence of multilevel regulation networks, perhaps with some overlapping pathways. It is possible that the stress-specific response observed in ATR, in some reports, resulted from differential activation of regulatory pathway(s) due to variations in the extrinsic and/or intrinsic factors related to the tested strains in each study. The findings that heat tolerance is mounted in S. enterica serotype Safra, but not in acid-adapted serotype Poona (Sharma et al., 2005), support a strain-specific response. Nevertheless, in addition to ATR, preexposure conditions such as growth conditions and media may also affect the development of cross-tolerance following acid challenge and contribute to the complexity of the regulatory systems. 

## 12.4.5 Potential mechanism for cross-tolerance following desiccation

While data regarding the response of Salmonella to multiple environmental stresses are being accumulated (Spector and Kenyon, 2012), little is known regarding the genetic and physiologic mechanisms that enable desiccated Salmonella to exhibit cross-tolerance to other stresses. Studies on resistance and survival strategies of Salmonella under different environmental stresses have revealed diverse mechanisms, involving a range of transcriptional regulators, sigma factors, and twocomponent regulatory systems, too numerous and complex to be considered extensively in this review; the reader is referred to review articles, such as that of Spector and Kenyon (Spector and Kenyon, 2012). Here, we will highlight a number of stress response mechanisms, which were reported to confer a general cross-tolerance in Salmonella and therefore are likely to participate in mounting cross-tolerance following desiccation as well. 

## 12.4.5.1 The general stress response

Given that different stresses may induce a common stress response, it is conceivable that exposure to a single stress will induce cross-protection against other stresses, which utilizes a similar cellular adaptation response. In E. coli and Salmonella, the stress response is controlled by several sigma factors, which serve as master regulators and control the expression of specific genes required for cellular adaptation to multiple stresses. Sigma factors interact with the core RNA polymerase to provide the holoenzyme transcription specificity for a subset of genes, commonly known as the regulon or sigmalon (Battesti et al., 2011; Navarro Llorens et al., 2010). One of the best studied sigma factors that confers a general stress response is the RpoS subunit of RNA polymerase (Hengge-Aronis, 2002b). Accumulation of RpoS leads to the transcription of RpoS-mediated genes, also known as the RpoS regulon (Battesti et al., 2011). The regulatory activity of RpoS is controlled at multiple levels: transcription, translation, proteolysis, and protein activity (Battesti et al., 2011; Hengge, 2009). Multiple systems are involved in RpoS regulation, including the Rcs phosphorelay and the PhoPQ two-component system. The molecular alarmone, (p)ppGpp, involved in stringent response has a major role in determining the level and activity of RpoS, through induction of transcription and translation, inhibition of degradation, and enhancement of RpoS activity (Battesti et al., 2011; Hengge, 2009). 

RpoS is commonly expressed in stationary phase cells and in response to starvation for C, P, or Mg, as well as under stress conditions, including high and low pH, temperature, osmolarity, and DNA damage (Hengge-Aronis, 2002a,b). Due to its wide spectrum of activity, RpoS may play a central role in the mechanisms involved in cross-protection to multiple stresses. Indeed, RpoS was shown to be essential for optimal desiccation tolerance in Salmonella (Humphrey et al., 1996; Jorgensen et al., 2000), as well as for carbon-starvation-inducible thermotolerance, and acid tolerance via the rpoS-dependent starvationstress response locus stiA, encoding a nitrate reductase (Spector et al., 1999). It is therefore conceivable that the RpoS-mediated general stress response plays a central role in cross-tolerance of desiccated Salmonella to heat and other stresses, as well. 

Interestingly, several recent studies that have examined the transcriptome of different Salmonella strains have failed to show induction of rpoS transcription during desiccation (Finn et al., 2013; Gruzdev et al., 2012a; Li et al., 2012). Yet this might be related to the experimental conditions used to prepare both control and desiccated bacteria. For example, both Li et al. (Li et al., 2012) and Gruzdev et al. (Gruzdev et al., 2012a) have utilized stationary phase bacteria under nutrient deprivation, conditions known to induce rpoS transcription and accumulation of high levels of active RpoS (Hengge-Aronis, 2002a). 

## 12.4.5.2 Cellular membranes

The bacterial outer membrane is the first interface with the external environment and is an important barrier against external stresses. Indeed, the outer membrane lipopolysaccharides of S. Typhimurium dried in blood droplets were shown to be required for desiccation tolerance (Garmiri et al., 2008). It has been shown that bacteria can modify their membrane composition in response to environmental signals present in their surroundings in order to maintain a degree of membrane fluidity compatible with life (Beney and Gervais, 2001; see Section 18). The fatty acids composition determines membrane fluidity, which affects stress tolerance in bacteria, including S. Typhimurium (Alvarez-Ord ´ o´nez ˜ et al., 2008, 2009; Yoon et al., 2015). The role of membrane’s fatty acids in desiccation tolerance is supported by the study of Li et al. (Li et al., 2012). These researchers have studied gene expression following airdrying for 2 h at 11% relative humidity in S. Tennessee and in S. Typhimurium LT2 (LT2) on sterile filter paper discs. LT2 strain harbors a mutated rpoS gene, which reduces its tolerance to desiccation. Microarray data indicated that the most significantly induced physiological activity (51% of total gene with differential expression) in air-dried cells was in genes related to fatty acid metabolism that is required for the cell’s membranes. It has been postulated that the increased fatty acid metabolism is linked to changes in fatty acids composition, which may affect a membrane’s fluidity, making the bacterial envelope more rigid to withstand the osmotic pressure developed during desiccation (Li et al., 2012). Changes in a membrane’s fatty acids composition was also linked to tolerance to other stresses. For example, exposure of S. enterica serovar Enteritidis to a sublethal concentration of trisodium phosphate or alkaline pH resulted in changes in fatty acids membrane composition and was correlated with increased thermotolerance (Sampathkumar et al., 2004). Likewise, S. Typhimurium cells exposed to low pH underwent a decrease in the unsaturated fatty acids to saturated fatty acids ratio and in the relative concentration of octadecenoic acids (C18:1), with a concomitant increase in the content in cyclic fatty acids (Yang et al., 2014). These studies support the notion that changes in a membrane’s fluidity following desiccation might play a role in the development of cross-tolerance to other stress. 

## 12.4.5.3 Compatible solutes

One of the basic physiological mechanisms that is common to both hyperosmotic stressed bacteria and dehydrating cells is the accumulation of intracellular molecules in order to balance cellular osmolarity to prevent further water loss (Potts, 1994). To adapt to hyperosmotic stress, bacteria have to maintain the proper intracellular osmotic pressure within tolerable limits. This commonly involves accumulation of charged solutes (e.g., potassium ions (K+) and glutamate), followed by accumulation of compatible solutes either through de novo biosynthesis or through uptake from the external environment (O’Byrne and Booth, 2002; Potts, 1994). Various molecules are utilized by bacteria to increase the intracellular osmotic pressure. These include the amino acids glutamate, glutamine, proline, and alanine; the quaternary amine glycine betaine; the tetrahydropyrimidine ectoine; and the disaccharides sucrose and trehalose (Spector and Kenyon, 2012). Transcriptomic analysis of dehydrated Salmonella revealed the induction of genes related to the transport of the compatible solutes, glycine, choline, and proline betaine, as well as those related to the biosynthesis of trehalose, which is involved in protection of cell membranes from osmotic stress (Li et al., 2012). Other transcriptomics studies performed with dehydrating Salmonella have shown the induction of K+ uptake transporter (Gruzdev et al., 2012a) as well as of transporters of other osmoprotectants, ProP, ProU, and OsmU (Finn et al., 2013). It is plausible that Salmonella cells exposed to desiccation stress will develop enhanced tolerance to hyperosmotic stress and vice versa. It is noteworthy that besides its role in osmotic and desiccation stress tolerance, intracellular accumulation of various compatible solutes is also linked to protection against heat stress in both E. coli (Pleitner et al., 2012) and Salmonella (Mattick et al., 2001). The compatible solute, trehalose, has been reported to protect Salmonella against both thermal stress and oxidative stress (Canovas et al., 2001; Franca et al., 2007; Hengge-Aronis et al., 1991; Howells et al., 2002; see Sections 10 and 13). It has been hypothesized that compatible solutes may act as thermoprotective molecules by preventing heat-related denaturation of proteins and subsequent degradation (Caldas et al., 1999; Diamant et al., 2001; Jain and Roy, 2009). Since compatible solutes and especially trehalose appear to have a central role in the cellular response to multiple stresses, it is likely that synthesis and accumulation of compatible solutes during desiccation serve as additional mechanisms responsible for cross-tolerance following desiccation. 

## 12.4.5.4 Other stress-induced genes

Gruzdev et al. have studied S. Typhimurium gene expression following dehydration for 22 h at $2 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ on a polystyrene surface (Gruzdev et al., 2012a). Among the 90 upregulated genes, the major functional groups induced were ribosomal proteins (22 genes) and amino acid metabolism (19 genes), which suggest that the cells underwent massive protein biosynthesis. This probably reflects the relatively long dehydration time that Salmonella had to adapt to the waterless environment. Other genes induced are genes involved in energy production, ion transport, transcription, and stress response, with the highest induction appearing with the kdpFABC operon, encoding a potassium transport channel. There are several stress response– induced genes, including dnaK, hflB, yhgI, ibpA, hslU, groEL, and sufD (see Table 12.4.3) (Gruzdev et al., 2012a). Among the desiccation-induced genes identified in this study were aceA encoding isocitrate-lyase; ddg encoding lipid A biosynthesis palmitoleoyl-acyltransferase; nifU encoding the modular iron– sulfur cluster scaffolding protein; fnr, a fumurate and nitrate reductase, a global regulator involved in anaerobic respiratory pathways; as well as rpoE, encoding the alternative sigma factor, RpoE. RpoE is known to be involved in the Gram-negative bacterial response against cell envelope stresses (Erickson and Gross, 1989; Rouviere et al., 1995). The RpoE regulon includes genes that encode proteins that take part in the folding or degradation of polypeptides in the periplasm, chaperones and thiol–disulfide oxido-reductases, as well as genes involved in lipopolysaccharide synthesis and/or modification (McMeechan et al., 2007; Rowley et al., 2006). The activity of RpoE is negatively controlled by the antisigma factor RseA, which binds RpoE, rendering it inactive. Free RpoE binds to core RNA polymerase and redirects the RNA polymerase holoenzyme to RpoE-dependent promoters enabling expression of the RpoE regulon (Rowley et al., 2006). Unlike in E. coli, the S. Typhimurium rpoE gene is not essential for cell viability, even at high temperature. Yet, the Salmonella

RpoE was shown to be involved in the starvation stress response (Kenyon et al., 2002; McMeechan et al., 2007); stationary phase survival; the oxidative stress response (Testerman et al., 2002); tolerance to hydrogen peroxide, superoxide, and antimicrobial peptides (Humphreys et al., 1999); as well as survival during osmotic shock and refrigeration (McMeechan et al., 2007). Mutagenesis of rpoE has confirmed the role of this gene also in the adaptation of Salmonella to desiccation (Gruzdev et al., 2012a), further supporting the role of RpoE in the development of cross-tolerance in desiccated Salmonella cells. 

## 12.4.5.5 Cell elongation



Battesti A, Majdalani N, Gottesman S. 2011. The RpoS-mediated general stress response in Escherichia coli. Annual Review of Microbiolog y 65: 189–213. 





the fate of Escherichia coli O157:H7 and Salmonella enterica serovar Typhimurium in manure, manure-amended soil, and lettuce. Applied and Environmental Microbiology 71(10): 6165–6174. 





Kirby RM, Davies R. 1990. Survival of dehydrated cells of Salmonella typhimurium LT2 at high temperatures. Journal of Applied Bacteriology 68(3): 241–246. 





Rychlik I, Barrow PA. 2005. Salmonella stress management and its relevance to behaviour during intestinal colonisation and infection. FEMS Microbiology Reviews 29(5): 1021–1040. 



Exposure of pathogenic and nonpathogenic foodborne bacteria to sublethal stresses may, in some cases, lead to cell elongation as bacteria stop the normal process of cell division. The resulting cells contain several nucleoids evenly spaced throughout the filamentous cells without septa. The formation of filamentous cells appears to be a reversible stress response. From a food safety perception, cell filamentation may result in underestimation of the real number of bacteria (Jones et al., 2013). Cell filamentation in E. coli and Salmonella occurs upon exposure to stresses, such as hyperosmotic, acidic, alkali, and low-temperature conditions (Kieboom et al., 2006; Mattick et al., 2000, 2003; Pratt et al., 2012; Stackhouse et al., 2012). Filamentation occurs in E. coli by repressing the activity of the Fts proteins essential for septum formation. During cell division, FtsZ is the first protein to move to the division site, and it is essential for recruiting other proteins that produce a new cell wall between the dividing cells (Dai and Lutkenhaus, 1991). Cell filamentation also occurs under conditions of a low-water-activity environment in various S. enterica serovars (Kieboom et al., 2006; Mattick et al., 2000). The filamentous cells demonstrate higher resistance to sodium hypochlorite (Kieboom et al., 2006), commonly used in the food industry, as well as greater survival in 10% bile salts after 24 to 48 h of incubation, during pH 2.0 acid challenges for 10 min, and under desiccation on stainless steel surfaces at 25 ◦C and 75.5% relative humidity for 7 days (Stackhouse et al., 2012). Since filamentation is not always observed during Salmonella desiccation (see, e.g., Gruzdev et al., 2012b), it is possible that this process requires certain stress conditions, or it is restricted to specific strains. Nevertheless, filamentation seems to serve as an additional mechanism enabling stressed Salmonella strains to exert cross-tolerance to other stresses. The formation of filamentous cells seems to be independent of rpoS expression (Mattic et al., 2000; Pratt et al., 2012), yet the exact mechanisms involved in this adaptation response are still not known.

## 12.4.6 Summary



Beney L, Gervais P. 2001. Influence of the fluidity of the membrane on the response of microorganisms to environmental stresses. Applied Microbiology and Biotechnology 57(1–2): 34–42.

Although much is known regarding stress response mechanisms in E. coli and Salmonella, we are still far from understanding the complex stress response mechanisms that control the ability of desiccated Salmonella cells to mount cross-tolerance to other stresses. Nevertheless, based on our current knowledge, it is possible to predict at least some of the underlying mechanisms. Among these, the sigma factors RpoS and RpoE are likely to have a central role. Several recent transcriptomic studies have begun to shed light on numerous other genes of different functional groups that are likely involved in desiccation tolerance of Salmonella strains. These studies have begun to shed light on the nature and potential role of specific genes, transcriptional regulators, sigma factors, and two-component regulatory systems in mounting cross-tolerance to desiccated cells as well. However, none of these studies has directly focused on understanding the cross-tolerance phenomenon. Future studies should harness transcriptomics, proteomics, and metabolomics approaches, using specific strains and model systems, in order to elucidate genes and mechanisms that participate in the development of cross-protection following exposure of Salmonella strains to desiccation. With the advent of new experimental approaches, such as messenger RNA (mRNA) sequencing and the availability of diverse data sources and bioinformatics tools, it is anticipated that it will be possible to unravel the underlying mechanism of desiccation stress cross-tolerance. This knowledge is essential for designing new strategies and tools to fight this persistent pathogen throughout the food production chain, and to lower the burden of salmonellosis worldwide.

## Heat shock responses

# Heat shock response in bacteria with large genomes: lessons from rhizobia

## Ana Alexandre and Solange Oliveira

Instituto de Ciencias Agrˆ arias e Ambientais Mediterr´ anicas (ICAAM), Instituto de Investigacˆ ¸ao e Formac˜ ¸ao Avanc˜ ¸ada (IIFA), Universidade de Evora, ´ Evora, Portugal ´ 

## 13.1.1 Introduction

Prokaryotes with large genomes have been intriguing microbiologists for a long time. Large genomes have the obvious advantage of accommodating wider metabolic diversity and environmental adaptability. This seems particularly advantageous in environments where nutrients are scarce and conditions are variable, as in the case of soil. Nevertheless, a large genome implies higher energetic costs upon cell division, since a larger amount of DNA has to be replicated, even if transcription and translation could be reduced to more essential genes. One of the strategies for reducing the time for replicating a large genome is the division of the DNA by several replicons that may replicate at the same time. 

A recent study that analyses soil bacteria habitat breadth shows that genome size is positively correlated with the number of sites where a given taxon can be found, suggesting that metabolically more diverse soil bacteria have wider environmental ranges (Barberan´ et al., 2014). Accordingly, most of the largest bacterial genomes described so far are from soil bacteria, as for example the 14.7 Mb genome of Sorangium cellulosum (Han et al., 2013). 

Rhizobia are soil bacteria usually with quite large genomes that often include large plasmids, as for example the 2.4 Mb plasmid pNGR234b of Sinorhizobium fredii NGR234 (Schmeisser et al., 2009). Rhizobial genomes, most of which vary between 6.5 and 9 Mb, accommodate all the “tools” required for their lifestyle, both saprophytes and plant symbionts. Rhizobia are able to establish symbiosis with legume plants and metabolize atmospheric N by converting it into N-compounds that plants can take up and use. These symbioses include important legume species for food and forage, as for example bean, soybean, chickpea, and alfalfa. Rhizobia enter the plant cells, differentiate, and lead to the development of specialized structures, the root nodules, in which biological nitrogen fixation takes place (Oldroyd and Downie, 2004). 

Plant growth is often limited by nitrogen availability, yet the extensive use of chemical N fertilizers in agriculture has significant production costs and negative consequences for the environment (Jensen et al., 2012). Thus, rhizobia–legume symbioses represent an important environmentally friendly way of supplying N to agricultural systems. 

Highly efficient rhizobia able to tolerate environmental stresses are surely of major interest, not only due to growing promotion of sustainable crop production (a Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO) concern) but also due to global concerns on the effects of climate change. 

As soil bacteria, rhizobia live in a dynamic and challenging environment that can rapidly change. As plant symbionts, rhizobia must go through several stages, from infection of the plant root to nodule maturation, and have to adjust to the particular conditions of life inside the nodule. Therefore, it is to be expected that rhizobia are able to endure challenging environmental conditions, and this resilience may rely on particular molecular mechanisms enabling an adequate stress response. 

The heat shock response can be described as the set of alterations in the cell caused by a sudden temperature upshift. This stress response has been investigated in many different organisms, especially at the transcriptional level. The major consequences of temperature increase is protein unfolding and aggregation, with the concomitant loss of function (Richter et al., 2010). 

The heat shock response has been extensively studied in Escherichia coli, and to some degree this knowledge may be extended to other organisms, since some temperature-induced genes in E. coli encode important heat shock proteins (HSPs) that are highly conserved from bacteria to humans (Guisbert et al., 2008). These HSPs are mostly chaperones, such as DnaK– DnaJ or GroES–GroEL, and proteases, as for example Lon or FtsH (see Section 14). Chaperone systems act as folding modulators, as their main function is to assist unfolded proteins to acquire their native conformation (Hartl and Hayer-Hartl, 

2009). This process is important not only for refolding of stressdenatured proteins but also for the de novo folding of newly synthesised proteins, and that is the reason why chaperones are also fundamental under standard growth conditions. Other important proteins involved in the heat shock response are the small heat shock proteins (sHSPs), such as IbpA and IbpB. The role of sHSPs is to prevent irreversible aggregation of denatured proteins, so they act as holdases, but they also facilitate the transfer of these substrates to the major chaperone systems for refolding (Ratajczak et al., 2009). 

In E. coli, the major regulator of the heat shock response is the sigma factor $\sigma ^ { 3 2 }$ , encoded by the rpoH gene. This transcription factor directs RNA polymerase to a particular set of promoters, in order to induce the expression of the genes involved in the heat response (Arsene` et al., 2000). 

As mentioned here, the heat shock response is well characterized in E. coli, which is phylogenetically not so distant from rhizobia. However, these legume-symbiotic bacteria have a particular lifestyle and genomic features which make them particularly interesting for the investigation of the stress response. Rhizobial genomes typically encode a high number of alternative sigma factors, including the common presence of multiple rpoH copies (which encode $\bar { \sigma } ^ { 3 2 }$ , the major regulator of the heat shock response in E. coli). The occurrence of multiple copies is also true for other important genes in the heat shock response, as is the case of the chaperones groEL and groES or the sHSP genes. 

The study of the heat shock response in rhizobia can contribute to the development of strains highly tolerant to more adverse environmental conditions, since it is known that temperature affects not only the saprophytic life of these bacteria but also the nodulation and nitrogen fixation processes (reviewed in Alexandre and Oliveira, 2013). 

The present chapter reviews the current knowledge on the molecular bases of heat shock response in rhizobia, with particular emphasis on the transcriptomics of this stress response. 

## 13.1.2 Rhizobia: large genome bacteria with a dual lifestyle

The soil is an environment where nutrients are frequently scarce, and the ability to use different nutrient sources is likely to be advantageous. Rhizobia are free-living soil bacteria that, in the presence of a compatible legume species, may colonize the root and initiate the symbiosis. 

A successful legume–rhizobia symbiosis requires a complex molecular dialog between the two partners, in order to recognize each other and initiate the infection process (Oldroyd, 2013). These signalling pathways involve the perception of plant flavonoids, which are an important component of root exudates, by the rhizobium; and, upon recognition of the appropriate flavonoid, rhizobia will synthesize specific Nod factors which will in turn be recognized by the legume host. This early rhizobia–legume interaction is crucial in most symbioses and determinant for host specificity. Some rhizobia are remarkably specific and are only able to induce nodule formation on a very limited number of host species or cultivars, as for example chickpea mesorhizobia (Laranjo et al., 2014b). Other rhizobia strains have a broad host range and may establish nitrogen-fixing symbioses with several legume species, as is the case of Sinorhizobium fredii NGR234 (Schmeisser et al., 2009). The rhizobial symbiosis genes include not only the genes directly involved in the nodulation process (nod, nol, and noe genes) but also the genes required for the nitrogen fixation process, such as nif and fix genes. In contrast, infection and nodule organogenesis also relies on the activation of many plant genes involved in the mechanisms that will allow the development of nitrogen-fixing bacteroids (reviewed in Oldroyd et al., 2011). 

In terms of taxonomy, rhizobia are polyphyletic, and at least 14 different genera have been described, mostly belonging to the Alphaproteobacteria class, with a few genera belonging to the Betaproteobacteria class (Moulin et al., 2014). It is noteworthy that these symbiotic bacteria are closely related to bacterial species with a completely different lifestyle, namely animal and plant pathogens. This is one of the reasons why rhizobia–plant associations are such interesting systems for the study of mutualistic evolution as well as prokaryote–eukaryote interactions. 

Similarly to other soil bacteria, rhizobia typically have large genomes that very often include multiple plasmids (MacLean et al., 2007). For example, the genome of Rhizobium etli CFN42 has seven replicons (one chromosome and six plasmids) (Gonzalez´ et al., 2006), whereas Sinorhizobium meliloti 1021 has three replicons (one chromosome and two plasmids) (Galibert et al., 2001). Both these strains have their symbiosis genes located on plasmids (pSym), yet in some rhizobia these genes are located in a chromosomal region designated as a symbiosis island, as in the case of Mesorhizobium loti MAFF303099 (Kaneko et al., 2000). 

The complexity of rhizobial genomes led to the definition of two main genome components: the core genome, which is mostly chromosomal, usually shows higher GC content and is shared by closely related organisms; and the accessory genome, usually encoded in plasmids or chromosomal islands, typically shows lower GC content and encodes genes more related to a lifestyle that may confer an adaptive advantage (Young et al., 2006). Nevertheless, some bacteria, including several rhizobia species, harbour large plasmids that encode core genes. The term chromid was proposed to designate these replicons that share some chromosomal and plasmidic characteristics (Harrison et al., 2010). 

The metabolic diversity of rhizobia is remarkable. Besides being diazotrophic, some rhizobia are photosynthetic (Giraud et al., 2007), and others are methylotrophic (Sy et al., 2001). Even the ability to nodulate the legume hosts relies on such varied strategies that in fact there is no gene simultaneously common and specific to rhizobia (Masson-Boivin et al., 2009). 

The fast-increasing number of sequenced rhizobial genomes is generating a more representative dataset of these large and complex genomes, not only in terms of species diversity but also in terms of host range. There are currently several genome sequences available for the main rhizobial genera, and the number of genomes in the scaffold state is even higher. The genomes of rhizobia belonging to less common genera from the Betaproteobacteria class, such as Burkholderia phymatum or Cupriavidus taiwanensis, have also been published (Amadou et al., 2008; Moulin et al., 2014). This large amount of data renders more reliability to genomic comparisons and allows the study of rhizobial genome evolution. 

Comparative genomics is a very useful tool to search for distinct features that could be related to symbiotic performance or saprophytic competence. For example, a recent study compared the genomes of 27 strains from two rhizobial genera that nodulate soybean, Bradyrhizobium and Sinorhizobium (Tian et al., 2012). This study showed that despite the fact that soybean bradyrhizobia share a much larger average genome size (9.8 Mb) than the soybean sinorhizobia (6.6 Mb), the core genome of the bradyrhizobia strains is smaller. In addition, the accumulation curve of these genomes showed that the diversity of genes comprised in these genomes is very large and the number of genomes analysed is insufficient to represent the pan-genome of these genera. This indicates that additional genome sequencing will certainly reveal more accessory genes. Another comparative genomics study analysed the genomes of 50 Sinorhizobium strains, including five species with different host ranges, and found that the core genome of this genus comprises only 8% of the 34,150 orthologue clusters identified (Sugawara et al., 2013), confirming that a massive proportion of accessory genome is common in rhizobia. 

The plasticity, adaptative potential, and ecological advantages that complex and multipartite genomes might confer have been recognized in rhizobia (Masson-Boivin et al., 2009). At the level of stress response, this genomic complexity probably contributes to more complex mechanisms employed by rhizobia to overcome stress, as compared to other bacteria. 

## 13.1.3 Effects of temperature stress on rhizobia–legume symbioses

Temperature stress influences not only rhizobial survival in the soil but also nodulation and nitrogen fixation (reviewed in Alexandre and Oliveira, 2013). 

The optimum temperature for most rhizobia varies between 25 and $3 1 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Somasegaran and Hoben, 1994). Nevertheless, soil temperature frequently exceeds $4 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ in the tropics or in arid and semi-arid regions; therefore, the study of how high temperatures influence rhizobial growth as well as the biological nitrogen fixation itself is extremely important (Hungria and Vargas, 2000; Rahmani et al., 2009). 

Several screening studies of rhizobial tolerance to temperature stress sampled geographical locations with more extreme temperatures, with the purpose of finding highly tolerant isolates that would render a more efficient nitrogen fixation under adverse conditions (Cloutier et al., 1992; Karanja and Wood, 1988; Rahmani et al., 2009). For example, soybean bradyrhizobia able to grow at $4 1 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ and with high symbiotic effectiveness under heat stress have been obtained from soils collected in high-temperature, semi-arid regions (Rahmani et al., 2009). Although rhizobia tolerant to high temperatures have been obtained using this approach, the temperature of the geographical origin does not always correlate with the isolates’ ability to endure temperature stress. For example, tolerance to heat of chickpea mesorhizobia seems to be unrelated to the origin site temperatures (Alexandre and Oliveira, 2011; Maatallah ˆ et al., 2002). 

The effects of high temperature in the rhizobia–legume symbioses begin in the very early steps of nodulation, since adherence of rhizobia to root hairs as well as the infection thread formation are inhibited by heat conditions (reviewed in Hungria and Vargas, 2000). The nodule functioning is also affected by high temperatures, with final constraints in the nitrogen fixed and provided to the host. The nitrogenase activity decreases, and nodule senescence is accelerated by heat (Hungria and Vargas, 2000). 

It is clear that tolerance to stress is highly diverse among rhizobia species and strains. Nevertheless, several studies have shown that the symbiotic performance of the isolates at high temperatures is correlated with their heat tolerance screened in culture media (Kishinevsky et al., 1992; Kulkarni and Nautiyal, 1999; Rahmani et al., 2009). Accordingly, performing surveys of native isolates is probably the most appropriate approach for the identification of rhizobia strains that are highly tolerant to temperature stress. 

## 13.1.4 Global transcriptional response to heat shock

Rhizobia have a dual lifestyle – as soil bacteria and plant symbionts – that naturally comprises diverse challenges to be overcome in order to survive. To adapt to these changes, a series of global regulatory networks manage the simultaneous control of a large number of genes. 

To our knowledge, there are currently three rhizobial species, from different genera, which have been investigated in terms of global transcriptional analysis of the heat shock response: Sinorhizobium meliloti (Barnett et al., 2012; Sauviac et al., 2007), Mesorhizobium loti (Alexandre et al., 2014), and Rhizobium etli (Lopez-Leal´ et al., 2014). These species have different genome size and composition, and they nodulate different legume hosts (Table 13.1.1). 

Rhizobia species contain among the largest genomes studied in terms of response to heat shock. As previously reported, the comparison of the number of genes over- and underexpressed in response to heat shock, using diverse species with a wide range of genome sizes and different lifestyles, suggests as a general trend a pronounced increase in the proportion of underexpressed genes with the genome size (Alexandre et al., 2014). Figure 13.1.1 shows that in the case of M. loti, the largest genome plotted, the number of downregulated genes in response to heat shock is more than twice the number of upregulated genes. For the remaining rhizobia species, with genomes approximately 1 Mb smaller, the number of downregulated genes is either smaller than or roughly the same as the number of upregulated genes in the heat shock response (for R. etli and S. meliloti, respectively).

Large genomes are particularly enriched in genes involved in regulation and secondary metabolism, which may confer advantage in ecological niches where the nutrient sources are scarce and varied (Konstantinidis and Tiedje, 2004). A large accessory genome is a common feature in rhizobia (Young et al., 2006), and pan-genome studies showed that the diversity of this accessory component of the genome is much larger than that of the core genome (Sugawara et al., 2013; Tian et al., 2012). The extensive gene downregulation reported for the heat shock response in M. loti could represent a way to reduce secondary metabolism and direct the cell machinery and energy to the synthesis of stress response proteins (Alexandre et al., 2014).

Note: In case of multiple heat shock transcriptome datasets for the same species, the dataset with the largest number of differentially expressed genes was chosen. Adapted from Alexandre A, Laranjo M, and Oliveira S. (2014) DNA Research 21:2, 195–206, doi:10.1093/dnares/dst050


S. meliloti is probably the most studied rhizobium species, including its stress response. The transcriptional responses to two heat shock conditions were analysed: a longer shock of 30 min at 40 ◦C and a shorter shock of 15 min at 42 ◦C (Barnett et al., 2012; Sauviac et al., 2007). The number of genes differentially expressed in response to these two shocks is very different, with a much higher number of genes both over- and underexpressed in the case of the shorter shock at higher temperature. These results point out the relevance of the stress conditions applied in transcriptional studies and show how different stress conditions (temperature, shock duration, and growth conditions) may shape the response. Nevertheless, the large majority of the genes differentially expressed in the lower temperature response were also detected as altered in the higher temperature response (Barnett et al., 2012). 

There is an overrepresentation of plasmid-encoded genes in the set of genes upregulated in response to heat shock in the three rhizobia species studied, yet the involvement of these replicons is much more expressive in the heat shock response of R. etli. In the case of R. etli, only approximately 32% of the protein-coding genes are encoded in the six plasmids of this strain; however, 59% of the upregulated genes after the heat shock are plasmid encoded (Lopez-Leal ´ et al., 2014). In addition, this study also pointed out that the contribution of the different replicons was distinct in the case of saline shock (see Section 11). 

All of the above-mentioned global transcriptional reports showed that the response to heat shock in rhizobia shares with other bacteria the upregulation of genes encoding important heat shock proteins, such as GroEL, DnaK, IbpA, and ClpB. Furthermore, in the case of HSPs that are encoded in multiple copies in rhizobial genomes, as is the case of GroEL and sHSP, there is a high degree of homology among heat shock–inducible copies in each genome. For example, the only groEL gene that was upregulated in the heat shock response in M. loti (which encodes three more groEL copies) shares the highest similarity with precisely the two groEL genes also responsive to heat shock in S. meliloti and R. etli (which encode a total of five and four groEL copies, respectively). 

Interestingly, the study of the transcriptional response of these strains to other stresses, namely saline and acidic shock (see Sections 11 and 16), shows that the upregulation of many chaperone genes and genes encoding other HSPs seems to be restricted to the heat shock response (Dom´ınguez-Ferreras et al., 2006; Hellweg et al., 2009; Laranjo et al., 2014a; Lopez-Leal ´ et al., 2014). 

Despite the fact that the genes overexpressed in M. loti in response to heat shock are widely distributed in terms of cluster(s) of orthologous group (COG(s)) categories, there are some COGs in common with the ones upregulated in the R. etli heat shock response, namely the COGs G-carbohydrate transport and metabolism, and K-transcription (Alexandre et al., 2014; Lopez-Leal´ et al., 2014). 

Rhizobial genomes usually encode a large number of alternative sigma factors. Sigma factors play a major role in transcriptional regulation, since they direct the RNA polymerase to a given set of genes, representing a rapid way of responding to alterations in the cell conditions (Fekl´ıstov et al., 2014; see Chapter 2.4 and Section 4). The rpoH gene is the major regulator in E. coli response to heat shock (Arsene ` et al., 2000). 

In rhizobia, multiple copies of the rpoH gene have been studied, and they seem to regulate responses to different stresses (Martinez-Salazar et al., 2009). In R. etli, RpoH1 is more involved in the response to heat and oxidative stresses, while RpoH2 is required for survival to osmotic stress. In S. meliloti, RpoH1 is also involved in heat tolerance as well as in acidic pH survival, while rpoH2 mutants have no major consequences on growth or stress tolerance (de Lucena et al., 2010; Ono et al., 2001). The expression of important heat shock proteins is rpoH1 dependent in S. meliloti (Barnett et al., 2012). However, the heat shock transcriptome showed upregulation of the rpoH2 gene, but not of rpoH1. This is consistent with the fact that the regulation of rpoH is known to be carried out mostly at the posttranscriptional level (Arsene ` et al., 2000). 

The M. loti heat shock response shows that four of the 25 putative sigma factors are heat inducible, yet none of these genes shares high homology with rpoH (Alexandre et al., 2014). In S. meliloti, five sigma factor–encoding genes were found to be heat inducible, whereas in R. etli the expression of many transcriptional regulators was upregulated in response to heat, yet no sigma factor was detected as heat shock inducible (Barnett et al., 2012; Lopez-Leal ´ et al., 2014). The only sigma factor upregulated in common between S. meliloti and M. loti is a rpoE copy, previously described as a general stress response regulator in the former species (Sauviac et al., 2007) (see Chapter 4.7). 

Rhizobial genomes encode a large number of adenosine triphosphate (ATP)-binding cassette (ABC) transporters (Young et al., 2006). These transport systems are involved in the flux of a wide variety of substrates, which include sugars, amino acids, and metal ions. The involvement of ABC transporters in stress tolerance has already been reported for rhizobia, especially during osmotic stress (Alloing et al., 2006). The upregulation of a large number of ABC transporter genes has been reported not only in response to acidity (Laranjo et al., 2014a) and salinity (Lopez-Leal´ et al., 2014), but also in the heat shock transcriptome of R. etli (Lopez-Leal´ et al., 2014) and M. loti (Alexandre et al., 2014). Their precise role in the heat shock response requires further investigation. 

In addition to the many protein-coding genes differentially expressed in response to heat shock, Lopez-Leal and collabora- ´ tors (2014) also reported eight heat-inducible noncoding RNAs (ncRNAs; see Section 5) in R. etli. Although these ncRNAs were already known (Vercruysse et al., 2010), further studies are required to understand their role in the stress response. 

Heat shock proteins have also been studied regarding their involvement in the symbiosis. Mutants in genes encoding important HSPs, such as DnaJ or ClpB, are affected in their symbiotic phenotype, most likely due to the role of these proteins in the folding of newly synthesized polypeptides or refolding of denatured proteins involved in the symbiosis (Br´ıgido et al., 2012; Labidi et al., 2000). The most studied chaperone in terms of its involvement in the symbiosis is GroEL. Particular copies of this chaperone, usually upregulated in the bacteroids, seem to play a fundamental role in the formation of functional NodD and the nitrogenase complex (Fischer et al., 1999; Ogawa and Long, 1995). Other genes involved in stress response, namely major regulators of the heat shock response, have been implicated in the symbiosis. For instance, rpoH mutants have also been shown to have defective symbiotic phenotypes (Mart´ınez-Salazar et al., 2009; Ono et al., 2001). 

## 13.1.5 Concluding remarks



in Bradyrhizobium japonicum. Archives of Microbiology 171(4): 279–289. 





Rahmani H, Saleh-Rastin N, Khavazi K, Asgharzadeh A, Fewer D, et al. 2009. Selection of thermotolerant bradyrhizobial strains for nodulation of soybean (Glycine max L.) in semi-arid regions of Iran. World Journal of Microbiology and Biotechnology 25(4): 591–600. 



Despite the fact that the three rhizobia species, so far investigated in terms of global transcriptional response to heat shock, are distinct in terms of genera, genome organization, and host range, these studies are still insufficient to fully understand the molecular mechanisms of rhizobial response to heat shock. Nevertheless, some common trends and particularities of the heat shock response in these bacteria were identified and discussed. 



Galibert F, Finan TM, Long SR, Puhler A, Abola P,¨ et al. 2001. The composite genome of the legume symbiont Sinorhizobium meliloti. Science 293(5530): 668–672.

Further gene-specific studies as well as global transcriptional analysis of other rhizobial species, including the comparison between heat-tolerant and heat-sensitive strains, are required to allow a more extensive understanding of the heat shock response. The mutational analysis of specific genes, particularly those upregulated upon heat shock, as well as target genes of major stress response regulators, is an important task yet to be accomplished to better comprehend the heat shock response in rhizobia.
