## Martin Haslbeck

Department Chemie, Technische Universitat M¨ unchen, Garching, Germany ¨ 

## 13.2.1 The evolution of sHsps

In the course of evolution, a network of proteins evolved, effectively protecting the cell against stress conditions (e.g., heat) (cf. Richter et al., 2010). The most prominent group of these stress proteins is represented by molecular chaperones (see Section 14), which are sorted into several families according to their molecular mass and evolutionary background (Bukau et al., 2006; Lindquist and Craig, 1988; Walter and Buchner, 2002). Small heat shock proteins (sHsps), which are present in all three domains of life, represent the most poorly conserved of these families of molecular chaperones (DeJong et al., 1993; Kriehuber et al., 2010). Phylogenetic analyses of sHsps indicate that they were already present in the last common ancestor of pro- and eukaryotes (Figure 13.2.1) (Kappe et al., 2002; Kriehuber et al., 2010; Waters et al., 1996). Prokaryotes usually contain only one or two sHsps (Kappe et al., 2002; Kriehuber et al., 2010; Narberhaus, 2002). However, a few mostly pathogenic bacteria do not encode sHsps, and in some (often symbiotic) living bacteria the number of sHsps reaches up to 12 different proteins (Figure 13.2.1) (Kappe et al., 2002; Kriehuber et al., 2010; Narberhaus, 2002). In comparison, in most multicellular eukaryotes, the number of sHsps is generally increased (Haslbeck et al., 2005a; Kriehuber et al., 2010); for example, at least 10 different sHsps are encoded in the human genome, and more than 20 sHsps are usually found in plants (Figure 13.2.1) (Kappe et al., 2003; Scharf et al., 2001). Analysis of the evolutionary history of sHsps further showed that especially bacterial sHsps are rather diverse. While the sHsp members of other phyla usually cluster together in specific branches of the phylogenetic tree, bacterial sHsp sequences are spread across the tree (Figure 13.2.1b) (Kriehuber et al., 2010). 

## 13.2.2 The structure of sHsps

sHsps are adenosine triphosphate (ATP)-independent molecular chaperones that act within the proteostasis network of the cell as a kind of buffer system. They stabilize labile, aggregation-prone proteins and protect unfolding proteins from irreversible aggregation. They keep their substrates in a foldingcompetent state until their refolding by other ATP-dependent chaperone systems (Basha et al., 2012; Delbecq and Klevit, 2013; Garrido et al., 2012; Haslbeck et al., 2005a, 2015). In this context, the two Escherichia coli sHsps, IbpA and IbpB (sharing 48% sequence identity), are very prominent family members as they are deposited together with their substrates in so-called inclusion bodies (IBs), which are also name giving for these respective sHsps (Ibp = inclusion body protein) (Allen et al., 1992). These IBs are also frequently observed upon overexpression of recombinant protein, which also represents a major proteotoxic stress situation for bacteria. The most prominent examples for sHsps, however, are the alpha crystallins that are highly abundant in the lens of the vertebrate eye, where they inhibit the aggregation of other lenticular proteins, preventing aggregateinduced scattering in the light path (Horwitz, 2003; Slingsby et al., 2013). 

The most striking feature of most sHsps is their ability to assemble into large oligomeric structures of a unique hierarchal arrangement mediated by different protein domains at different levels (Delbecq and Klevit, 2013; Garrido et al., 2012; Haslbeck et al., 2005a, 2015; Haslbeck and Vierling, 2015). The primary structure of sHsps can be dissected into a variable Nterminal sequence, a conserved α-crystallin domain, and a variable C-terminal extension (Figure 13.2.2a) (Caspers et al., 1995; De Jong et al., 1998; Kriehuber et al., 2010). The α-crystallin domain (or Hsp20 domain) represents the conserved signature motif of sHsps. This domain is on average 94 amino acids long (Kriehuber et al., 2010) and forms a compact β-sheet sandwich similar to the immunoglobulin fold (Figure 13.2.2b). α- crystallin domains usually form stable dimers, which seem to be the basic building block and the first level of internal order in the assembly of the oligomers (Bagneris et al., 2009; Baranova et al., 2011; Clark et al., 2011; Delbecq and Klevit, 2013; Laganowsky et al., 2010). The dimerization in bacterial sHsps occurs via reciprocal strand swapping of the β6 strands into the β-sandwich of the neighboring monomers (Figure 13.2.2b) (Bepperling et al., 2012; Hanazono et al., 2013; Hilario et al., 2011; Kim et al., 1998; van Montfort et al., 2001). The highly variable N- and C-terminal sequences flanking the α-crystallin domain are essential for the assembling of higher oligomers.

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/d070d93a-9a38-4d08-ab41-83365fc0221e/1d89d86057f62afc4ebf16f7a1f09ef1cf891a1a9776b19cb5468cf7b55f6435.jpg)



Figure 13.2.1 (a) Analysis of the number of sHsps per bacterial genome (Kriehuber et al., 2010). The distribution of pathogenicity (dark blue) and nonpathogenicity (blue) is indicated. (b) Phylogenetic tree of the evolution of the α-crystallin domain (Kriehuber et al., 2010). The taxonomic groups are color-coded and labeled with their names. The localization of the sHsps from E. coli and D. radiodurans is indicated. From Kriehuber et al. (2010). Reproduced with permission of The FASEB Journal.


Another key feature of sHsps is their tendency to form a polydisperse ensemble of different oligomeric states, which are in equilibrium and constantly exchange subunits (cf. Haslbeck et al., 2015). For example, E. coli IbpB and IbpA present complex mixtures ranging from dimers to roughly 50-mers or even fibrils, and it is still enigmatic whether any major species exist in these ensembles (Matuszewska et al., 2005; Ratajczak et al., 2010; Shearstone and Baneyx, 1999). 

## 13.2.3 The chaperone activity of sHsps

It has been shown for many sHsps from different species that they bind denatured proteins and prevent them from irreversible aggregation (Figure 13.2.3) (Basha et al., 2004b; De Jong et al., 1993; Haslbeck et al., 1999; Horwitz, 1992; Ingolia and Craig, 

1982; Jakob et al., 1993; Lee et al., 1997; Mchaourab et al., 2002; Studer and Narberhaus, 2000) (see Section 14). sHsps fulfill their task as molecular chaperones by stabilizing early unfolding intermediates of aggregation-prone proteins, which arise upon diverse stress conditions (e.g., temperature). These early unfolding intermediates can often dissociate from the sHsps and refold spontaneously (Bepperling et al., 2012). When the unfolding is too severe, many sHsps bind their substrate proteins tightly and form stable complexes (Figure 13.2.3) (Basha et al., 2012; Bova et al., 2002; Garrido et al., 2012; Haslbeck et al., 2005a; Horwitz, 1992; Mchaourab et al., 2009; Stengel et al., 2010). Thus, within the proteostasis network of the cell, sHsps function as a buffer system to bind and complex unfolding proteins under stress and to protect them from irreversible aggregation (Figure 13.2.3). In vitro experiments revealed that the nonnative proteins trapped in sHsp–substrate complexes remain folding-competent, and additionally present ATP-dependent chaperones can trigger the release of the substrate (Figure 13.2.3). 

For bacteria such as E. coli, it has been demonstrated that bound nonnative proteins are specifically transferred to the

Figure 13.2.3 Scheme of the chaperone function of sHsps in bacteria. Upon stress conditions, sHsps bind partially unfolded proteins (I) in an energy-independent manner and keep them in a folding-competent state. The substrate is stabilized and might reactivate spontaneously or is captured in stable sHps–substrate complexes. The bound substrates are subsequently refolded by the ATP-dependent DnaK–DnaJ–GrpE and/or ClpB chaperone systems.


DnaK–DnaJ–GrpE chaperone system and subsequently reactivated (Bepperling et al., 2012; Mogk et al., 2003; Ratajczak et al., 2009; Veinger et al., 1998). However, this reactivation mechanism appears to be largely dependent on the ratio of sHsp to substrate, and the Hsp70–Hsp40 system alone is only effective in refolding the substrate proteins if sHsps are present at stoichiometric or excess concentrations under which soluble, defined sHsp–substrate complexes form. At excess levels of the substrate protein, sHsps incorporate into aggregates of the substrate protein. The refolding of substrates from these aggregate-like sHsp–substrate complexes is achieved via a conserved mechanism involving DnaK–DnaJ–GrpE and ClpB in E. coli (Bepperling et al., 2012; Cashikar et al., 2005; Haslbeck et al., 2005b; Mogk et al., 2003; Ratajczak et al., 2009). Dissecting the specific functions of the two E. coli sHsps, it was demonstrated that IbpA reduces the size of the substrate complexes but inhibits their further processing by disaggregases (like ClpB), while IbpB facilitates substrate transfer to the DnaK–DnaJ–GrpE and ClpB machinery (Ratajczak et al., 2009; Strozecka´ et al., 2012). 

Taken together, these results suggest that sHsps provide organisms with the capability to separate the prevention of aggregation from ATP-dependent refolding steps. Furthermore, the incorporation of sHsps into nonnative substrate aggregates is necessary for efficient refolding of the substrates (Figure 13.2.3). 

It is still enigmatic how sHsps recognize proteins as substrate. Overall, they act very promiscuous, and several proteomic approaches in different organisms showed that a significant number of cytosolic proteins are maintained soluble by sHsps under heat shock conditions (Basha et al., 2004a; Bepperling et al., 2012; Fu et al., 2013; Haslbeck et al., 2015). Interestingly, a preference for translation-related proteins (e.g. ribosomal proteins and amino-acyl transfer RNA (tRNA) synthases) 

and metabolic enzymes has been observed in bacteria (Bepperling et al., 2012; Fu, 2014; Fu et al., 2013). 

The majority of sHsps requires the dissociation of the oligomer and, in consequence, the remodeling of the ensemble composition for the efficient recognition and binding of substrate proteins (cf. Haslbeck et al., 2005a, 2015). Dissociation leads to an ensemble with a higher content of smaller species, correlating with the active state, whereas the inactive state shows a higher fraction of larger oligomers that seem to represent storage species (Basha et al., 2012; Delbecq and Klevit, 2013; Garrido et al., 2012; Haslbeck et al., 2005a; Mchaourab et al., 2009). A variation of this activation pathway exists in bacteria where solely dimeric sHsps exist. An example is Hsp17.7 from Deinocaldococcus radiodurans that interacts with its substrates only transiently without forming stable sHsp–substrate complexes (Bepperling et al., 2012). 

For the oligomeric members of sHsps in bacteria, three different regulatory stimuli exist, which lead to dissociation and activation. First is the presence of unfolded or partially folded substrates. Second are changes in the environmental temperature. And, third, is the formation of hetero-oligomers. However, phosphorylation or posttranslational modification as it is observed in eukaryotes does not seem to be involved in bacterial sHsp regulation (cf. Haslbeck et al., 2015). 

In particular, the first regulatory principle establishes sHsps as the cell’s first line of defense guaranteeing protein homeostasis at physiological conditions as well as under stress conditions. The dynamic assembly–disassembly behavior allows the substrate binding sites, which seem to be hidden in the oligomers, to become exposed by dissociation (Giese and Vierling, 2002; Lindner et al., 1998; Mchaourab et al., 2009; Shashidharamurthy et al., 2005; Yang et al., 1999). However, there will always be some molecules with exposed binding sites in the ensemble that are able to bind unfolding substrate proteins. This leads to a gradual shift toward more active species in the presence of unfolding substrate proteins, indicating a sensing mechanism that monitors the presence of nonnative proteins in the cellular environment (Benesch et al., 2008; Haslbeck et al., 2015; Mchaourab et al., 2009; Shi et al., 2013). 

Heat stress temperature seems to be another general trigger activating sHsps. Interestingly, the temperature range of activation is adapted to the respective physiological temperature of the respective organism (cf. Haslbeck et al., 2005a, 2015). The slight shift from physiological to heat stress temperatures, which usually comprises only a few degrees, provides sufficient activation energy to shift the equilibria toward enhanced dissociation. Temperature stress itself as an activation mechanism is additionally important on the level of translational control (Cimdins et al., 2014). Studies on messenger RNAs (mRNAs) encoding sHsps from mesophilic and thermophilic cyanobacteria as well as from Pseudomonas putida indicate “RNA thermometers” (RNATs), where internal hairpins in the 5′ -untranslated region (UTR) inhibit translation of the sHsp RNAs at physiological temperatures (Cimdins et al., 2014; Krajewski et al., 2014). At heat stress temperatures, the RNATs melt, allowing effective translation of the sHsp mRNA. Thus, bacteria seem to control the activity and amount of sHsps by heat stress (σ32)-dependent transcription, RNAT-dependent translation, and temperatureinduced dissociation and activation of the protein oligomers. 

Similar to the presence of substrate, the temperature stimulus represents a very effective and even faster trigger for the activation of sHsps when the amount of unfolded protein increases within a cell. It is tempting to speculate that this activation stimulus of sHsps might be the evolutionarily oldest regulatory mechanism. It allows the organism to rapidly stabilize its proteome without the need of new protein synthesis. 

The dynamic sHsp structure, which constantly exchanges subunits, provides the opportunity to form hetero-oligomers with other sHsps present in the cytosol of bacteria. This heterooligomer formation represents another stimulus that allows the activation of sHsps (Haslbeck et al., 2015). 

In E. coli, IbpA itself shows only low chaperone activity, but it enhances the chaperone activity of IbpB when both form a hetero-oligomer. This indicates that one sHsp is used as the modulator of the activity of a “partner” sHsp (Ratajczak et al., 2009). Even more interesting, the roles of IbpA and IbpB in terms of disaggregation of proteins seem to be different. The presence of IbpA changes the macroscopic structure of the aggregates and enhances the incorporation of IbpB into the aggregates. Thus, IbpA interacts first with the aggregating substrate and stimulates the association of IbpB. The enhanced IbpB recruitment to the aggregate in turn leads to a more efficient refolding of the substrate by the ATP-dependent chaperone systems (Ratajaczak et al., 2009; Strozecka´ et al., 2012). 

However, it is unclear if the E. coli two-component sHsp system can be generalized as the prevailing bacterial scenario. In bacteria encoding two sHsps (two-component sHsp systems), either the sHsps are close relatives (located in the same branches of the phylogenetic tree; e.g., E. coli) (Figures 13.2.1b and 13.2.4) or they are rather distantly related (located in different branches) (Bepperling et al., 2012; Kriehuber et al., 2010; Haslbeck and Vierling, 2015). The second type of two-component sHsp system was recently studied in Deinococcus radiodurans, an extremotolerant, Gram-positive bacterium (Bepperling et al., 2012). Its two-component sHsp system comprises two sHsps differing substantially in their quaternary structure and their functional role within the chaperone network. Intriguingly, the two sHsps of D. radiodurans seem to work independently of each other, and Hsp17.7 forms only chaperone active dimers while Hsp20.2 is predominantly a 36 mer. Although Hsp20.2 cooperates with the ATP-dependent chaperones, Hsp17.7 keeps substrates in a refolding-competent state solely by transient interactions (Figure 13.2.4) (Bepperling et al., 2012). 

In this context, it is of special interest that not all sHsps, simultaneously present in the same compartment, form heterooligomers or exchange subunits. For example, the sHsps from the soybean symbiont Bradyrhizobium japonicum sort into two classes, where hetero-oligomerization is restricted to the respective class (Studer and Narberhaus, 2000). Similarly, in P. putida, a tricistronic operon of three sHsps (encoded by the hspX, hspY, and hspZ genes) and a separately encoded ibpA gene exist. The four sHsps cooperate but seem to form no (or only weak) hetero-oligomers (Krajewski et al., 2014). Interestingly, this tricistronic operon was found to be conserved in environmental bacteria metabolically related to P. putida, indicating a scenario where several, rather independently acting sHsps might be quite common in bacteria. 

Furthermore, note again that many bacteria encode only one sHsp. Here, the respective sHsp commonly seems to be essential for thermotolerance or for resistance to other specific stress situations. For example, Xamthomonas campestris HspA was shown to be necessary for thermotolerance (Lin et al., 2010), and the sHsps (Hsp20) from Azotobacter vinelandii, a cystforming bacterium, is essential for cyst desiccation resistance (Cocotl-Yanez et al., 2014; see Chapter 12.2). Similarly, inactivation of the single hsp17 gene of the cyanobacterium Synechocystis sp. PCC6803 resulted in decreased thermotolerance (Giese and Vierling, 2002). Additionally, a reduced activity of the photosynthetic apparatus and disrupted integrity of thylakoid membranes have been observed (Horvath´ et al., 1998; Tor¨ ok¨ et al., 2001). This observation is indicative of a putative interaction of the bacterial sHsps with the membranes of their hosts, which might be conceivable because in bacteria changes in membrane integrity activate σ32 regulons (cf. Nakamoto and Vigh, 2007; Tor¨ ok¨ et al., 2001). However, it remains enigmatic if sHsps directly interact with the membrane or only recognize membrane-associated proteins, which unfold upon stress. 

In summary, the correlation of structural dynamics and chaperone activity of sHsps has become more evident in recent years. However, especially for bacterial sHsps, no 3D structures of complete oligomers are available so far. Furthermore, it is still enigmatic how sHsps work together in different types of component setups and which structural features define and allow the formation of hetero-oligomers.

Figure 13.2.4 Comparative model of the two component sHsp systems of E. coli and D. radiodurans. In D. radiodurans Hsp20.2 and Hsp17.7 work in parallel and independently of each other, whereas in E. coli IbpA and IbpB interact and cooperate. Oligomeric IbpA and IbpB, which are able to form hetero-oligomers, or dimeric Hsp17.7 and the oligomeric Hsp20.2 form the E. coli or D. radiodurans two-component sHsp system, respectively. Stress conditions (e.g., heat shock) lead to the unfolding of substrate proteins, which are stably trapped by the IbpA/B hetero-oligomers or the Hsp20.2 oligomers. Hsp17.7 only transiently interacts with its substrates. DnaK–DnaJ–GrpE and ClpB can reactivate the substrates bound to IbpA/B or Hsp20 in an energy-dependent manner. Substrate proteins bound to Hsp17.7 release and refold (if possible) spontaneously (Bepperling et al., 2012).

# Transcriptome analysis of bacterial response to heat shock using next-generation sequencing

Kok-Gan Chan 

Division of Genetics and Molecular Biology, Institute of Biological Sciences, Faculty of Science, University of Malaya, Kuala Lumpur, Malaysia 

## 13.3.1 Introduction

The heat shock response is widespread in bacteria (Craig, 1985). Previous studies show that the heat shock response is characterized by expression of, inter alia, heat shock proteins (Hsps) or chaperones (see Section 14) that help to stabilize cellular protein during the elevated heat. Because similar proteins are also being produced when bacteria are subjected to other abiotic stresses, these are collectively known as stress response proteins. 

Stress responses in bacteria are essential for effective adaptation to changes in various habitats. These responses are usually mediated by global regulatory mechanisms, which affect a myriad of physiological and biochemical pathways. The heat shock response is arguably the most well-studied stress response in both Gram-negative and Gram-positive bacteria. In the case of the former, the best characterized bacterium is Escherichia coli, where the induction of Hsps is positively regulated by the sigma factor, $\sigma ^ { 3 2 }$ , encoded by the rpoH gene. 

In the last few decades, by using traditional approaches, previous heat shock studies have been conducted but primarily focused on a very limited microbial cellular response, and hence a vast amount of information remains uncovered in microbial response to elevated heat. For example, study of Hsps gene has only focused on one or two genes at a given time, hence missing a global view on the genome expression profile. 

The first studies on the bacterial heat shock response have been performed in E. coli K-12 (Yura et al., 1984). It has been reported that this bacterium’s heat shock genes are under the regulation of a positive regulator termed $\sigma ^ { 3 2 } ;$ , an Hsp-specific subunit of RNA polymerase that modulates the heat shock gene activity at the transcriptional level (Bukau, 1993). The activity of $\sigma ^ { 3 2 }$ is then negatively regulated in E. coli via the binding of chaperones named DnaK, DnaJ, and GrpE (Gamer et al., 1996). These Hsps then help to stabilize by preventing over-aggregation and wrong folding of bacterial proteins when exposed to elevated temperature, thus preserving the homeostasis of cellular proteins and maintaining the cellular functions in unfavourable growth conditions. Under optimal temperature (30 ◦C), the cellular concentration of $\sigma ^ { 3 2 }$ is very low and consequently results in low expression of the heat shock genes (Arsene` et al., 2000). 

Other studies also show that whereas Hsps prevent and preserve the cellular proteins being intact, heat-denatured proteins need to be treated to prevent their accumulation in the bacteria cell, hindering the cell biochemical processes. To achieve this, proteolytic proteases, but not Hsps, play a vital role in the bacterial heat shock response. For example, proteins of the Clp family of ATPases in E. coli have been reported to be associated with the ClpP proteolytic subunit to form ATP-dependent proteases that degrade denatured protein (Derre´ et al., 1999; Gottesman, 1996) (see Chapter 14.4). 

The paradigm of the heat shock response in E. coli has been shared in most Gram-negative bacteria, but complex regulatory networks exist in other bacteria, notably the groESL operon in Bradyrhizobium japonicum (Fischer et al., 1993), and Bacillus subtilis has four distinct classes of heat-inducible genes distinguished by their regulatory mechanisms (Derre´ et al., 1999; Hecker et al., 1996). In B. subtilis and other Gram-positive bacteria, the expression of groESL and dnaK genes is negatively modulated, and the operons of B. subtilis possess an inverted repeat (consensus TTAGCACTC-N -GAGTGCTAA) (Wetzstein et al., 1992). 

In sharp contrast to eubacteria, members of the Archaea have very distinctive Hsps from those of E. coli. Interestingly, the Hsps of Archaea are more similar to those of Eukarya. For example, the Cct family is a group of chaperonins that is distinct from Hsp60/GroEL and Cct and has thus far been found only in Archaea and Eukarya (Gupta, 1995). But unlike Eukarya and Eubacteria, little is known about the mechanism of the heat shock response in Archaea. 

Collectively, it is clear that the heat shock response involves complex and comprehensive Hsps, operons, promoters, and other regulator mechanisms, which are too complicated to be analysed by traditional molecular techniques. Thus, alternative approaches are needed to address these issues, and it is desired that the new approaches should provide a comprehensive view on the global gene expression in bacteria, thus providing valuable input for downstream work such as proteome studies. 

With the advent of next-generation sequencing (NGS), a bacterial genome can be sequenced within a short timeframe and at a cost which is affordable by most laboratories working on bacterial genomics studies. Among the NGS platforms, MiSeq (Illumina, USA), Ion Torrens (Life Technologies, USA), and Pacific BioSciences (PacBio, USA) Single Molecule Real Time Sequencer (SMRT) can be used to sequence the whole genome of a typical bacterium (genome size range from 5 to 10 Mbp). The latter is particularly useful to yield the complete bacterial genome sequence. 

Typically, these NGS platforms produce a bacterial genome sequence in <40 h, and genome assembly and annotation enable the genome sequence to provide useful data on the genes and operons. 

In the late 1990s, microarrays were the choice of wholegenome studies on bacterial response to heat shock, and many bacteria have been studied using this approach, such as E. coli (Richmond et al., 1999), the phytopathogen Xylella fastidiosa (Koide et al., 2006), B. subtilis (Helmann et al., 2001), Shewanella oneidensis (Gao et al., 2004), the food pathogen Campylobacter jejuni (Stintzi, 2003), the group A Streptococcus (Smoot et al., 2001), Mycoplasma pneumoniae (Weiner et al., 2003), Yersinia pestis (Motin et al., 2004), and Neisseria meningitidis (Guckenberger et al., 2002). Although microarrays have been useful for the bacterial genomics study of heat shock response, the main drawback is the microarray chip, for which the probes used are obtained from existing known genes that may limit the discovery of novel genes in the heat shock response. Hence, RNA-Seq, which is the NGS of complementary DNA (cDNA) whole-genome sequences, can be used to address this shortcoming as RNA-Seq does not depend on any probes and literally every molecule of messenger RNA (mRNA) (converted to cDNA) can be sequenced. Thus, it is a valuable tool for the discovery of novel genes for bacterial response to a myriad of biotic and abiotic stresses. In the author’s laboratory, RNA-Seq has been used to enable heat shock transcriptome analysis of the opportunistic pathogen Pseudomonas aeruginosa (PAO1) and also phytopathogens such as Erwinia carotovora. Through this transcriptome analysis using NGS, it is demonstrated that extensive information on the global genome can be obtained by a single transcriptome analysis of the bacteria heat shock response. 

With the availability of whole-genome sequences, the global response to temperature increase can be studied at the transcriptional level in many bacteria. But several considerations must be taken into account before any transcriptome work is initiated. First, although the cost of an NGS instrument has dropped since the first launch in the 2000s, it is still a relatively expensive instrument for most laboratories, although it is now possible to outsource to an NGS service provider. Second, prior to RNA-Seq, the reference genome is needed in order to map RNA-Seq data to the reference genome. It is advisable to check the availability of the reference genome in public databases. Third, bioinformatics analysis is required, and often this is the limiting factor for biological laboratories as bioinformatics requires high-performance computers and appropriate software for gene expression analysis, gene annotation, genome assembly, and gene profile statistical analysis, among others. Fourth, after any NGS work and bioinformatics analysis, it is often desired to validate transcriptome data with the aid of, for instance, real-time polymerase chain reaction (PCR) or other molecular studies. This will ensure robustness of transcriptome analysis of bacterial genomics research in the heat shock response. 

In the author’s laboratory, we applied NGS (HiSeq 2500, Illumina) to study the heat shock response of P. aeruginosa PAO1 by comparing the transcriptome data from bacteria grown in optimal $( 3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } )$ and elevated temperatures $\left( 4 6 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } \right)$ . These RNA-Seq data are of sufficient quality to determine a pattern of gene expression, as depicted in Figure 13.3.1. In this analysis, it was found that the previously reported Hsp genes (e.g., groEL and ${ g r p E } ,$ , to name just two), heat shock gene clusters and operons $( \mathrm { e . g . } ,$ , dnaK, dnaJ, and dnaG), and even small RNAs can be detected. Selected significantly expressed genes affected by heat shock are listed in Table 13.3.1. This demonstrates the practicality and utility of transcriptome analysis of bacteria response to heat shock, and perhaps also to other abiotic shocks.

# Comparative analyses of bacterial transcriptome reorganisation in response to temperature increase

## Bei-Wen Ying1 and Tetsuya Yomo2–3

1Faculty of Life and Environmental Sciences, University of Tsukuba, Ibaraki, Japan 

2Graduate School of Information Science and Technology, Osaka University, Osaka, Japan 

3Graduate School of Frontier Biosciences, Osaka University, Osaka, Japan 

## 13.4.1 Introduction

Transcriptomic analysis is widely performed to detect specific changes in gene expression when living Escherichia coli cells encounter external perturbations (Durfee et al., 2008; Gunasekera et al., 2008; Jozefczuk et al., 2010; Rutherford et al., 2010; Tao et al., 1999) and to identify reasonable pitfalls or theories at the global gene expression level (Allen et al., 2003; Jeong et al., 2004; Passalacqua et al., 2009). In particular, studies on the stress responses to external perturbations have been performed to investigate the transcriptional changes involved in specific pathways and/or regulatory networks (Durfee et al., 2008; Lobner-Olesen et al., 2003; Traxler et al., 2008). As chromosome organisation is established during DNA replication (Niki et al., 2000; Viollier et al., 2004), the structural configuration of the chromosome is crucial for genome dynamics and plasticity (Esnault et al., 2007; Sherratt, 2003), leading to differentiation in gene expression profiles. Nevertheless, whether and how global nonspecific genetic interruptions induce genome-wide transcriptional reorganisation have rarely been examined. As chromosome organisation could be biased by the gene expression level (Rocha, 2004), structural changes due to genome reduction may disturb the sequential order and speed of genome organisation, leading to a restructured expression profile. Therefore, our studies on the bacterial stress response transcriptome largely focus on the comparison between different types of perturbations (i.e., heat shock response and genomic disturbance). 

Furthermore, high-throughput analyses more often provide conclusions limited to quantitative extension of the known molecular mechanisms. Regulatory mechanisms associated with gene expression and stress conditions have been intensively reported, such as the heat shock–induced rpoH regulation (Durfee et al., 2008; Tilly et al., 1986), the general stress response triggered by rpoS (Durfee et al., 2008; Hengge-Aronis, 2002), the stringent response mediated by ppGpp (Durfee et al., 2008; Traxler et al., 2008) (see Section 7), and so on. These wellknown mechanisms generally consist of multiple genes in regulatory networks of varied sizes. Indeed, a certain stress response induced by a certain environmental change not only causes changes in gene expression but also arrests cell growth. That is, the magnitude of transcriptional changes is usually linked to the growth physiology. As any type of fluctuation could initiate a global change in gene expression, a comparative analysis focussing on extracting the potential common features among different transcriptome reorganisations is required to achieve a general principle of the adaptive transcriptome in relation to cell growth physiology. Thus, our studies report the generality of transcriptome reorganisation in adaptation to temperature increase and other environmental perturbations. 

In brief, this chapter does not provide any detailed regulatory mechanism of a certain gene or pathway in response to temperature increase, but instead offers global and comparative views of transcriptional reorganisation commonalities. The high similarity in transcriptional changes in response to heat shock and to genomic disturbance and the universal correlation between growth fitness and transcriptome reorganisation in adaptation to both temperature increase and osmotic or nutritional perturbations are described here. The studies provide evidence not only for potential linkage and/or cross-talk between the genomic and environmental contributions to the transcriptome (Ying et al., 2013) but also for the generality of growth-coordinated transcriptome reorganisation (Matsumoto et al., 2013). 

## 13.4.2 Analytical approaches and data acquisition

## 13.4.2.1 Precise control for quantitative evaluation

Special technical comments and concerns related to transcriptomic analyses of living bacterial cells are provided here for reference. As our analyses generally focus on the quantitative evaluation of the relations between transcriptional changes and cell growth physiology, the accuracy of both the analytical platform and the experimental manipulation is crucial to reaching a reliable conclusion. Intensive normalisation among experimental replicates or datasets is commonly performed in microarray analysis. This provides a highly averaged view of global transcriptional activity but potentially masks the delicate variance among the samples. When highly controlled manipulations and well-tuned analytical tools are applied, the microarray datasets can show very similar distributions of probe intensities and resultant close means and deviations in messenger RNA (mRNA) concentrations, despite the independent operations (replications). Normalisation of the output datasets is nonessential, and slight but critical differences in strains and environments are clearly observable. 

The short-term response requires the collection of cells soon after stress application. For example, exponentially growing cells were transferred to a neighbouring water bath incubator previously set at $4 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ After incubating at $4 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 5 min, the cell culture was immediately poured into cold phenol–ethanol solution for the subsequent sample preparation for microarray analysis. In long-term reactions, an ignored practical point is often to control for the culture conditions and cell concentration. Component-known minimal media give a constant and defined growth condition, and flow cytometry provides the exact number of cells in the medium. Moreover, the preculture conditions and serial transfer timing need to be precisely controlled. This was why the difference in growth rate was slight but statistically significant (Ying et al., 2013). As the growth rate represents cellular activity and/or fitness, the precise control of a cell culture definitely decides cell activity–correlated gene expression profiling. 

## 13.4.2.2 Microarray platform

The precision of transcriptomic analyses largely depends on the microarray platform in addition to the experimental controls and manipulations. Our studies used a customised platform with a fine-tuned microarray that covers the whole genome of E. coli strain W3110 (GenoBase, Japan). This platform was developed according to the Affymetrix CustomExpress Arrays (Affymetrix, USA). Both strands of the genome are alternately tiled at single-base resolution using 21 mer perfectly matching (PM) probes. For each PM probe, there are three mismatching (MM) probes, the centre bases of which (11th base of the 21 mer oligo) are replaced by one of the three possible substitutions. A library consisting of a total of 18.6 million probes is arranged on three GeneChips for microarray analysis, covering the four types of probes (1 PM and 3 MM) throughout the 4.6 Mb E. coli genome. The high-density tiling array along with a highly tuned model provide a wider range (i.e., ∼6 orders) in perfect linear correlation between probe intensity and mRNA concentration (Ono et al., 2008, 2013; Suzuki et al., 2007). Owing to such high density, a single mutation within the open reading frame (ORF) will not influence the gene expression evaluation. Because each ORF has hundreds of probes or signals, a single mutation only affects a small portion of probes. 

In addition, the accuracy of this platform is close to that of the next-generation resequencing technologies (Ono et al., 2013; Suzuki et al., 2011). Although next-generation sequencing (NGS) technologies may provide high-resolution datasets, their cost is higher than that of the traditional microarray systems. Datasets acquired based on this original platform are deposited at the Gene Expression Omnibus (GEO) of the US National Center for Biotechnology Information (NBCI) with the platform ID of GPL13336. The raw datasets have access numbers of 33212 and 49296, and the detailed experimental controls can be found. We would like to remind the readers that the traditional microarray systems can provide high-quality and precise results as well as those resulting from the current popular NGS techniques. 

## 13.4.2.3 Computational tools

In addition to the commonly used analytical approaches such as K-means clustering analysis and principal component analysis (PCA), the following packages and/or software are commonly used in our studies and are highly recommended. First is the Bioconductor package RankProd (Hong et al., 2006), which is based on the rank product method for detecting differentially expressed genes caused by genome reduction and heat shock response. The rank product method is a nonparametric statistical method derived from biological reasoning that detects items that are consistently highly or lowly ranked in a number of lists. The method has the advantage of being able to address fewer samples for identifying biologically relevant expression changes (Breitling et al., 2004). Second, gene set enrichment analysis (GSEA) (Subramanian et al., 2005) is a computational tool provided by the Broad Institute that determines whether an a priori defined set of genes shows statistically significant differences between two biological tests. That is, the significant changes of a certain gene group among the whole transcriptome can be determined.

To divide the genes into various groups for GSEA, the following data banks are supposed to be used in E. coli transcriptome studies. As microarray analysis reports gene transcription, RegulonDB targeting transcriptional regulation is highly valuable for determining specific fluctuating transcriptional networks. Additionally, because the gene categories include all genes, including those of unknown function, it is highly recommended for use in enrichment analysis, in particular in studies related to genes with non-essential functions. 

## 13.4.3 Comparable reorganisation: heat shock versus genomic disturbance

## 13.4.3.1 Difference in E. coli genomes

In addition to the commonly used wild-type E. coli strain MG1655 (GenBank No. U00096), the strain MDS42, which carries a reduced genome, is intensively used in our studies. MDS42 was constructed by Posfai et al. by removing non-essential genome sequences (Posfai et al., 2006). The precise genome sequence of MDS42 has previously been determined using the Applied Biosystems ${ \mathrm { S O L i D } } ^ { \textregistered }$ system. The circular genome of MDS42 is 3,976,195 base pairs in length and can be aligned end to end with the reference sequence constructed from the wildtype MG1655 sequence with the analysis tool BioScope® (Figure 13.4.1). Among 3778 protein-encoding genes present in MDS42, 3759 have identical counterparts in MG1655. In addition, 22 genes and 24 non-ORF regions contain small InDels or single nucleotide polymorphisms (SNPs), with differences from their MG1655 counterpart at the protein level ranging from singleamino-acid substitutions to complete deletions. These mutations might have occurred either in the genome reduction process or in the culture for genome resequencing. All detected mutations were confirmed by the Sanger method. As the average coverage throughout the whole genome was ∼300 (Figure 13.4.1), the genome regions of low coverage (<30) were randomly picked up for further verification by the Sanger method. Except for a few that failed, most results were identical to those observed from the resequencing mapping. The genome sequence with the full set of gene annotations is available for public use (DNA Data Bank of Japan (DDBJ) No. AP012306). A total of 3710 genes that are common between MDS42 and MG1655 are used in our studies. 

## 13.4.3.2 Differential gene expression

As heat shock response is known as a universal physiological reaction, transcriptional changes due to transient temperature upshift were examined to identify differences in stress response between MG1655 and MDS42. Cells growing exponentially at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ responding to the high temperature of $4 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ were analysed. Both MG1655 and MDS42 showed large alterations in global transcription induced by the high temperature and showed expression patterns with equivalent correlation coefficients at both regular temperature and heat shock, despite the diversity in genome size (Figure 13.4.2b,c). The genes that up- or downregulated in both strains or in one strain only are summarised in a Venn diagram (Figure 13.4.2a). In addition to a number of genes whose expression was reorganised in one or both strains, a total of seven genes (sfmC, ybgD, ymdA, yhaB, metA, metF, and yodA; Figure 13.4.2, numbers in red) showed reversed transcriptional changes between the two strains. This suggests that there must be some genes that behave distinctively in response to heat shock in MG1655 and MDS42. 

Indeed, we found 159 structure-sensitive genes presenting large fluctuations at the transcriptional level in response to heat shock in MG1655 (Figure 13.4.2d). The magnitude of transcriptional changes in the DEGs was much higher than that presented by 696 deleted genes (Figure 13.4.2f), which presented a nearly equivalent fluctuation as that observed in the 3710 common genes (Figure 13.4.2b,c). In addition, the transcriptional fluctuation of the same 159 genes due to heat shock was largely suppressed in MDS42 (Figure 13.4.2e), which led to a correlation coefficient comparable with that of the common genes. Statistical tests confirmed that the structure-sensitive genes were significantly high-responsive to heat shock $( p < 0 . 0 0 0 1 )$ ). 

Large fluctuations in response to heat shock were found in genes close to the scars derived from the deleted non-essential genes, suggesting that the easily changing genes might have been natively gathered. Such location specificity may be shaped by nature during evolution, which could be advantageous for unexpected genomic damage. Studies on artificially reduced genomes could offer novel insights into genome evolution (e.g., the chromosomal distribution of exons and introns in eukaryotic cells) that would be invisible from studies on wild-type cells in nature. 

## 13.4.3.3 Reorganisation in transcriptional networks

A total of 42 (out of 175) regulatory networks comprising more than 15 downstream genes and controlled by transcriptional regulators and/or sigma factors were further analysed. Overall, both genome reduction and heat shock had a broad effect on transcriptional reorganisation, as a number of genes, including the regulators, changed their expression levels (Figure 13.4.3). A few regulators showed remarkable transcriptional changes induced by either genomic disturbance or heat shock individually; for instance, the expression of gadE was disturbed only by genome reduction (vivid green), whereas the expression of rpoH was induced significantly by heat shock (dark orange). Additionally, a comparative number of regulators were found to have overlapping responses to both genome reduction and heat shock, such as rpoS, rpoE, soxS, and marA (light orange). 

By contrast, the genes downstream of these regulators did not always show significant fluctuation identical to that of the corresponding regulators. Among a total of 175 regulatory networks, 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/d070d93a-9a38-4d08-ab41-83365fc0221e/4371ce2b240d6d436e38b66dc7fb8d143d6b433d6ac4db7226c22a7d770d2540.jpg)



Figure 13.4.1 Circle diagram of the MG1655 genome showing the results of MDS42 resequencing analysis. The coverage of SOLiD reads from MDS42 mapped against the MG1655 genome is shown on the outer circle. The MG1655 genome sequence was obtained from GenBank (U00096). In the next ring, gold boxes indicate deleted segments. Deleted genes encoded on the forward and complementary strands are indicated by blue and red tick marks, respectively. The inner ring shows the GC contents of the MG1655 genome.


24 and 12 networks showed evident transcriptional changes (false discovery rate (FDR) < 0.25) associated with genome reduction and heat shock, respectively (Figure 13.4.4). Heat shock triggered approximately equal numbers of induced and repressed regulatory networks, whereas genome reduction triggered many more upregulated than downregulated networks. Most regulatory networks responsive to heat shock seemed to be highly sensitive to genome reduction as well. These overlapping regulatory networks (e.g., rpoH, metJ, purR, and fur) often showed a tendency toward identical direction in transcriptional changes. The only exception showing changes in the reverse direction was the network controlled by gadX. 

Taken together, the transcriptional reorganisation in regulatory networks not only owed to the expression change in the regulators (e.g., metJ and rpoH), but also occurred in those controlled by the regulators of conserved expression (e.g., lrp and hns). The latter case suggested either weakened transcriptional control by the regulators or high sensitivity of the downstream genes, that is, slight transcriptional changes in regulators result in remarkable transcriptional changes in downstream genes. These results indicate that the transcriptional reorganisations in response to heat shock and genomic disturbance present high similarity.

## 13.4.4 Generality in reorganisation: thermal adaptation versus other adaptations

## 13.4.4.1 Adaptiveness of bacterial cells

Cell growth control is crucial for acquiring the precise correspondence between transcriptome and adaptiveness under certain growth conditions. Generally, in long-term adaptation, the adaptiveness of the cells is determined according to the specific growth rate in the exponential phase. When the cells are transferred to fresh media, particularly to a new culture condition, cell growth usually pauses for a certain time before initiating the exponential phase (Figure 13.4.5). Only in the exponential phase is the adaptiveness of the cells steady and quantitatively reliable. For instance, only cells in exponential growth were collected for analysis of the relation between the transcriptome and growth fitness (Figure 13.4.5). The adaptiveness (fitness) of growing cells is designated as the growth rate. The growth rate $( \mathrm { h } ^ { - 1 } )$ is evaluated by the speed with which the number of offspring increases during the exponential growth phase, and it is calculated according to the following commonly used formula: growth rate (μ) = ln (Ct / C0) /t, where Ct, C0, and t represent the final and initial cell concentrations (cells/mL) and the culture time from initial to final time points (hours), respectively. 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/d070d93a-9a38-4d08-ab41-83365fc0221e/5d11185922c2bab266c5789988d850953ef6e7e00dfa99b9aa27fbaacf5d8670.jpg)



Figure 13.4.3 Transcriptional changes in regulators. Nodes and arcs represent genes and regulation, respectively. Large nodes denote sigma factors or regulators of more than 15 genes in the control. The genes with conserved expression are shown in grey, and those of notable transcriptional changes are shown in colour. Larger transcriptional changes due to heat shock or genome reduction are shown in gradation from yellow to orange or green, respectively, and the equal changes are shown in yellow. The scale bar indicates the statistical significance in logarithmic scale. The statistical significance of the changes in gene expression are represented in gradation in accordance with FDR values determined by the product rank method. Vivid colours correspond to high significance. Warm and cold colours correspond to increased and decreased expression, respectively.


## 13.4.4.2 Adaptive transcriptome in common

Changes in gene expression under unfavourable environmental perturbations, unlike the transcriptional changes in response to pulse-like perturbations such as heat shock, are accompanied by a decrease in growth fitness (Figure 13.4.5). Thus far, the dissimilarity among the studies is considered to be the divergence in time scales, that is, the time of exposure to the changed environment, the speed of gene regulation in response to stressors, and the lag time between the changes in transcription and in growth. Consequently, the timing of the changes in gene expression and that in growth most likely significantly differ among those regulatory mechanisms, which led to the disagreement among the studies that targeted varied environments and/or were based on different experimental designs (e.g., measurement timing). To link the gene expression to the cellular physiology represented by the growth rate, it is essential to analyse the global gene expression patterns in exponentially growing cells that show constant growth rates with a variation. Testing the steady transcriptome at the exponential phase is crucial to detecting the common features among varied types of adaptation. 

K-means clustering analysis categorises the genes showing similar patterns in transcriptional changes in response to each culturing condition. Statistically significant correlations between the growth rate and the clustered expression pattern could be observed. For instance, when the total 3740 genes were divided into three clusters (1629, 993, and 1118 genes in C1, C2, and C3, respectively), the relationships between growth and gene expression were highly conceptually extracted (Table 13.4.2). The transcriptional changes of the genes in clusters C2 and C3 exhibited negative and positive correlations with growth rate, respectively. These correlations were commonly observed in all three types of environmental controls, that is, the growthrelated transcriptional changes were universal in the genes in C1 and C2 and were independent of the type of environmental condition. The transcriptional changes in these genes presented positive, negative, and no correlations with growth rate in the environmental controls of osmotic, temperature, and nutritional conditions, respectively. The results demonstrate that growth-coordinated transcriptome reorganisation, regardless of the details of the growth environment, did occur in E. coli cells. The three clusters neatly explain the coordination of the gene expression patterns and growth fitness. 

Previous studies have been performed to determine the global changes in gene expression of cells in response to varied

external perturbations (Jozefczuk et al., 2010). These studies have generally provided insights into transcriptome reorganisation at short time scales (from minutes to a few hours), which represent the relations between transcriptional fluctuations and the degree or magnitude of the stress. The present analysis, focussing on steady growth states (10– 12 h in exponential growth conditions) rather than stress responses, highlights the relations between growth fitness (i.e., the adaptiveness of the growing cells under a certain environment) and global gene expression patterns. GSEA shows the differentiated functions among the three gene clusters (Figure 13.4.6). The growth rate–related differentiation in gene functions reflects the balanced bacterial lifestyle. A trade-off in gene expression must occur in living cells to maintain cellular homeostasis in the face of external and genetic perturbations. In addition, the alternative expression of the three gene clusters may trigger common patterns of transcriptome reorganisation in response to varied perturbations. 

## 13.4.5 Concluding remarks

So far, bacterial gene expression has been studied more regarding specific stress responses rather than the generality of the global gene expression profile. Fruitful insights have been achieved regarding the pulse-like transcriptome reorganisation in response to environmental changes. Our studies show that cross-talk in transcriptome reorganisation contributes to environmental perturbations and genetic interruptions in E. coli, and they strongly suggest there must have been common

# Participation of Ser–Thr protein kinases in regulation of heat stress responses in Synechocystis

Anna A. Zorina, Galina V. Novikova, and Dmitry A. Los 

Institute of Plant Physiology, Russian Academy of Sciences, Moscow, Russia 

## 13.5.1 Introduction

The diversity of cyanobacteria and their ways of adaptation to various, sometimes stressful environmental conditions are often reflected by the organization of their signaling systems. Rapid reactions of cells to changes in the environment require adequate signal perception and transduction systems, which often employ phosphorylation–dephosphorylation events (see Section 3). Phosphorylation is a fundamental reaction in all living organisms, catalyzed by protein kinases – a large and diverse group of enzymes. Protein kinases are divided into several groups according to their substrate specificity: histidine, serine–threonine (Ser–Thr), and tyrosine kinases. For a long time, these enzymes also have been divided into two subgroups: prokaryotic and eukaryotic protein kinases. 

Prokaryotic protein kinases consist of histidine protein kinases, which usually belong to a class of two-component regulatory systems (Dutta et al., 1999) (see Section 3). In the unicellular cyanobacterium Synechocystis sp. PCC 6803, these systems are represented by 92 genes: 47 genes code for histidine kinases, and 45 genes code for response regulators (Los et al., 2010; Mizuno et al., 1996). The canonical two-component regulatory system comprises a sensor histidine kinase and cognate response regulator (Klumpp and Krieglstein, 2002). Sometimes, these two components may be fused into a single polypeptide and may form a hybrid protein kinase (Mascher et al., 2006). 

In eukaryotes, proteins are phosphorylated mostly by tyrosine or serine–threonine protein kinases (STPKs; 2.7.1.37). The latter catalyze phosphorylation of the hydroxyl group of serine or threonine residues of their substrate proteins (Hanks and Hunter, 1995). All STPKs analyzed to date include a conserved domain of 250–300 amino acids, which binds first to adenosine triphosphate (ATP) (and, in a few cases like casein kinase 2, to guanosine triphosphate (GTP)) and then binds to a substrate protein for transfer of the phosphate group derived from ATP (GTP) to the substrate protein (Pereira et al., 2011; Zorina, 2013). 

The complete sequences of the genomes of Arabidopsis thaliana, rice, human, and rat reveal the presence of hundreds of genes for STPKs. Homologs of eukaryotic-like STPKs have been found in many bacterial (Hunter, 1995; Kennelly, 2002; Krupa et al., 2004) and Archaea (Kennelly, 2014) species. For example, Bacillus subtilis has three (Mijakovic et al., 2005) and Mycobacterium tuberculosis has 11 genes (Baer et al., 2014) for STPKs. Myxococcus xanthus carries nearly 100 genes for eukaryotic-like STPKs (Perez´ et al., 2008). By contrast, Escherichia coli has no such genes; however, Ser–Thr protein kinase activity was found in E. coli cells (Enami and Ishihama, 1984). Substrate proteins for Ser–Thr phosphorylation have been identified in E. coli by phosphoproteomics (Macek et al., 2008), and the protein YihE, which has no homology to eukaryotic STPKs but possesses Ser–Thr protein kinase activity, has been identified and crystallized (Zheng et al., 2007). 

In bacteria, STPKs are used to regulate basic metabolism (Novakov ´ a´ et al., 2010) or globally control gene expression (Donat et al., 2009; Saskova´ et al., 2007). In bacteria, sometimes mutations of genes for STPKs lead to multiple changes in overall metabolism and/or gene expression (Novakov ´ a´ et al., 2010; Panichkin et al., 2006). However, the effects of such mutations cannot be seen as a distinct change in phenotype. Correspondingly, the identification of the original targets of Ser–Thr phosphorylation is complicated (Donat et al., 2009; Zorina et al., 2011). 

The unicellular freshwater cyanobacterium Synechocystis sp. PCC 6803 (hereafter, Synechocystis) has 12 genes for STPKs (Kaneko et al., 1996, 2003; Leonard et al., 1998). Seven STPKs of Synechocystis belong to the PKN2 subfamily of STPKs, and five STPKs belong to the ABC1 subfamily. The protein kinases of the PKN2 type are designated as SpkA, SpkB, SpkC, SpkD, SpkE, SpkF, and SpkG; and those for kinases of the ABC1 type are designated as SpkH, SpkI, SpkJ, SpkK, and SpkL (http://genome.kazusa.or.jp/cyanobase/).

ORF, Open reading frame. 


The known functions of the STPKs and of the corresponding genes of Synechocystis are listed in Table 13.5.1. 

The SpkA (Kamei et al., 2001; Panichkin et al., 2006) and SpkB (Kamei et al., 2003) proteins are responsible for the formation of thick pili, and they control cell motility (see Chapters 22.1 and 22.2). The SpkC is involved in the regulation of nitrogen metabolism (Galkin et al., 2003) (see Chapter 2.19). The involvement of SpkE in the control of cell motility has been proposed but not yet confirmed (Kamei et al., 2002). SpkD has been associated with the cycle of tricarbonic acids (TCAs) (Laurent et al., 2008). SpkG has been reported as a participant in high salt resistance (Liang et al., 2011) (see Section 11). Recently, SpkE has been associated with some aspects of cold stress responses (Zorina et al., 2014) (see Section 15). A mutant deficient in SpkE has lost the ability to induce some cold stress genes. This mutant also demonstrated changes in the pattern of protein phosphorylation compared to wild-type cells. The protein substrates, which are the targets for Ser–Thr phosphorylation in Synechocystis, are still poorly characterized (Table 13.5.1). 

Here, we describe the identification of proteins that change the extent of their Ser–Thr phosphorylation upon heat stress in Synechocystis. Eleven genes for STPKs (spkB through spkL; the properties of the spkA-deficient mutant have been described by Panichkin et al. (2006)) have been systematically inactivated by site-directed mutagenesis (Zorina et al., 2011). Inactivation of spkC, spkF, or spkK leads to inability of cells to phosphorylate the small chaperonin GroES (see Section 14), suggesting the existence of a regulatory circuit or cascade in which these three STPKs interact. 

## 13.5.2 Methods

## 13.5.2.1 Strains and culture conditions

Synechocystis sp. PCC 6803, a glucose-sensitive strain (GS), was obtained from Department of Genetics, Moscow State 

University, Moscow, Russia (Grigorieva and Shestakov, 1982). It served as a wild-type strain for construction of the gene knockout library of individual spk genes. This strain already possesses a spontaneous frame shift mutation in the spkA gene (Kamei et al., 2001). All cyanobacterial cells were grown in liquid or on agar-solidified BG11 medium (Rippka, 1988) that had been buffered to pH 7.6 with 20 mM HEPES-NaOH. Cells in 50 ml liquid cultures were grown photoautotrophically in 100 ml glass tubes in a water bath at $3 2 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ with continuous illumination at 50 μmol photons m $^ { - 2 } \ s ^ { - 1 }$ , which was provided by luminescent lamps, and with continuous bubbling with air that contained 1% $\mathrm { C O } _ { 2 }$ (v/v) (normal growth conditions). The concentration of cells in liquid cultures was estimated from the optical density at 750 nm $( \mathrm { A } _ { 7 5 0 } ) _ { : }$ , as determined with a spectrophotometer (Genesis 10UV; Thermo Fisher Scientific, USA). Cultures for experiments were inoculated with cells from liquid pre-cultures at a concentration of cells that corresponded to $\mathrm { A } _ { 7 5 0 } = 0 . 1$ and were grown under normal growth conditions. Cells at a density that corresponded to $\mathrm { A _ { 7 5 0 } } = 0 . 4 – 0 . 6$ were used for analysis. Mutant strains were maintained on agar-solidified or liquid medium that contained 25 μg ml−1 kanamycin, 4 μg ml−1 gentamycin, $1 2 . 5 \mu \mathrm { g } \mathrm { m l } ^ { - 1 }$ chloramphenicol, or 30 μg ml−1 spectinomycin. No antibiotics were added to the final cultures used for analysis to avoid the possibility of phenotypic changes due to the presence of the antibiotic. 

For heat shock experiments, cultures were incubated at 44 $^ \circ \mathrm { C }$ at the same light intensity for 30 min. After treatment, the cells were collected by centrifugation (5000 g, 10 min, $4 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } )$ and washed twice with 50 mM Tris-HCl (pH 7.6), 10 mM MgCl . Washed pellets were used immediately for protein isolation. 

Competent cells of E. coli strains JM109, DH5α, or BL21 (DE3 pLysS) were obtained using a standard CaCl protocol (Sambrook et al., 1989) and were used for the cloning and isolation of plasmids. Cells were grown overnight at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ in lysogeny broth (LB) medium (Sambrook et al., 1989) supplemented with ampicillin at 50 μg ml−1. 

## 13.5.2.2 Construction of a library of strains with mutated genes for Ser–Thr kinases (spkB–spkL), histidine kinase Hik34 (slr1285), and transcriptional regulator HrcA (sll1670)

Partial sequences of individual spk genes were amplified by polymerase chain reaction (PCR) with the primers listed in Table 13.5.2. 

The amplified DNA fragments were ligated into pGEM®-T or pGEM®-T Easy (Promega Inc., USA), or into pT7Blue-T, or pUC18 (Yanisch-Perron et al., 1985). Antibiotic-resistance gene cassettes (Prentki et al., 1991; Rose, 1988; Schweizer, 1993; Vieira and Messing, 1982), a transposon EZ::TNTM <KAN-2> (Epicentre, USA), or pGPS2.1 (New England BioLabs, USA) was inserted into the cloned DNA fragments. Each of the resultant plasmids was used for transformation of Synechocystis cells (Figure 13.5.1a). The transformed cells were selected on agarsolidified BG11 medium that had been supplemented with the appropriate antibiotic (Grigorieva and Shestakov, 1982). Each cyanobacterial cell contains several copies of the cyanobacterial chromosome (Labarre et al., 1989). The complete replacement of native copies of the chromosome by mutated copies was confirmed by amplification by PCR (Figure 13.5.1b) with the pairs of primers listed in Table 13.5.2. Positions of insertions are shown in Figure 13.5.1a. 

The hrcA (sll1670) gene, which encodes a transcription factor that negatively controls transcription of some heat stress genes (Nakamoto et al., 2003), was amplified with forward and reverse primers (5′ -TTGGACTGGAATTCTAAGATG and 5′ -TTAATTTTTGCTCAATGCTTCCG, respectively), agarose purified, and cloned into pT7-Blue. The HincII Kmr -cassette derived from pUC4K (Vieira and Messing, 1982) was inserted into the blunted Eco91I site of the hrcA to substitute a part of a gene (positions from 223 to 939 of the gene sequence). Complete segregation of the mutated chromosomes was confirmed by PCR with the primers described above. The construction of the Hik34-deficient mutant was described by Suzuki et al. (2005).

## 13.5.2.3 Complementation of mutations in spkC, spkF, and spkK genes

The spkC, spkF, and spkK genes were amplified together with their upstream regions with Pfu DNA polymerase (Fermentas, Lithuania) from the genomic DNA of Synechocystis, with synthetic oligonucleotides (Table 13.5.2) used as primers for PCR. Amplified DNA fragments, which contained spkF and spkK genes, were ligated into the SmaI site of pVZ321 (Zinchenko et al., 1999). An amplified DNA fragment that contained the spkC was ligated into the EcoRI site of pVZ321 blunted with Klenow exo− enzyme. Mutant cells of Synechocystis defective in spkC, spkF, and spkK genes were transformed with recombinant $\mathrm { p V Z - } s p k C , \mathrm { p V Z - } s p k F ,$ and pVZ–spkK plasmids, respectively. The clones that carried the spkF and spkK genes were selected on spectinomycin $( 3 0 ~ \mu \mathrm { g } ~ \mathrm { m l ^ { - 1 } } )$ and chloramphenicol $( 5 \mu \mathrm { g } \mathrm { m l } ^ { - 1 } )$ . The clones that carried the spkC gene were selected on kanamycin $( 1 0 \mu \mathrm { g } \mathrm { m l } ^ { - 1 } )$ and chloramphenicol $( 1 2 . 5 \mu \mathrm { g } \mathrm { m l } ^ { - 1 } )$ . 

## 13.5.2.4 Other manipulations with DNA

DNA was isolated from Synechocystis cells with hot phenol as described previously (Kiseleva et al., 2000). Restriction analysis with restriction enzymes (Fermentas), isolation of plasmids, transformation of E. coli, and ligation of DNA fragments were performed by standard methods (Sambrook et al., 1989). 

## 13.5.2.5 Protein isolation

All procedures were carried out at $4 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ with chemicals purchased from Sigma-Aldrich Rus LLC (Russia). Cell pellets were suspended in ice-cold buffer, which contained 50 mM Tris-HCl (pH 7.6), 10 mM $\mathrm { M g C l } _ { 2 } ,$ 2 mM EDTA, 1 mM dithiothreitol (DTT), 1 mM phenylmethylsulfonyl fluoride (PMSF), 1 mM EGTA, 2 mM ${ \mathrm { N a } } _ { 3 } { \mathrm { V O } } _ { 4 } ,$ , 1 mM benzamidine, 10 mM NaF, 50 mM β-glycerophosphate, and 250 mM sucrose. Cells were disrupted with a French press (2.8 MPa; SLM, American Instruments Co., USA). The crude extracts were centrifuged at 7500 g to remove unbroken cells and then at 16,000 g for 20 min. The final supernatants were transferred into an appropriate buffer by gel filtration on NAP-5 columns (GE Healthcare ZAO, Russia) and used for biochemical assays. Protein concentration was determined using the BCA protein assay. 

## 13.5.2.6 Protein phosphorylation in vitro, 2D PAGE, and Western blotting

Protein phosphorylation in vitro was performed in a mixture that contained 20 mM MES-KOH (pH 6.5), 10 mM MgCl , 10 μM ATP, and 740 kBq $( \gamma ^ { - 3 2 } \mathrm { P } ) \mathrm { A T P }$ (148 TBq mmol−1). The reaction was initiated by the addition of 50 μg protein, and it was continued for 15 min at $3 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ After labeling, the proteins were precipitated $\mathrm { a t } - 2 0 \ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ with 80% (v/v) acetone in the presence of 15% TCA and 1% 2-mercaptoethanol, and pelleted by centrifugation. The pellets were washed twice with 80% (v/v) acetone. Labeled proteins were dissolved in the rehydration buffer (130 μl) containing 6 M urea, 1.5 M thiourea, 3% CHAPS, 66 mM DTT, 0.2% Bio-Lytes (pH 3–10, Bio-Rad, USA), and traces of bromophenol blue. The samples (125 μl each) were loaded on IPG strips (ReadyStrips, 7 cm, pH 4–7; Bio-Rad) for passive rehydration at $2 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 12 h. The strips were then run using a Bio-Rad Protean IEF Cell at $2 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ as follows: 200 V constant voltage for 15 min, 500 V constant voltage for 15 min, linear increase up to 1000 V for 30 min, slow increase up to 5000 V for 30 min, and 5000 V constant voltage until a total of $1 0 , 0 0 0 \ \mathrm { V } \ \mathrm { h } ^ { - 1 }$ was reached. After IEF, the strips were equilibrated for 15 min with the buffer (50 mM Tris-HCl, pH 8.8, 6 M urea, 30% glycerol, 2% SDS) containing 10 mg $\mathrm { m l ^ { - 1 } }$ DTT followed by equilibration for 15 min with the same buffer, which contained 25 $\mathrm { m g \ m l ^ { - 1 } }$ iodoacetamide instead of DTT. The strips were then placed on the top of 12.5% polyacrylamide mini gels (0.75 mm thick) and subjected to sodium dodecyl sulfate polyacrylamide gel electrophoresis (SDS-PAGE) at 200 V in a Bio-Rad MiniProtean III unit. After electrophoresis, the gels were fixed in 50% methanol with 3% phosphoric acid to remove the phosphate groups associated with His residues. After acidic treatment, the gels were stained with the colloidal dye Coomassie brilliant blue (CBB) G-250, dried, and autoradiographed on Kodak Biomax MR film (Eastman Kodak Co., USA). The resultant autoradiographs were aligned with corresponding images of gels, and stained with CBB, using ImageQuant 5.2 gel analysis software (Molecular Dynamics, USA). Selected protein spots were excised, digested in-gel with trypsin, and subsequently analyzed by matrix-assisted laser desorption/ionization time-of-flight mass spectrometry (MALDI-TOF-MS) at the service Center of Postgenomic Technology at the Institute of Biomedical Chemistry (Russian Academy of Medical Sciences, Russia). 

After 2DE separation, phosphoproteins were transferred onto 45 μm nitrocellulose filters (Hybond-C Extra, GE Healthcare) for Western blotting using a Trans-Blot SD Electrophoretic Semi-Dry Transfer Cell (Bio-Rad) at 0.8 mA cm−2 for 2 h at room temperature. Air-dried membranes were subjected to autoradiography on Kodak Biomax MR films. After exposure with X-ray films, membranes were probed with rabbit antisera raised against a mixture of heat shock protein 60s (Hsp60s) (homologous GroEL and Cpn60) of Thermosynechococcus vulcanus (Tanaka et al., 1997). Protein bands were visualized with secondary antibodies coupled with horseradish peroxidase. 

Phosphoserines and phosphothreonines were determined with the corresponding anti-pSer and anti-pThr monoclonal antibodies produced in mouse (product nos. P 3430 and P 3555, with dilution of 1:1000 and 1:500, respectively; Sigma-Aldrich, USA). Rabbit anti-mouse secondary antibodies conjugated with horseradish peroxidase were used for visualization. Protein extracts (25 μg) isolated from control and heat-treated wild-type cells were probed with anti-pThr antibodies. Protein extracts (25 μg) isolated from control and heat-treated wildtype cells were probed with anti-pThr antibodies after phosphorylation in vitro with exogenously added recombinant GroES (2.5 μg).

Figure 13.5.2 Purification of recombinant GroES. (a) Stained with CBB SDS-PAGE (18%) of crude lysate of non-induced E. coli cells carrying pET-22b with groES gene of Synechocystis (lane 1); the lysate of induced E. coli cells carrying pET-22b with groES (lane 2); soluble protein fraction from induced E. coli cells (lane 3); affinity purified GroES protein fraction (lane 4); GroES purified on Mono Q column (lane 5). (b) Purification of recombinant GroES on Mono Q column. Peak of GroES is gray-shaded. Zorina et al. (2011). Reproduced with permission of Oxford University Press.


## 13.5.2.7 Expression and purification of the recombinant GroES

The recombinant GroES protein was expressed in E. coli strain BL21 (DE3 pLysS) harboring the recombinant plasmid pET22b(+) (Novagen, Merck KGaA, Germany) with His-tagged groES. A DNA fragment carrying the groES gene was amplified from the genomic DNA of Synechocystis sp. PCC 6803 by PCR with synthetic oligonucleotides (5′ -CATATGGCCGCTATTTCC and 5′ - CTCGAGGGCAACGGAGGC) as forward and reverse primers, respectively. The amplified DNA fragment was digested with NdeI and XhoI restriction endonucleases and cloned into the corresponding sites of the pET22b(+). The resultant plasmid, pET-groES, was used for transformation of E. coli cells. The transformed cells were grown in LB medium until the $\mathrm { O D } _ { 7 5 0 }$ of suspension reached 0.5. Overproduction of the recombinant protein was initiated by addition of isopropyl thio-β-D-galactoside (IPTG) to a final concentration of 1 mM and continued for 3 h at $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ Cells were harvested by centrifugation, disrupted by French press, and the expressed protein was purified from the soluble fraction by affinity chromatography on a $\mathrm { N i } ^ { 2 + }$ resin using a Protino® Ni-IDA Kit (Macherey-Nagel Gmbh & Co., Germany) according to the manufacturer’s protocol. Alternatively, the recombinant GroES was purified by fast protein liquid chromatography (FPLC) on a Mono Q column (GE Healthcare). Expression and purification protein profiles are shown in Figure 13.5.2. 

## 13.5.2.8 Ser–Thr protein kinase assay on GroES

Protein fractions (5–10 μg of protein), obtained from Synechocystis wild-type and its 11 mutant (ΔspkB-L) cells, were incubated with purified GroES (0.04 mg $\mathrm { m l ^ { - 1 } } )$ for 15 min at $3 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ in a final volume of 25 μl of buffer that contained 20 mM MES-KOH (pH 6.5), 10 mM $\mathrm { M g C l } _ { 2 } ,$ , 10 nM ATP, and 74 kBq $( \gamma ^ { - 3 2 } \mathrm { P } ) \mathrm { A T P }$ (148 TBq mmol−1). The reaction was terminated by adding half of the reaction volume of three times concentrated Laemmli SDS sample buffer (Sambrook et al., 1989), and samples were boiled for 5 min. Phosphorylated GroES was resolved using 18% SDS-PAGE, stained with CBB in 40% (v/v) methanol with 10% (v/v) acetic acid, dried, and autoradiographed as described above. The same kinase assay was performed with the protein fractions obtained from the Synechocystis mutants defective in $s p k C , s p k F ,$ and spkK, which were complemented with the $\mathrm { p V Z - } s p k C , \mathrm { p V Z - } s p k F .$ , and $\mathrm { p V } Z – s p k K ,$ respectively. 

## 13.5.2.9 pH dependence of the phosphorylation of the GroES protein in vitro

Purified GroES (2.5 μg) mixed with either protein extracts derived from control $( 3 2 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } )$ wild-type cells (10 μg) or heattreated $( 4 4 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 30 min) cells (10 μg) was phosphorylated in vitro with $( \gamma ^ { . 3 2 } \mathrm { P } ) \mathrm { A T P } .$ The reaction was terminated with three times concentrated SDS–PAGE sample buffer and immediately subjected to SDS–PAGE (15% PAG). After electrophoresis, the proteins were transferred onto polyvinylidene difluoride (PVDF) membrane. The membranes were stained with Ponceau red and incubated at $4 5 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 2 h in 50 mM KCl– HCl (pH 1.0), 0.1 M Tris–HCl (pH 7.0), or 1 M KOH (pH 14.0). The radioactivity remaining in the membrane was revealed after its exposure onto X-ray film. 

## 13.5.3 Results and discussion

## 13.5.3.1 A library of mutants with defective STPK genes

Eleven genes for STPKs (spkB through spkL) were systematically inactivated in Synechocystis by insertional mutagenesis (Figure 13.5.1a). In the GS strain, the spkA gene has already been inactivated by a spontaneous frame shift mutation (Kamei et al., 2001). The extent of replacement of wild-type copies of individual genes was examined by PCR in each individual mutant, and the complete segregation of the mutated chromosomes was confirmed (Figure 13.5.1b). The increase in length of PCR-amplified fragments in the mutants was due to the insertion of the antibiotic-resistance gene cassette, except for the case of the spkF::Spr and spkH::Spr mutants, where both insertion and deletion occurred (Figure 13.5.1). No mutant cells yielded an amplified fragment of the same length as the parental wildtype gene. This fact indicates that there were no copies of the respective wild-type genes in any of the 11 mutants. The phenotypes of all mutants were similar to those of wild-type cells in terms of size, color, the shape of colonies during cultivation on solid medium, or the color and growth rates of cultures grown in liquid medium under standard growth conditions. 

## 13.5.3.2 Ser–Thr phosphorylation of the soluble proteins under heat shock

Cells that were grown at $3 2 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ were subjected to heat shock $( 4 4 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } )$ for 30 min. The influence of heat stress on Ser–Thr protein phosphorylation was studied in soluble protein fractions using an in vitro phosphorylation technique. Heat stress for 30 min had no significant effect on soluble protein composition as demonstrated by 2DE gels stained with CBB (Figures 13.5.3a and 13.5.3b). However, the extent of phosphorylation of the individual polypeptides changed upon heat treatment (Figures 13.5.3c and 13.5.3d). The phosphorylation signals from polypeptides presented by spots nos. 1 and 2 increased two times, whereas the phosphorylation signal from spots nos. 3a/3b decreased 1.5 times. Phosphopeptide no. 4 did not show any temperaturedependent change in phosphorylation. The high-intensity signal from phosphopeptide no. 5 dropped significantly upon an increase in temperature from 32 to $4 4 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Figures 13.5.3c and 13.5.3d). 

Thus, the increase in temperature causes significant changes in the intensity of Ser–Thr phosphorylation of proteins in Synechocystis. These changes might be due to autophosphorylation of the individual polypeptides, or due to temperaturedependent changes in the activity of different STPKs. For example, high temperature may inactivate certain STPKs. Protein kinases may phosphorylate either the same or a similar set of proteins, or, alternatively, each individual STPK may phosphorylate its own substrate in vitro. To discriminate between these two possibilities, it is necessary (i) to identify the proteins that correspond to the phosphorylated spots, and (ii) to clarify which of the 11 STPKs is able to phosphorylate the identified substrate in vitro.
