Figure 13.5.3 Proteome of soluble fractions of Synechocystis sp. PCC 6803. 2DE separation of soluble proteins (50 μg) from wild-type GS cells grown at $3 2 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } \left( \mathrm { \bar { a } } \right)$ and treated for 30 min at $4 4 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } \left( \mathrm { b } \right)$ stained with CBB. Corresponding autoradiographs (c,d). Spot numbers of the identified proteins correspond to those presented in Table 13.5.3. Zorina et al. (2011). Reproduced with permission of Oxford University Press.


## 13.5.3.3 MALDI-TOF-MS identification of phosphorylated proteins

Fourteen phosphoproteins (seven proteins from cells grown at 32 ◦C and seven proteins from heat-shocked cells) were subjected to MALDI-TOF-MS analysis to ensure that the visible temperature-dependent changes in Ser–Thr phosphorylation corresponded to the same protein(s) (Table 13.5.3). The phosphoproteins observed at $3 2 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Figure 13.5.3c) were identical to those observed at 44 ◦C (Figure 13.5.3d). This means that heat shock causes changes in their Ser–Thr phosphorylation. 

In general, we obtained rather high compliance with calculated and experimental data concerning molecular mass, pI, and peptide location in 2DE gels and autoradiograms. For example, in all samples of GroES protein (no. 5), they were digested with trypsin into five polypeptides with the 83% coverage of the GroES amino acid sequence of Synechocystis. 

The identified phosphoproteins belong to several functional groups: (i) the enzymes for basic metabolism (nos. 1, 2, 3a, 3b, and 6), (ii) proteins for general stress response (nos. 4 and 5), and (iii) proteins with unknown function (no. 7). Among the first group, there is a protein that participates in the Calvin cycle (no. 2, RuBisCo large subunit), and another protein is involved in protein biosynthesis (no. 1, methionyl-tRNA synthetase). It is possible that an increase in phosphorylation of these proteins correlates with an increase in their enzymatic activity, similarly to some enzymes of the tricarbonic acid cycle (Levine et al., 2006). 

6-Phosphogluconate dehydrogenase (no. 3a), which also belongs to the first group of identified proteins, participates in the first phase of the pentose phosphate pathway. The phosphorylated status for proteins that are involved in this pathway has been demonstrated in Bacillus subtilis (Eymann et al., 2007). 

Elongation factor Tu (no. 3b) exists in its phosphorylated form in all organisms, from Archaea to humans, and it is characterized by conserved sites of phosphorylation (Macek et al., 2008). Phosphorylation of factor Tu is probably required for optimization of the rate of translation. 

Among the identified phosphoproteins, two heat shock proteins (nos. 4 and 5) were identified, GrpE and GroES, which were highly phosphorylated at both normal and high temperatures. Heat stress had no visible effect on phosphorylation of GrpE, and it caused a decrease in phosphorylation of GroES (Figures 13.5.3c and 13.5.3d). These changes might reflect the effect of a heat shock on the rate of translation of these proteins, on the activity of phosphatases, and/or on the activity of STPKs that use these proteins as the substrates. 

## 13.5.3.4 GroES is a substrate for phosphorylation by SpkC, SpkF, and SpkK

Earlier, it was demonstrated that the expression of the groESL operon and the amount of GroES protein increase after 60 min of heat shock (Slabas et al., 2006; Suzuki et al., 2006). Our experimental setup (44 ◦C for 30 min) did not reveal any significant changes in the amount of GroES (Figure 13.5.3b); however, distinct changes in its Ser–Thr phosphorylation in vitro were observed (Figure 13.5.3d). The phosphorylated form of GroES was reported in E. coli (Macek et al., 2008), although the functional significance of such phosphorylation is still unknown. In Synechocystis, GroES is phosphorylated by STPKs at normal growth conditions and under heat stress, which even caused a reduction in GroES Ser–Thr phosphorylation. 

To confirm that GroES is a putative substrate for STPKs in Synechocystis and to identify the individual STPK that may participate in phosphorylation of GroES, we applied in vitro phosphorylation with the purified recombinant GroES (see Figure 13.5.2) as a substrate for the protein kinase. Wild-type and 11 mutant (ΔspkB-L) cells were treated at 44 $^ \circ \mathrm { C }$ for 30 min, and soluble protein fractions were isolated from each strain. Twelve protein fractions isolated from those cells were equalized by protein content and used for phosphorylation of equal amounts of the purified recombinant GroES in the presence of $( \gamma - ^ { 3 2 } \mathrm { P } )$ ATP. The products of reaction were resolved with SDS-PAGE, and phosphorylated GroES was visualized by autoradiography (Figure 13.5.4a). Since equal amounts of isolated proteins were incubated with equal amounts of the recombinant GroES in the presence of equal amounts of $( \gamma ^ { \mathrm { - } 3 2 } \mathrm { P ) A T P } ,$ it is possible to reliably validate the data on GroES phosphorylation presented in Figure 13.5.4a.

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/d070d93a-9a38-4d08-ab41-83365fc0221e/ab6e4f55df6eca2ba776387f28c43b1de4c13f4b2337785ba68a2ce6a1f81080.jpg)



Figure 13.5.4 Phosphorylation in vitro of recombinant GroES by soluble protein fractions isolated from Synechocystis sp. PCC6803 (GS), spk mutants, and some of the complemented mutants. (a) Autoradiographs and CBB-stained gel correspond to GroES, which was in vitro phosphorylated by protein extracts obtained from the wild-type cells of Synechocystis (GS) and spk mutants grown $\mathrm { a t } 3 2 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ and incubated for 30 min at $4 4 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ . (b) Similar reactions have been performed with wild-type cells of Synechocystis (GS), and with the mutant strains defective in SpkF, SpkC, and SpkK, which have been complemented with $\mathrm { p V Z - } s p k C , \mathrm { p V Z - } s p k F ,$ and pVZ–spkK, respectively. Zorina et al. (2011). Reproduced with permission of Oxford University Press.


Phosphorylation of GroES was relatively low with the protein extracts isolated from wild-type cells and the mutant defective in spkG that had been grown at $3 2 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ Phosphorylation, however, increased in the extracts obtained from heat-stressed cells. Other mutants could be divided into the following groups according to their pattern of GroES phosphorylation. Compared to wild-type cells grown at $3 2 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ , mutants defective in spkB and $s p k L$ actively phosphorylated GroES at $3 2 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ Heat stress at 44 $^ \circ \mathrm { C }$ caused a severe decrease in the level of GroES phosphorylation. Mutants defective in spkD, spkE, spkH, spkI, and spkJ phosphorylated GroES less intensively than the ΔspkG-mutant or wild-type cells at $3 2 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ . Heat stress at $4 4 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ also caused a decrease in GroES phosphorylation in those mutants. Finally, three mutants defective in spkC, spkF, and spkK displayed nearly null levels of GroES phosphorylation either at normal or at high temperatures. Thus, in Synechocystis, small co-chaperonin GroES is a target of phosphorylation by STPKs. It is unlikely that SpkC, SpkF, and SpkK all phosphorylate GroES. If this would be the case, the other two kinases should successfully phosphorylate GroES in vitro when the third kinase is knocked out in a mutant. However, this is not the case. We suggest that these three protein kinases compose a chain or a cascade, and they phosphorylate one another with the final target of GroES. The place of each kinase in this cascade and the order of their functioning are still unclear, but it could be suggested that they might work in the following order: SpkF (transmembrane) → SpkC (membrane associated) → SpkK (soluble). In any case, only a cascade mode of STPKs action may explain a loss of GroES phosphorylation in each and any of these three mutants. 

In order to prove such a sequential (one after another) way of action, we complemented these three mutations in the mutants defective in SpkF, SpkC, and SpkK with corresponding genes expressed in the autonomously replicating plasmid pVZ321 under control of their own promoters. Complementation of any of the STPKs restores the ability of cells to phosphorylate GroES (Figure 13.5.4b). This confirms the suggestion that the SpkF, SpkC, and SpkK might comprise a cascade to phosphorylate the GroES. 

To ensure that the recombinant GroES is not phosphorylated by E. coli cells, we compared the patterns of phosphorylation obtained with Synechocystis and E. coli soluble protein extracts. Figure 13.5.5 demonstrates that E. coli cells did not phosphorylate expressed and/or externally provided recombinant GroES protein. Thus, we conclude that phosphorylation of GroES demonstrated in Figure 13.5.4 entirely results from the activity of the cyanobacterial STPKs. 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/d070d93a-9a38-4d08-ab41-83365fc0221e/fce3dbd6956d289e30f90cdca3e8892f16e7a035947320d6924ab75ed9d41cce.jpg)



Figure 13.5.5 Phosphorylation of the recombinant GroES protein by soluble protein fractions obtained from Synechocystis (a) and E. coli (b). Soluble protein fraction was obtained from cells of Synechocystis GS and E. coli BL21 transformed with pET-GroES. GroES was induced by 50 mM IPTG for 3 h. The purified recombinant GroES was added to each protein fraction in the presence of $( \gamma ^ { . 3 2 } \mathrm { P } ) \mathrm { A T P } .$ Upper panel represents the results of phosphorylation of exogenous GroES. Lower panel shows parts of gels stained with CBB. Adapted from Zorina et al. (2011).


To make sure that we were dealing with a Ser–Thr type of protein phosphorylation, the stability of the in vitro phosphorylated proteins was examined at different pHs. Proteins were isolated from control and heat-stressed wild-type cells, phosphorylated with $( \gamma ^ { \mathrm { - } 3 2 } \mathrm { P ) A T P } ,$ separated by electrophoresis, blotted onto PVDF, and stained with Ponceau red. The portions of the membrane containing the GroES were incubated in solutions with different pHs: 1.0, 7.0, and 14.0. The majority of phosphate incorporated into GroES was released upon incubation at $4 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 2 h at pH 14 (Figure 13.5.6a). N-Phosphate amino acids (phoshohistidine, phospholysine, and phosphoarginine) are stable at pH 14.0 but not at pH 1.0, whereas O-phosphate amino acids (phosphoserine and phosphothreonine) are stable at pH 1.0 but not at pH 14.0 (Duclos et al., 1991). On the other hand, acyl-phosphate amino acids (phosphoaspartate and phosphoglutamate) are labile at both pH 1.0 and 14.0, whereas the phosphotyrosine and phosphocysteine are stable at both pHs. Therefore, the stability profile for phosphorylated GroES is in agreement with the Ser–Thr nature of protein phosphorylation (Figure 13.5.6a). 

Additional confirmation of the presence of phosphoserines and phosphothreonines in the protein was obtained by Western analysis with the anti-pSer and anti-pThr specific monoclonal antibodies (Figure 13.5.6b). This experiment confirmed the presence of the Ser–Thr phosphorylation in the protein extract obtained from the wild-type cells of Synechocystis (Figure 13.5.6b, left panel), and the ability of this extract to phosphorylate Ser and Thr residues of the exogenous GroES (Figure 13.5.6b, central and right panels). 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/d070d93a-9a38-4d08-ab41-83365fc0221e/02bdc96caa09c83d558096d318f431f1ef637864c0df86946c3724e4e3aec672.jpg)



Figure 13.5.6 Stability of phosphorylated GroES at different pHs (a) and immunoblots of proteins from the wild-type cells of Synechocystis probed with monoclonal antibodies against phosphorylated Ser and Thr (b). Adapted from Zorina et al. (2011).


Direct determination of the phosphorylated amino acid residues in GroES by quadruple tandem mass spectrometry (MS-MS) analysis of the phosphorylated GroES protein revealed that $\mathrm { T h r } ^ { 6 1 }$ and ${ \mathrm { S e r } } ^ { 6 3 }$ were phosphorylated. 

## 13.5.3.5 Ser–Thr phosphorylation of the soluble proteins isolated from the ??hrcA mutant

The exact number of heat-inducible genes that are under negative control of the histidine kinase Hik34 is not known (Suzuki et al., 2005). Some of these genes (like the groESL operon) are also negatively controlled by the transcription factor HrcA (Sll1670) (Nakamoto et al., 2003). It is reasonable to suggest that HrcA- and/or Hik34-deficient mutants would actively express heat shock genes and demonstrate the maximum level of Ser– Thr phosphorylation already at $3 2 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ . Indeed, total Ser–Thr phosphorylation was much higher in ΔhrcA cells than in wildtype cells (Figure 13.5.7). Among individual polypeptides, those with nos. 1, 2, 4, 6, and 7 were phosphorylated most intensively. In ΔhrcA cells grown at $3 2 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ the phosphorylation level of polypeptides 1, 2, and 6 was higher (Figure 13.5.7a) than in wildtype cells exposed to $4 4 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Figure 13.5.3d). When ΔhrcA cells grown at $3 2 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ were exposed to $4 4 \ { } ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ the extent of phosphorylation of these polypeptides increased at least two times compared to $3 2 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ –grown mutant cells (Figures 13.5.7a and 13.5.7b), whereas that of the polypeptide no. 4 decreased more than three times. Heat treatment at 44 $^ \circ C$ did not affect phosphorylation of phosphoprotein no. 7, resembling its temperature-independent behavior in wild-type cells (Figures 13.5.3c and 13.5.3d). The observed patterns of phosphorylation are in agreement with the repressive nature of HrcA regulation. Our results suggest that most genes for Ser–Thr-phosphorylated proteins might be under the control of transcription factor HrcA (Figure 13.5.7). 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/d070d93a-9a38-4d08-ab41-83365fc0221e/5e26b0d90bdb4862c030ae5e3660d9c15b9f7412ac56649b5d8a8da0b73df4a1.jpg)


![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/d070d93a-9a38-4d08-ab41-83365fc0221e/f5aec0ebc5b606225afa5b64661b5b9db4293cdaaa73bbb6ff99b6fb69feb216.jpg)



Figure 13.5.7 Phosphorylation in vitro of soluble protein fraction isolated from Synechocystis GS ΔHrcA mutant treated at $4 4 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 30 min. Autoradiographs of phosphoprotein patterns at $3 2 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (a) and $4 4 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (b) and the corresponding Western blots (c,d) developed with anti-Hsp60 (GroEL) antibodies are presented. Dashed circles designate the GroEL positions. Zorina et al. (2011). Reproduced with permission of Oxford University Press.


The most striking difference between wild-type and ΔhrcA cells was that the latter displayed a phosphorylated protein, which was similar to 60 kDa chaperonin GroEL (see Chapter 14.1), in terms of its molecular weight and pI characteristics. This protein in its phosphorylated form was visible only in ΔhrcA cells grown at $3 2 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Figure 13.5.7a), and it was not observed in cells exposed to $4 4 ~ { } ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Figure 13.5.7b). Crossreaction of this polypeptide with antibodies against GroEL (Figures 13.5.7c and 13.5.7d) revealed the presence of GroEL in protein extract obtained from both $3 2 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ –grown and $4 4 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } -$ treated cells. Since in vitro phosphorylation demonstrates only de novo incorporation of exogenously provided $( \gamma ^ { - 3 2 } \mathrm { P } ) \mathrm { A T P }$ into polypeptides due to intrinsic protein kinase activities, it did not display any phosphorylation that had already occurred in cells or cell extract before the addition of radioactively labeled ATP. In the ΔhrcA cells, GroEL should be expressed constitutively at 

$3 2 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ and $4 4 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ . It is very likely that GroEL was actively phosphorylated at $3 2 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ and, therefore, the exposure of cells to $4 4 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ did not contribute significantly to Ser–Thr phosphorylation in vitro of GroEL, as shown in Figure 13.5.7. It is also possible that phosphorylation of GroEL occurs within a few minutes under heat stress conditions, and additional incubation (30–60 min) at 44 $^ \circ \mathrm { C }$ did not further affect its phosphorylation status (Figure 13.5.7b). 

It is known that chaperonin GroEL can prevent misfolding of polypeptides and promote the refolding and proper assembly of unfolded proteins that had been generated under heat stress (see Chapter 14.1). It is generally accepted that in E. coli cells, heat stress promotes reversible phosphorylation of GroEL (Sherman and Goldberg, 1992, 1994), which is necessary for the formation of an active oligomeric GroESL complex. However, in Synechocystis mutant cells that lack the transcription regulator HrcA, GroEL is produced, and it might be constitutively phosphorylated by STPKs. 

Phosphorylation of GroES (phosphoprotein no. 5) was lower in the ΔhrcA mutant (Figure 13.5.7a) than in wild-type cells at $3 2 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Figure 13.5.3c). After 30 min heat treatment at $4 4 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ the signal from GroES in ΔhrcA mutant cells (Figure 13.5.7b) became comparable with that of wild-type cells exposed to $4 4 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Figure 13.5.3d). Therefore, it seems that in the ΔhrcA mutant, phosphorylation of GroES also occurred constitutively at $3 2 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ , and a decrease in its phosphorylation at $4 4 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ can result from a decrease in the activity of the corresponding STPK(s). 

## 13.5.3.6 Ser–Thr phosphorylation of the soluble proteins isolated from the ??hik34 mutant

Histidine protein kinase Hik34 (Slr1285) is a negative regulator for the expression of heat shock genes. Inactivation of hik34 upregulates transcription of groESL, htpG, hspA, dnaK2, and slr1634 (Suzuki et al., 2005), whereas inactivation of hrcA upregulates only groESL and cpn60 (Singh et al., 2006). DNA microarray analysis demonstrated that a mutation in hik34 does not affect transcription of the hrcA gene at 34 or 44 ◦C (Suzuki et al., 2005). Possible interaction of Hik34 and HrcA has been suggested (Singh et al., 2006), although experimental evidence is still unavailable. 

Here, we examined the phosphorylation patterns in wild-type and in mutant cells that lack histidine kinase Hik34. It should be noted that the parental strain for the Hik34 mutant possessed the glucose-tolerant (GT) phenotype, whereas all other mutants were generated with a parental strain, which had a glucosesensitive (GS) phenotype (Ikeuchi and Tabata, 2001). The protein patterns of GS and GT cells were similar (Figures 13.5.3a,b and 13.5.8a,b). The intensity of Ser–Thr phosphorylation in the protein extracts of GT cells was, in general, lower than that of GS cells (Figures 13.5.3c,d and 13.5.8c,d). 

Heat stress caused alterations in the phosphorylation pattern in Δhik34 cells that differed from those observed in GS cells. There was no effect of temperature on phosphoproteins nos. 1 and 3a/3b. Phosphorylation of phosphoproteins 2 and 6 increased, although to a lesser extent than was observed in GS cells. In Δhik34 cells, constitutive phosphorylation of phosphoprotein 7 was lower than in GS cells. Heat stress, however, caused the increase in its phosphorylation (Figure 13.5.9a,b). 

In Δhik34 cells grown at $3 2 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ phosphorylation of GroES was lower than in GS cells, but higher than in ΔhrcA cells. Heat stress for 30 min diminished GroES phosphorylation (Figure 13.5.7b), although to a lesser extent than in GS and ΔhrcA cells (Figures 13.5.3 and 13.5.7). 

The GroEL chaperonin appears in equal amounts in Δhik34 cells at both 32 and $4 4 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ as revealed by Western blotting (Figures 13.5.9c,d). However, we could not find even traces of its phosphorylation in vitro. This implies that GroEL might be rapidly and intensively phosphorylated already at $3 2 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ . Slabas et al. (2006) identified five spots that corresponded to GroEL1 and three spots that correspond to GroEL2 (Cpn60). The antibodies used in our assay were raised against a mixture of GroEL1 and GroEL2, and they certainly recognize both forms of this chaperonin. There is no doubt about the constitutive expression of GroEL in ΔhrcA and Δhik34 cells, but there is a serious doubt about its temperature-dependent phosphorylation. Also, comparison of the phosphorylation patterns for GroEL in ΔhrcA and Δhik34 mutant cells leads to the suggestion that phosphorylation of GroEL somehow depends on the activity of HrcA, but not on the activity of Hik34. 

A recent phosphoproteomic study of E. coli, which employed the enrichment of phosphorylated proteins, also did not demonstrate any phosphorylated GroEL at normal growth temperature (Macek et al., 2008). However, the phosphorylated GroES was apparent in E. coli cells. Since GroES and GroEL are encoded in one operon in E. coli and Synechocystis (the latter organism has an additional gene for a GroEL-like protein, called groEL2 or cpn60), it is reasonable to expect their co-transcription, cotranslation, and, finally, 1:1 ratio in cells. Differences in their Ser–Thr phosphorylation observed in Synechocystis imply that either (i) they have different affinities to similar or the same STPKs, or (ii) they are phosphorylated by different protein kinases. Anyhow, it is now evident that the expression of the groESL operon is regulated by HrcA and Hik34, but phosphorylation of GroEL and GroES is regulated differentially, with possible participation of HrcA but not Hik34.

## 13.5.4 Conclusions



Kamei A, Yuasa T, Geng X, Ikeuchi M. 2002. Biochemical examination of the potential eukaryotic-type protein kinase genes in the complete genome of the unicellular cyanobacterium Synechocystis sp. PCC 6803. DNA Research 9: 71–78. 





Suzuki I, Kanesaki Y, Hayashi H, Hall JJ, Simon WJ, et al. 2005. The histidine kinase Hik34 is involved in thermotolerance by regulating the expression of heat shock genes in Synechocystis. Plant Physiology 138: 1409–1421. 



STPKs are involved in phosphorylation of several proteins during routine growth and under heat stress in Synechocystis. The small co-chaperonin GroES is phosphorylated by STPKs, as it was previously found for 60 kDa chaperonin GroEL. Three STPKs, SpkC, SpkF, and SpkK, might comprise a regulatory cascade that ensures phosphorylation of the GroES. Phosphorylation in vitro of the recombinant GroES demonstrated that heat stress at 44 $^ \circ C$ caused an increase in its phosphorylation by STPKs. However, such increase was not observed in our in vitro assays that did not contain externally provided GroES. Our data imply that Ser–Thr phosphorylation in Synechocystis occurs intensively and constitutively at normal growth temperature.

## Chaperonins and stress

# GroEL/ES chaperonin: unfolding and refolding reactions

Victor V. Marchenkov, Nataliya A. Ryabova, Olga M. Selivanova, and Gennady V. Semisotnov 

Institute of Protein Research, Russian Academy of Sciences, Pushchino, Moscow Region, Russia 

## 14.1.1 Introduction

Long-term studies of denaturation and renaturation of proteins confirm Anfinsen’s hypothesis that all necessary and sufficient information about the spatial structure of the protein is contained in its amino acid sequence (Anfinsen, 1973; Dill and Mac-Callum, 2012). However, the study of cell metabolism under a variety of cellular stresses revealed several protein factors called molecular chaperones, which are involved in folding of proteins or regulation of distribution of newly synthesized proteins among competing pathways of folding and aggregation (Ellis, 1987; Frydman, 2001; Hartl et al., 2011). Furthermore, chaperones assist assembly of oligomeric complexes, transmembrane transport of polypeptide chains, and their degradation (Ellis, 2006; Frydman, 2001; Gething, 1997; Hartl and Hayen-Hartl, 2002). Thus, molecular chaperones create optimal conditions for substrate protein folding by eliminating incorrect or unfavorable intermolecular contacts. Molecular chaperones are found in both prokaryotic and eukaryotic organisms, and some of them represent complex oligomeric proteins consisting of many subunits (with a molecular weight of 10 to 100 kDa each), usually combined in a single- or double-ring structure (Horwich et al., 2007; Yebenes et al., 2011). Therefore, their own folding is an intriguing issue. 

In this chapter, we analyze our own and literature data on denaturation (unfolding) and renaturation (refolding) of the most intensively studied chaperones – heat shock proteins of Escherichia coli cells GroEL (Hsp 60) and its partner GroES (Hsp 10), often called chaperonins (Horwich et al., 2007) (see Chapters 14.2–14.5). GroEL is an oligomeric protein consisting of 14 identical subunits (with a molecular weight of 60 kDa each), arranged in two interacting ring-like structures containing seven subunits each (Braig et al., 1994). In the presence of adenine nucleotides (adenosine diphosphate (ADP) or adenosine triphosphate (ATP)) and $\mathrm { M g } ^ { 2 + }$ , this protein interacts with another oligomeric protein, GroES, that consists of seven identical subunits (with a molecular weight of 10 kDa each) combined into a dome-like structure (Hunt et al., 1996; Xu et al., 1997). 

Using a number of physical-chemical techniques such as transverse urea gradient electrophoresis, native and denaturing electrophoresis, fluorescence, circular dichroism (CD), light scattering, and electron microscopy, we studied equilibrium and kinetic processes of GroEL and GroES unfolding and refolding; in addition, we revealed and characterized some intermediates accumulating during these processes. 

Investigation of denaturation and renaturation of chaperonin GroEL and its co-chaperonin GroES is important for two major issues: first, the mechanism of folding of proteins involved in other proteins’ folding; and, second, the mechanism of selforganization of large oligomeric proteins that has hitherto been poorly understood due to difficulties in selection of conditions for their effective folding in vitro. 

## 14.1.2 Materials and methods

Solutions: For buffer preparations, we used ATP and ADP Na salts, phenylmethylsulfonyl fluoride (PMSF), L-histidine (all Sigma-Aldrich, USA); white egg lysozyme, NaCl, KCl, $( \mathrm { N H _ { 4 } } ) _ { 2 } \mathrm { S O _ { 4 } } , \mathrm { u r e a } , \mathrm { M g C l _ { 2 } } , \mathrm { M g C O _ { 3 } }$ (all Reakhim, Russia); and β-mercaptoethanol (βME), Coomassie brilliant blue G250, aminonaphtalensulfonate (ANS), and Tris (all Serva, Germany). All solutions were prepared using bidistilled water. 

Proteins: GroEL and GroES were purified from E. coli cells (strain HB101) transformed with the multicopy plasmid pGroE4 (GroE operon E. coli cloned at the EcoR I site of the pACYC 184 vector) according to the published protocols (Clark et al., 1998; Lissin et al., 1990; Ryabova et al., 

Dedication: This work is dedicated to the memory of Professor Alexander S. Girshowich, who initiated studies on GroEL functioning and folding at the Institute of Protein Research, Russian Academy of Sciences, 25 years ago. 

2014). The pET-based construct, including T7SR1, as well as the SR1 purification protocol were kindly provided by Dr Arthur L. Horwich and Dr Wayne A. Fenton (Yale School of Medicine, New Haven, CT, USA), and they are described in Fenton et al. (1994) and Weissman et al. (1995). The folded monomeric form of GroEL was prepared by size exclusion chromatography on Superose 6 of the protein incubated in 6 M urea for 40 min (Lissin et al., 1990). The elution buffer contained 20 mM Tris-HCl, pH 7.5, 100 mM (NH4)2SO4, 5 mM βME, and 10 mM MgCl2. The protein purity was controlled by denaturing and nondenaturing electrophoresis as well as by fluorescence and absorption spectra. 

Protein refolding and reassembly: Refolding and reassembly of GroEL and GroES were performed by dilution of the protein solution in 6 M urea to 0.4 M urea in the presence of various factors with intensive stirring. In some cases, GroEL reassembly was started at $0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ to diminish its nonspecific aggregation, followed by incubation at $2 0 { - } 2 2 \ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ . Slow reactions of GroEL reassembly (minute time interval) were initiated manually, whereas fast reactions of protein refolding and reassembly (millisecond and second time intervals) were registered using the stopped-flow mode of a Chirascan spectropolarimeter (Applied Photophysics, UK). 

Electrophoresis: Sodium dodecyl sulfate polyacrylamide gel electrophoresis (SDS-PAGE) was performed according to the well-known Laemmli method (Laemmli, 1970). Urea transverse gradient electrophoresis was performed mostly according to Goldenberg and Creighton (1984) and Ryabova et al. (2014). Blue native electrophoresis allowing evaluation of the native protein molecular weight was performed according to Schagger and von Jagow (1991) using Coomassie brilliant blue G250 dye. The second-dimension SDS-PAGE was performed according to a published protocol (Ryabova et al., 2014). 

Circular dichroism, fluorescence, and light scattering: Far-UV CD spectra were followed using the steady mode of a Chirascan spectropolarimeter (Applied Photophysics) at a range of 190–250 nm with a light path length of 0.1 mm. Fluorescence intensity and anisotropy as well as light scattering at 90◦ were measured with a Cary Eclipse spectrofluorimeter (Varian, USA) at protein concentrations of 0.1–1.0 mg/mL (light scattering) and 0.05–0.15 mg/mL (fluorescence). Slow kinetics of GroEL reassembly was performed in a standard 1×1×4 cm quartz cell with magnetic stirrer mixing. The approximation of kinetic curves was made using the computer program Sigma Plot (Systat Software, Inc., USA) and equation f $= \mathrm { A _ { 1 } e x p ( k _ { 1 } t ) } + \mathrm { A _ { 2 } e x p ( k _ { 2 } t ) }$ . The protein concentration was determined from absorption spectra at a wavelength of 280 nm using a Cary 100 Bio spectrophotometer (Varian, USA) and extinction coefficients (A0.1%1 cm): 0.22 for full-size GroEL14, 0.19 for the GroEL1 folded monomer, and 0.14 for GroES (Lissin et al., 1990). 

Electron microscopy: To visualize the products of GroEL oligomerization, a JEM-100C transmission electron microscope (Jeol, Japan) was used. Aliquots of the protein reassembly reaction mixture (10 μL) 40 min after reaction initiation were loaded on 400 mesh copper net covered with a thin film. After 3 min incubation for protein adsorption, the net was washed with distilled water for 40 s. The contrasting by 1% uranilacetate was performed for 90 s, and the net was aired before measurements. 

## 14.1.3 Results and discussion

## 14.1.3.1 Equilibrium unfolding and refolding of GroEL and GroES

GroEL denaturation and renaturation were first studied more than 20 years ago, to a large extent initiated by Lissin et al. (1990). The authors reported that the complex tetradecameric GroEL particle can be reconstructed in vitro from the ureaunfolded monomeric state in the presence of $\mathrm { M g ^ { 2 + } }$ and ATP. Reconstruction began with formation of a folded monomeric form $\mathrm { G r o E L } _ { \mathrm { m } } ,$ after urea removal from the protein solution by gel filtration. This monomeric form of GroEL (subunit) was characterized by a high level of the secondary structure and compactness, but was less temperature- and urea-resistant than when it was within the full tetradecameric GroEL particle (see also Lissin and Hemmingsen, 1993; Surin et al., 1999). The folded monomeric form $\mathrm { ( G r o E L _ { m } ) }$ was also characterized by a high content of substantially solvent-exposed hydrophobic clusters, as compared with the subunit of the GroEL oligomer (Horowitz et al., 1995; Surin et al., 1999). GroEL equilibrium unfolding and refolding reactions in the absence of $\mathrm { M g } ^ { 2 + }$ and ATP were recently studied by us using transverse urea gradient electrophoresis (Ryabova et al., 2014). A change in electrophoretic mobility of the protein at a constant charge reflects the change in its hydrodynamic volume (Creighton, 1979; Goldenberg and Creighton, 1984). It was shown that the GroEL hydrodynamic volume changes in two stages: First it decreases at 2 M urea, and then markedly increases. The former is apparently a result of dissociation of the oligomeric GroEL particle. This assumption is supported by the fact that, in this range of urea concentrations, there is a sharp decrease in the intensity of light scattering (Gorovits et al., 1995) and X-ray scattering (Arai et al., 2003). The second stage is connected with further unfolding of dissociated GroEL. During GroEL renaturation from its urea-unfolded state in the absence of $\mathrm { M g } ^ { 2 + }$ and ATP, its hydrodynamic volume decreases in two well-distinguished stages (Ryabova et al., 2014). However, these stages reflect changes in hydrodynamic volume during GroEL monomer (subunit) refolding because the product of GroEL renaturation near 0.5 M urea has a monomer-like electrophoretic mobility (Ryabova et al., 2014). The presence of two stages in refolding of the GroEL monomeric form seems to be associated with sequential folding of GroEL subunit domains. Figure 14.1.1 represents the changes in electrophoretic mobility, as well as in CD at 220 nm, tyrosine fluorescence, and anisotropy, during urea-induced unfolding of the folded monomeric form of GroEL. 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/d070d93a-9a38-4d08-ab41-83365fc0221e/d3b86f1c2c6a73251f04962986cc289ee475849d27da102f720b8d9d74d1f4bf.jpg)


![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/d070d93a-9a38-4d08-ab41-83365fc0221e/153729dd4f615ef457b2c58dc4e4afec2635e19e38cde46e03f6db49d580298f.jpg)


![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/d070d93a-9a38-4d08-ab41-83365fc0221e/69d2e14194ead4b45a0a4b7c74f5c43a4c50fb77bd753a8985709f5ba70b5d2d.jpg)



Figure 14.1.1 (a–c) Denaturation (unfolding) of the GroEL folded monomeric form (subunit): (a) transverse urea gradient gel electrophoresis (folded monomer was loaded on the gel, Cprot = 0.5 mg/mL, 20 mM Tris-HCl, pH 7.5, 5 mM βME, 10 mM MgCl ); urea-induced unfolding transitions registered by ellipticity at 220 nm (b), and by tyrosine fluorescence (∙) and anisotropy (■) (c) (Cprot = 0.1 mg/mL, 20 mM Tris-HCl, pH 7.5, 5 mM βME, 10 mM MgCl ).


It is clear that urea-induced unfolding of the GroEL monomeric form (subunit) displays the two-stage nature similar to its refolding (Ryabova et al., 2014). Under conditions favorable for oligomeric GroEL dissociation (∼2 M urea), the monomer is partly unfolded probably due to unfolding of the equatorial domain, which is predominantly responsible for intersubunit interactions (Braig et al., 1994). This assumption is consistent with the literature data (Gorovits et al., 1995), indicating that the apical domain of the GroEL subunit retains the residual structure at moderate concentrations of urea. 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/d070d93a-9a38-4d08-ab41-83365fc0221e/89bef88a485f213e7cebe240e7e6403acdaab8d3ec97c10d62ba103c5045c183.jpg)



Figure 14.1.2 (a,b) Transverse urea gradient gel electrophoresis of GroES: (a) unfolding (native GroES solution was applied to the gel, Cprot = 0.5 mg/mL, 20 mM Tris-HCl, pH 7.5, 5 mM βME, 10 mM MgCl2); (b) refolding (urea-unfolded GroES was applied to the gel, Cprot = 0.5 mg/mL, 20 mM Tris-HCl, pH 7.5, 5 mM βME, 10 mM MgCl , 6 M urea).


Urea-induced unfolding of heptameric co-chaperonin GroES is also a fully reversible process. Figure 14.1.2 clearly shows that, similar to GroEL, a change in GroES hydrodynamic volume during its urea-induced unfolding occurs in two distinct steps. In the range between 1.5 and 3.5 M urea, the GroES oligomeric structure dissociates with a decrease in the hydrodynamic volume, and then final unfolding of the dissociated form increases the hydrodynamic volume (Figure 14.1.2a). The protein refolding is fully reversible. GroES monomers (subunits) reach a certain (probably partially folded) conformational state competent to a specific oligomerization. Then, the subunits undergo oligomerization to form a native GroES heptamer (Figure 14.1.2b). This result is entirely consistent with the literature data on denaturation and renaturation of GroES (Hsp 10), indicating the high reversibility of these processes. GroES stability studies by differential scanning calorimetry and CD with various compositions of the solvent (Boudker et al., 1997) show that temperature-induced unfolding/refolding of this protein is a spontaneous reversible process, and describe a highly cooperative transition between assembly and dissociation of GroES heptameric structure. The folded state of monomeric GroES is energetically unfavorable, and therefore, it does not accumulate in significant quantities in the course of unfolding. In this regard, stabilization of the GroES structure occurs mostly due to intersubunit interactions (Boudker et al., 1997). High reversibility of GroES denaturation has also been shown for its unfolding by guanidine hydrochloride (Guidry et al., 2000; Higurashi et al., 1999) or urea (Seale et al., 1996). The possible existence of a folded monomeric form of GroES under nondenaturing conditions was demonstrated by mechanical unfolding of covalently linked GroES subunits using atomic force microscopy (Sakane et al., 2009). However, reversibility of GroES denaturation depends substantially on the time of incubation of the protein in the denatured state (Boudker et al., 1997), and may be due to its aggregation (Iwasa et al., 2011; Seale et al., 1996). 

## 14.1.3.2 Refolding kinetics of GroEL and GroES

Refolding of the GroEL monomeric form (subunit) at moderate concentrations occurs with high efficiency (Ryabova et al., 2014) and does not require any additional factors (Lissin et al., 1990; Ybarra and Horowitz, 1995). Moreover, here we demonstrate that refolding of the GroEL monomeric form (subunit) is sufficiently fast, despite its high molecular weight (∼60 kDa) and domain organization (Braig et al., 1994). Figure 14.1.3 represents the refolding kinetics of the GroEL monomeric form, registered by restoration of tyrosine fluorescence and CD at 220 nm. 

It can be seen that kinetics of tyrosine fluorescence recovery is well described by the single exponential process with a rate constant of ${ \sim } 0 . 2 0 ~ \mathrm { s } ^ { - 1 } ~ ( \mathrm { t } _ { 1 / 2 } \sim 3 . 5 ~ \mathrm { s } )$ . The secondary structure of the GroEL monomeric form is also rapidly recovering, but this process occurs in three well-separated kinetic stages. The major part of the secondary structure of the GroEL subunit (∼60%) is formed within the dead time of kinetic experiments $( < 1 0 ^ { - 2 } \ s )$ . The rest is formed within a measurable time interval through two kinetic stages: (i) about 30% with the characteristic time $\mathrm { t } _ { 1 / 2 }$ ${ \sim } 0 . 7 \ s ,$ and (ii) ∼10% with $\mathbf { t } _ { 1 / 2 } \sim 7 ~ \mathbf { s } .$ This is typical for refolding kinetics of numerous globular proteins and goes through fast formation of a “molten globule”–like intermediate state followed by formation of less stable secondary structure elements (Kuwajima et al., 1993). Probably, sequential refolding of GroEL subunit domains also plays some role in this process. Interestingly, under conditions initiating specific oligomerization of the GroEL monomeric form (e.g., in the presence of $\mathrm { M g ^ { 2 + } }$ and glycerol) (Lissin, 1995; Ryabova et al., 2014), the characteristic times of GroEL subunit refolding kinetic stages remain practically unchanged, but an amplitude of the burst phase and the slowest third step are significantly increased (Figure 14.1.3c,d). This may indicate that some additional secondary structure elements of GroEL subunits, which are possibly responsible for intersubunit interactions, are stabilized under oligomerization conditions.

Figure 14.1.4 (a–c) Identification of GroEL reassembly reaction components: (a) native blue electrophoresis, SR1 GroEL mutant (1), 2400 s reassembly in the absence (2) and presence (3) of GroES; (b) the second-dimension SDS electrophoresis of protein bands presented in native blue electrophoresis, SR1 GroEL mutant (1), complex of $\mathrm { \ G r o E L _ { 1 4 } }$ with GroES7 (2), and complex of an intermediate oligomer of GroEL with GroES7 (3); (c) electron microscopy data: butt-ends of $\mathrm { G r o E L _ { 1 4 } }$ or GroEL7 free from GroES (1) and occupied by GroES (2), side views of symmetric (3) or asymmetric (4) complexes of GroEL14 with $\mathrm { G r o E S } _ { 7 } ,$ and complex of the one-ring GroEL7 intermediate with GroES7 (5).


Kinetics of GroEL oligomerization was studied by us previously using light scattering and native blue electrophoresis (Ryabova et al., 2014). It was shown that the time-dependent increase in the intensity of light scattering during oligomerization of GroEL folded monomeric forms initiated by $\mathrm { M g ^ { 2 + } }$ and ATP or $\mathrm { M g } ^ { 2 + }$ and glycerol is biphasic. The first phase is one order of magnitude faster than the second (final) phase. Both kinetic phases of the GroEL oligomerization reaction are dependent on protein concentration (Ryabova et al., 2014), thereby indicating their bimolecular nature. Two-phase kinetics of GroEL particle reassembly reflected accumulation of an intermediate oligomeric state containing a significant amount of GroEL subunits. 

An analysis of the GroEL oligomerization reaction by native blue electrophoresis (Ryabova et al., 2014) showed accumulation of an oligomeric intermediate with a molecular weight close to that of the one-ring GroEL mutant (i.e., consisting of seven subunits) (Weissman et al., 1995). Interestingly, this intermediate is able to interact with full-size GroES when it is present in a GroEL reassembly mixture (Ryabova et al., 2014). Here, we performed an electron microscopy analysis of products of the GroEL reassembly reaction in the presence of full-size GroES that revealed a complex of GroEL one-ring intermediate with full-size GroES, as well as asymmetric and symmetric complexes of full-size tetradecameric GroEL particles with GroES (Figure 14.1.4). Thus, the native blue electrophoresis (Ryabova et al., 2014) and electron microscopy (Figure 14.1.4) data unequivocally show the existence of a one-ring (seven subunits) intermediate in the GroEL reassembly pathway. Moreover, this intermediate interacts with full-size GroES. 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/d070d93a-9a38-4d08-ab41-83365fc0221e/590d02250a2efb65ce0c2922a997e2b88be88e130d686a38b9f490da65484521.jpg)


![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/d070d93a-9a38-4d08-ab41-83365fc0221e/3e4f4f231d41d05cb66e6206244d7892f84af725f42b319192af7c02a5bbe64d.jpg)



Figure 14.1.5 (a,b) Refolding kinetics of GroES at two protein concentrations registered by ANS probe binding (a) and light scattering (b). Renaturing buffer: 20 mM Tris-HCl, pH 8, 5 mM βME, 10 mM MgCl . Protein concentrations: 0.3 mg/mL (1) and 1 mg/mL (2). Rate constants of protein refolding are shown at kinetic curves.


GroES refolding kinetics was registered by affinity for hydrophobic probe (ANS) and by light scattering (Figure 14.1.5) at two protein concentrations. One can see that time-dependent increase of ANS fluorescence during GroES refolding occurs quite rapidly (rate constant ${ \sim } 2 ~ s ^ { - 1 } )$ and is practically independent of protein concentration. Thus, it can be assumed that this process is intramolecular and reflects, apparently, refolding of GroES subunits into some conformational state competent to further oligomerization. Note that this conformational state is characterized by high exposure of the protein hydrophobic clusters to solvent and, apparently, is not tightly packed. However, the rate constant of this process is consistent with literature data on the possible rate of GroES subunit refolding (Bascos et al., 2004). In contrast, a light-scattering intensity observed during GroES refolding rapidly increased with increasing protein concentration, which is indicative of the bimolecular nature of this process (i.e., GroES oligomerization). Note that the rates of both intramolecular and intermolecular processes of GroES refolding are considerably higher than those of GroEL (cf. Figure 14.1.5, Figure 14.1.3, and data reported by Ryabova et al. (2014)). 

## 14.1.3.3 The role of GroES in GroEL reassembly reaction

The study of GroEL reassembly reveals that in some cases the presence of only $\mathrm { M g } ^ { 2 + }$ and adenine nucleotides (ADP or ATP) is not enough for effective GroEL reassembly (Lissin and Hemmingsen, 1993; Lissin et al., 1990; Ryabova et al., 2014). This is true for low or moderate ionic strength of the solvent (Ryabova et al., 2014). 

Another reason may be low concentrations of GroEL. At GroEL concentrations below 0.1 mg/mL, protein reassembly is effective only in the presence of GroES (Ryabova et $a l . _ { \cdot }$ , 2014).Third, this may result from inappropriate temperatures (Figure 14.1.6), because at a temperature below $2 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ or above $4 5 \ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ reassembly of GroEL induced only by $\mathrm { M g ^ { 2 + } }$ and ATP is not effective, probably due to partial denaturation of its monomeric form (Lissin and Hemmingsen, 1993; Lissin et al.,

Figure 14.1.6 The effect of GroES on GroEL reassembly at various temperatures. Reaction mixtures contained 20 mM Tris-HCl, p ${ \mathrm { ~ H ~ } } 7 . 5 , 5$ mM βME, 100 mM $( \mathrm { N H } _ { 4 } ) _ { 2 } \mathrm { S O } _ { 4 } ,$ 10 mM $\mathrm { M g ^ { 2 + } }$ , and ATP in the absence and presence of two molar excess of GroES. $\mathrm { C p r o t } = 0 . 5$ mg/mL; incubation time $= 2 4 0 0 \ s .$ .


1990); addition of GroES remarkably stimulates this process (Figure 14.1.6). 

It seems that, under the above conditions, the intermediate one-ring oligomer that accumulates in the GroEL reassembly pathway fails to form a full-size GroEL particle because of its instability. At the same time, interaction of this one-ring intermediate with GroES increases its stability, thereby providing formation of a full-size GroEL particle that is stable in unfavorable conditions.

## 14.1.4 Conclusions



Kuwajima K, Semisotnov GV, Finkelstein AV, Sugai S, Ptitsyn OB. 1993. Secondary structure of globular proteins at the early and the final stages in protein folding. FEBS Letters 334(3): 265–268. 



Recently, we proposed a scheme of GroEL–GroES chaperonin refolding (Ryabova et al., 2013). According to this scheme, unfolded monomeric forms of GroEL and GroES (in vitro) or newly synthesized subunits of these proteins (in vivo) are able to assume quite rapidly a conformational state that is competent to oligomerization. At the same time, the rate of this process for GroES is one order of magnitude higher than that for GroEL. Furthermore, GroES subunits are able to undergo specific oligomerization in vitro (and possibly in vivo) in the absence of any additional factors (Figure 14.1.2) (Boudker et al., 1997; Guidry et al., 2000; Seale et al., 1996), whereas for GroEL, the presence of magnesium ions (presumably to suppress electrostatic repulsion of negatively charged subunits) and adenine nucleotides (presumably to stabilize some secondary structure elements responsible for specific intersubunit interaction) is critical (Lissin et al., 1990). In vitro, some solvent components (ammonium sulfate, glycerol, and high ionic strength) (Arai et al., 2003; Lissin, 1995; Ryabova et al., 2014; Ybarra and Horowitz, 1995) can play the role of GroEL physiological ligands. GroES is essential to stabilize the one-ring intermediate of GroEL important for full-size chaperonin formation. Note that the presence of GroES always increases the full-size GroEL yield during its reassembly (Lissin et al., 1990; Ryabova et al., 2014). However, the presence of GroES is of particular importance under unfavorable conditions (low GroEL concentration, low ionic strength, and unfavorable temperature) when stability of the GroEL one-ring intermediate seems to be very low. Probably for this reason, the GroES gene is placed before the groEL gene within the GroE operone of E. coli cells. Thus, GroEL ligands are actively involved not only in chaperonin functioning (Frydman, 2001; Gething, 1997; Hartl et al., 2011; Marchenkov and Semisotnov, 2009) but also in its assembly in vitro (and possibly in vivo) (Arai et al., 2003; Lissin et al., 1990; Ryabova et al., 2014; Ybarra and Horowitz, 1995).

# Functional comparison between the DnaK chaperone systems of Streptococcus intermedius and Escherichia coli

Toshifumi Tomoyasu and Hideaki Nagamune 

Department of Biological Science and Technology, Institute of Technology and Science, Tokushima University Graduate School, Minami-josanjima-cho, Tokushima, Japan 

## 14.2.1 Introduction

Streptococcus intermedius is a low-GC, Gram-positive bacterium and a common commensal organism of the oral cavity and gastrointestinal tract (Whitworth, 1990). S. intermedius is a member of the Anginosus group of streptococci and an opportunistic pathogen that is a known cause of deep-seated infections such as brain and liver abscesses (Whiley et al., 1990, 1992). This pathogen secretes a cytolysin called intermedilysin (ILY), which is encoded by the ily gene and is considered the major virulence factor for infectivity and cytotoxicity toward human cells (Nagamune et al., 1996; Sukeno et al., 2005). ILY is a member of the cholesterol-dependent cytolysin (CDC) family. In contrast to other CDC family members, this toxin can bind specifically to the human glycosylphosphatidylinositol-linked membrane protein, CD59, a regulator of the terminal pathway of the complement system in humans (Giddings et al., 2004). Therefore, S. intermedius seems primarily adapted to be a human pathogen. 

DnaK (Hsp70) is known to be a major molecular chaperone and is conserved in most living organisms. The activation of DnaK ATPase activity, which is closely coupled to its chaperone activity, requires the presence of co-chaperones DnaJ and GrpE (Genevaux et al., 2007; Mayer and Bukau, 1998). Accordingly, DnaK and its co-chaperones are generally called the DnaK chaperone system. The in vivo functions of DnaK have been well characterized using a dnaK null mutant of the Gram-negative bacterium E. coli (Paek and Walker, 1987). This mutant exhibits pleiotropic defects in cell division and stress resistance. The requirement of the DnaK chaperone system seems to vary in each species of microorganism. The DnaK chaperone systems of many bacteria have essential or important cellular functions similar to those in E. coli. However, in some Gram-positive bacteria, the system seems to have a less crucial function, and the system is not conserved in some archaea and a few Gramnegative bacteria (Gribaldo et al., 1999; Schulz et al., 1995; Warnecke, 2012). Recently, we succeeded in creating dnaK null mutants of S. intermedius and functionally expressing its DnaK chaperone system in an E. coli dnaK null mutant (Tomoyasu et al., 2012). Thus, in this review, we present data allowing comparison between the properties of the DnaK chaperone systems from S. intermedius and other organisms, with particular reference to E. coli. 

## 14.2.2 Properties of the S. intermedius DnaK chaperone system

As with DnaK of other Gram-positive bacteria and of archaea, S. intermedius DnaK lacks segments 79–101 and 208–211, which are found in the ATPase domain of DnaK from E. coli and most other proteobacteria (Gribaldo et al., 1999; Tomoyasu et al., 2012). S. intermedius DnaK showed 54, 48, and 26% identity to E. coli DnaK, DnaJ, and GrpE, respectively. Similarly to other Gram-positive bacteria, these genes encoding the DnaK chaperone system are localized at the hrcA–grpE– dnaK–dnaJ operon (dnaK operon). The heat shock response of many Gram-positive bacteria is controlled by the HrcA repressor, which binds to the CIRCE (controlling inverted repeat of chaperone expression) operator sequence (consensus sequence: TTAGCACTC-N9-GAGTGCTAA, N = any base) (Hecker et al., 1996; Schulz and Schumann, 1996). This operator sequence is known to occur in the promoter regions of the dnaK operon and the groES–groEL (groESL) operon (Hecker et al., 1996) (see Chapter 14.3). We searched the entire S. intermedius genome sequence and confirmed that only three regions are highly homologous with the CIRCE sequence. These are located in the promoter regions of hrcA and groES (unpublished data). In Streptococcus pneumoniae, the promoter regions of groES and hrcA have one CIRCE operator sequence and two CIRCE operator sequences, respectively (Kim et al., 2001). We also confirmed two CIRCE operator sequences in the hrcA promoter region of S. intermedius and other streptococci (Figure 14.2.1). Therefore, we hypothesized that two CIRCE sequences are required to control the cellular levels of HrcA and to fine-tune the level of heat shock induction in streptococci.

In addition to the DnaK chaperone system, ClpB, IbpA, and IbpB have important roles in protein quality control in E. coli (Kuczynska-Wi ´ ´snik et al., 2002; Mogk et al., 1999b). In particular, ClpB works with the DnaK chaperone system to disaggregate protein aggregates, and IbpA and IbpB are small heat shock proteins that prevent protein aggregation. However, no proteins showing significant homology to these chaperones exist in S. intermedius. 

## 14.2.3 Phenotypes of S. intermedius dnaK null mutants

S. intermedius dnaK null mutants exhibited a thermosensitive phenotype above 37 ◦C and a slow-growth phenotype at permissive temperatures (Tomoyasu et al., 2012). In addition, accumulation of GroEL was also observed as reported in a Lactococcus lactis dnaK null mutant and a Streptococcus mutans DnaK-depleted strain (Koch et al., 1998; Lemos et al., 2007). These phenotypes could be rescued by transcomplementation with an E. coli–Streptococcus shuttle vector containing the S. intermedius grpE–dnaK–dnaJ operon (Tomoyasu et al., 2012). Unlike E. coli dnaK null mutants, S. intermedius dnaK null mutants did not show detectable acid sensitivity. Refolding of acid-damaged protein(s) by the DnaK chaperone system might be less important for acid tolerance in S. intermedius than in E. coli. 

## 14.2.4 Function of the S. intermedius DnaK chaperone system in an E. coli dnaK null mutant

Since DnaK in Gram-positive bacteria and archaea lacks segments of the DnaK ATPase domain, it was considered possible that intrinsic DnaJ and GrpE in E. coli would not (or would only partially) be able to activate the Gram-positive DnaK (Mogk et al., 1999a; Sugimoto et al., 2007; Zhang et al., 2007). As expected, the expression of S. intermedius DnaK alone or with only one co-chaperone (DnaJ or GrpE) in E. coli dnaK null mutants (Paek and Walker, 1987) could not complement the thermosensitive phenotype (Figure 14.2.2). However, expression of the entire S. intermedius DnaK chaperone system perfectly complemented the phenotype. 

Indeed, the ATPase activity of purified S. intermedius DnaK could be induced by purified S. intermedius co-chaperones (DnaJ, GrpE), whereas only slight activation was observed using purified E. coli co-chaperones (unpublished data). Although S. intermedius dnaK null mutants did not show acid sensitivity, the S. intermedius DnaK chaperone system could complement the acid sensitivity of E. coli dnaK null mutants (Tomoyasu et al., 2012).

ΔK+Vec, E. coli dnaK null mutant transformed with an IPTG-inducible expression vector pZE13; ΔK+pECK, transformed with E. coli dnaK expressing vector; ΔK+pSIK, transformed with S. intermedius dnaK expressing vector; ΔK+pSIEK, transformed with S. intermedius grpE and dnaK expressing vector; ΔK+pSIKJ, transformed with S. intermedius dnaK and dnaJ expressing vector; ΔK+pSIEKJ, transformed with S. intermedius grpE, dnaK, and dnaJ expressing vector.


The HrcA repressor is a negative regulator of both the dnaK operon and the groESL operon of many Gram-positive bacteria, and its activity is positively controlled by the DnaK chaperone system and GroESL (Koch et al., 1998; Mogk et al., 1997). On the other hand, the heat shock response in many Gramnegative bacteria, including E. coli, is regulated by the heat shock transcription factor $\sigma ^ { 3 2 }$ (Arsene` et al., 2000; Yura and Nakahigashi, 1999). Because the activity and stability of $\sigma ^ { 3 2 }$ are negatively controlled by the DnaK chaperone system through the formation of a DnaK–DnaJ– $- \sigma ^ { 3 2 }$ ternary complex, constitutive heat shock induction and the accumulation of $\sigma ^ { 3 2 }$ were observed in the E. coli dnaK null mutant (Bukau and Walker, 1990; Gamer et al., 1992). Nevertheless, whereas S. intermedius does not have $\mathsf { a c } ^ { 3 2 }$ homologue, its DnaK chaperone system was able to reduce the accumulation of σ32 and constitutive heat shock induction in E. coli dnaK null mutants to wild-type levels (Tomoyasu et al., 2012). E. coli DnaK and DnaJ induce conformational changes in $\sigma ^ { 3 2 }$ and, consequently, can modulate stability and activity in vivo (Rodriguez et al., 2008). Similarly, S. intermedius DnaK and DnaJ might form a ternary complex with $\sigma ^ { 3 2 }$ despite the fact that S. intermedius DnaJ has little similarity with E. coli DnaJ. 

Since $\sigma ^ { 3 2 }$ is encoded by rpoH, the E. coli rpoH null mutant has lower levels of the cytosolic chaperones and the heat shock proteases (Tomoyasu et al., 2001). The depletion of the chaperones and proteases results in an accumulation of aggregated proteins in the cell, especially at high temperatures. Expression of the S. intermedius DnaK chaperone system in E. coli rpoH null mutants prevented aggregation of proteins with the same efficiency as the E. coli DnaK chaperone system (Tomoyasu et al., 2012). 

Overall, our findings strongly suggest that the typical Grampositive S. intermedius DnaK chaperone system can show similar activity as the Gram-negative E. coli DnaK chaperone system. The lack of segments 79–101 and 208–211 in the S. intermedius DnaK ATPase domain may affect association with the E. coli cochaperones but not chaperone activity. 

## 14.2.5 Pathogenicity of S. intermedius dnaK null mutants

Heat shock chaperones (e.g., DnaK and ClpB) and heat shock proteases (e.g., Clp and Lon) seem to regulate pathogenicity to sense stresses such as those presented by an effective immune system and accompanying fever in the host organism (Chastanet et al., 2004; Henderson et al., 2006; Ingmer and Brøndsted, 2009; Lourdault et al., 2011). In the case of Gram-negative pathogens, the cellular levels of heat shock chaperones and heat shock proteases are regulated by the DnaK chaperone system to control the stability and activity of σ32 (Arsene` et al., 2000; Yura and Nakahigashi, 1999). Therefore, the Gram-negative DnaK chaperone system seems to be the main regulator of pathogenicity in these organisms. Furthermore, it has been reported that the Grampositive DnaK also plays a role in controlling pathogenicity (Kohler ¨ et al., 2002; Singh et al., 2007). Therefore, we investigated whether DnaK proteins participated in the expression control of virulence factors of S. intermedius. S. intermedius dnaK null mutations in a high-ILY-producing strain (highly pathogenic strain) and a low-ILY-producing strain (low pathogenic strain) could not affect the levels and activities of secreted pathogenic factors such as ILY and hyaluronidase in the culture medium, compared to the wild-type strain (Tomoyasu et al., 2012; unpublished data). However, dnaK null mutants from the high-ILY-producing strain caused attenuation of cytotoxicity. 

Since S. intermedius is normally present in the human oral cavity, these bacteria require mechanisms to utilize nutrients, such as sugar, in order to survive. Interestingly, the catabolite control protein A (CcpA) and the lactose phosphotransferase system repressor (LacR) can regulate transcription of ily by binding to its promoter region (Tomoyasu et al., 2010, 2013). Consequently, the expression of ily was repressed by addition of glucose and activated by addition of galactose to the medium. These findings strongly indicated that the amount and the type of sugar surrounding this bacterium are important factors in its pathogenicity. S. intermedius produces sialidase (NanA), which is a putative virulence factor and is required for the degradation and modification of carbohydrate chains (Takao et al., 2010). This pathogen also produces multisubstrate glycosidase A (MsgA) that shows β-d-galactosidase, β-d-fucosidase, N-acetyl-β-d-glucosaminidase, and N-acetyl-β-d-galactosaminidase activities, and the expression levels of msgA are regulated by the activity of LacR (Imaki et al., 2014). Interestingly, the catalytic products of sugar chains (possibly galactose) by NanA and MsgA were able to activate ILY production (unpublished data). These data strongly suggest that this pathogen has evolved the ability to modulate the levels of ILY in response to sugars such as galactose and glucose, to survive in the human oral cavity. 



virulence and resistance to stress conditions. Infection and Immunity 79(9): 3711–3717. 



In conclusion, S. intermedius DnaK seems to play a fundamental role in vital functions such as growth, thermoresistance, and heat shock regulation but less so in modulating the expression of pathogenic factors.
